### Concerning their nation.

<note>The following text describes the genealogy and reigns of early Northumbrian kings and mentions the Cynfeirdd (the "First Bards" or early Welsh poets).</note>

<unclear>A</unclear>dda, the son of Ida <note>original: "Ada filius Ebba"; the scribe likely confused Ida and Ebba</note>, held the regions in the northern part of Britain, that is, north of the Humber Sea, and he reigned for twelve years; he joined Dinguardi <note>Bamburgh</note> to Bernicia <note>original: "Guurthberneich"</note>.

At that time, Outigirn <note>original: "Dutigirn"</note> fought bravely against the nation of the Angles. Then Talhaearn "Father of the Muse" <note>original: "Tat Aguen"</note> was renowned in poetry; and Aneirin <note>original: "Neirin"</note>, and Taliesin, and Bluchbard, and Cian (who is called "Wheat of Song" <note>original: "Guenith Guaut"</note>), all flourished at the same time in British <note>Welsh</note> poetry.

Maelgwn <note>original: "Mailcunus"</note>, the great king, was reigning among the Britons, specifically in the region of Gwynedd <note>original: "Guenedote"</note>. This was because his ancestor, namely Cunedda <note>original: "Cunedag"</note>, had previously come from the northern part—that is, from the region called Manaw Gododdin <note>original: "Manu Guotodin"</note>—with his sons, who numbered eight. They arrived one hundred and forty-six years before Maelgwn reigned, and they drove out the Scots <note>Irish settlers</note> from those regions with a massive slaughter, and they never returned again to dwell there.

<unclear>A</unclear>dda, the son of Ida, reigned for eight years.
<unclear>A</unclear>ethelric <note>original: "Aedric"</note>, the son of Adda, reigned for four years.
Theodric <note>original: "Deoric"</note>, the son of Ida, reigned for seven years.
Frithuwald <note>original: "Friodolguald"</note> reigned for six years.

## EDITORIAL NOTE.

---

### “TALIESIN” BY SIR JOHN MORRIS-JONES.

The Council of the Honourable Society of Cymmrodorion <note>A learned society established in 1751 for the encouragement of Welsh literature, science, and art.</note> feels great satisfaction in presenting this important contribution to the study of Welsh Literature to the members of the Society. Originally, this work was intended to be a comparatively short review of one of Dr. Gwenogvryn Evans’s valuable reproductions of Early Welsh Texts. However, the Editor of the Society’s publications was fortunate enough to persuade the Author to share with the world—in addition to his critique of Dr. Evans’s theories—the results of many years of close study regarding some of the earliest existing specimens of Welsh Poetry. 

It is not for us to praise the immense service rendered to Wales, its language, and its history by Sir John Morris-Jones. We wish only to express the deep sense of gratitude that will be felt by every lover of literature, and especially by every member of the Cymmrodorion Society, for the unselfish and unpaid labor that has added an invaluable treasure to our store of knowledge. 

For the addition of a helpful Index to the Author’s work, we are much indebted to his daughter, Miss Rhiannon Morris-Jones of the University College, Bangor. The Editor wishes to add that, beyond securing the production of the work in the manner described, his own assistance has been merely nominal.

*On behalf of the Council,* **E. VINCENT EVANS,**
*Honorary Secretary and Editor.*

# Y Cymmrodor.

---
->VOL. XXVIII. “CARED DOETH YR ENCILION.” 1918.<-
---

## Taliesin.

->By J. MORRIS JONES, M.A.,<-

->*Professor of Welsh at the University College of North Wales.*<-

---

Tradition is now generally admitted to be worthy of more respect than was paid to it in the nineteenth century. When it is not the obvious product of popular etymology, it usually contains some element of truth. And it may carry its message from a very remote age. At Mold, there stood a cairn called Bryn yr Ellyllon. “It was believed to be haunted; a specter clad in golden armor had been seen to enter it. That this story was current before the mound was opened is a fact beyond dispute. In 1832 the cairn was explored. Three hundred cartloads of stones were removed, and beneath them was a skeleton ‘laid at full length, wearing a corselet of beautifully-wrought gold, which had been placed on a lining of bronze.’ ”<note>E. Sidney Hartland, *Ethnographical Survey of the United Kingdom*, p. 6, referencing Boyd Dawkins, *Early Man in Britain*, p. 431.</note> The “corselet” is at the British Museum, but it is now stated to be “a peytrel <note>A piece of armor worn on a horse's chest.</note> or breastplate for a pony.”<note>*A Guide to the Antiquities of the Bronze Age* (in the British Museum), p. 149.</note> It is, however, “obvious that before a warrior would decorate his horse with the precious metal, he had doubtless satisfied his own personal needs in this direction.”<note>Ibid., p. 150.</note> Here then we

<sig>B</sig>

<summary>Professor J. Morris Jones argues that historical traditions and folklore often contain elements of truth, citing the discovery of a Bronze Age golden "corselet" in a cairn at Mold as evidence. This archaeological find confirmed long-standing local legends of a golden-clad ghost haunting the site.</summary>

<keywords>Tradition, Taliesin, Bryn yr Ellyllon, Mold Gold Cape, archaeology, Bronze Age, folklore, Welsh history, J. Morris Jones</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the reliability of tradition, moving from a local example in Mold to broader traditions regarding the origins of the Irish and their presence in Britain.</meta>

<page-num>2</page-num>
<header>Taliesin.</header>

have “evidence of a tradition which must have been handed down from the prehistoric Iron Age—that is, for more than two thousand years.”¹ This is a purely local example; but more general traditions are no less likely to be based on fact. The tradition of the Irish that their ancestors came to Ireland directly from the continent has been vindicated by Zimmer against the dominant theory of the last century that they came across Britain.² This theory was first proposed by Edward Lhuyd;³ it was adopted by Theophilus Evans, who quoted in its support a vague tradition about the presence of the Irish in Britain.⁴ The existence of such a tradition in the seventeenth century is confirmed by a statement in Gibson’s *Camden*, 1695, p. 670, “that it is a common tradition among” the inhabitants of the hilly districts of Caernarvonshire, Brecknock and Radnorshire, “that the Irish were the ancient owners of their country.”⁵ That is a fact; but it does not in any way prove Lhuyd’s theory, for those Irish had come over from Ireland.⁶ Here, then, is a fairly widespread tradition that must have been handed down from about the sixth century. Tradition is thus part of our data, to be accounted for and interpreted. Where there is no other apparent reason for it, it may well be what it seems to be—a popular account of what once took place; and where more reliable data are scarce it may be of value in directing inquiry and confirming conclusions.

Among the most persistent of the Welsh traditions is

² *By which path did the Goidels come from the Continent to Ireland?* 1912, p. 31, *and elsewhere*; see H. Gaidoz in the *International Review of Education*, 1917, pp. 104-114.
³ *Archæologia Britannica*, 1707, *To the Welsh*, pp. [xvi-xviii].
⁴ *Mirror of the First Ages* (1740), Reprint, 1902, pp. 11, 12.
⁵ *Y Cymmrodor*, ix, p. 131.
⁶ H. Zimmer, *Nennius Vindicated*, 1893, pp. 89-91.

<summary>The author argues that general traditions, such as the continental origins of the Irish or their historical presence in Wales, often contain factual historical elements. He emphasizes that oral tradition serves as valuable data for historical inquiry, especially when other evidence is scarce.</summary>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Welsh tradition, specifically focusing on the historical authenticity of the sixth-century bards like Taliesin, moving from a general defense of tradition to the specific case of the Cynfeirdd (early bards).</meta>

<page-num>3</page-num>
<header>Taliesin.</header>
<sig>B 2</sig>

that which tells us that a group of famous bards, of whom Taliesin was the chief, flourished during the period of the struggle between Briton and Saxon in the sixth century. If it is objected that this is not a genuine but a spurious tradition based upon a memorandum in the <term>Nennian additamenta</term>, one may reply that the reverse is the case, and that the memorandum is based upon a form of the tradition. For the Welsh tradition is not a reproduction of the memorandum; the memorandum records an early North British variant of it. The sixth-century bards of Welsh tradition are Taliesin, Aneirin, Myrddin, and Llywarch Hen; those of the memorandum are Talhaern, Taliessin, Neirin, Bluchbard, and Cian. The names Taliesin and Neirin are common to both; the Welsh and Nennian variants overlap but do not coincide, which proves their mutual independence and points to both being genuine. The tradition as reflected in Welsh literature bears all the marks of genuineness. It is not advocated or explained like a new theory or discovery; it is taken for granted as common knowledge. Thus in the oldest Welsh copy of the Laws (the Black Book of Chirk), when reference is made to an expedition led by Rhun ap Maelgwn, it is simply stated that Taliesin composed an <term>englyn</term> on the occasion.¹ There is no mention of the date, or even of the century. It is not explained that Taliesin was a contemporary of Rhun; it is only implied. The tradition is not superimposed on the subject matter of Welsh literature; it is a substratum which underlies it. Moreover, the conditions for handing down such a tradition were favorable. The bards formed an important body whose status was acknowledged; their privileges and duties are defined in the Laws of Hywel: the

---

¹ *Ancient Laws and Institutes of Wales*, 1841, i, p. 104.

<summary>The author argues that the tradition of Taliesin and his contemporary bards is a genuine, historical substratum of Welsh literature rather than a later invention. He notes that the discrepancies between Welsh tradition and the Nennian memorandum suggest they are independent, authentic accounts of the same sixth-century events.</summary>

<keywords>Taliesin, Nennian additamenta, Aneirin, Myrddin, Llywarch Hen, Black Book of Chirk, Rhun ap Maelgwn, englyn, Laws of Hywel, Welsh bardic tradition</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Welsh bardic tradition, focusing on the legal status of bards in the Welsh Laws and the specific values assigned to certain poems for use in competitions.</meta>

<page-num>4</page-num>
# Taliesin.

The household bard is the eighth officer of the Court;¹ the chief bard is to sit next to the judge and stay with the Heir Apparent.² Bardism was one of the three professions (the other two being scholarship and smithing) for which no serf was to be trained without his lord's permission;³ it was clearly an ancient institution when the Laws were compiled, and no break in its continuity is likely between the sixth and the tenth centuries. Part of a bard's training consisted of memorizing the works of the ancients. The recitation of this traditional poetry formed an important feature of bardic contests; and for this purpose, certain poems attributed to the bards of the sixth century (and doubtless others) had fixed values attached to them. There are indications of this in the thirteenth-century *Book of Taliesin*, in which the titles of some of the poems are followed by their values; for example (pp. 30–40):

->Glaswawt Taliessin. xxiiii. a tal.    Kadeir Kerrituen. ccc.<-
->Kadeir Taliessin. xxiiii.    Kanu y gwynt. ccc. a tal.<-
->Kadeir Teyrnon. ccc.    Kanu y med. xxiiii.<-
->Kanu y cwrwf. xxiiii.<-

The expression "xxiiii. a tal" means literally "24 is what it is worth"; similarly, "ccc. a tal" means "300 is what it is worth"; in the other cases, only the figure is given. The matter is made quite clear in a note which follows the third of the *gorchaneu* (epilogues?) appended to the *Gododdin* in the *Book of Aneirin* (p. 28):

->Eman e tervyna gwarchan kynvelyn. Canu un canuawc a dal pob awdyl or gododin herwyd breint yng kerd amrysson. Tri chanu a thriugeint a thrychant a dal pob un or gwa-r chaneu. Sef achaws yw am goffau ene gorchaneu rivedi e<-

That is:

> Here ends the *gorchan* of Cynvelyn. Each stanza of the Gododdin is worth a song of one measure <note>that is, of one unit</note> according to privilege in a song-contest. Each of the *gorchaneu* is worth 363 songs <note>that is, units</note>, the reason being that the number of the men who went to Catraeth is commemorated in the *gorchaneu*. Just as a man should not go to battle without arms, no bard should go to a contest without this song <note>in his repertoire</note>. Here now begins the *gorchan* of Maelderw. Taliesin composed it, and gave it privilege equal to <note>that given</note> to all the stanzas of the Gododdin and its three *gorchaneu* in a song-contest.

The ancient poetry which thus formed part of the bard’s stock-in-trade was called *hengerdd*, as seen in the following triad from the *Red Book of Hergest*, col. 1142.

->Tripheth a beir y gerdawr vot yn amyl. kyfarwydyt<-
->ystoryaeu. a bardoniaeth. a *hengerd*.<-

‘Three things that cause a minstrel to be well-equipped: lore of stories, the art of poetry, and ancient verse.’ In the above quotations we obtain a glimpse of the activities of the medieval bards. They formed an organized body, not only fitted to be the vehicle of tradition, but actually having this as one of its recognized functions. Their *hengerdd*, or ancient verse, included poems attributed to Taliesin and Aneirin: “*Hengerdd Talyessin*, the ancient song of Taliesin,” says Phylip Brydydd, “was new for nine times seven years” (*Myv. Arch.* ²259a); and Dafydd Benfras prays for a muse “to sing a panegyric like Aneirin of old on the day when he sang the Gododdin” (*Myv. Arch.* 217a). The tradition, of which the bards were thus the special custodians, was generally

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the transmission of ancient Welsh poetry, shifting from the role of medieval bards to the earliest written records of the language.</meta>

<page-num>6</page-num>
# Taliesin.

accepted in the Middle Ages, and handed down to modern times.

The oldest examples of written Welsh are found between the lines and in the margins of Latin manuscripts. They consist of (1) glosses, specifically single words and short phrases explanatory of the Latin, and generally inserted immediately above the words explained, and (2) short memoranda and fragments of prose and verse, written in margins and blank spaces. They date from the eighth or ninth to the eleventh century; and their language is called Old Welsh. They are written in the so-called Hiberno-Saxon character, and their orthography differs widely, but regularly, from that of Medieval Welsh. Medieval scribes, copying Old Welsh, converted its orthography into that of their own day, except when they failed to understand it, in which case they transcribed it mechanically. Complete books in Old Welsh must have existed; but not one has survived. The literature recorded in them is to be found, if at all, only in later copies.

The oldest extant manuscript written entirely in Welsh is the *Black Book of Carmarthen*, which dates from about the end of the twelfth century. It contains verse only; two poems are by the twelfth-century bard Cynddelw; but most of the pieces and collections of stanzas and *englynion* are anonymous. The first poem is set out in the form of a dialogue between Myrddin and Taliesin. The rhymes prove it to be a late production.<note>See my *Welsh Grammar*, 1913, p. 73. The suggestion (*Black Book*, ed. J. G. Evans, p. 161) that two dialogues have been run into one by turning over two leaves of the copy is mistaken. The "change of meter at 4.7" is a change from lines of 9 syllables to a *cyhydedd hir* of 19. But lines of 9 immediately follow, so that there is no abrupt total change as the above assumption implies. The two measures are often linked together, and in combination form a variety of the meter called *gwawdodyn*.</note> There is

---

<page-num>7</page-num>
# Taliesin.

There is another dialogue in which Taliesin appears, his companion being a person named Ugnach vab Mydno. The collection of stanzas called *Hoianeu* and *Afallenneu*, commonly attributed to Myrddin, deal mostly with events of the twelfth century, although some of them are concerned with Rhydderch Hael and Gwenddoleu, the northern kings with whom tradition associated Laloecen<note>¹ Joceline’s *Life of St. Kentigern*, xlv, in *Historians of Scotland*, vol. v, p. 241.</note> or Myrddin Wyllt. These stanzas may be old, and, in any case, probably form the type after which the others—which belong to the large class of spurious prophecies—were modeled. The manuscript also contains a stanza on page 46 that is found in the Book of Taliesin on page 44, and several *englynion* usually attributed to Llywarch Hên.

The *Book of Aneirin*, a manuscript from about the middle of the thirteenth century,<note>² The date, “*circa* 1350,” in the *Report on Manuscripts in the Welsh Language*, ii, 91, is an error, corrected to “*circa* 1250” in a footnote to page iv of the Introduction. Such an error in reporting the most important fact in the volume ought at least to have been corrected in large type in a prominent position, and not hidden away without even an apology, as if it were a matter of no account.</note> contains the Gododdin and its *gorchaneu*, referred to above. The Gododdin appears as a string of stanzas, evidently recovered from oral tradition in which the sequence and relation of the parts had been lost. The last five pages of the manuscript contain a number of stanzas in Old Welsh spelling, only slightly modified here and there; the scribe resorted to literal transcription due to the difficulty and corrupt state of the text in his original. A few of these stanzas correspond to stanzas in later spelling in the body of the book; but the differences between them show that the originals must have differed, and suggest that the later readings are in some cases mere conjectures. It is, however, clear

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the transmission of the Gododdin and introduces other major Welsh manuscripts including the Book of Taliesin and the Red Book of Hergest.</meta>

<page-num>8</page-num>
# Taliesin.

...that the Gododdin was written down in the Old Welsh period, and that the text was then uncertain, which implies that it had been handed down from an even earlier period. The *gorchaneu* seem to show that additions were made to the original poem; and in oral transmission, interpolations from these were natural, and may explain the references in the poem in its present state to Aneirin’s death and to Dyfnwal Frych, who died in 642.<note>¹ Skene, *Four Ancient Books of Wales*, ii, 360.</note> The evidence of the manuscript then, so far as it goes, is not inconsistent with the tradition regarding the date of the original poem.

The *Book of Taliesin* is a manuscript of the late thirteenth century containing a collection of poems rightly or wrongly attributed to Taliesin.

The *Red Book of Hergest*, in the Library of Jesus College, Oxford, is a manuscript of the late fourteenth century containing a large quantity of Welsh prose and verse. Most of the poetry is the work of medieval bards, written in the standard medieval meters, each poem being duly ascribed to its author. The rest consists of what passed at the time as ancient verse. The first poem in the *Book of Taliesin*, which is there incomplete because the first leaf has been lost, is found complete in the *Red Book*. Another poem professing to be by Taliesin (*Anrec Urien*, Skene, *F.A.B.*, ii, 291) is found in the detached portion of the *White Book of Rhydderch*<note>² *Report on Manuscripts in the Welsh Language*, i, 324.</note> in Peniarth MS 12, and it is possible that this, and one or two other poems found here, may have been included in the lost portion of the *Book of Taliesin*. The *Red Book* also contains the *Gwasgargerdd* and *Kyvoesi*, *englynion* of prophecies (written after the events) pretending to be by Myrddin, and a large collection of the *englynion* usually attributed to Llywarch Hen.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Welsh poetic forms and the manuscript history of poems attributed to early bards like Taliesin and Myrddin, focusing on the formation of the Hengwrt collection.</meta>

<page-num>9</page-num>
# Taliesin.

Most of the *englynion*, especially the last ones mentioned, are of the forms called in the Red Book Grammar (column 1129) *o'r hen ganiad*—meaning "of ancient composition"—and are actually exemplified in Old Welsh writing from the ninth century in the Cambridge Juvencus codex.

Some stray poems and stanzas attributed to the early bards are found in other medieval manuscripts. For instance, in Peniarth MS 3, part ii, written about 1300,<note>¹ *Report on Manuscripts in the Welsh Language*, i, 303.</note> are found the *Kyvoesi*, *Hoianeu*, and *Afallenneu*, attributed to Myrddin. Additionally, the detached portion of the White Book (early fourteenth century) contains the *Gwasgargerdd Vyrddin* in addition to the Taliesin poem mentioned above.<note>² Ibid., 325. The text of the detached portion of the White Book was printed by Phillimore in *Y Cymmrodor*, vii, pp. 123-154.</note>

There are, of course, many later copies of the poems; the bards continued to read and study them and seem to have become owners of some of the old copies. According to entries in the margins of page 20, the Book of Aneirin belonged to Gwilym Tew and Dafydd Nanmor in the fifteenth century. In the seventeenth century, when bardism as a profession was rapidly declining, the antiquary Robert Vaughan of Hengwrt (1592–1666) brought together almost all the most valuable Welsh manuscripts then in existence, except for the Red Book of Hergest. He secured the Black Book of Carmarthen, the Black Book of Chirk, the Book of Aneirin, the Book of Taliesin, the White Book of Rhydderch, and many others; in the entire history of collections and collectors, it may be doubted whether such a complete acquisition of all the choicest material was ever made by one man.<note>³ It is not suggested that Robert Vaughan had no agents or helpers. Lewis Morris, in a list of over forty manuscript collections known to him, says that the Hengwrt collection was “made by Robert Vaughan and his friends John Jones of Gelli Lyfdy and William Morris of Cefn y Braich.” — *Report on MSS. in the Welsh Lang.*, ii, p. 838.</note> The Hengwrt Library with some

<header>Taliesin.</header>

Additional volumes became the Peniarth Library, which is now, through Sir John Williams’s noble gift to the nation, accessible to students in the National Library of Wales. But the Book of Aneirin had disappeared from the Hengwrt collection at the end of the eighteenth century;¹ it was bought "from a person at Aberdâr," became the property of Carnhuanawc, and then of Sir Thomas Philipps, and is now safe in the Cardiff Free Library.² The term <term>Cynfeirdd</term>, by which the bards of the period from the sixth to the eleventh century are now known, seems to be due to the happy inspiration of Robert Vaughan, who transcribed their reputed works in a volume which he called "Y Kynveirdh Kymreig."³

In the eighteenth century, Lewis Morris of Anglesey (1701–1765) and his brothers Richard (1703–1779) and William (1705–1763)—who by their own application had acquired considerable proficiency in most of the literary and scientific learning current in their day—found the most absorbing of their many intellectual interests in the literature and antiquities of Wales. They copied and collected manuscripts, corresponded with each other and with Welsh and English scholars, and instructed and encouraged younger bards and scholars such as Goronwy Owen and Evan Evans. Richard and Lewis were the founders of the Honourable Society of Cymmrodorion in 1751, and through the Society and by their own personal efforts, they succeeded in interesting all classes of Welshmen in the history and

literature of their native land.”¹ Evan Evans (1731–1789), whom the Morrises called “Ieuan Brydydd Hir,”² the nickname by which he is generally known, rather than by his own bardic name of Ieuan Fardd ac Offeiriad, corresponded with them regularly, as well as with Gray, Percy, and others. He explored the little-known field of Welsh manuscripts and communicated his discoveries to the Morrises. Lewis Morris, writing to Edward Richard of Ystrad Meurig on August 5th, 1758, tells him that he has at his elbow “no less a man than Ieuan Brydydd Hir, who has discovered some old manuscripts lately that no one of this age ever as much as dreamed of; and this discovery is to him and me as great as that of America by Columbus. We have found an Epic Poem in the British language called *Gododin*.”³ Later, Lewis Morris acquainted himself with the poetry of the <term>Cynfeirdd</term> in the Hengwrt Library itself, and “had a design of printing many, or most of those ancient poems.”⁴ Evans’s *Specimens of the Poetry of the Ancient Welsh Bards*, including his *De Bardis Dissertatio*, was published by Dodsley in 1764. “It was first thought of, and encouraged,” he says in his Preface, “some years before the name of Ossian was known in England.” During the greater part of his life, Evans spent much of his time in copying Welsh manuscripts; he transcribed all the works of the old and medieval bards, together with the <term>Bruts</term> and other prose works, which he hoped, in vain, to see published. In great poverty in his last days, he handed over his transcripts to Paul Panton of Plas Gwyn, Pentraeth, Anglesey, in return for an annuity of £20 a

year.¹ The project he had envisioned was, however, carried out later by Owen Jones (*Owain Myfyr*), who edited it in collaboration with William Owen [Pughe] and Edward Williams (*Iolo Morganwg*). At his own expense, he published the *Myvyrian Archaiology of Wales* in three large octavo volumes; the first two appeared in 1801, and the third in 1807. The editors had access to Lewis Morris’s collection of manuscripts, which contained “mostly ancient poetry,” and they dedicated the first volume to Paul Panton, the son, for “his generosity in lending his manuscripts.” They acknowledge (vol. i, pp. xiii, xiv) that these consisted mostly of transcripts by Evan Evans. They describe Evans’s work at some length, mentioning his intention to “send a portion of what he had thus collected to the printing press.”² The first volume of the *Myvyrian* contains only poetry and includes all the poems attributed to the <term>Cynfeirdd</term>, and practically all the known work of the medieval bards. The reproduction was, for its time, a very commendable achievement; it clearly follows the copies used with great care, and for much of our medieval poetry, it remains the only available printed text to this day. The entire work was reprinted in one large volume by Gee of Denbigh in 1870.

The *Myvyrian* text of the <term>Cynfeirdd</term>, which was printed from late copies, was superseded in 1868 by the appearance of Skene’s *Four Ancient Books of Wales*. The second volume of this work contains a reasonably accurate reproduction of the text of the *Black Book of Carmarthen*, the *Book of Aneirin*, the *Book of Taliesin*, and the old poetry in the *Red Book of Hergest*. In recent years, Dr. Gwenogvryn Evans has published the entirety of the first three both in photographic facsimile and in printed reproductions that attain the highest degree of accuracy that seems humanly possible.<note>¹ I have discovered only one actual error in these reproductions: *waeawawr* instead of *waewawr*, B.A., 9.18. In the *Black Book*, 51.9, two *i*’s are printed as a *u* with two accents above it, which represents the appearance of the original, but the correct reading is *ii*.</note>

The first substantial contribution to the discussion of the authenticity of these poems was *A Vindication of the Genuineness of the Ancient British Poems of Aneurin, Taliesin, Llywarch Hen and Merdhin, with Specimens of the Poems*, by Sharon Turner, F.A.S., 1803. The author had cited the Welsh bards in his *History of the Anglo-Saxons*; the *Vindication* was prompted by an attack on that work in the *Critical Review* of January 1800, and by the declarations of Pinkerton and Malcolm Laing against the authenticity of the poems. Sharon Turner effectively exposes his critics’ ignorance of the subject, but he is more interested in following up the inquiry which their criticisms led him to undertake. His *Vindication* is a sensible and moderate statement of the case. His arguments, untangled and summarized as briefly as possible, are as follows:

1.—British bards existed in the sixth century. This is proved, among other things, by the denunciations of Gildas, who accuses the British kings of listening only to “their

<meta>This page continues the summary of Sharon Turner’s arguments from his "Vindication," providing evidence for the historical existence and authenticity of the early Welsh bards.</meta>

<header>Taliesin.</header>

own praises . . . from the mouths of scoundrel proclaimers” pp. 105-6.

2.—The Nennian memorandum clearly names Taliesin among the bards of the sixth century; and Turner, following Evans, correctly interprets the misreading *Nuevin* as Aneirin, and supposes *Bluchbard* to be Llywarch, pp. 116-7.

3.—He quotes numerous passages from the bards of the twelfth and thirteenth centuries which show that they knew of Taliesin, Aneirin, Myrddin, and Llywarch as bards of a past age, and were acquainted with their reputed works, pp. 40-82. Geoffrey of Monmouth, in a Latin poem, makes Taliesin and Myrddin contemporaries of Gildas, p. 123.

4.—Giraldus says that the bards in the twelfth century had “ancient and authentic books written in Welsh,” p. 142. The memory of Myrddin’s prophecies had been retained among them, “verbally by many, in writing by very few,” p. 147. They had added many of their own to the genuine ones; but Giraldus could distinguish between the genuine and the spurious by the language and style, p. 147.

5.—Manuscripts from the twelfth and the two following centuries, containing the reputed works of these bards, have been preserved, pp. 25-30. The lack of earlier manuscripts is satisfactorily explained: “Time and accident consume manuscripts as well as buildings and men. Old copies decay or are lost, and new ones follow,” p. 21. Wales was particularly exposed to the destruction of invaders, p. 24. The value and importance of *old manuscripts* is a relatively late discovery; manuscripts were preferred for considerations other than age, p. 22. <note>He might have added that Old Welsh manuscripts were written in a difficult system of spelling and an obsolete script.</note> “It is therefore a matter of

<meta>This page continues Sharon Turner’s argument regarding the authenticity of ancient Welsh poems, specifically addressing the scarcity of early manuscripts and introducing internal evidence from the poems themselves.</meta>

Lack of ancient manuscript authority is not peculiar to these poems: “Of the numerous Greek and Latin works which we possess, how few are there of which very ancient manuscripts can be cited!” p. 21. We admit their genuineness although, if we examined the evidence for it, “we should find that as far as antiquity of manuscripts was concerned, it is very slight,” p. 22. In this respect, therefore, these Welsh poems stand on the same footing as most other ancient writings.

6.—Summing up the above evidence, Turner submits “that unless the internal evidence of these poems is very clearly and decisively hostile to their antiquity, no reasonable man can discredit their genuineness,” p. 151. For our present purpose, the internal evidence may be classified thus: the evidence of (*a*) the matter, (*b*) the versification, and (*c*) the language.

(*a*) Under this head Turner maintains “That the subjects of this poetry could serve no purpose of interest in the twelfth century. That their subjects were the most unlikely of all others for a forger to have chosen. That Arthur is spoken of in a manner inconsistent with the supposition of forgery. That the subjects are such as, if genuine, might be expected from their real authors. . . That their historical allusions are true,” pp. 19-20, 151 ff.

(*b*) Rhyme is the most conspicuous feature of the versification. It had been objected that rhyme was unknown in Europe before the eighth century; Turner shows that it was practiced in the fourth, pp. 250-254.

(*c*) “On the language of these bards, it is very favorable to the genuineness of these poems, that . . . they have not been found intelligible by many modern Welshmen. Evans . . . mentions this several times,” p. 197.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Sharon Turner’s defense of the antiquity of ancient Welsh poems, transitioning from historical and thematic evidence to philological considerations and the critiques of later scholars like Thomas Stephens.</meta>

<page-num>16</page-num>
<header>Taliesin.</header>

This is the only point made by Turner under this heading; and it is, of course, a substantial one, since it establishes some presumption of antiquity. But with the progress of linguistic science, philological considerations have formed the chief obstacle to the acceptance of the view which he shows to be probable on other grounds. In a later section of this paper, I propose to discuss the question of whether these considerations are of such weight as to tip the scales decisively against all other evidence.

Turner knew, of course, as Giraldus knew in the twelfth century, that many poems attributed to these bards are later productions. He therefore gives a list of the poems which he considers genuine; those of Taliesin are: “The Poems to Urien, and on his battles. His dialogue with Merdhin. The Poems on Elphin. And his Historical Elegies,” p. 33. He adds, “There are several others, however, especially of Taliesin, which may be genuine,” p. 34.

Thomas Stephens, in his *Literature of the Kymry*, 1849, finds himself in general agreement with Turner’s conclusions: “I regret,” he writes, “being compelled to differ in opinion, respecting this <note>Armes Prydein Vawr</note> and the poems of Merddin, from the eminent historian and critic, to whose learning, intelligence, and candor, the literature of my native land is so greatly indebted; but it is a source of sincere gratification to reflect, that in nearly every other essential point, my own researches have tended to ratify his conclusions, as to the genuineness of most of the poems attributed to the early bards, Aneurin, and Llywarch, and many of those of Taliesin,” p. 288. With respect to the last mentioned poems, he says that, as many of them “may upon most substantial grounds be shown to be genuine, it becomes of importance to distinguish between those which are, and those which may not

The actual number of poems in his list is not 77, but 73; and many of these are not found in the Book of Taliesin, while only the fourth of the *gorchaneu* <note>A term for a specific type of ancient Welsh song or incantation found in the Book of Aneirin</note> (Gorchan Maelderw) is attributed to Taliesin in the Book of Aneirin. In a series of articles which appeared subsequently in the *Archaeologia Cambrensis*, 1851–1853, Stephens added to the class of genuine poems three previously classed as doubtful and two others.

In 1853, Stephens wrote for the Abergavenny Eisteddfod a valuable treatise on the *Gododdin*, with text and translation, which was edited by Professor Powel and published by the Honourable Society of Cymmrodorion in 1888. In the historical illustrations and the Introductory Essay, "he had on certain points," says the editor, "anticipated by a whole generation conclusions which have been subsequently drawn by other writers." Two poems of Taliesin are translated in the Essay, pp. 67, 73.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of scholarship on Taliesin, shifting from Thomas Stephens to examine D. W. Nash's 1858 critique of Edward Davies's druidical and mythological interpretations of the ancient Welsh poems.</meta>

<page-num>18</page-num>
# Taliesin.

In 1858, **D. W. Nash** published his *Taliesin; or, the Bards and Druids of Britain*. The greater part of the work is devoted to the refutation of the druidical interpretation of the *Cynfeirdd* poems proposed by the **Rev. Edward Davies**, whose two volumes, *Celtic Researches*, 1804, and *The Mythology and Rites of the British Druids*, 1809, Nash rightly characterizes as “monuments of misapplied learning,” p. 7. **Davies** had embraced the fantastic theory of **Jacob Bryant** that all ancient mythology was derived from a corruption of revealed religion which took place after the Flood. It occurred to him that this grand idea must supply the key to the obscurities of ancient Welsh literature; he satisfied himself that druidism was a form of <term>helio-arkite</term> worship; that the bards cherished its doctrines in secret down to the middle ages; and that the works of the *Cynfeirdd* are full of helio-demonic lore and arkite mysteries. Now, to approach these poems with a preconceived theory is fatal; their language is so obscure and difficult that a person with a fixed idea, especially if his notions of Welsh inflection and syntax are a little vague, is certain to discover in them exactly what he is looking for. **Edward Davies** looked for his mysteries, and found them; and his views gained such acceptance that it was perhaps necessary, even in the late fifties, to refute them. This task **Nash** accomplishes vigorously and successfully. The apparent good sense of his translations heightens by contrast the obvious nonsense of **Davies’s**. The contrast is often more striking than it would be if that sense were the sense of the original; but in many cases **Nash’s** renderings, when compared with the text, are even more ludicrous than **Davies’s** own. Thus *Pawb i Adonai ar weryd Pwmpai*, rendered by **Davies** “We all attend on Adonai on the area of Pwmpai,” which is literal except that “we” and “attend” are

<summary>This page discusses D. W. Nash’s 1858 critique of Edward Davies, who had incorrectly interpreted ancient Welsh poems as containing secret Druidical mysteries. Nash refutes Davies's "helio-arkite" theories, though the author notes that Nash's own translations could sometimes be as inaccurate as those he criticized.</summary>
<keywords>D. W. Nash, Edward Davies, Taliesin, Cynfeirdd, Druidism, Jacob Bryant, helio-arkite worship, Welsh literature, literary criticism</keywords>

<sig>c 2</sig>

supplied, is translated by Nash as “Every one of the idiots banging on the ground,” p. 257. Not being limited by any particularly precise knowledge of Welsh inflections and word formations, Nash plays fast and loose with them, producing whatever seems sensible and fits the situation. Thus, *Unynt tanc gan aethant golluddion*, which means “They made peace, for they had become weary,” is translated by him as “They were quiet whose entrails went (out of their wounds),” <note>1 The *ll* in *go-lluddion*, meaning “rather weary” (compare *go-llwng*, etc.), should have prevented the possibility of it being even briefly mistaken for *coluddion*, meaning “entrails.” Nash could not, of course, be aware of the fact that the verb “to go” is used to mean “become” with *all* adjectives in Welsh, just as it is in English with some—for example, “to go mad.” He could not, therefore, have guessed the meaning “had become weary”; so he read *unynt* (“joined”) as if it were *oeddynt* (“were”), and turned the noun *tanc* (“peace”) into the adjective “quiet.”</note> p. 98. But despite all the freedom he allows himself, he does not always succeed in creating any possible meaning, though he is not in the least bothered by that; he possesses that naive mindset which naturally assumes that whatever it does not understand is simply nonsense.

For example, four lines referring to a cow, which mean “At midday it will be lowing, at midnight it will be boiling; it will be boiled on land, it will be eaten in ships,” are translated by him as “On a fine day lowing, on a fine night boiling; in the land of the boiler the timid shall be in tranquility,” with a footnote pointing out that “these lines, though unintelligible, are not more so than the prophecies of Merlin in Geoffrey,” etc., p. 258. He never suspects that there may be a meaning which he has failed to discover. His actual contribution to the interpretation of the poems has been overestimated; even **Matthew Arnold** was deceived by how plausible he seemed. He translates, on the most absurd grounds, “Politeness is—

The refutation of Davies's theories formed only part of Nash's purpose, which was to cast doubt on the authenticity of all the old Welsh poetry. He held that it was forged in the twelfth century or later. He is as intent on proving his theory as Davies was. Matthew Arnold is under no misapprehension with regard to this: "his determined skepticism about Welsh antiquity seems to me, however, to betray a preconceived hostility, a bias taken beforehand, as unmistakable as Mr. Davies's prepossessions." <note>2 *Ibid.*, pp. 28-29.</note> His bias comes out most clearly when he is dealing with the historical poems, for here he seems half conscious that he cannot establish his contention. His treatment of the *Gododdin* is hesitating and uncertain; he seems almost to admit that the poem may be ancient, then suggests a qualification, and when you think he is about to come to a decision he goes off to something else, and leaves the decision in the air. Even when he deals with the historical poems of Taliesin, we have the admission that the corrupt state of one of them may arise "perhaps from the antiquity of the original songs, fragments of which have thus been orally preserved," p. 90; but the idea is not followed up. Having given his own translations of the poems, he proceeds to show that they must be spurious by taking that which "bears the most apparently genuine character," namely *Gwaith Argoed Llwyfain*, and showing that it contains an anachronism which proves it late, pp. 119-120. This is done as follows: in the poem the Saxon chief appears and calls for hostages; two speeches follow—a reply by Owein ap Urien, and a call to his men by Urien himself. Nash puts only the first part of this speech in quotation marks, and claims that the rest represents a speech by Ceneu ap Coel, who had long been dead. He gives a genealogical table to show that Owein was a descendant of Ceneu ap Coel in the fifth degree. But the obvious meaning of the passage is that Owein declares that he will not be unworthy of his ancestor; and in the translation in Sharon Turner’s book, *the whole speech is included in quotation marks*. It is therefore difficult to acquit Nash of the charge of disingenuousness brought against him by Skene (*F.A.B.*, i, 15). After this piece of special pleading, Nash goes on to say that "without venturing to decide that these Songs to Urien were not rewritten in the twelfth century from materials originally dating to the sixth, and that there are no poetical remains in the Welsh language older than the twelfth century, we may nevertheless assert that the common assumption of such remains dating to the sixth century has been made on very unsatisfactory grounds," and puts the burden of proof on those who differ from him. He ends the chapter by stating that "the internal evidence, even of the so-called ‘Historical Poems’ themselves, is, in some instances at least, opposed to their claims to an origin in the sixth century," which Matthew Arnold calls "an unsatisfactory turn to give to the matter, and a lame and impotent conclusion to his chapter."<note>1 *The Study of Celtic Literature*, popular edition, 1891, p. 48.</note> His weakness here is no doubt caused by a feeling that in his examination of these particular poems he has not found much to support his theory; indeed, he has found some facts which seem to run counter to it. <meta>This page continues the discussion of scholarly approaches to Welsh literature, specifically contrasting Nash's skepticism with the work of W. F. Skene and others in the nineteenth century.</meta>

# Taliesin.

<page-num>22</page-num>

rooted in the *a priori* probabilities of the case. It was an attitude natural to him, and typical of the time. “It has been the mission of the nineteenth century,” says Mr. Sainsbury, “to prove that everybody’s work was written by somebody else, and it will not be the most useless task of the twentieth century to betake itself to more profitable inquiries.”

The texts which appear in the second volume of *The Four Ancient Books of Wales* had been transcribed and printed some years before the appearance of the work in 1868, and Skene had obtained translations of the printed texts from two Welsh scholars: the Book of Taliesin was translated by the Rev. Robert Williams, and the other texts by the Rev. D. Silvan Evans. Most of the historical poems relate to the struggles between the Britons of the north and the men of Deira and Bernicia; and Skene, with the aid of the translations, examined them in the light of such information concerning the Northern kingdoms in that obscure period as can be gleaned from other sources, and made out a strong case for their substantial genuineness. He came to the conclusion that it was not till the seventh century that they “were brought into shape, and assumed a consistent form . . . . It is in the seventh century that I place these poems in their earliest consistent shape, and I do not attempt to take them further back”, i, p. 243. He was, of course, aware of the fact that in oral transmission the pronunciation of the words changed insensibly with the change that went on in the spoken language, and that when written down they appeared in the orthography in use at the date of the manuscript, i, p. 216. Some of Skene’s identifications of places in the North are more than doubtful; and his philological chapters are of no value. But the work as a whole forms a most important contribution to the study of the poems.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of late nineteenth-century scholarly skepticism regarding the authenticity of early Welsh historical poems, specifically examining the views of Sir John Rhŷs and others who questioned the existence of poets like Taliesin and Aneirin.</meta>

# Taliesin.

<page-num>23</page-num>

Since the publication of Skene’s work, it has been generally recognized that the *Gododdin* and other historical poems are older than the twelfth century; however, there has been little inclination to admit that any of them are what they claim to be. Sir John Rhŷs, in his *Lectures on Welsh Philology* (1879, p. 139), says that “they date, in some form or other, from the ninth century, if not earlier.” In his *Arthurian Legend* (1891, p. 241), he points out that parts of the *Gododdin* appear in the manuscript in an orthography which may be as old as the ninth century; yet he would not think of calling its author Aneirin—no, he is “the poet of the *Gododdin*” (p. 242). Similarly, in his *Hibbert Lectures* (1888), he treats the *Book of Taliesin* as a repository of traditional lore rooted in Celtic paganism; but he suggests that Taliesin himself is a myth and equates the second element of his name with the name of Ossīn, better known as Ossian, “the great mythic poet of the Goidels” (p. 551). In 1892, he tacitly abandoned this equation by explaining Ossīn or Oisīn as Pictish (*Proc. Ant. Scot.*, 329); however, the name actually occurs as the Irish form of the Saxon name Oswin (Plummer’s *Bede*, ii, p. 163). A cruder attempt to mythologize Taliesin through etymology was made by Mr. J. Rogers Rees in the *Arch. Camb.* (1898, p. 331): “Taliessin, Telessin, or Telyessin is clearly Norse for bard or skald . . . *tal* (*tali*) = speech, language, a tale . . . *á sýn* = appearance, shape.” The futility of this sort of argument was wittily exposed by Henry Rogers in the *Eclipse of Faith* (1852), where it is shown how a future Dr. Dickkopf would prove the impossibility of the “papal aggression” of 1850. The names “tell their own tale, and almost, as it were, proclaim of themselves that they are allegorical. Wiseman, Newman (two of them, it should be noted) . . . Thus the name ‘Wiseman’ is evidently chosen to represent the proverbial

He carefully guards himself against drawing hasty conclusions about the late date of the poems from certain isolated allusions; for example, "the reference in line 885 of the *Gododdin* to the death of Donald Brec, who died in 642, shows that the line, *at any rate in the form found there, is subsequent to that date*"; and a reference to Bede in the *Book of Taliesin* proves "that the poem containing the reference was later than his time, *that is, unless the line or the reference was interpolated*," in the same work. The words which I have italicized show that he did not wish to commit himself to the statement that the *Gododdin* was not older than 642, or that the Taliesin poem was not older than Bede. Yet he is even more careful not to suggest that the poems are by Aneirin or Taliesin. To show what trust is to be placed on such attributions, he points out a discrepancy which, by his own error, he thinks he has found in the *Book of Aneirin*. The stanzas in old spelling follow *Gorchan Maelderw*; but Anwyl mistakenly took them to be part of it and notes that *Gorchan Maelderw* is attributed to Taliesin, while some of the old stanzas appear in the *Gododdin* proper, which is attributed to Aneirin, page 70. But who were those families? It may therefore be said that the glorification of imaginary families has not been shown to provide even a probable motive for the composition of the poems. (2) How were the poems written—how could Welsh bards in later centuries know of the persons and places in the North with which the poems deal? Anwyl’s reply is given in his first paper, p. 64. There were annals such as the old memoranda appended to the work of Nennius; and “it is highly probable that chronicles of similar type [to these] supplied the personal and local names which have been incorporated in the poems of the *Four Ancient Books*.” The systematic faking implied here seems to me, I confess, improbable. ...this view. He is speaking of a tradition of Northern names in Wales: “It does not follow necessarily, however, that the tradition should have been continuous on its poetic side (though it is possible that this may have been the case to some extent).” Thus, it is possible that some ancient poetry was handed down. This admission of the possibility of a living tradition seems to make it unnecessary to assume that the "dry bones" of historical records were merely dressed up to look like a living tradition.

It is evident that the difficulties of explaining the historical poems as late fabrications have not been overcome. The possibility of a continuous poetic tradition is admitted “to some extent.” Why is it not admitted frankly and fully as the simple and obvious explanation of the facts? The reason is that linguistic theory stands in the way. The reason is not explored at length, but it is mentioned once in Anwyl’s “*Prolegomena*,” page 63: “it is obvious from the rhyme alone that all the old poems were composed after the old declensional and conjugational endings had been entirely lost.” That is true: but unless we know at what period that loss took place, it determines nothing. The period is not stated, probably because it was considered to be common knowledge. It was generally assumed that the inflections were not lost in the sixth century; therefore, the poems must be later. Through the loss of those endings, the <term>Ancient British language</term> became the <term>Welsh language</term>; the British name *Maglocunos* became the Welsh name *Mailcun*, and later *Maelgwn*. What the above statement implies, then, is this: No poems can have been written in the Welsh language in the sixth century, because in the sixth century the Welsh language itself did not exist.

It is clear that, if this hypothesis as to the age of the language is well-founded, it overrules every other consideration; and it is not to be wondered at that under

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the linguistic transition from Ancient British to Welsh, specifically addressing the evidence from Gildas and early inscriptions to determine if Welsh poetry could have existed in the sixth century.</meta>

<page-num>28</page-num>

# Taliesin.

Due to its influence, little value has been attached to the tradition concerning the poems. But it must not be forgotten that this is no more than a hypothesis. It is based on the following facts: Gildas uses the British forms of proper names; he calls Maelgwn *Maglocune* and Cynlas *Cuneglase*, where the final *e* is in each case the ending of the Latin vocative (it would doubtless be the same in British). In the sixth- and seventh-century inscriptions, the names have their inflected forms: Cadvan appears as *Catamanus* (Rhys, *Welsh Phil.*,² p. 364), and Brochfael in the genitive as *Brohomagli* (ibid., p. 372). It was therefore naturally assumed that these were the ordinary forms of the names in the language of the period, and that the revolution that converted British into Welsh occurred between the sixth or seventh and the ninth centuries. Rhys in 1879 considered that there was no occasion to prove the “identity” of the language “in the first and sixth century, though that, it must be admitted, would—owing to the scantiness of our data—be only less difficult than to establish the negative” (ibid., p. 141). He was fully aware that it was an unproved assumption; but he thought it probable and was willing to “wait until the latter <note>the negative</note> has found an advocate.”

Before attempting to state the case for the negative—that is, for sixth-century Welsh—it will be well to examine more closely the case for sixth-century British. It rests entirely upon the names in Gildas and in the inscriptions. Because Gildas, writing in Latin, addressed a certain prince as *Maglocune*, it is therefore assumed that the prince cannot have been called *Mailcun* in the vernacular. To put it another way: if the prince were generally called *Mailcun*, Gildas would probably have written *Mailcune*. What the argument implies is that it is improbable that an old form was preserved in Latin

<summary>This page evaluates the linguistic theory that the Welsh language did not exist in the sixth century, based on the use of inflected British names in the writings of Gildas and in stone inscriptions. The author questions whether these formal Latinized names truly represented the spoken vernacular of the time.</summary>

--- Page 39 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that archaic linguistic forms in Latin texts do not necessarily reflect the contemporary spoken language, using the example of traditional Latin names and Irish Ogams.</meta>

<page-num>29</page-num>

# Taliesin.

side by side with a new form in the vernacular. But in reality, it is not unlikely, but likely. In a modern Latin document, the name of a man called Henry is not written *Henrius* but *Henricus*, because the latter is the traditional Latin form—a form preserved in Latin from the time when the guttural sound existed in the name. Latin was in use in Britain from the British period, and native names entered into it in their British forms. Personal names were mostly compounds of pairs of common elements, such as *cuno-*, *maglo-*, *vindo-*, *catu-*, etc. Since Latin was preserved in writing and in the speech of the educated, these forms would naturally be preserved in it; thus, when *Maglo-*, for example, had been reduced to *Mail-* in spoken British, it remained unchanged as the standard Latin form. The work of Gildas and all the inscriptions are written in Latin; we may maintain, then, that they prove nothing regarding the forms of the spoken British of the period. Archaic forms were also preserved in the Irish Ogams: "the Ogam language," says Macbain,¹ "seems to have been a preserved literary language; its inflections were antique compared to the spoken language, and Old Irish, so near it in time as almost to be contemporary, is vastly changed and decayed compared to it." The change was more complete, and therefore probably earlier, in British than in Goidelic.

To come to the evidence for Rhys’s "negative," the inscriptions do not suggest that Old British was a living language. Most of the names are in the genitive case, ending in *-i* for the masculine and *-e* (equivalent to classical Latin *-ae*) for the feminine. The simplest inscriptions contain the name only, as if the implied noun were "stone," thus "[the stone] of A, son of B." But the genitive is frequently retained when *hic jacit* follows, as if it were mistaken for

---

<summary>This page argues that the archaic forms of names found in Gildas’s writings and early inscriptions are likely traditional Latin spellings rather than reflections of the contemporary spoken language. It suggests that British had already evolved into a newer form even while Latin preserved older, more complex structures.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument that linguistic changes from Ancient British to Welsh occurred earlier than generally recognized, as evidenced by the decline of Latin grammatical standards in inscriptions.</meta>

<page-num>30</page-num>

# Taliesin.

a nominative, though, of course, it may be that a different noun is then implied, such as "[the body] of A son of B lies here." But the numerous grammatical errors suggest very strongly that the Latin was not written by people who themselves spoke a highly inflected language; for example, *Culidori jacit et Orvvite mulier secundi*, which Rhys interprets to mean <note>¹ He later attempted, though not very successfully, to explain it as better Latin, *Y Cymmrodor*, xviii, p. 12.</note> *mulieris* (for *uxoris*) *secundae* (*Welsh Phil.*, p. 363); likewise, *Dervaci filius Justi ic jacit* (*ibid.*, p. 381), etc. Furthermore, the distinction between British declensions is lost; the stem *catu-* has a genitive in *-i* as in *Dunocati* (*ibid.*, p. 381), which seems to be formed by adding the usual *-i* to *Dunocat* (the *Dinogat* found in the Book of Aneirin). In some cases, uninflected forms are used, such as *Victor* for the genitive *Victoris* (*ibid.*, p. 172); these become more common in later inscriptions as the tradition fades.

Bede, writing in 731 at the age of fifty-nine, uses the suffixless forms *Car-legion*, *Ban-cor*, and *Broc-mail* (*H.E.*, ii, 2). He probably found them in older English accounts of the Battle of Chester (613 or 616?), which they describe, and was likely unfamiliar with earlier British forms. Welsh, therefore, dates back to the seventh century, and there is no time for the two or three centuries required for a new language to develop.

But a clearer sign that the language changed earlier than has been assumed is shown by the practical identity of Welsh and Breton in the ninth century. Zimmer observes that it required the sharp insight of a scholar like Bradshaw to discover the criteria by which they can be distinguished. <note>² *Auf welchem Wege, etc.*, p. 15.</note> If the Bretons had taken the Ancient British language with them to Armorica, it is unlikely that they would have evolved from it

<meta>This page continues the argument regarding the early development of the Welsh and Breton languages, building on the evidence of inscriptions mentioned on the previous page to suggest that the linguistic transition from British occurred earlier than previously assumed.</meta>

<page-num>31</page-num>

# Taliesin.

between the sixth and ninth centuries, a new language identical to that which evolved independently at the same time in Wales. For the development of the new language did not consist merely of the mechanical loss of syllables—which was bound to produce the same result wherever it occurred—but it involved considerable reconstruction in building a new system of inflection from the remains of the old. Thus the British <term>*catus*</term> ‘battle’ would naturally result in <term>*cat*</term> in both Old Welsh and Old Breton, and its plural <term>*catoues*</term> would as regularly result in <term>*catou*</term> in both (<term>*cadau*</term> in Modern Welsh). The <term>*ou*</term>, which originally formed part of the stem, now seemed to be a plural ending. It originally belonged to <term>*u*-stems</term> only, which formed the equivalent of the Latin fourth declension. Other stem-endings, such as <term>*-ion*</term>, <term>*-i*</term>, <term>*-et*</term>, <term>*-er*</term>, etc., also survived as plural endings. But it happened that <term>*-ou*</term> was chosen as the ending to be added to most nouns which, by the loss of the old inflections, had no distinctive plural forms; and thus an element which belonged to a small class of words in British became the most common plural termination in Breton and Welsh. Is it probable that such an accident occurred independently in Welsh and Breton in the seventh or eighth century? It seems more reasonable to suppose that the reconstruction had taken place before the separation of Breton and Welsh; and if so, the new language was already in existence in the first half of the sixth century.

On general grounds, this view is also more probable. In the first century, the language was undoubtedly British, largely retaining its <term>Aryan</term> inflections. But why should “its identity in the first and sixth century” be assumed as a sort of axiom requiring no proof? Surely the interval between the first and sixth centuries is the one that naturally offers itself as the transition period; it pre-

<meta>This page continues the argument regarding the development of the Welsh language, comparing the linguistic transition from British to Welsh with the transition from Anglo-Saxon to English.</meta>

<page-num>32</page-num>

# Taliesin.

presents the necessary conditions and provides the requisite time. During the Roman occupation, the well-to-do classes among the Britons adopted the speech of their rulers. British became the despised patois of the lower layers of the population and ceased to be under the control of the more important social influences that contribute to conservatism in speech—emulation and regard for precedent, pride, and prejudice. That this is not mere fancy is shown by the fact that precisely the same thing happened later in England. After the Norman conquest, the nobility and those who imitated them spoke French down to the fourteenth century, and the old Saxon speech, says Ranulph Higden about 1340, "hardly survived among a few rustic people." <note>It has hardly remained among a few rustic people. — *Polychronicon*, i. 59.</note> It was during this period that the old language threw off its remaining inflections: it entered the period as Anglo-Saxon and emerged as English. The parallel is still more complete: the new vocabulary in each case contained a large mixture of words from the speech of the conquerors; at the end of the period in each case, the new language spread from the lower classes to the higher and became the national speech; and, lastly, the new speech was employed in each case as the vehicle of song—by Talhaearn, Taliesin, and Neirin on the one hand, and by Chaucer and Gower on the other. <term>Talhaearn</term> is styled <term>tataguen</term>, "the father of the muse"; Chaucer is "the father of English poetry"; <note>Dryden, *Poetical Works*, Globe ed., 1894, p. 499.</note> obviously, it must be for the same reason: the one is the earliest Welsh poet, the other the earliest English. Other parallels inevitably suggest themselves: Ennius (*summus poeta noster*, "our greatest poet," says Cicero) marks the definite formation of classical Latin, and Dante of modern Italian. That the <term>Cynfeirdd</term>, famed in later ages, composed their poems in an effete

<meta>This page continues the argument regarding the development of the Welsh language, specifically asserting that the early poets composed in a living tongue rather than a dying idiom, and explaining why British changed faster than other languages.</meta>

<sig>D</sig>

tongue that was soon to become obsolete seems to me to be not only contrary to analogy but unlikely in itself; their names would have perished with the idiom they used. It is more probable that they sang the birth-song of the new speech, and that their names and songs were handed down because the language lived.

One other point remains to be considered: can the change in the language be as early as is suggested here, seeing that in some languages it took place so much later? Yes, because it is not merely a matter of time; on the documentary evidence alone, it must have happened exceptionally early in British. "Parent Aryan," says Sweet,¹ "is an example of a naturally unstable language"; and the remark applies, of course, to the derived languages that still preserved its inflections. But it is not to be supposed that the phonetic decay to which the old Aryan tongues were peculiarly liable must always proceed at a uniform rate; conditions may arise at any time that will hasten and precipitate it. That such conditions existed in Britain during the period in question cannot, I think, be doubted. Zimmer describes the state of things about <term>A.D.</term> 400 thus:² "In the vicinity of the towns, part of the population was bilingual; those of less culture, like Patrick, spoke a Low Latin dialect as well as their native British, while Latin was the language of the educated. In this connection, it is noteworthy that even in the first half of the sixth century Gildas still calls Latin (by which he doubtless means the literary as distinguished from the popular form) 'nostra lingua'." Popular Latin was subject to the same conditions as British and suffered a similar reduction: thus we learn that in the mouth of the

<meta>This page continues the argument regarding the development of the Welsh language, asserting that the transition from Ancient British to a stable, modern form occurred much earlier than previously thought, thereby validating the historical authenticity of early Welsh poetry.</meta>

<page-num>34</page-num>
<header>Taliesin.</header>

illiterate Patrick, *gratias agimus* had become *gratzacham* in the early fifth century.<note>Ibid., p. 52; Stokes, *Tripartite Life of Patrick*, 1887, p. 291.</note> A period of about three and a half centuries, extending from the middle of the second to the end of the fifth, would be sufficient under these conditions for the accomplishment of the change in British. The new language evolved from it; having discarded its perishable features, it was of a remarkably stable character, comparable in this respect with Arabic,<note>Sweet, loc. cit.</note> and very similar to it in structure. It made much use of vowel permutations and had no useless endings to be lost. It underwent no material change between the sixth and ninth centuries—this is proved by the practical identity of Old Welsh and Old Breton. Since the ninth century, it has changed much less than the varying orthography of different periods would lead us to suppose. It has been modified in detail in many ways, but it retains its essential character. As Rhys says, "we need not hesitate to assume the identity of the Welsh language of the ninth century with that of the nineteenth."<note>*Welsh Philology*, 1879, p. 141.</note> In this sense, its identity goes back to the sixth. The term <term>heniaith</term>, which is sometimes applied to it, is not a mere rhetorical flourish; it has the additional merit of being true to fact.

If this is well-founded, as I believe it to be, the poems attributed to the <term>Cynfeirdd</term> assume quite a new interest and importance. They have been under a cloud for more than a generation. It was believed that not a single line, not a single word, in any of them could represent anything written or spoken by the bards of the sixth century, because it was supposed that those bards (their existence was not generally denied) must have written in Ancient British. This theory is based upon the Latin forms of native names; it is the corollary of the improbable assump-

<meta>This page continues the argument regarding the historical development of the Welsh language. Its effect on the criticism of these poems has been detrimental. Anwyl, in his two papers, is mostly engaged in an unsuccessful struggle with the difficulties it creates—speculating on "motives" and attempting to discover possible materials for the great forgery, a forgery for which we would have to assume that even the models had to be conjured up. The idiosyncrasies and mannerisms, and the verse forms that characterize and distinguish the songs of Taliesin, the stanzas of <term>Aneirin</term>, and the <term>englynion</term> of <term>Llywarch</term>—all of this is easily imitated, and has been imitated; but could it all have been invented, imagined, and created out of nothing?

As soon as the theory is dropped, these difficulties vanish, and a more promising field of inquiry opens up. I am not arguing for the substitution of a rival theory, but only for an unbiased investigation of the facts. Abandoning this theory allows us to return to the traditional and natural view that the language of the <term>Cynfeirdd</term> was the <term>New British</term> which we call Welsh, and therefore admits the possibility that some of their verses have been passed down. Let this possibility be tested by a patient examination of the poems as we find them. Their vocabulary should be studied with minute care so that their obscurities may, if possible, be cleared up; for it must not be forgotten that both those who defended and those who condemned them in the past understood them only imperfectly, and those who condemned them most loudly, like Nash, understood them least.¹ The inquiry should be

<meta>This page continues the argument for an unbiased investigation into early Welsh poetry. It shifts from criticizing previous philological theories to establishing standard critical principles for dating and identifying the authorship of sixth-century works, specifically addressing the poems of Taliesin and Aneirin.</meta>

<page-num>36</page-num>

->Taliesin.<-

...taken up at the point where it was left by Stephens and Skene; and the ordinary standards of criticism should be applied. It should be admitted that a description, for example, of a sixth-century battle—especially if it gives the impression of nearness and vividness—is more likely, other considerations aside, to have been written at the time than at any subsequent time. It should be recognized that the age of a manuscript imposes only one limit on the age of the content: it cannot be later; it is seldom contemporary—original handwritten copies and first drafts are rare. In the great majority of cases, the manuscript is a copy of another, which in turn is a copy of another, and so on; the age of the content may be anything that other evidence indicates. Due weight should be accorded to tradition, which may suggest and corroborate even if it cannot be accepted as absolute proof. These are not theories but axioms which must guide all such inquiries; all questions of the date and authorship of ancient works rest on internal evidence, the evidence of manuscripts often centuries later than the date of the work, and tradition. Many of Sharon Turner’s arguments (such, for example, as the extreme improbability of a disaster like the battle of <term>Catraeth</term> being chosen in later ages as the subject of a long poem) have never been answered; and we may hold that there is a *prima facie* case for the survival, in some form, of sixth-century verse. And, to quote Matthew Arnold once more, "since a continuous stream of evidence shows the enduring existence... from the sixth century to the twelfth, of an old national literature, it seems certain that much of this..."

But there is still one theory that stands in the way; it is Dr. Gwenogvryn Evans's Taliesin theory, explained in the introduction and notes which unfortunately accompany his beautiful reproduction and facsimile of the text of the *Book of Taliesin*, and in the companion volume of "amended" text and translation.<note>*Facsimile and Text of the Book of Taliesin*, Llanbedrog, 1910; *Poems from the Book of Taliesin, edited, amended and Translated*, 1915. Although dated this way, the volumes were not issued until 1916. References here are to the first volume, unless marked II.</note> Dr. Evans is a late follower of the Nashian school of thought; his theory rests ultimately on an unwillingness to believe that anything contained in the manuscript can be much older than the manuscript itself—it may be a little older, say a century, but to admit any real antiquity would be considered foolish gullibility. This is mid-nineteenth-century skepticism, which is similar to that of a person who is too clever to be taken in by the truth. So Mr. Evans, like Nash, denies that anything in the *Book of Taliesin* was written before the twelfth century. But a mere denial provides no peace of mind; how is the composition of these poems in the twelfth century to be explained? Dr. Evans's great contribution is his answer to this question: Taliesin himself, though nobody suspected it before, lived in the twelfth century. That is the theory. He examined the text and naturally, like Edward Davies before him, found confirmation of the theory everywhere. He believes in it passionately; after many brave attempts, he fails to adequately express his contempt for those who take the traditional view, and is "reduced to a melancholy, thoughtful silence", p. xxv.

I yield to no one in my admiration for Dr. Gwenogvryn

<meta>This page continues the critique of Dr. Gwenogvryn Evans’s theories, shifting from his denial of the poems' antiquity to an evaluation of his skills as a transcriber versus his shortcomings as a theorist.</meta>

<page-num>38</page-num>

Evans’s reproductions of old Welsh texts, or in my appreciation of their value to students of the Welsh language and literature. I believe they surpass anything that has ever been achieved in terms of printed texts; Dr. Evans has developed the art of printing in facsimile to a point never before reached. As a decipherer of Welsh manuscripts, no one has ever come close to him; I have seen him identify in a few seconds an obliterated initial that had not only baffled me, but had been misread by a previous copyist at least 200 years earlier. He possesses the endless patience that absolute accuracy demands, a very exceptional power of sight, and a real talent for finding the original ink marks in what appears to be a hopeless blotch. The service he has provided through his talent far exceeds in lasting value that of any theorist, however brilliant, for he has provided the student with the fundamental facts. Unfortunately, for himself and for us, he is not satisfied with this. Why should he be a mere transcriber, a mere machine? Why should he not also have his say on matters of textual criticism and literary history? His friends, he says, have tried to dissuade him. "Do you think that *you* are going to get to the bottom of stuff like that?" he is asked (II, p. vii). In spite of discouragements like these, he has persuaded himself that he does understand Taliesin and has discovered the bard’s secret. He issues his revelation in the tone and manner of a challenge to the world. A reply is necessary, not so much for the sake of demolishing the theory—for it has little chance of being accepted—but because in the process, some constructive work can perhaps be done.

It is strange that the accurate <term>palaeographer</term> who most faithfully supplies us with the absolute facts of the manuscripts has no idea how to use the facts himself, or of the value of any evidence. His reasoning—the

When the evidence is against it, he simply asserts it against the evidence. Thus Llywarch ap Llywelyn, a bard who flourished about 1200, is called *Prydydd y Moch*. Mr. Evans calls him “Prydydd y·Mochnant”, and in English “the Bard of Mochnant”; but, since his interpretation of *y* means the preposition *ym* [in], the expression “Prydydd y·Mochnant” is vague and can only mean “a poet in Mochnant”—he really has only the vaguest notions of Welsh syntax. There is no analogy whatever for such a formula.<note>There is no *Eos yn Nyfed*, *Bardd ym Môn*, etc., but only *Eos Dyfed*, *Bardd Môn*, etc.</note> What evidence is there for it here? He admits that there is none: “Always incorrectly written ‘Prydydd y moch’”, page xii. In other words, it is “never written anywhere ‘Prydydd y·Mochnant’”. Stephens (*Lit. Kym.*, 1849, p. 276) mentions as one suggested explanation of *Prydydd y Moch* that the word *Moch* here means not “pigs” but “the men of Mochnant”: and he rejects the explanation on the ground that the *Prydydd* [Poet] had nothing to do with Mochnant.<note>Stephens’s own explanation of the name Prydydd y Moch is that the bard was the author of the stanzas called *Hoianeu*, each of which begins with “Listen, little pig”, and which are attributed to Myrddin. <meta>This page continues the critique of Dr. Gwenogvryn Evans's subjective methodology, specifically regarding his renaming of the bard Prydydd y Moch and his dismissal of the historical existence of a 6th-century Taliesin.</meta>
