He considers whether [the poet] could or should have such a name as "the poet of the pigs"; in reading Stephens, an idea strikes him: "he *must be* the Bard of Mochnant"; then he asserts, in defiance of all the evidence, that the bard is "Prydydd y Mochnant". The evidence of manuscripts is nothing to him—it is merely the record of a mistake; his inner light tells him that the name is the solecism which he himself has invented.

That is a characteristic sample of his method. Now let us see how it is applied to the history of Taliesin:—

> That Taliesin flourished in the middle of the twelfth century there can be no manner of doubt; and he was held in such high esteem that his manner, his style was imitated. "But", it has been objected, "there might have been another Taliesin who lived in the sixth century". Where is the evidence for this ghost, this birth of fraud, this tattle of public platforms? Might-have-been is not evidence, but the offspring of indolent belief, which avoids the effort to think. To say this is to fly in the face of Providence which provides for the simple, but the man who has seen a truth cannot act as if he had not seen it (p. iii).

It is no use quarreling with the style; the style is the man. But this tirade means only that there is no evidence for the Taliesin of tradition. The answer to it is that there is evidence; that he himself quotes some of it, misquotes some more, and ignores the rest.

The oldest reference to Taliesin occurs in the well-known entry in the additions to Nennius in the British Museum Harleian MS. 3859, folio 188b, printed below. A facsimile of part of the original page containing the words appears as the frontispiece to this volume.

(I)da, son of Eobba, held regions in the northern part of Britain, that *is*, of the Humber sea, and reigned for twelve years, and joined Dinguayrdi to Guurth Berneich.

(T)hen Dutigirn at that time fought bravely against the nation of the Angles. Then Talhaern Tat Aguen was famous in poetry, and Neirin, and Taliesin, and Bluchbard, and Cian (who is called Gueinth Guaut) were famous together at one time in British poetry.

Dr. Evans begins his Introduction by quoting these words in a translation in which errors occur only in the first part and do not affect the argument. He notes that "Ida founded the kingdom of Northumbria in the year 547 and died in 559. In his report on the Welsh manuscripts in the British Museum^4 he had dated this manuscript "circa 1100." He now says on page viii that it "was written after 1125," giving no reasons and making no reference at all to his own official report from 1910. This we shall now attempt to do by directing attention to the earliest recorded use of words in our text,” that is, by inquiring into something else. He looks the fact straight in the face and passes by on the other side.

It should be observed that the argument which ends in this pitiful evasion attempts only to discredit the memorandum as evidence for the Taliesin of the sixth century; the question of how it is to be reconciled with a twelfth-century Taliesin is not discussed at all. Since the one and only Taliesin was the poet who “from 1146 to 1176” sang “the fortune of the house of Gwynedd” (p. xxxiii), no one else can possibly be alluded to in the memorandum. This is touched upon once: Taliesin “shone early: to be chosen for special mention in the <term>Nennian additamenta</term> <note>Nennian additions</note> he must have attained a foremost place about 1130” (p. xxxvi). It is seen that “after 1125” now becomes “about 1130.” Resulting from this, “Taliesin lived between 1105 and 1175, or thereabouts” (p. xxix). “To be chosen for special mention” is a very curious phrase for being the subject of a gross blunder. Mistakes in dating people who have been long dead are common enough, but it is not usual to mistake distinguished contemporaries for ancients; such an error is so unlikely as to be almost inconceivable, and no trick of language which attempts to disguise its extreme improbability can be allowed to pass. Now let us put the whole case of how the memorandum relates to Dr. Evans’s

<page-num>43</page-num>
<header>Taliesin.</header>

theory. Taliesin, who sang "from 1146 to 1176," might have been born about 1105, and might have distinguished himself "around 1130"; he might have been brilliant in youth and prolific in old age. The Harleian Manuscript might have been written "about 1130" (though previously dated "around 1100" by our paleographer himself); the memorandum might have been a composition from that date (this assumption involves the "well-known error" of taking "the date of the first record of a fact to be the date of the fact itself"¹); and its author might have been confused enough to name a young poet of the day among the British bards of the sixth century. It might have been; but I have to remind Dr. Evans that "a hypothetical is not evidence." Or rather, perhaps one ought to say that all these hypotheticals, many of which are highly improbable, are very convincing evidence that the theory which requires them is a delusion.

But this, of course, is only half the matter. We now have to consider the credentials of the document that Dr. Gwenogvryn Evans challenges. The manuscript is written, according to the Keeper of Manuscripts in the British Museum, in "an English hand of the early twelfth century" (*Y Cymmrodor*, ix, p. 146). The *<term>Historia Brittonum</term>* proper is followed by a number of shorter tracts relating to British history. The memorandum in question occurs in a tract called "<term>Saxon Genealogies</term>," which, according to Mr. Phillimore, is "itself of earlier composition than the *Historia*," and "was included with it (approximately at the end of the ninth or beginning of the tenth century)," *ibid.*, p. 143. "The Genealogies, according to M. de la Borderie, are the most authentic and, from a historical point of view, the most valuable portion of

¹ <note>Professor Hugh Williams in *Journal of Celtic Philology* (*Zeitschrift für celtische Philologie*), iv, p. 557.</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the dating and authenticity of historical tracts, specifically examining the scholarly consensus on the "Saxon Genealogies" to support the 6th-century dating of Taliesin.</meta>

<page-num>44</page-num>
<header>Taliesin.</header>

the whole work" (*Y Cymmrodor* vii, p. 157). Zimmer, in his *Nennius Vindicatus* (1893, p. 78), analyzes the tract and finds that it contains genealogies extending down to 685, with interpolations reaching down a century later. This is followed by "a history [beginning with our quotation, *Ida filius Eobba*, etc.] of North and Mid-Britain down to the death of Ecgfrid of Northumberland, who fell in 685 in a battle against the Picts. We thus have a history of the Britons and Angles from 547 to 685 in the short memoranda before us." He concludes that "the time of its composition can therefore only be the year 685 itself or 686" (p. 78), though he thinks the tract in its first form must have been written in 679 (p. 96). The interpolations are no later than the date of the *<term>Historia</term>* proper, which he dates to 796 (p. 82). As an authority, he says the *<term>Historia</term>* proper is "absolutely worthless"; however, on the other hand, the tract is "of the very highest importance (*von allerhöchster Bedeutung*) for the history of Welsh and Irish literature" (p. 282). Mommsen, in the preface to his 1894 edition of the *<term>Historia Brittonum</term>* (p. 119), gives the date of the manuscript, Harleian 3859, as the end of the eleventh century according to Henry Bradshaw (*Collected Papers*, p. 466), or the beginning of the twelfth century in the opinion of Sir E. Maunde Thompson (the Keeper of Manuscripts already quoted above). He accepts Zimmer's conclusion regarding the date of the tract *<term>Saxon Genealogies</term>*, but believes that the *<term>Historia</term>* proper may have been written about the same time and expanded during the eighth century, as was the case with the tract itself (p. 117). Thus, despite differences on other points, there is a very weighty and authoritative consensus of opinion regarding the date and value of our tract. To the question "where is the evidence" that Taliesin lived in the sixth century, we can then reply that it is found in a document pronounced by all the

<summary>This page discusses the scholarly consensus among experts like Zimmer and Mommsen regarding the dating of the "Saxon Genealogies" and "Historia Brittonum." --- Page 55 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the dating of the historical tracts, arguing for their 7th-century origins and refuting the idea that Taliesin lived as late as the 12th century.</meta>

<page-num>45</page-num>
# Taliesin.

Most competent judges consider this to have been written in the seventh century and to be of the highest historical value. The date of the manuscript that contains it is, in the judgment of the most eminent authorities on ancient handwriting, the end of the eleventh or the beginning of the twelfth century. There is no need to waste words on the absurdity of the suggestion that the Taliesin named in it is a person who was composing poetry in 1176.

The Welsh names in the tract are in archaic spelling. *Talhaern* for *Talhaearn* is an Old Welsh form, similar to *gaem* for *gaeaf* (*Welsh Grammar*, 1913, p. 100). *Neirin* must be the old form of this name. Though written as *Aneirin*, it counts as a two-syllable word in the *Gododdin*, *Book of Aneirin*, p. 12:—

> ->mi na vi (a)neirin  5<-
> ->ys gwyr talyessin  5<-
> ->ovec kywrenhin     5<-

The *a-* is a non-essential sound that is sometimes introduced before an initial *n-*, as in *anadred* for *nadredd* (‘snakes’), or *annifer* for *nifer* (‘number’) (*Welsh Grammar*, p. 25). The form starting with *A-* became the common written version of this name, and the correct form is known to us only through this memorandum. Zimmer, though he noted the “high antiquity and clarity” of the Welsh names in this tract (*Nennius Vindicated*, p. 80), thought that *Neirin* was an error (p. 103). He suggests that the author of the 679 text wrote *Aneirin*, and Nennius in 796 mistook this for the Welsh “a Neirin” (meaning “and Neirin”), and therefore wrote *et Neirin* (Latin for “and Neirin”), “destroying the construction and sense,” as it seems to the mechanical German mind. Most of us would say that *et Neirin* is a much more likely reading. He then takes this as proof that the Welshman in 796 did not know the sixth-century North British bards (“even by their names,” p. 283). He claims the poems attributed to Aneurin, Taliesin, etc., are, at the earliest, products of the ninth and tenth centuries.

...evoked by the Northern tract known through Nennius. This is quite a large deduction based on his own flawed assumption. One would like to ask how it happened, in that case, that the Nennian form *Neirin* is not found in Welsh manuscripts; where the Welsh obtained the "correct" *Aneirin* from, since their only knowledge of him was derived from Nennius; why Talhaearn, the father of the muse, was so poorly treated by the forgers, who have not honored him with even a single fake note: Talhaearn—what a name it would have been to conjure with! No, Nennius is right; the correct reading is *Neirin*. It hardly needs to be said that there is no authority for *Aneurin*, which is simply due to the false notion that the name contains the adjective *eurin* ('golden'). The name of *Talhayarn* occurs twice (20·4, 21·16), and *Kian* once (19·4) in a poem in the Book of Taliesin. *Bluchbard* is unknown elsewhere. <note>Evan Evans suggested (see above p. 14) that it is an error for Llywarch, which seems to be the name required. But one hesitates to accept this, even though there are many mistakes in the manuscript due to the ignorance of one or more transcribers regarding Welsh (*Y Cymmrodor*, ix, 146). The oldest form of Llywarch is *Loumarc* (ibid. 178), but *lugmarc* might be an earlier form, and an initial *dl-* is possible (compare Loth, *Voc. Vieux-Bret.*, 107). But the errors in the manuscript hardly justify the required transformation, so the suggestion is improbable. *Bluchbard* should be classified with *Cian* and *Talhaearn* as poets whose works did not survive.</note>

The next piece of evidence occurs on folios 29a-b of the oldest Welsh copy of the Laws, the Black Book of Chirk, which Dr. Evans dates to "around 1200." <note>*Report on Manuscripts in the Welsh Language*, vol. i, p. 359.</note> It is the record of a tradition concerning the origin of certain privileges enjoyed by the men of Arvon. The text will be found in the *Ancient Laws and Institutes of Wales*, vol. i, p. 104. The spelling of this manuscript is strange; the footnotes to the following translation provide the manuscript spelling of the names where they have been altered or corrected.

Some say that Maeldaf the elder, the lord of Penardd, adjudged it to the men of Arfon; Iorwerth son of Madog, on the authority of the story, affirms that it was Idno Hen who adjudged it to the men of the black-headed shafts. This is at least a genuine piece of tradition. In Adamnan's *Life of Columba*, written about 695, there is an anecdote "concerning King Roderick son of Tothail who reigned in Petra Cloithe," Reeves' ed., p. 43. He is called Rederech by Jocelin and described as a Christian king and friend of Kentigern (*Historians of Scotland*, v, pp. 213, 218, 241). He fought at Arfderydd in 573. *Clydno Eidyn* was his father's cousin (*Y Cymmrodor* ix, 173); *Mordaf Hael* and possibly *Nudd Hael* were his cousins (Skene, *F.A.B.* i, 169). *Elidir Mwynfawr*, who had been slain in Arfon, was his father's cousin (ibid.). Here, then, we have a tradition which seems to preserve the memory of a historical event pretty accurately^1; and it represents Taliesin as the contemporary of Rhun ap Maelgwn and Rhydderch Hael. It corroborates, on Welsh ground, the testimony of the Northern memorandum in the "Saxon Genealogies." Even by itself, it is absolutely decisive against the theory that Taliesin lived in the twelfth century.

How does Dr. Gwenogvryn Evans deal with this evidence? He does not mention it at all as bearing on the question of Taliesin's date. He misquotes part of it to prove that *y Gogledd* (the North) was the district around Chester. Thus (p. xvii):—

<meta>This page continues the critique of Dr. Gwenogvryn Evans’s theories regarding the date of Taliesin, focusing on the misinterpretation of the Chirk Codex and the historical identification of Rhun.</meta>

# Taliesin.

The Chirk Codex of the Laws adds the further testimony that “Rhun . . . . . raised an army and went with the men of Gwynedd to the bank of the Gwerydd in the North [Gogledd] where they spent a considerable time in disputing who should lead through the Gwerydd”, i.e., the sacred stream, or Dee. Now, in this border expedition Rhun, son of Owain Gwynedd, sickened and died, hence the dispute as to who should lead; and the “dispatch of a messenger to Gwynedd” on the subject.

In his “quotation” he gives the name of Rhun, son of Maelgwn, as “Rhun . . . . ”, and then passes him off as Rhun son of Owain Gwynedd. He represents the expedition as having taken place in the twelfth century by omitting every detail which proves it to have taken place in the sixth. If there is anything at all that this paragraph in the Chirk Codex proves, it is that the North meant Cumbria or Strathclyde to the Welsh of the twelfth century; he makes use of it to prove that the North meant Cheshire by flagrant misrepresentation, and by omitting the names of the Northern leaders, including the famous Rhydderch Hael. Another thing that the paragraph conclusively proves is the date of Taliesin in twelfth-century tradition; of this he says nothing at all.

There is abundant evidence of this tradition. Sharon Turner has collected a large number of references to Taliesin and the other Cynfeirdd <note>The earliest bards</note> in the works of the bards of the twelfth and thirteenth centuries, the natural interpretation being in each case that the bard referred to lived in the remote past. Turner also quotes passages from a Latin poem attributed to Geoffrey of Monmouth, in which Telgesinus and Merlinus appear as fellow-bards and as contemporaries of “the king of the Cumbrians, Rodarcus” [rex Cumbrorum, Rodarcus], *Vindication*, pp. 120-3. The poem is not by Geoffrey, but it bears witness to the twelfth-century tradition.<note>^1 The poem, “Vita Merlini”, was printed by San Marte in *Die Sagen von Merlin*, Halle, 1853, pp. 273-316. It was written after Geoffrey’s “Historia” had become famous, and was addressed to</note> Wace,

<meta>This page continues the critique of Dr. Gwenogvryn Evans’s theories, specifically refuting his claim that Taliesin was a "fraud" or a later invention by citing numerous twelfth-century literary references to the bard.</meta>

<page-num>50</page-num>

# Taliesin.

Wace, who wrote his *Roman de Brut* around 1155, speaks in it of Thelesin as a prophet who foretold the birth of Christ!¹ The tale of *Kulhwch* was written, according to Dr. Evans (*W. B. Mab.*, p. xiv), before 1135; and in that, *Teliessin penn beirð* <note>Taliesin, Chief of Bards</note> figures as one of Arthur's men (ibid., col. 462). In the tale of *Branwen*, Talyessin is one of the seven who escaped from Ireland in the time of Bendigeidvran (ibid., col. 56-7). These are tales and prove nothing about Taliesin's real date, but they prove that he had become a legendary character by the twelfth century. Avaon ap Talyessin is named with Uryen and Aneirin in a triad in the detached portion of the White Book (*Y Cymmrodor*, vii, p. 128). In the Black Book of Carmarthen, as we have seen, the first poem is in the form of a dialogue between Myrddin and Taliesin. That was written down in the twelfth century; and in the twelfth-century *Life of Gruffudd ap Cynan* (ed. Arthur Jones, p. 110), Myrddin is an ancient prophet who foretold this prince. In the *Gododdin*, Aneirin mentions "Talyessin of noble thought" (see above, p. 45); the words may not be his, but they prove that it was believed that the two bards were contemporaries. What does Dr. Gwenogvryn Evans say about all this? Not one word. And he has the audacity to ask, "Where is the evidence for this ghost, this birth of fraud?" I will not trade accusations; the facts speak for themselves.

We come now to the subject matter of the poems. Our editor, turning aside, as we have seen, from the

# Taliesin.

inconvenient Nennian memorandum, proceeds to consider certain proper names in his text. He makes a great show of following a scientific method, and shows his usual confusion of thought. He will deal with Welsh names and their use in times before the surviving Welsh records as an English student would deal with *steamer*, *railway*, *airplane*, etc., in contemporary records. The Latin *Britannia* meant ‘Wales’ in the twelfth century; and “it is precisely at this stage that *Prydein* appears in our text as an expression for” Wales, p. ix. He imagines that by making that statement he has proved that the text was written in the twelfth century and that *Prydein* in it means Wales, whereas, of course, he has only assumed both. There is no evidence that *Prydein* is used for Wales. Where it is not an error for *Prydyn* ‘Pictland, Scotland’, it means the same as it does in *Ynys Prydein* <note>The Island of Britain</note>. For example, the ninth-century poem “Armes Prydein Vawr” <note>The Prophecy of Great Britain</note> contains a prophecy that the men of Brittany, Cornwall, Ireland, Wales, and Strathclyde will unite to drive the Saxons out of *Prydein*; they will ask them “what is their title to the land they hold”, pp. 16, 17; see Ifor Williams in *Y Beirniad*, 1916, pp. 207-12.

The word *Cymry* ‘the Welsh, Wales’ is of some importance. “It is significant”, says Dr. Evans, “that the term *Kymry* takes the place of *Prydein* for the first time in *Brut y Tywysogion* <note>The Chronicle of the Princes</note> in the year 1135”, p. x. The unsuspecting reader will gather from that that *Prydein* is regularly used for Wales in the *Brut* down to the year 1135—that is, in pages 257-308 of the Red Book Bruts—and that *Kymry* then takes its place. The unsuspecting reader will be deceived. I look the name up in the index, and find that in those fifty-two pages *Prydein* occurs once only, and *then* it means Scotland, p. 278. But even if the statement were true, it would be significant of nothing but

<page-num>52</page-num>

# Taliesin.

the fact that the editor assumes that tracing a word in a history book is the same as tracing it in a fully recorded contemporary literature. The word *Kymry* is not only older than any record, but, as a common noun, it is as old as the Welsh language itself. It is the plural of *Cymro*, and there is no foundation for the modern modification that treats it as *Cymru* when it means ‘Wales’. The feminine form *Cymra-es* and the feminine adjective *Cymra-eg* can hardly have been formed after the Old British period, since the change of *o* to *a* in the root seems to belong to British rather than to Welsh phonetics.<note>See my *Welsh Grammar*, p. 85. The only other word in which the interchange has survived is *troed*, plural *traed*, from British \**troget-*, \**traget-* respectively, just as *Cymro* is from \**Kom-brog-os* and *Cymraes* from \**Kom-brag-issa*.</note> *Cymro* means ‘a fellow-countryman’, and to a Briton—who alone used it—it naturally denoted a Briton. Skene says that the first appearance of the word as a national name occurs in Ethelwerd’s Latin Chronicle, written between 975 and 1011, in which the *Peohtas* (Picts) and *Straecled Wealas* (Strathclyde Welsh) of the Saxon Chronicle are rendered as *Pictis Cumbrisque*.<note>*Historians of Scotland*, vol. v, p. 332.</note> But, surely, this can be no indication whatever of the first use of the word as a national name by the Welsh themselves; the fact that it was used by the Welsh of both the North and of Wales seems to imply, as *Rhys* suggests in his *Celtic Britain* (1884, p. 116), that it “acquired the force and charm of a national name” before they were separated as a result of the battle of Chester in 616. But *Rhys* is wrong in seeing in it an expression of the union of Northern and Southern Britons; it was more probably at first a common noun representing the British equivalent of the Latin *civis* as opposed to *hostis* or *peregrinus*. When Gildas wrote *Ex eo tempore nunc cives, nunc hostes, vincebant* <note>From that time now citizens, now enemies, were victorious</note>, § 26, 1, a phrase echoed

<summary>The author argues that the word "Cymry" is an ancient common noun meaning "fellow-countryman" that predates written records, refuting the idea that it emerged as a national name as late as the 12th century. He cites phonetic evidence and historical usage to suggest the term was established before the 7th-century separation of the Northern and Southern Britons.</summary>

if these can be otherwise dated. Dr. Gwenogvryn Evans

<meta>This page continues the analysis of the use of the term "Cymry" in early Welsh literature, specifically contrasting its occurrence in poems attributed to Taliesin with its use in later medieval works to refute Dr. Gwenogvryn Evans’s chronological arguments.</meta>

<page-num>54</page-num>
<header>Taliesin.</header>

Of the sixteen examples of *Cymry*, only five appear in poems dealing with events of Taliesin’s time: the first is antithetical to *arallvro* ("stranger"), 29.15; the second and third come together and refer to the Cumbrians from Penren Wleth (in Glasgow) to Luch Reon (Loch Ryan), 34.1-2; the fourth follows *arallvro*, 38.15; the fifth, *kymry kaeruedawc* (41.23), is obscure. In no case does a phrase such as "Kymry a Saesson" (Cymry and Saxons), for example, occur, as it does in the later "*Armes*," 14.17. How does Dr. Evans handle this matter? He lumps together the earlier and later poems. He states that *Brython* and *Prydein* occur together in eleven poems, both with *Kymry* in five, and *Kymry* alone in nine; he calls this "conclusive evidence of a transition period." The bards from 1150 to 1225 similarly use *Prydein*, *Brython*, and *Kymry*; he tells us how many times they appear, and then—

->Taliesin was certainly contemporary with Gwalchmei and<-
->Kyndel, for all three sing to Owein Gwynedd, who died in 1170.<-
->Taliesin, therefore, lived in the 12th century (p. xii).<-

There is, of course, no mention of Owein Gwynedd in the Book of Taliesin; that seems to be all that is necessary to say in response to this *non sequitur*. In this connection, there is one other assertion to be noticed:

> As the Welsh rendering of *Scotti* was *Brithon*, or *Brithion*,
> confusion with *Brython* was inevitable. And to this day learned
> professors, writing on our history and literature, do not appear
> to have noticed the distinction (p. xi).

They do not. These professors are perverse people; and I fear they will be unreasonable enough to demand some proof of the equation "*Scotti = Brithon*," and will simply refuse to accept it on the mere word of the seer of Tremvan. *Brithon* is, of course, only an old spelling of *Brython*, which is the Welsh continuation of the British *Brittones*, just as *Prydein* is the continuation of the British *Pritannia*.

<meta>This page continues the critique of Dr. Gwenogvryn Evans’s attempts to redate the poems of Taliesin to the 12th century by examining his interpretation of British place names.</meta>

<header>Taliesin.</header>

Another class of names that are even more important for determining the dates of the poems consists of the names of towns, rivers, and districts in Britain. The poems deal largely with the northern districts, which is consistent with an early origin rather than a medieval one. Dr. Evans is therefore forced by the demands of his theory to show that all northern names refer to places in Wales or its borders. Let us examine his attempts.

**Y Gogleð.**—I have already quoted his flagrant misuse of the Chirk Codex to prove that “the North” is the “country bordering on the lower half of the Dee.” He opens his argument in his own genial manner, thus:

> We have been taught to look for *Gogleð* in the land of mists, but as Owein Gwyneð was never in Scotland, it seems as if our mentors have lost their way in a fog of their own creation. The warrior-poet Gwalchmei, who was always at Owein’s side, knew his geography better. “Owein bears the palm within the four corners of Wales: Homage is rendered to him from the fort on the Clud in *Gogleð*; and he is a dragon in Dyfed—in the far away South.”

<margin>ᵃ *Myv.* 144.</margin>
> Dy·chlud glod Brydein bedrydaneu:
> Dy·wystlir iðaw o ðin al-Clud Ogleð—
> draig yw yn·Yved, draw yn·Eheu.ᵃ

Here *Gogleð* and *Deheu* [the South] are clearly opposites, indicating the extreme limits of Owein’s dominance (p. xvii).

In order to represent *Prydein* (“Britain”) as Wales, and to include in it both North and South, he foists the words *YN·YVED* (*in Dyfed*) into his quotation. In *Myv.* 144, to which reference is made, the last two lines read:

> Dygwystlir idaw o Din Alclud gogled
> Draig yw yn dyhed drawen yn deheu.

The word *dyhed* is *dyheð* (“war”); it rhymes with *Gogleð* (as is usual, though not essential, in this meter), and corresponds in consonantism to *Deheu*. Thus he begins with a gross misquotation; he substitutes one word for another and says nothing about it. The next point is that in his text—

13.13 “The 'men of the North' (*gwyr Gogledd*) are the men of Hugh Lupus, Earl of Chester.” It hardly needs to be said that Hugh Lupus does not appear in the text, which is that of the ninth-century *Armes*. We have here only another example of the editor’s habit of circular reasoning—of proving a thing by assuming it in his premises. It is because he assumes *Gogledd* is Cheshire that Hugh Lupus is brought into the argument; and because the “men of the North” are supposedly Hugh’s men, he concludes that *Gogledd* must be Cheshire. We are referred next to the *Red Book Bruts*, pages 292–3, where it is stated that a host “from the North and Scotland” (*o’r Gogledd a’r Alban*) is led by Alexander mac Malcolm and a son of Hugh Lupus. Dr. Evans simply reverses the order and says “Scotland and the North” (*Alban and Gogledd*), to assign them “their respective princes”; the result is “Scotland and Cheshire”—what a likely combination! In *Brut y Saeson* (*Myvyrian Archaiology*, 672), only “all *Prydyn*,” that is, “all Scotland,” goes with them, so that “the North and Scotland” (*y Gogledd a’r Alban*) is merely another way of saying “all Scotland.” Next, there is a statement about Mwrchath, without any reference—clearly because it will not withstand scrutiny; and, lastly, there is the outrage of the “further testimony” of the *Chirk Codex*. And that is the proof! It is as notable for what it omits as for what it contains. We have seen that all the details in the passage from the *Chirk Codex* which show what *y Gogledd* really meant are suppressed. The same is true with the *Bruts*. One passage, in which “the North and Scotland” can be twisted into “Scotland and Cheshire,” is quoted; but the passages which show undeniably what *y Gogledd* meant are not mentioned. ### Penryn Blathaon

There is, however, one other reference in Dr. Evans's Introduction to *the North* (*Gogledd*) in the Bruts; it is as follows: "*Penryn Blataon yn y Gogledd*, B. 292·25. This is the headland of Wirral between the *Balas* of the Dee and the Mersey. Blataon is clearly a <note>scribal error</note> for Balaon," p. xxiv. Unfortunately for him, *bala* is not the mouth of a river but its "efflux from a lake"; the *bala* of the Dee is at Bala in Merioneth. This disposes of the Wirral. Now for the facts. In the Chirk Codex, the length of this Island from *Penryn Blathaon* in *Prydeyn* to *Penryn Penwaeδ* in *Cornwall* (*Kernyw*) is stated to be 900 miles, *Anc. Laws*, i, p. 184. The latter point is in the extreme southwest of Cornwall, and is marked on modern maps as "Tolpedn-penwith"; clearly, therefore, *Penryn Blathaon* is in the extreme north, and *Prydeyn* here is *Prydyn* (Scotland). The same measurement is given in the White Book, "from Penryn Blathaon in Britain to Penryn Penwaed in Cornwall" (*o Benryn Blat[h]aon ym Brydein hyt ym Penryn Penwaeδ yg Kerniw*), *Y Cymmrodor*, vii, p. 124. In "*Brut y Saeson*" *Myv.* 672, we have "from the extreme point of Cornwall which is called *Pengwayδ* to the extreme point of Prydyn which is called *Penblathaon*." This is clearly the Medieval

*Caer Liwelydd.*—This is the Welsh name for Carlisle; the Old Welsh form occurs in the Nennian Additamenta in Harl. 3859 as *Cair ligualid* (*Y Cymmrodor*, ix, p. 183). The name occurs in the Book of Taliesin, 69·12. The editor, of course, will have nothing to do with Carlisle, because it is in the real North. > ->*Lliwelydd will spread wider your fame, Llewelyn.* M. 212a'47.<-

There is no reference to anything of the kind. The bard, Llywarch, Prydydd y Moch, after speaking of Llewelyn's kindness to himself, proceeds as follows (to give the full sense of the couplet): "to Lliwelydd your praise will spread, Llewelyn, and Llywarch has sung it". Llywarch is the bard himself. It is clear that the words omitted by Dr. Evans put a different light on the matter. *Lliwelydd* is the accusative of motion toward, and the verb in both referred texts has the initial *ll-*, not *l-*. We have the usual misquotation, mistranslation, and suppression of context. I do not need to labor the point, or notice the etymology "Lli + Gwelydd", which belongs to the same class as "the Balas". <meta>This page continues the linguistic and historical argument proving that Caer Liwelydd is the Welsh name for Carlisle, further refuting Dr. Gwenogvryn Evans's attempts to relocate these northern sites to the Cheshire region.</meta>

LVGV-BALLIVM, or LVGVBALIA, as Bede testifies, “the Saxons called **Luell** . . . . . Nennius called it *Caer Lualid* . . . we call it **Carlisle**, and the Latins called it *Carleolum*, a more recent name.” The passage referred to is in the *Life of St. Cuthbert*, chapter 27: “the man of God, Cuthbert . . . came to Lugubalia, which the English people corruptly call Luel.” The form in the Antonine Itinerary is *Luguval-lium* (see *Route V*). Now it is well known that British *-ion* (Latinized as *-ium*), being pronounced -ion, under certain conditions of accent resulted in the Welsh *-ydd*; this was discovered by Rhys long ago.<note>It is to Rhys that we owe the discovery of a phonetic fact, today considered common: the transformation of the Indo-European *j* [that is *i*] into the Welsh sibilant represented today by *dd*. In Germany, where they give the name “law” to the smallest discovery, this would be called “Rhys’s Law.”—Gaidoz, *International Review of Teaching*, 1917, p. 19.</note> It is therefore clear that, just as the British *Lugu-belinos* became the Welsh *Llywelyn*, so the British *Lugu-balion* became the Welsh *Llywelydd* <note>*Llywelydd* may represent *Lugubalion* or *Luguvalion*, but it implies a single *l*.</note> exactly. The spelling *Lliwelydd* is due to the fact that the word had gone out of common use before *i* and *y* were distinguished in writing in the thirteenth century. It is no exaggeration to say that nothing in British topography is more certain than that *Caer Liwelydd* means Carlisle.

Caer Llion.—In the Nennian *Additions* this name occurs in the ancient form *Cair legion* (*The Cymmrodor*, volume 9, p. 183). The Medieval Welsh form is *Caer Llion* or *Caer Lleon*; in the spoken language the second element is now lost, and the name is simply *Caer*, meaning “Chester.” Dr. Evans, having shifted *Caer Liwelydd* to Chester, is forced to seek another location for *Caer Llion*. “Could it be that the Cair legion of the [Nennian] list means Holt, to which the name of ‘Caer lleon’ has adhered?” Volume II, p. xiii. The name has not “adhered” to Holt. The medieval Welsh name of

<meta>This page continues the linguistic and historical argument regarding site identification, moving from the proof of Carlisle's identity to the identification of Caer Lleon as Chester and Alclud as Dumbarton, further refuting Dr. Gwenogvryn Evans's geographical relocations.</meta>

<page-num>60</page-num>

Holt appears to have been *Castell Llion*, which is found written as *Chastellion* in 1311 (*Arch. Camb.*, 1907, p. 9); this was translated into Latin as *Villa Leonum* (*ib.*, p. 4), or *Castrum Leonis* (*ib.*, p. 11), and into English as *Castle of Lions*. The form *Caerleon* is found only once, in a license by Edward II dated 1319 (*ib.*, p. 10). But the name *Caer Lleon* has remained attached to Chester by a tradition so universal and so insistent that our editor himself cannot escape its grip: he writes “Caer lleon on the Dee, or Chester” (p. ix), and translates *Iarll Kaer Llion* as “earl of Chester” (p. 100). The attempt to turn *Gueith Cair Legion* in the “Annales” under the year 613 into “the Battle of Holt” instead of “the Battle of Chester” can be easily refuted by two quotations: Bede, in his account of the battle, uses the words “*ad civitatem Legionum, quae a gente Anglorum Legacaestir, a Brettonibus autem rectius Carlegion, appellatur*” (at the city of the Legions, which is called *Legacaestir* by the English people, but more correctly *Carlegion* by the Britons), *H.E.*, ii, 2. William of Malmesbury, also speaking of the battle, says “*Legionum civitas, quae nunc simpliciter Cestra vocatur*” (The city of the Legions, which is now simply called Chester), i, 47 (cited in Plummer’s *Bede*, ii, p. 77). Thus *Carlegion* is the city of the legions, which in the early twelfth century was called simply “Chester.”

*Alclud.*—The Welsh name *Alclud* became *Alclyde* in English, which was later replaced by the Irish name *Dūnbrettan* (“fortress of the Britons”), now Dumbarton. Bede calls the town “*Alcluith, quod lingua eorum significat petram Cluith; est enim juxta fluvium nominis illius*” (*Alcluith*, which in their language means the rock of Cluith; for it is near the river of that name), *H.E.*, i, 12; Adamnan, even earlier, calls it, as we have seen, *Petra Cloithe*; these spellings represent the Irish pronunciation of the name. The Welsh form is seen in the “Annales” under the year 870: “*Arx alt clut a gentilibus fracta est*” (The fortress of Alclud was destroyed by the heathens), and in the *Red Book Bruts*: “870 was the year of Christ when . . . the fortress of Alclud was destroyed by the pagans,” p. 259. No one has ever suggested before that *Alclud* in Welsh literature means anything other than Dumbarton; *Din Alclud* is in the North (*Gogledd*) according to

Gwalchmei, see above p. 55; Kaer Alclut was opposite Scotland (i.e. Scotland north of the Clyde), *R. B. Bruts*, p. 63. But for Dr. Evans, Dumbarton will not do at all, and he has by hook or by crook to find an Alclud in his North. There is a Clutton in Cheshire; the brook which flows by is therefore the Clud. “This brook name is old and authentic, for it appears in the Domesday Clut-tone”, p. xix. Because the town-name is old, therefore Clud must be the brook! Aldford is on this brook; this supplies the *Al-*. Therefore Alclud is Aldford. Our good editor invites us to accept a name made by himself, in the style of Lewis Carroll, from the *Al* of Aldford and the *Clut* of Clutton, in place of the Alclyde of history. But even this will not always do: “*ryt alclut* is pure gibberish here. ? *Ryt y Gors*”, p. 97; and *Din Clut*, which happens to be spelled *dynclut* in the text, 73.7, is explained in the index as “O. ap Kadwgan”.

**Caer Weir.**—This occurs twice in the text. The first time, 13.7, the editor suggests that it is an error for “Weri, i.e. Gwerydd, a name of part of the lower Dee”, see below; the second time, 69.12, it is an error for “K(aer) Veir, i.e. Bangor Cathedral, which is dedicated to Mary”. So is the church of Llanfair Pwllgwyngyll, and I put in a claim for this, though, I confess, I have never heard either church called a *caer*. But, unfortunately for both of us, Kaer Weir is in the North; it is named in the text with Kaer Liwelydd, and is probably, as has been suggested, Durham. The ancient name of the Wear was *Vedra*; and as Latin *cathedra* gives Welsh *cadeir*, so the ancient *Vedra* gives Welsh *Gweir* exactly.

**Gweryd.**—This is the Welsh name of the Forth. Skene, *F.A.B.*, i, p. 56, quotes a description of Scotland written in 1165 and printed in his *Chronicles of the Picts and Scots*, p. 136, which says that the river was “called in Scottish <note>Gaelic</note>

The Welsh *Gweryd* is, as Skene dimly saw, the phonetic equivalent of the Irish *Forth*; the Irish *th* implies that a vowel once separated it from the *r*—it corresponds to Welsh *d* from the original *t*.<note>For the shift of *o* to *e* in Welsh, compare the verb *gweryd* ('saves') which appears in Old Welsh (*Juv. cod.*) as *guorit*; compare also *cegin* from Latin *coquina*, etc.</note> The name occurs in our text: *o wawl hyt weryt*, 18·5, 'from the (southern) wall to the Forth'. But the editor insists that *wawl* is "Domesday's *Waure*, now Woore in the parish of Mucklestone (!) . . . This *Wawl* has nothing to do with any Roman, or other Wall, or *vallus*", p. 86. To this assertion, it is only necessary to oppose the testimony of the "Historia Brittonum" (Harl. 3859) that the wall was called "Guaul in the British tongue",<note>Welsh *gwawl* is the equivalent of the Irish *fāl* ('a hedge'), and is not derived from the Latin *vallum*; compare Fick-Stokes, p. 275 f.</note> § 23. The editor's next note begins, "*weryt=Gweryd*, i.e. Dee". But final *-t* in this manuscript does not mean -*δ*, but -*d*: and it is simple misrepresentation to write the name *Gweryδ*, as he persists in doing. But what is the evidence for associating the name with the Dee? Here it is:

> According to *Descriptio Albanie*<note>This is another title invented by Dr. Evans. He quotes at second hand from *F.A.B.* and gives no reference. If he had consulted the *Chronicles* he would have seen that the Latin title is *De Situ Albanie*, p. 135.</note> the river Forth divided 'the kingdoms of the English and Scots' at Stirling in 1165, and was 'called *Froch* in Gaelic and *Werid* in British'. Similarly, part of the lower Dee divided Saxon and Brython (p. 86).

Because the Dee is "similar" to the Forth in being on a border, he considers himself justified in asserting, without a scrap of evidence, that it had the same name.

Irish *Cruithni* means 'Picts'. It means 'Picts' and 'Pictland', just as *Cymry* means 'Welshmen' and 'Wales', and as *Ffrainc* means 'Frenchmen' and 'France'. Naturally, *Prydyn* is often used more loosely to refer to Scotland; it occurs, as we have seen, as a synonym for *y Gogledd* (the North), and is often misspelled as *Prydein*. What is it called in the new geography? It is "the modern counties of Flint and Denbigh"! This is "proved" in the usual way. The words "Gwydyl iwerdon mon aphrydyn" in the text, 13·9, which mean 'The Goidels of Ireland, Mona (probably the Isle of Man), and Scotland', are quoted thus: "Gwydyl (rhy·doethon) Von a Phrydyn", and translated as "the Gwydyl, who had come to Mon and Prydyn", p. xx. Again, the line "Kymry eigyl gwydyl prydyn", 75·19, that is, 'Welshmen, Angles, Goidels, Picts',<note>Compare Nennius § 7, "in ea habitant quattuor gentes, Scotti Picti Saxones atque Brittones" (four nations inhabit it: the Scots, Picts, Saxons, and Britons). Scotti equals the Welsh *Gwyddyl*, or 'Goidels'.</note> is misquoted as "Gwydyl, Eingl, a gwyr Prydyn" and translated as "the Gwydyl, the Angles, and the men of Prydyn", ibid. *Kymry* is omitted so that it may appear that "gwyr Prydyn" (the men of Prydyn) are Welshmen; and thus we have the absurd grouping "Irishmen, Englishmen, and men of Flint-and-Denbigh", which reminds one of the saying "Cymru, Lloegr, a Llanrwst" (Wales, England, and Llanrwst). Then we are told that a poet in the *Black Book*, 49·3, "prophesies that there will be war in Prydyn: (the Kymry) will defend their coast". The proof here relies on the phrase "the Kymry" which the editor himself has inserted—it is not in the original text. It is clear that he is quite unable to detect the childish fallacy of *petitio principii* (begging the question) in his own reasoning. He has convinced himself that Prydyn is Denbighshire, and states that "It was the translators of the Bruts, toward 1200, who started the Scottish figments," p. xxi. translators of the *Bruts*, who by a sort of miracle anticipated the discovery of modern philology that the Welsh *Prydyn* equals the Irish *Cruithni*. I have only to say that no such miracle ever happened. The word *Prydyn* with its meaning was handed down by tradition like any other Welsh word which philologists equate with its Irish cognate; it was used long before 1200 in the lines which the editor misquotes from the *Book of Taliesin*, and it clearly bears its traditional meaning in these lines *as they appear in the text*.

*Rheged.*—Urien of Rheged, who is mentioned in the “Saxon Genealogies” as having fought against Ida’s sons Hussa and Deodric, is the subject of several of the Taliesin poems which have some claim to be considered authentic. The exact position of his kingdom has not been determined with certainty. In the Welsh translation of Geoffrey’s *Brut*, Rheged is identified with Geoffrey’s Mureif, which, according to the context, was in the neighborhood of Loch Lomond. Dr. Evans charges all modern scholars with blindly accepting this identification:

> And the learned of all ages and eminence have walked the path of faith, lost in ‘wandering thoughts’ and vain notions, without once verifying their references (p. xiii).

This weak and blustering rant bears the same relation to the truth as his “further testimony” regarding the Chirk Codex. The facts are as follows. Lewis Morris says that “Rheged is supposed to be Cumbria, now Cumberland” (Stephens, *Lit. Kym.*, p. 267). Sir Francis Palgrave placed Rheged in the South of Scotland around Dumfriesshire; see the map opposite page 30 in his *History of England*, i, 1831. The Reverend T. Price (*Carnhuanawc*), in his *Hanes Cymru*, 1842, p. 278, identified it with the modern Cumberland. It is the country surrounding Oswestry; the name "means a Run or March, for it is obviously a metathesis of *Rhedeg*," p. xiii. "Obviously"! though neither *rhedeg* in Welsh nor *run* in English ever means a "march" or "border."¹ He quotes a line from Cynddelw in which *redeg* has its usual meaning, gives the word a capital *R*, and because *terfyn Caer Lleon* [the boundary of Chester] occurs four lines before, he claims that *Rheged* is not far from Chester. The only other proof offered is the following:

> Again, Hywel, son of Owain Gwynedd, approaching it from the south, mounts his roan horse, traveling from Maelienydd to the land of Rheged in one night.
->Esgyneis, ar Felyn, o Faelienydd<-
->hyd yn hir Reged rhwng nos a dydd. (*Myv.*, 198b.)<-
Rheged thus lies between the border of the earldom of Chester and a night’s ride from Radnorshire (p. xiii).

Here he is borrowing Stephens’s mistake without acknowledgment; *rhwng nos a dydd* cannot in any case mean "in one night," nor does it mean the interval of twilight; but *rhwng* has the meaning in which it is still used when we say *rhwng y naill beth a’r llall* ("between one thing and another"), i.e., "taking one thing with another." The exact sense of the phrase in question is put beyond doubt by a passage in the *Red Book Mabinogion*, p. 88, where it is stated that Maxen’s messengers rode from Caernarfon to Rome *rhwng dydd a nos*, "and as their horses failed they bought fresh ones." This shows that "a night’s ride" is nonsense; Rheged is a long distance from Maelienydd. The context, which Dr. Evans as usual omits, not only confirms this but supplies a clue to its position:

<note>¹ "Run or March" shows that he believes that *march* ("a border") is a verbal noun (like *run*) coming from the verb *to march*. "of the Echwydd" is parallel to *Llyw Catraeth* ("Prince of Catterick"). The scribe of the Book of Taliesin evidently understands *yr* as the definite article; but the article could hardly occur in a name which is undoubtedly old. The element is more probably the prefix *Er-* (equivalent to *Ar* in *Ar-von*, with *a* regularly affected by the *y* of *ydd*); the name is written *Erechwydd* four times in the Red Book, columns 1040-1, *F.A.B.*, ii, p. 271. It occurs once without the prefix: “when he returned to *Echwydd* from the land of the Clydemen,” 38.21, but this may be an error. The prefix denotes ‘a district adjoining,’ as in *Ar-von*; and *Erechwydd* is not the town but the district, as seen in “*gwenwlat yr echwydd*,” 40.2, ‘the fair land of Erechwydd.’¹ In the ninth century “*Armes*” it is extended to the king-

Evans quotes two examples from *Llyfr yr Ancr* under this meaning, though both translate the Latin *hora diei tertia* (the third hour of the day). The meaning ‘evening’ has been wrongly deduced from the South Wales dialectal *god-echwydd* or *gwed-echwydd*, ‘afternoon,’ as if the prefix meant nothing; but the *god-* or *gwed-* is *gwedy* ‘after’ (Old Welsh *guotig*, *guetig*), so that *echwydd* is ‘noon.’ There is no foundation for the meanings ‘evening’ or ‘west.’

---

¹ Ptolemy's Κατου- (equivalent to *Catu-*) is certain, see Müller's edition, Paris, 1883, pp. 96-7; and it proves that the original British name had nothing to do with a cataract. It had two forms: (1) a shorter form *Caturacto*, genitive *Caturacton-os*, and (2) a derived form *Caturacton-ion*; the former may have been the name of the camp at Thornbrough (the original site), the latter the name of the town. *Caturacto*, liable to become later *Cataracto* (compare Gaulish *Catumandus*, late British *Catamanus*), was very naturally taken by the Romans for *Catarrhacta*. Popular etymology is apt to substitute for a name of unknown or forgotten meaning another similar in sound but made up of familiar words, such as *sparrowgrass* for *asparagus*. Place-names are peculiarly liable to be treated this way; English examples are *The Rivals* for *Yr Eifl*; *Barmouth* for *Abermaw*; *Money Farthing Hill* (Herefordshire) for *Mynydd Fferddun*; *Bridgewater* for *Burgh de Walter*; *Waterford* for *Widder Fjord*, etc. As these examples show, the appropriateness (if any) of the substituted homonym is accidental and fanciful. *Catarrhacta* is such a homonym, and it would be a mere coincidence if there were falls at the spot; Codrington's suggestion that “the river may have been, for defense, held up by a weir,” op. cit., 1905, p. 178, is therefore

70

kingdom of Rheged: after “from the Wall to the Forth” quoted above on page 62, comes the line—

->Llettatawt eu pennaeth tros yr *Echwyð*, 18.6<-

‘Their sovereignty will extend (*llettahawt*) over Erechwydd’.

How Geoffrey [of Monmouth] came to assign Mureif to “Urien”—whether he confused him with the Pictish king Brude Urgant, or whether he thought Mureif had something to do with the wall, I cannot say; but since Mureif was assigned to Urien in the original, it was natural for the translator or copyist to gloss it as Rheged. The South Wales Rheged is also a fairly obvious fiction; if there had been a Rheged “between the Tawy and the Towy,” medieval Welsh literature would not have been entirely silent about it. It appears to be one of the numerous fabrications of the authors of the *Iolo MSS.* Urien had already been relocated to Gower and Kidwelly, seemingly to bolster the claim of Gruffudd ap Nicolas to be descended from him. The pedigree is provided in *Gwaith Lewis Glyn Cothi*, 1837, p. 130, and represents Gruffudd as a descendant of Urien in the fifteenth degree—fifteen generations in nine centuries! In a biography of his grandson, Sir Rhys ap Thomas, written in the early seventeenth century and printed in the *Cambrian Register*, 1795, Gruffudd’s “descent” is said

unnecessary. Camden derived the name from the falls “hard by” (*loc. cit.*), though he added later, “but nearer Richmond” (1600 ed., p. 656). Horsley “did not perceive or hear of any fall of water closer than Richmond, which is three miles from Catterick bridge... though Thornborough stands higher up the river and a little closer to the falls” (*Britannia Romana*, 1732, p. 399). We may assume that these falls were called *Echwydd* (or its Old British equivalent), and that the district of *Erechwydd* took its name from this. The fact that the town stood in this district must have seemed a very satisfactory explanation for the coincidental name *Catarracta*, which apparently replaced the original forms in common use. The Welsh *Catraeth* cannot be derived from *Cataractō* or any case of it, but represents *Catarracta* (even including the double *rr*, see above, p. 67, fn. 2).

<summary>The author argues that the South Wales location for Rheged is a historical fabrication, likely created to support genealogical claims, and discusses the etymological link between the name "Catraeth" and the Latin "Catarracta" related to local waterfalls.</summary>

to go “upward in a direct series and long chain of worthy ancestors up to Sir Urian Rheged, king of Gower in Wales, prince of Moray (*Murriff*) in Scotland, lord of Kidwelly, and knight of the round table to King Arthur”, p. 56. This is mythical enough; but Rheged is not yet identified in it with Gower and Kidwelly—that was the finishing touch of the Iolo romancers.

*Llwyfenydd.*—Dr. Evans had already in his introduction to the *Black Book*, 1906, p. xxvi, identified a Coed Llwyfein, the scene of one of Owein Gwynedd’s battles (*Myv.* 150a) with a mountain pass in Flintshire; he now refers to Edward Lhwyd’s *Parochialia* (*Arch. There is an Argoed in the parish of Mold (as in several other places); so this country is “demonstrably” Argoed Llwyfein. “It has been pointed out that a forest 15 miles long by 4½ miles wide stretched along the Flintshire coastline (Prof. Lloyd, *Trans. Cymmr.*, 1899-1900, p. 139) . . . . I identify this forest . . . . as the *Llwyvenyð* of Kymric poetry”, p. xxii. This forest is the smiling land in the North described by Taliesin, p. 65, the land whose riches, comforts, and luxuries it is his to enjoy! There was, no doubt, a Llwyvein in Flintshire, and there are plenty of Levens, which may possibly represent the same name. Skene, accepting the *Brut* story of a Rheged north of the Clyde, found a Leven nearby, running from Loch Lomond to the sea at Alclyde, and placed Urien’s Llwyvenydd there, right under the nose of Rhydderch Hael. There is a Leven in Fifeshire, and a Leven in Cumberland, running from Windermere to the sea; but there is nothing to connect these with Urien. *Llwyvenydd* and *Llwyvein* represent different accentuations of the same British stem **Leimanio-*. <meta>This page continues the philological and geographical investigation into the locations mentioned in Taliesin’s poetry, specifically moving from potential sites in Flintshire and Scotland to the Roman road near Catterick as the possible "Road of Llwyvein."</meta>

# Taliesin.

72

The Roman road running south from Catterick, in a line so straight that it is noticeable on the map, is called *Leeming Lane.* This may well be the Road of *Llwyvein*, which in sixth-century Welsh would be **Lēmein*, with a slightly softened *m* and a palatal *n* liable to become *ng* in Welsh itself, as in *Eingion* for *Enion*. Taliesin’s *Lloyfenyδ tireδ* (65.13) are the homelands of Urien; and this district, with its Roman road and Roman town, and doubtless many Roman villas at that time, corresponds well to Taliesin’s description of Llwyvenydd as a land of affluence and refinement.

*Godeu.*—One of the most amusing things in Dr. Evans’s Introduction is his explanation of this name. It is the plural of *cod*: “this carries the same meaning as the second element in the English word peas-cod,” p. xiv. Apparently, he does not know that the Welsh *cod* is borrowed from the English. To explain the initial *G*, he assumes that it is the softened form found in the compound *Gwyδ-godeu*, with the second element being “used poetically” for the whole! Can any Welshman imagine a name such as *Hengoed*, for example, being treated “poetically” as *Goed*? As if a *g* in that position were not, to every Welsh speaker’s instinct, fundamentally a *c*, even now—to say nothing of the twelfth century! Next, we are told that *gwyδ* (“wood” or “trees”) means brushwood, and “*gwyδ-godeu* signifies *pod-bearing scrub*. What is Shropshire but the *schrobbes* (plural) country of which our *Gwyδ-godeu* is a translation,” ibid. But if *gwyδ-godeu* is a compound as is assumed here, it must mean “tree-pods,” just as *cannwyll-brennau* means “candlesticks.” It appears that footnote 21 is due to a vague awareness of this difficulty: “Bean-pods = *codeu ffa*; but *ffa-godeu* = bean-stalks tied in bundles.” But if *ffa-godeu* means “bean-stalks,” why does *gwyδ-godeu* not mean “scrub-stalks”? It is unnecessary to dwell on this helpless

<meta>This page continues the philological critique of Dr. Evans's Introduction to the Book of Taliesin, specifically refuting his etymological theories regarding the name "Godeu" and establishing its meaning as "forest" rather than "pods."</meta>

[is] floundering, other than to say that *ffagodeu* has nothing to do with *ffa* or *codeu*, but is simply the Welsh plural of the English word *faggot*. The usual pronunciation of the name is not *Godeu* but *Goδeu*, and there is good evidence that this is right. As a common noun, *goδeu* in the Book of Taliesin clearly means a forest¹; and the expression *gwyδ goδeu* (32.18) is not a compound, but two separate words, written separately in the manuscript, and meaning "trees of a forest"—"*a blaen gwyδ goδeu*" and "the tips of forest trees." The other example referred to is not even *gwyδ goδeu*, but *gwyδ a goδeu* (25.24) (*gwyd* was first written *gwydeu*, and the *eu* was deleted by underdots); the context is as follows:

> o vriallu a bloδeu | bre, o vlawt gwyδ a goδeu,
> o priδ, o priδret, | pan ym digonet.

"Of primroses and the flowers of the hill, of the blossom of wood and forest,
Of soil, of earth, have I been made."

A poem entitled "Kat goδeu" (pp. 23–27), "the battle of the forest," contains a fanciful account of the mustering of the trees (the second *goδeu* in 24.8 is the unrelated abstract noun *goδeu* meaning "intention"). As a place-name, *Goδeu* seems to mean the country between the two walls. Taliesin speaks of Urien, "*Ae varch ydanaw yg goδeu gweith mynaw*" (59.11); as "*Goδeu gweith Mynaw*" is a peculiar expression, we may perhaps assume that *Goδeu* is a gloss, and translate it as, "with his horse under him at the battle of Mynaw (Goδeu)." The battle of Mynaw or Manaw is probably the "*Cath Manand*" mentioned by Tighernach and the "*Bellum Manonn*" in the Annals of Ulster, which both

In British Museum Vespasian A. xiv, folio 11a (early thirteenth century), among the daughters of Brychan is named “Gurycon Godheu . . . wife of Cathraut calchuynid,” *Y Cymmrodor* xix, p. 26, that is, Gwrygon Godeu, wife of Cadrawd Calchfynydd. Skene has identified Calchfynydd with Kelso, formerly Calchow, where there is “a calcareous eminence . . . still called the Chalk Heugh,” *Four Ancient Books of Wales* i, p. 173. Dr. Evans refers to the memorandum in a footnote on page xiv: “Gurycon (Vricon-ion) is not a person but a place, i.e., the Wrekin, which is in *Godheu* [*dh* = modern *d*, not δ].” The statement in brackets is not true; *dh* occurs only three other times in the document: twice in *Tudhistil*, which appears in the Domitian version as *Tutbistyl*, the original form of the name being probably *Tuduistil*; and once in *Gugan Cledyburdh*, folio 11b, which is clearly Gwgawn Gledyfrud, *Red Book Mabinogion*, pp. 159, 304, 306, and proves that *dh* means δ. But how did the Wrekin become a saint? We are to read “[Meilien of] Gurycon.” In support of this, we are told that “Milburga established a convent at Much Wenlock, which tradition asserts to have been called Llan Meilien”—no reference, no proof of any kind that “Meilien” is meant, or was a daughter of Brychan, or the wife of Cadrawd. “Thus,” continues the editor, “a Saxon lady becomes a Welsh saint, though the manuscript judiciously omits her name.” “Judiciously omits”—as when he, for example, writes “Rhun . . . ” for “Rhun vab Maelcun”—it saves so much explaining away. Whatever view is taken of the children of Brychan, we have in the memorandum quoted above a record of a tradition which connects *Godheu* with Calchfynydd; and if Skene is right in his identification

Concerning the latter, it implies that *Godeu* extended down to the Scottish border. In the historical poems in the *Book of Taliesin*, “*Godeu* and *Reget*” occurs twice (60.10, 62.7), and seems to represent the British regions of the North, just as Deira and Bernicia stood for the Anglian regions.

*Aeron.*—According to Dr. Evans, this was “a district—extending, apparently, from Eulo to Chester” (p. xix), a distance of about six miles as the crow flies; however, it has been difficult to find room for this “district.” This identification is supported by three “quotations.” In the first, *Vlph* is assumed to be Ranulf, son of Hugh Lupus, and *Urien* is interpreted as “Owein” and assumed to be Owein Gwyneð; thus, the proof is founded, as usual, on his own assumptions. The second is similar: “When Henry II disappeared from the Ceiriog valley in the rain, he went to ‘stay and shelter in Aeron,’ 63.6.” In the passage referred to, there is no mention of Henry II, or of Ceiriog, or of “staying” or “sheltering.” The third is as follows: “Owein Gwyneð extended his rule from Anglesey to Tegeingl, to Aeron, to Chester.” Here we find the names beautifully arranged so that Aeron takes its place neatly between Tegeingl (Flintshire) and Chester, as it should according to the theory. Turning to the reference *Myv.* 153, we find something quite different. The words occur in *Cynddelw’s* elegy on Owein Gwyneð; here is a literal translation of as much of the context as is necessary to make the meaning clear—the first two and last four lines of the stanza:

> It is no falsehood to say that he was the best hero
> From the German Ocean to the Irish Sea;
> . . . . . . .
> To Canterbury, to maintain the privilege of the Britons,
> To Leicester and to Chester,
> To East Anglia, to Anglia, to Aeron, went forth
> His supremacy from Penmon.

->*Priodawr clodvawr Clud ac Aeron. (Myv. 160.)*<-

The line occurs in Cynddelw’s elegy on Cadwallawn mab Madawc; it means “the renowned ruler of Clud and Aeron,” and refers to “Gwryal Gwron,” to whom the dead man is compared. (As an aside, is not the “renowned ruler” of two little brooks a trifle absurd?) The person meant is obviously the first named in the triad of the “kings who rose from serfs,” namely, “*Gwryat vab Gwryon yn y Gogled*” <note>Gwryat son of Gwryon in the North</note>, R. B. Mab., p. 308, *Y Cymmrodor* vii, p. 132. Aeron then is, like Clud, in the North; which is nothing new, for it is clearly in the North in the Book of Taliesin and the Book of Aneirin, though in the former the Cardiganshire Aeron is also mentioned, 73.4. But where is this northern Aeron? <meta>This page continues the philological argument regarding the location of Aeron, specifically refuting Dr. Evans's placement of the region in Cheshire and arguing for its location in Scotland (Ayr).</meta>

# Taliesin.
->77<-

[...so he] must [abandon] his own hesitant suggestion that Aeron may have been south of Rheged. Dr. Evans is undoubtedly correct in emphasizing the importance of the association between *Aeron* and *Clud* in the line he quotes—his error is the fundamental one of mistaking the Clyde for a small Cheshire stream. The passage proves that a tradition existed in the twelfth century stating that the kingdoms of Clud and Aeron were united under Gwryat. *Clud* is Strathclyde; what can *Aeron* be but Ayr, which lies between Strathclyde and the sea—the outer Firth of Clyde? The union of these kingdoms took place after the period we are discussing; and it may be that Gwryat was the king whose death is recorded in the *Annals of Ulster* under the year 658: “Death of Gureit, king of Alocluaithe <note>Alt Clut or Dumbarton</note>,” *Chronicles of the Picts and Scots*, p. 349. It is not inconsistent with this identification that in one of Taliesin’s poems Urien “came” to Aeron (61.9), or that in another he is called a “defender in Aeron” (63.5-6). He is the first of the four kings named in the “Saxon Genealogies” as having fought against the sons of Ida; the three others were Rhydderch, Gwallawg, and Morgant. The last two, like Urien himself, were descended from Coel, whose kingdom was in Aeron and whose name still clings to the district of Kyle; either or both may have ruled in that region. In any case, by fighting in Aeron, Urien would have been defending the territories of his kinsmen.

*Eidyn.*—Dr. Evans writes this name as *Eiðin*. This is a late and incorrect form; the *-in* ending is due to the assimilation of the unaccented *y* in the last syllable to the *i* in the penult <note>the second-to-last syllable</note>, as seen in the spoken *gwreiddin* instead of *gwreiddyn* (see my *Welsh Grammar* p. 111, iii). However, the use of *ð* instead of *d* can only be explained as due to

---

Curiously enough, *aeron* seems from 38.2 to be the true reading, and *dylleinw*, as Mr. Williams has seen, is an error for *dyleith*. Consequently, there was originally no “flood” and no “river” in the stanza, and Skene’s *Avon* is a ghost <note>a non-existent or illusory reference</note>.

<meta>This page continues the philological argument regarding the name "Eidyn" (Edinburgh), specifically refuting Dr. Evans's spelling of the name as "Eiðin" and his subsequent geographic misidentification.</meta>

# Taliesin.

...misreading the written form, owing to the fact that both *d* and *ð* are written *d* in late medieval orthography. In the old poetry, the name always rhymes with *-yn*; thus in the Black Book—in which *i* and *y* are not distinguished in the spelling, but are distinct in the rhymes—*Eidyn* rhymes with *a chinbin* (*cynbynn*, ‘dog-heads’) and *cuitin* (*cwyddyn* ‘they fell’), 95.8, and with *tytin* (*tyddyn*) in one *englyn*, and *brin* (*bryn*) in another in 64.1.4; in the Book of Taliesin, it rhymes with *brithwyn*, 22.22, and *kyverbyn*, 30.1; in the Book of Aneirin with *arwynn*, 5.9, and *gwehin* (*gwehyn*), *disgin* (*disgyn*), 33.16; in each case, the rhyme is *-ynn*, distinguished from *-yn* in early verse. In the Black Book, medial *ð* is generally written *t*, but this name is consistently written with *d*, as *eidin* 94.14, 95.7, *idin* 64.2.5—contrast *tytin*, *cuitin*, above. In the Welsh Genealogies in Harl. 3859, it is written *eitin* (*Y Cymmrodor* ix, p. 173), which is decisive, for in the Old Welsh spelling of this document, medial *t* regularly stands for *d*, and medial *d* for *ð*. The correct form of the name then, in modern and late medieval spelling, is *Eidyn*. 

In spite of the clear and conclusive evidence of the manuscripts, our editor not only writes the name *Eiðin*, but actually cites this as the form in the Black Book. “*Eiðin*”, he says, “is in the border country”—the Welsh border. The proof divides itself into two parts. The first part is as follows:—“In *Prydein* in *Eiðin* (Owain Gwynedd) is acknowledged chief: also at Gavran on the Brecon border, 30.20”. History, he says, accords with this, “but there is not so much as an old wife’s tale to vouch for his sway on the Forth”, p. xxiii. The editor himself indicates that the name “Owain Gwynedd” is not in the text by including it in brackets—it is his own assumption; and on this assumption the proof entirely depends. The second part consists of identifications

# Taliesin.

which depend entirely upon the mistaken form *Eiðin*. Thus, the Black Book is said to refer to “*mynyð Eiðin* 95.7, which is synonymous with Bre Eiðin, now Breiðin Hill.” First, the reading in the Black Book is “minit eidin,” which, correctly transcribed, is *mynyð Eidyn*; and medieval *d* cannot be equated with modern *ð*. Second, the argument implies that *Breiðin* is a modern contraction of a name which was *Bre Eiðin* in the twelfth century; but as the hill was already called *Breiðin* in that century, the argument fails. The name occurs twice in Gwalchmai’s “Gorhoffedd”; first in *Dygen Freiðin*, rhyming with *hin*, *trin*, *ffin*, and many other words all ending in *-in* (*Myv.* 142b); second in *bre Freidyn*, which is *bre Freiðin*, since it rhymes with *trin* (ibid. 143a); in the original manuscript, the symbol for *ð* is used in both cases. *Bre Freiðin* finally disproves the *Bre Eiðin* theory; and the twelfth-century *Breiðin* cannot possibly have anything to do with the *Eidyn* of the Book of Taliesin. The other identifications rest upon the same error, so it would be a waste of time to discuss them. Eidyn is certainly in the North; this is sufficiently evidenced by the name of Clydno Eidyn, one of the four leaders of the men of the North whose names Dr. Evans “judiciously omits” when giving the “testimony” of the Chirk Codex. Ab Ithel identifies “Eiddin” with Edinburgh (*Gododin*, 1852, p. 99); Stephens, around the same time, writes it “Eidyn, or Eiddyn, or Eiddin,” and equates it with the *Edin* of “Edinburgh” (*Gododin*, 1888, p. 178). Reeves (*Vita S. Columbæ*, 1857, p. 202) says that *Etan* (in “the siege of Etan,” Tig. 638, An. Ult. 637) “is not Edinburgh . . . but *Cair Eden*, the *Eiddyn* of Aneurin . . . now Carriden, a parish on the Forth, in Linlithgowshire, the identification of which we learn from the interpolator of Gildas’ History: ‘Kair Eden, a city

very ancient, at a distance of nearly two miles from the monastery of Abercurnig, which is now called Abercorn.’ (Chapter 9, Monument. p. 5.)”. It is therefore futile to ask “where is there anything to connect *Eidin* with Edinburgh?” *Mynyδ Eidyn* in the Black Book is undoubtedly Edinburgh as well. In the Book of Aneirin, Mynyδawc Mwynvawr is ‘lord of Eidyn’, *ut*$^2$ *Eidyn*, 35.11, about the end of the sixth century; a generation earlier, Clydno Eidyn led the raid to Gwynedd to avenge Elidyr Mwynvawr, see above, p. 47. B.T. 29.26 should be *o barth Manaw*<note>1 *Maw* results from the most common of scribal errors, the error of anticipation, where the scribe, in writing the first *a*, mistook it for the second.</note> *ac Eidyn*. I have now dealt with the more important northern names occurring in the Book of Taliesin and with Dr. Gwenogvryn Evans's attempt to prove that they refer to places on the Welsh border. His argument is a web of false reasoning that reveals a mind which has never properly understood the meaning of "evidence" or "proof." He bases absolute statements on purely speculative grounds. He has not realized that the foundation of an argument must be an undisputed fact; he even builds upon the very suppositions that are in dispute. The greater part of his argument is one gigantic *petitio principii*, or begging the question. *What he must prove* is that Taliesin lived in the twelfth century and sang to Owein Gwynedd; in order to prove this, he must show that northern names refer to places on the Welsh border. He attempts to show they are on the border and not in the North by insisting that "Owein Gwynedd was never in Scotland" (p. xvii) and that "there is not even a folk tale to attest to his rule on the Forth" (p. xxiii). However, this is only the minor premise; it has no validity without the implied major premise that Owein Gwynedd is intended (though not named) in the poems. It is quite clear that this is implied and forms the basis of the argument; it is also the conclusion of the argument, and the whole is worthless because there is nothing you cannot prove if you begin by assuming

# Taliesin.

The misquotations of the text are driven by the same fallacy; it is because the poems were assumed to be written in the twelfth century that the text is altered to adapt it to the conditions of that time—there is absolutely no other reason for the numerous alterations of lines that were originally perfect. The text altered in this way is then quoted to prove that the geography belongs to the twelfth century, and *therefore* that the poems *were* written in that century—bringing us back to where we began: it is always the same vicious circle. The rest of the "proof," aside from ridiculous etymologies such as *Godeu* from *cod*, consists of deductions from misquoted passages from the medieval poets and from the Chirk Codex. The context and all details which, if quoted, would reveal the true meaning of the passages, are suppressed; a common noun, *dyheδ*, for example, is quietly changed into a proper name, *Dyved*; names are rearranged to create an effect foreign to the original. Dr. Evans is, of course, completely unaware that he is practicing any deception; he has unconsciously practiced it all on himself, and in his introduction, he presents in good faith the arguments by which he himself was convinced. Like many others who labor under delusions, he believes that he alone is sane:

> It will be more useful to the student to examine the geography of such names as Gogleδ, Prydyn, Aeron, Clud, Argoed Llwyvein, Llwyvenyδ, and Eiδin, which have so hypnotized my predecessors as to paralyze their critical faculty (p. xix).

He has arrived at this conclusion through intuition, not by studying the works of his "predecessors," with which he shows little familiarity. The literature on the subject is referred to only in the vaguest terms; there is no hint

This discovery, which has been revealed only to Dr. Evans, is the complement of another revelation: namely, that sixth-century personal names are eponyms for twelfth-century characters. To his mind, the one proves the other. Urien is Owein Gwynedd because Urien prevailed "in Gogledd"; and Gogledd is Cheshire because "Owein Gwynedd never was in Scotland." That is exactly the reasoning found at the bottom of page 16 and the top of page 17. I have shown that the last statement is worthless for the argument without the assumption that Owein Gwynedd is Urien—that is, without assuming the very thing that has to be proved. The geographical theory is based on the eponym theory and on misrepresentations; the proof of the eponym theory from the geographical theory must therefore be rejected. Let us see if there is any independent evidence for it:

<margin>a Myvyrian Archaiology, 152.</margin>
Turn we then to Urien, of whom we read that he fought Ida’s successors and was pre-eminent among his peers as a military leader. So was Owein Gwynedd among Cymric princes: hence, Urien is a *nom de guerre* for Owein. To show that this is not a mere assumption, witness the elegy by his contemporary, Cynddelw, who describes Owein as "lord of Penmon . . . shepherd of Môn . . . the war-lord of the conflict of Argoed Llwyfain . . . dragon of Coeling . . . terror of Bernicia . . . <note>a</note> blessed dragon of the West." These lines sound so familiar that they might have come from Taliesin’s poems to Urien (p. 16).

A detail which is omitted here, and which invalidates the comparison, is that in Cynddelw’s elegy, Owein Gwynedd is actually named:

->Am Owein Gwynedd yd gwynant.—*Myvyrian Archaiology,* 151b.<-

Owein Gwynedd’s "lordship" undoubtedly "extends from Penmon," and he is the "shepherd of Môn," but where is Urien ever called "lord of Penmon" or "shepherd of Môn"? The imagined resemblances, then, amount to this: that *Cynddelw* calls *Owein Gwynedd* one thing, and Taliesin calls *Urien* another thing, and that *Cynddelw* compares *Owein Gwynedd* to Taliesin’s hero *Owein ap Urien*. There is no *Arδunwen*<note>Professor Lloyd says that the name is properly *Arddynwent*, and that Montalt is not situated in this township, although the township is in the *parish of Mold*.</note> in the text, but *Arddunyon* occurs (30.9), which is also not indexed; there was a battle at *Arddunyon*, but it is not stated who fought or who fell. I look up *Rudlan* in the index and find the references “xvi, n. 35.7.” The reference “xvi” refers to this very statement; the other refers to this note: “35.7 teir caer = ? Conwy, Deganwy, and Rhudlan.” The expression *teir caer* [three forts] occurs in a cryptic poem in which Arthur is mentioned but Urien is not. It is clear then that based on the assumption—which the editor himself questions—that Rhudlan *might be* one of the “three forts” alluded to in an obscure poem that has nothing to do with Urien, he bases the categorical statement that “Taliesin’s Urien prevailed at Rhudlan.”

Such are the grounds on which the editor confidently asserts that Taliesin’s Urien is Owein Gwynedd. But this equation leads him into many difficulties, of which he makes no mention in his Introduction. *Glyw Reget* (‘prince of Rheged’, 57.7) is “corrected” to *glyw rygas* (‘hated prince’, p. 109, II, p. 158); but since this prince is Urien, the Urien here must be O. ap Kadwgan, and the editor claims that “Urien (57.8) is used for Owein because of *cynghaned*.” By *cynghaned* here he means rhyme; similarly in 42.7, Urien is said to be “Owein (K.) to rhyme with Dygen” (p. 100). So, if the simple requirements of the rhyme demand it, Urien—the specific namesake of Owein Gwynedd—may be used for Owein ap Kadwgan or Owein K(yveiliog) at will! Again Taliesin’s

# Taliesin.

(p. 112; Vol. II, p. 114). Again, Taliesin's Urien, and the historical Urien, had a son—the famous Owein ap Urien—whose elegy appears in the text. Now, Owein Gwynedd cannot be the son of Urien if he *is* Urien, for even Dr. Evans understands that a man cannot be his own son; and Owein Gwynedd had no son named Owein. Therefore, the very name Owein ap Urien shatters the theory—unless it can be eliminated. Let us see how the editor attempts to explain it away. The elegy opens thus:

->Eneit Owein ap Vryen<-
->Gobwyllit y ren oe reit.<-

Each line contains seven syllables, and the last word in the first (*Uryen*) rhymes with a word in the middle of the second (*ren*); all the couplets end in *-eit*. Dr. Evans's notes are as follows:

> 67.18 *ap Urien* does not occur elsewhere in Taliesin. The *ap* is unintelligible here. Urien might be a gloss on Owein. <term>Cynghanedd</term> and meter make both impossible.
> 67.18 *ren* is an anticipation of *reid*. Read: *ner* = Bishop of Bangor . . . . *Ren*, i.e., *röen*, is always a disyllable.

The *ap* is perfectly intelligible and is stated to be "unintelligible" only because it is inconsistent with his theory. There is no justification whatever for the statement that "cynghanedd and meter make both [Owein and Urien] impossible." *Ren* is claimed to be a scribal error in which the *re* of *reid* is anticipated, so the editor changes it to *ner*, destroying what <term>cynghanedd</term> exists in the line (the alliteration of *ren* and *reit*), as he does in dozens of other cases in his absurd list of scribal errors. Turning to Vol. II, p. 124, we find that he gives the couplet thus:

->Eneid Owein, rhy wyssid,<-
->Gobwyllid y ner o'i raid,<-

<meta>This page continues the critique of Dr. Gwenogvryn Evans's editorial methods, specifically his attempts to alter the text of the elegy for Owein ap Urien to fit his identification of Urien with Owein Gwynedd.</meta>

# Taliesin.

and translates it—

> The Soul of Owein *has been summoned*,
> & his (spiritual) lord has come to his rescue.

Instead of *ap Uryen*, he inserts his own “rhy·wyssid” to rhyme with *Gobwyllid*; and he claims in his note that <term>cynghanedd</term> and meter demand this distortion, when nothing but his own theory demands it. Now let us see what the meter actually does demand. He is right in saying that *ren* is a disyllable, *reen*; therefore, the second line is too long. But it is obvious that *reen* here denotes the Deity, and in that sense, it is never preceded by the article *y*, and such a proclitic as *y* ('his') is unusual. The only correction required, therefore, is the omission of this *y*. The lines will then read—

->Eneit Owein ap Uryen,<-
->Gobwyllit Reen oe reit,<-

that is—

> The soul of Owein ap Uryen,
> May the Lord have regard to its need.

This very simple, almost beautiful, couplet has to be mutilated and turned into bathos in the interests of a mad theory. But the theory alone does not account for the vandalism; there is incompetence and blindness as well. Dr. Gwenogvryn Evans has the haziest ideas of tenses and of the uses of words. He translates *gobwyllit* as a perfect tense: the third-person singular active ending *-it* (or *-id*) can only be either the present indicative or the present imperative. The latter is a survival of the old optative and is the form generally found before *Duw* (God) or *Rhên* (Lord), as in *gwrthleðit Duw pob drwc* ('may God ward off all evil'), *Llyfr yr Ancr*, p. 26. Of the meaning of *reit* (modern *rhaid*, 'need') in this connection, he does not have a glimmer of understanding, even though it and *angen* are frequently used in this way, as in the Red Book, *gwares Duw dy anghen* ('may God relieve your need'), *F.A.B.*, ii, p. 220.
