The author demonstrates that a simple omission of an article restores the meter while preserving the original meaning and the name of Owein ap Urien.</summary>

# Taliesin.

and as late as the sixteenth century, *Gras Duw i’n rhaid*—“the grace of God (provide) for our need!” (*Gorchestion*, 1773, p. 296). For his mistranslation, he has no excuse but his own blindness, for Robert Williams translates the line correctly, except that he includes *y* (“its”) before *Ren*: “May its Lord consider its need.” Another example of the same dullness is seen in our editor’s rendering of the last line of this poem. The line is

->Ny rannet rac y eneit.<-

The initial *n* is a <term>Hiberno-Saxon</term> *r* misread as *n*; reading the positive *ry* instead of the negative *ny*, the line means “it <note>his wealth</note> was given away for his soul.” The editor never recognizes any reading errors from the <term>Hiberno-Saxon</term> script because it would upset his theory; but he sees that the phrase is positive and alters *ny* to *in* (“to us”), which is a jarring false note. He gives the rest of the line as “*rhagor i Eneid*” (which makes it too long) and translates this as “his soul goes marching on”! As “*rhagor i Eneid*” is an idiom of his own invention, he can, of course, make it mean anything he likes. But why make any change at all? Clearly, it is because he is ignorant of the common expression *rhag i enaid* (“for his soul”), although it occurs, for example, in the Black Book 84.4.5, *ba beth oreu rac eneid* (“what is best for the soul?”) and in the most familiar of Dafydd ap Gwilym’s “poems of the fancy”:

->*A’r gog rhag f’enaid a gân . . .*<-
->*Paderau ac oriau . . .*<-

“And the cuckoo for *my soul* shall sing paternosters and hours,” 1789 edition, p. 60. Dr. Evans has often transcribed but never grasped the idiom; and so, while he flatters himself that he knows “the dainty tread of the Chief of Bards” (p. xxix), he recognizes it as the tramp of “John Brown’s Body.” To return to his difficulties, there is

# Taliesin.

rather a formidable one in the eleventh line of this elegy, which reads—

->Pan ladawð Owein Fflamðwyn.<-

It means, ‘When Owein killed the Flamebearer’. Now Owein Gwynedd did not kill Henry II. How is the theory to be saved in the face of this? By mistranslating the line as follows: “When Owein pressed the Flamebearer hard”! It will not do. *Lladd* does not mean ‘to press hard’; in other combinations it may mean ‘to cut’, or ‘to strike’; but *lladd dyn* means ‘to kill a man’, and no quibbling can make it mean anything else. No one has ever dreamed that *Na ladd* means ‘thou shalt not press hard’, or imagined the ghost of an ambiguity in this very form *lladdodd* in the story of Cain and Abel, Genesis 4:8; and what is true in this respect of the Welsh Bible is true of Welsh literature generally.

It is certainly a little unsatisfactory that Owein Gwynedd’s eponym should be used for others; to add to the confusion, Owein Gwynedd has other eponyms. The first of these is Cunedda. The editor has found it rather difficult to discover reasons for the appropriateness of this eponym. He tells us that Cunedda “is said to have been the first to bestow land on a church”, p. xxiv. By whom, or where, it “is said” we are not told. Baring-Gould and Fisher know nothing of it; they give “S. Cunedda” a place “among the Welsh Saints more as the ancestor of one of the three great lines of Saints than for any other claim he may have had”, *Lives of British Saints*, ii, p. 191. Dr. Evans goes on to say that Cunedda “became thus the eponym of such as did likewise”. We have only his unsupported statement that Cunedda “is said” to have done it, and the proof that Owein Gwynedd “did likewise” is the fact that he was buried at Bangor:

<header>Taliesin.</header>

Maelgwn [has] the credit of being the first benefactor of Bangor Cathedral, which, after it was burned down in 1102, still found friends in the house of Gwynedd. Witness the honor of burial near the altar given to the remains of Gruffydd ap Cynan, and to those of Owain Gwynedd.

As a proof of the theory that Cunedda means Owain Gwynedd, this reasoning may be allowed to speak for itself. It is followed by an account, summarized from Professor Lloyd's history, of Owain Gwynedd's ecclesiastical troubles, which the editor shows to be referred to in [lines] 69–70 by quoting his own translation of the text as "amended" by himself. In the emendations, the theory is of course assumed, so that we have here only another example of the editor's persistent habit of proving a thing by assuming it in his premises. After this circular argument, there is a digression intended to throw discredit on the Welsh genealogies in Harleian MS 3859:

> But "Cuneda" is an impossible derivative of Cuno-dag, ª
> which would give Cynda in twelfth-century Welsh. The very
> form "Cuneda" shows that the compiler of the Harleian pedi-
> grees was combining material of various dates and origin.

<note>ª I do not question that Cuno-dag is an ancient form picked up somewhere. But its transformation into "Cunedda" proves that the compiler was a late, i.e., twelfth-century fabricator.</note>

The form in the Welsh genealogies is *Cuneda*; see *Y Cymmrodor IX*, pp. 170, 172, 178 twice, 181, 182; and this is a quite possible form for the end of the tenth century, when these pedigrees were compiled. In the "Saxon Genealogies," we find the older form *Cunedag*; see the frontispiece to this volume, line 14; this is the form that would be written at the time of the final redaction of the tract about the end of the eighth century, or even at the time when it was first written, a century earlier. "Cuno-dag" does not occur at all! It is a hypothetical or imaginary form "picked up somewhere" by Dr. Evans himself. The statement that the *fact* (Cunedda) is "im-"

A third eponym of Owain Gwynedd is Maelgwn:

> Maelgwn, likewise, is the eponym of Owain Gwynedd, whose son Rhun is said, in the Chirk Codex, to be the son of Maelgwn . . . the first Gwledig of Gwynedd (pp. xxv-xxvi).

This is the "further testimony" of the Chirk Codex all over again. There is not a syllable of truth in the statement that Owain Gwynedd's "son Rhun is said, in the Chirk Codex, to be the son of Maelgwn." Rhun ab Owain Gwynedd is not mentioned at all in the manuscript; nothing "is said" about him. Rhun ab Maelgwn is mentioned, and the context and the whole purpose of the memorandum, which is to account for ancient privileges, show that Rhun ab Maelgwn is intended. Dr. Evans is not, of course, consciously telling a lie; he is

---

<note>¹ The first element in *Cunedda* is not the usual British *Cuno-* or *Cune-*, which results in Old Welsh *Cin-*, and Medieval and Modern *Cyn-*, as in *Cin-glas* (*Y Cymmrodor* ix, p. 172), now *Cyn-las*, but rather the equivalent of *cun* 'lord', which would be **kouno-* in British (perhaps *Caune* is the feminine genitive). The British form then would be **kouno-dagos*. The stem-vowel of the first element of a compound is usually lost, so that one would expect *Cun-da*; but it may be retained, as shown by *Dino-gat*, which exists as well as *Din-gat*. The vowel in the inscriptions often appears as *e* or *i* (see my *Grammar*, p. 190), possibly representing the obscure vowel *e*, written *y* in Welsh. But the obscure vowel tended to become *e*, so that we have, for example, *re-medau(t)* in Old Welsh (Juvencus) for *rhy-feddawd* from the original **(p)ro-med-*. In Ogam, the stem-vowel appears as *a*, which is the Irish modification; it appears as *a* in Welsh in *Dinacat*, another form of *Dingat*. It regularly appears as *a* before *w*, as in *Tudawal* and *Dyfnawal*, beside *Tudwal* and *Dyfnwal*. The *o* of *Dino-gat*, the *e* of *Cunedda*, and the *a* of *Dina-cat* probably mean that the forms became standardized at different times. The last element **dagos* in the original form of *Cunedda* is the adjective which is now *da* ('good').</note>

He probably intended to say that *Rhun ab Owain* is called the son of Maelgwn in the Codex; but saying “*is said, in the Chirk Codex, to be the son of Maelgwn*” is more effective and gives the impression that the Codex itself says so. Undoubtedly the editor deceives himself with these verbal tricks as much as he deceives his trusting readers. The underlying assumption in this statement is that Maelgwn in the memorandum is an eponym of Owain Gwynedd; this explains, though it does not excuse, the “Rhun . . .” on page xvii (see above, page 49). It is only necessary to further note the confusion of thought which, from assuming the use of eponyms for special reasons by Taliesin, proceeds to extend the assumption to a prose record to which no such reasons apply.

Brochfael Ysgythrog was Prince of Powys in the middle of the sixth century and was succeeded by his son Cynan Garwyn. The poet says that he sang before Brochfael of Powys (33.7); and a poem to his son, Cynan Garwyn, appears on page 45. Dr. Evans notes these references to Brochfael and Cynan and comments as follows:

> If we credit the *Chronicles*, Brochfael died in 662; while his son, Cynan Garwyn, was living in 870—that is, 208 years later.<gloss>ª Lloyd, 250.</gloss> Add to this the account of Taliesin being “renowned” around 550. History of this sort reduces one to a melancholy, thoughtful silence—not regarding Taliesin, but in respect to his commentators (page xxv).

One need waste no time discussing the rhetoric of the last sentence; but the libel against the writers of Welsh history, which is insinuated throughout the whole quotation, is not to be tolerated, and its misrepresentations must be exposed. # Taliesin.

Brochfael Ysgythrog. Mr. Phillimore, in the standard edition of the text, expressly says in a note that "Brochwel Ysgythrog . . . cannot possibly be meant if the date 662 is right," *Y Cymmrodor* ix, p. 158. Next, Professor Lloyd is accused of stating in his *History of Wales*, p. 250, that "Cynan Garwyn was living in 870." Professor Lloyd, of course, says nothing of the kind; what he says is that the dynasty of Meirionydd was "represented about 870 by a certain Cynan ap Brochwel"; and he refers in a footnote to "Pedigree xviii in Harl. MS. 3859 (*Cymmrodor* ix, 178)," which shows that this Cynan was the son of "Brochmail map Iutnimet," while Cynan Garwyn was the son of "Brocmayl map Cincen," pedigree xxii. Further, on p. 180, Professor Lloyd, speaking of the "Brocmail" who, according to Bede, was responsible for the protection of the monks at the Battle of Chester, adds this footnote:

> "Brocmail" can hardly be Brochwel Ysgythrog, ruler of Powys, for his grandson, Selyf ap Cynan, was killed in this very battle. Nor is it likely that he is the "Brocmail" of the year 662 in Harl. MS. 3859 (*Cymmrodor* ix, 158). The name was, in fact, a very common one; see *Cymmrodor* ix, 177, 178, 179, 181, 182, and for the early form, "Brohomagli," *Inscr. Chr.* No. 158, *Lap. W.* 202, *W. Phil.* (2) 372.

Even if our historians were guilty of the anachronisms which they are thus careful to avoid, what has that to do with Taliesin's commentators? The argument apparently is this: the historians do not know the period of Brochfael Ysgythrog and Cynan Garwyn, who are assigned to the seventh or ninth centuries, and therefore could not be the contemporaries of a mid-sixth-century Taliesin; hence words fail the editor to express his contempt for the "commentators" who regard a sixth-century Taliesin as singing to the real Brochfael and Cynan.

# Taliesin.

Garwyn? They are respectively the eponyms of Owein Keveilog, prince of Powys, and of Kynan ap Owein Gwynedd, lord of Meirionydd, which was once ruled by Kynan Garwyn.

A “Correction” on p. xlvii instructs us to “For Owein Keveilog [*sic*] read Owein Gwynedd”; so while the book was passing through the press, Owein Gwynedd acquired a fourth eponym. The purpose of the alleged “History of this sort” is now clear. If the figures in the poems lived in the age when tradition says Taliesin flourished, a twelfth-century hypothesis is unnecessary and improbable. But if the persons named lived at different times, the eponym theory provides a possible “explanation.” For the editor’s theory, then, this is a test case; and there is one simple fact which decides it against him. Selyf, the son of Cynan Garwyn, fell in the Battle of Chester in 616 at the very latest; his father Cynan and his grandfather Brochfael must therefore, by normal calculation, have been contemporaries of the traditional Taliesin.

“Besides the eponymous, we also have an epithetic class,” p. xxvi. This class includes Haeardur, Hyfeidd, Gwallawg, and Mabon. The first name is changed by the editor to “Haearneidd” and explained as a “man of iron.” “Hyfeidd means the intrepid, courageous—suggested, perhaps, by Courcy”! Someone seems to have pointed out to him that *courageaux* is a rather unusual spelling, so he inserts the correction, “Delete *x* in ‘courageau-x’,” p. xlvii; he evidently misread his friend’s *a* as an *x*! “Gwallawg means missing or lost (at sea),” and therefore denotes Henry fitz Henry! It is scarcely necessary to remark that *gwall-* in personal names (e.g., Cad-wall-on) has nothing to do with *gwall* meaning ‘want’ or ‘defect’¹; if it had,

<meta>This page continues the author’s critique of Dr. Evans’s theories, moving from the "eponym theory" to the "epithetic class" theory, specifically examining the names Gwallawg, Mabon, and others.</meta>

# Taliesin.

*Gwallawg* would be the adjective that means ‘negligent’. There are, of course, *gwall-gof* and *gwall synnwyr*, so that, if something is to be supplied, ‘wanting (in sense)’ is more likely than ‘lost (at sea)’; and there are plenty of people to whom such an “epithet” would apply. “Mabon is the scion of a princely, or royal, line,” and there are two or three of him. The method is a very elastic one, especially in Dr. Evans’s hands: he alters *Haearddur* to bring it within his comprehension; he translates *Hyveidd* into French which he cannot spell; he mistranslates a false etymology of *Gwallawg*, in order to get rid of Gwallawg vab Lleenawg, the contemporary and kinsman of Urien. Such is the proof of the theory of the “epithetic class.”

In what he calls “The Argument” in volume II, p. 1, Dr. Evans writes: “The bard, or bards, after the fashion of this time, sang of contemporaries under assumed names.” The *suggestio falsi* [false suggestion] “after the fashion of this time” is inserted in order to make the statement more convincing. Anyone who will glance over the contents of the *Myvyrian* will see at once how false the suggestion is; all the bards of the twelfth century sing of the princes under their own names. The nearest parallel that Dr. Evans has discovered to what he postulates of the Taliesin poems is Cynddelw’s elegy on Owein Gwynedd; but in that, as pointed out above on page 84, Owein is called by his own name, a fact which Dr. Evans omits to mention. It is, however, a fact that is vital; it constitutes a difference not in degree, but in character, between the things compared. A bard commonly enough styles his patron “Nudd” if he is generous, “Arthur” if he is valiant, “Cei Hir” if he is tall, and so on; but it is always a passing compliment, attributed to an explicitly named subject. There are no poems “To Nudd,” “To Arthur,” or “To Cei,” in which, as in

--- Page 107 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of Dr. Evans’s theory regarding the use of pseudonyms in Welsh poetry, specifically addressing his confusion between pseudonyms used as descriptive titles and those used as the actual subjects of poems.</meta>

<page-num>97</page-num>

# Taliesin.

In Milton's *Lycidas*, for example, the pseudonym is itself the *subject* of the poem, taking the place of the real name. Yet Dr. Evans's assumption is that all of Taliesin's poems are of this nature. The puzzle to me is how anybody could have conceived such an improbable idea. He is, of course, honestly convinced of it. When he implies that the use of "assumed names" was "the fashion of this time," he is not consciously deceiving; he is thinking of the use of pseudonyms as predicates (descriptions), which was common; he confuses that with their use as subjects, which was, to say the very least, unusual; and he cites the commonness of the first use in support of his assumption of the second. In spite of this self-deception, he is at other times conscious of the fact that the practice which he attributes to Taliesin is exceptional, for he gives special reasons for the poet's resorting to it:

> When times are out of joint, men and places are not spoken of by their normal names. Mystification becomes expedient in order to protect life and liberty . . . . Under such circumstances when the friendships of one day were the enmities of the next, a border bard like Taliesin could not, perhaps, sing with safety to himself and his patrons, except cryptically and pseudonymously (p. xv).

So he did it to save his skin. And the curious thing is that he was never found out—until our editor discovered his secret! His being "a border bard" is a rather poor excuse for the despicable cowardice which so clearly distinguishes him from his fellow bards. The works of the twelfth-century poets present many difficulties, but this particular form of intentional "mystification" is not one of them.

It will have been noticed that in the above quotation from volume II, page 1, Dr. Evans speaks of "The bard, or bards." The reason is that he believes that the historical poems "deal with events from the death of Rhun <note>Rhun ab Owain Gwynedd, a Welsh prince who died in 1146/1147.</note> in 1147 to that

They cannot, therefore, all be by a “Taliesin” who was born about 1105, and so “those referring to Richard and John, as well as their Cymric contemporaries, are, in my opinion, by some other poet or poets,” p. xxix. Only two references to the text are provided in the Introduction to support the view that some of the poems deal with Richard and John. The first is as follows:

> We have an echo of the Crusades, and complaints about taxing the monasteries to release King Richard from the *Hual Eurin*, 51.6 (p. xxviii).

The reference is to a poem about Alexander the Great, the beginning of which is lost. Pages 49–50 of the manuscript are missing. The catchword at the end of p. 48 is *kyneilwat*, which means ‘poet’ or ‘supporter.’<note>The suffix *-(i)ad*, when it affects a preceding vowel, always denotes the agent. It affects *a* as in *ceidwad* from *cadw*, or *e* as in *neirthiad*, from *nerth*. The base here is *cynnelw* ‘composition, song’, from which comes the verb *kynnelwaf* ‘I compose, sing’; both are generally followed by *o* ‘of’ as in *kynelv o douit*, B.B. 18, ‘a song of the Lord’; *kynnelw o ddewi* (*o* misprinted as *a*) *Myvyrian Archaiology* 194a; *Teithi cerddorion cynnelwi o haelon*, ibid. 123a, ‘it is the privilege of minstrels to sing of the generous’; *a’m cerdd a’m kynhelw ohonaw*, 176a, 187a. The person is also introduced by *gan*, as in *Neud cennyd cerdd glyd cynnelwaf*, 190a ‘Of Thee I will compose a song of praise’, cf. 162a, 166a. Cf. also *Prydesteu cymry, cymrodyal kynhelw*, 188a, and *kynnelwaf* as a synonym of *prydaf* in 189a 54-5. *Kynheilweit* ‘singers’, 182b. The verb has the above meaning when it is intransitive, or has *cerdd*, etc., for its object; when a person is the direct object, the verb means ‘to support’, such as by testimony in a court of law, *Ancient Laws*, i, p. 156.2. *Kynheilwat* ‘supporter’, *Myvyrian Archaiology* 183a, 205b.</note> But to Dr. Evans, it “suggests a calling together of men for the Crusades,” p. 105; he mistakes the *-at* for an abstract noun ending, and takes the stem to be *galw* ‘to call’. In this misconception, he finds a clue to the meaning of the lines at the top of p. 51, two pages further on. He therefore takes the poem on that page to be an

But Alexander occurs in line 6; the editor suggests that it means “? Saladin” there! As Mr. Ifor Williams has pointed out in *Y Beirniad*, 1916, p. 137, Dr. Evans has not seen that the name of Darius also occurs twice in the first nine lines (it is willful blindness, for Skene’s translator gives the name in each case). Thus:

->Ef torres ar dar teir gweith yg kat.<-

“He defeated Darius three times” rendered literally in Skene (i, p. 566): “He broke upon Darius three times in battle.” In Irish, *brissim cath* for “I break battle on” is a common idiom (Windisch, *Ir. Texte*, I, p. 404): and Mr. Williams has pointed out to me that it occurs several times in the Irish story of Alexander printed in the *Irische Texte*, II, ii, as *bris in cath sai for Dair*, p. 25, literally, “broke that battle on Darius.” But Dr. Evans changes *ar dar* into *ardal*, and renders the sentence “He burst our borders three times in war”! II, 131. Further on, he changes the name *dar* into *i dad* “his father”! But the whole poem is clearly about Alexander and Darius; in line 6 Alexander overtakes him in his flight from the third battle (Arbela); thus:

->gyrth y godiwawd alexander;<-
->yn hual eurin gwae a garcharer,<-
->ny phell garcharwyt; angeu dybu<-
->ac lle ef kafas ergyr o lu.<-

> “Swiftly Alexander overtook him; in golden fetters woe to him who is imprisoned, he was not imprisoned long; death came, and there he (*ac ef lle*) was wounded by [his own] army.”

tory" or "Ubbanford" or a "King," or about leaving anyone to his fears. In volume II, p. 193, the "*John*" is "*He*," with "*John*" as the antecedent rendering *Ynyr* in a previous line. *Ynyr*, then, is an "eponym" of John. These later poets, it seems, imitated "Taliesin" in the use of eponyms; were they also "border poets" afraid to name even English kings? There are several more of their eponyms in the notes. "The Teyrnon of 34·15—26 is, clearly, Richard I, who figures as Arthur in 54·15 to 56. Richard was a poet," etc., p. 94. Corroi m. Dayry, the Irish Cūrōi mac Dairi of the Cūchulinn legend, is John, p. 115; and "Mab Dairi = mab Harri, i.e., John," p. 116. I find from the Index that John also "= Caw 72·11; = Ercw(lff) 65·26, 66·2·6; = Erov 65·24." Cocholyn, that is Cūchulinn, is Llywelyn ap Iorwerth, p. 115. I do not propose to tax the reader's patience (or my own) with a detailed examination of these absurdities.

The reconstruction of Taliesin's biography is perhaps as remarkable as anything in the book. "I quit the question of authorship," we read on p. xxix, "to summon the poet to tell his own story." This theatrical utterance represents the editor's attempt to express vividly, and thus to communicate to his readers, the illusion under which he himself labors—the illusion, namely, that when he is quoting his own translation of his own sophisticated text, it is Taliesin that speaks.

->The first item connects him with a border settlement.<-
->I played at Llychwr—I slept at Pulford, 26·8.<-

Pulford is situated five miles south of Chester on the road to Wrexham. To the north of Pulford is the township of Lache. The *English Dialect Dictionary* defines Lache as "a pond, a pool, a swamp," etc. This too is the meaning of the Welsh Llychwr. Now every child's "sleeping place" is his home, and his "playground" is usually near, as Lache, or Llychwr, is to Pulford (p. xxx).

# Taliesin.

In the “Corrections,” p. xlvii, this is to be amended as follows—

> The rhyme suggests *Llychfforð* for *Llychwr*, and topography confirms it, for the English translation of the emendation is Lache Lane. Lache means “a pool, ditch, deep cart-rut,” etc. Now what playground can be imagined as more delightful to a boy than a lane full of ruts filled with water? The lane runs, etc.

There are boys and then there are boys, no doubt; speaking for myself, puddles awaken no joyful memories in me. But all this about Taliesin’s being born at Pulford and delighting in puddles is derived from a hopeless misunderstanding of the following short couplet:

| | |
|---|---|
| Gwaryeis yn llychwr, | I played in the daytime, |
| Kysceis ym porffor, 26·8. | I slept in purple. |

The name of Pulford is written *Porforð* in *Breuddwyd Rhonabwy*, *R. B. Mab.*, p. 144; and as the place is near Chester, it was perhaps inevitable that Dr. Evans should jump to the conclusion that *porffor* in the couplet was a mistake for *Porforð*. At first he was satisfied with *Llychwr*—the rhyme did not matter; but then it occurred to him that if this were changed into *Llychfforð* it would correspond better to Lache Lane, and then the rhyme *did* matter. And now observe “the ‘Art of Lubrication,’ or, so to speak, ‘greasing’ the descent from the Premises to the Conclusion.” <note>Edwin A. Abbot, *Philomythus*, 1891, p. 213.</note> Taliesin slept at Pulford—that is absolutely all the text says on that point, even if we admit that *porffor* is Pulford; it is quoted as if it meant that he slept there *regularly*; thus we are led smoothly to the first conclusion, that Pulford was the bard’s home (when young, for he played); this again is almost imperceptibly stretched so as to mean his original home, and “the Pulford origin of *Tal.*” is spoken of as proved, p. 102. Thus by adding a mere *-ð* as a suffix, and adding a *little*

<meta>This page continues the critique of J. Gwenogvryn Evans's interpretation of a specific Welsh couplet, disputing his claim that "sleeping in purple" identifies Taliesin as a native of Pulford.</meta>

# Taliesin.

meaning at each step, the editor believes that from words which mean "I slept in purple" he has legitimately drawn the conclusion that Taliesin was a native of Pulford. It is not cunning, but mere inability to handle words with accuracy; a plain example of this is seen in note 44·2, p. 102, which speaks of "Tal. being *formerly* a native of the district": the italics are his own, and show that he wrote "native" without the least thought of what it means. Now let us examine the couplet and its context. The rhyme is *-or*, which is continued in the next couplet, as is often done in these poems. The *-wr* of *llychwr* is a half-rhyme, which is called *proest* by Welsh prosodists, and frequently takes the place of exact rhyme in the oldest poetry. There is therefore every reason to suppose that both *llychwr* and *porffor* are correct readings. The meaning of *llychwr* may be gathered from its compound *cyf-lychwr*, which, like *cyf-nos* and *cyf-ddydd*, means "twilight"; *llychwr* then must mean either "night" or "day", "darkness" or "light", and as it may be derived from the fertile root which gives the Latin *lux*, we cannot be far wrong in inferring that it means "daylight".<note>The prefix *kom-* (cognate with Latin *com-*) is regularly *cyf-* before *n-* and *l-*, as in *cyf-nos* and *cyf-lychwr*; but *cyf-ddydd* must be due to false analogy (for *kom-d-* becomes *kon-d-*, and ultimately *cyn-n-*), and is therefore necessarily a late formation. It seems to be a form that sprang up to take the place of *cyf-lychwr*.</note> Again, *gwaryeis* does not necessarily refer to the period of youth at all, as is proved by the phrase *pan aeth pawb allan y chware* "when everybody went out to play", *R. B. Mab.*, p. 116, or rather "to disport", for it is not said of little children. The meaning of our couplet, then, is that the bard at the time spoken of led a royal life; he amused himself during the day, and at night slept in purple. It occurs in "Cad Godeu", one of the so-called transformation poems, which are the last in which most people would

I have been a word . . . a book . . . a bridge . . . a coracle . . . a drop in a shower . . . a sword (again) . . . a shield . . . a harpstring,” etc. There follows the fanciful account of the trees fighting in the battle of Godeu or the Forest; then, a statement that he was not born of father and mother, but created out of fruits and flowers (see above p. 73); he was called into existence by the spell of Math, by the spell of Gwydion. He is a fine poet. He has lived royally. That is the context. The next two couplets are:

Dylan Eil Môr is Dylan Eilton of the *Mabinogion*, connected with the sorcerers Gwydion and Math, who are purely mythological figures—degraded gods, in fact; all three are related to Dôn, the Welsh equivalent of the Irish *dea Danu* or *Donu*, ‘the goddess Danu’ or ‘Donu’, the mother of the Irish gods, the *tūatha dē Danann*. And out of this mystical-mythical material Dr. Evans derives the information that Taliesin was a native of Pulford! To proceed, we are next told that

| | |
|:---|:---|
| nyt mi wyf ny gan, | I am not one who does not sing, |
| keint yr yn bychan; | I have sung since I was little; |

The first point to note is that two prepositions are wrongly translated by our editor. The use of *er yn* for *er fy mod yn* (“though I was”) is modern journalistic Welsh, imitating the English word *though*; in idiomatic Welsh, *er yn* means “since I was.”<note>The modern *er yn ieuanc* (“though young”) is, like the modern *pan yn ieuanc* (“when young”), a servile translation of the English; but the literal equivalent of the idiomatic *er yn ieuanc*, namely “since young,” is not standard English.</note> In a poem attributed to Dafydd ap Gwilym in the appendix of the 1789 edition, page 498, the poet says (of Morfudd), “I have set my affection on her *er yn fab*, since I was a boy”; in the *Bardd Cwsc*, page 28, we read “*agos er yn blant* (*yn* misprinted as *ein*), almost since they were children”; and Hugh Maurice, in *Eos Ceiriog*, volume 1, page 313, uses the very words of our text:

->Goreu ’i fuchedd *er yn fychan*,<-
->“His life has been best *since he was little*,”<-

speaking of a man advanced in years. As we have seen more than once, Dr. Evans lacks the instinct for these things: his feeling for the spirit of the language is so undeveloped that he mistakes an old idiom for modern journalistic jargon. Next, *rhag* is translated by him as “against”; and the chief point of his application of the quotation rests on this mistranslation, which is not only given the emphasis of italics, but is further specially insisted upon in a footnote. And yet the very words *keint rac* (33.6, 34.5), “I sang before,” are translated correctly by him on pages xxv and xxxv. Why does *keint rac*, which meant “I sang before,”

Simply because "against" is what the theory requires here; and the editor is free to follow its requirements because he is not bound by any precise understanding of the words' usages. The mistranslation of these two prepositions, along with the omission of the first line and the second *keint*, falsifies the whole meaning of the passage, which, it is hardly necessary to add, has nothing to do with Henry I or the ruler of Powysland.¹

he went to a congress of the bards where he was tested:
> I was sifted in every faculty by the Brython bards, 7·13.

The use here of the adjective Brython suggests that he himself was not a Brython. If he was a native of Aeron, this would be true geographically, and might be true ethnologically (p. 31).

The argument rests on the second quotation. Turning to 7·13, I find that the words have been evolved out of the obscure opening lines of "Buarth Beirdd"; the text is—

->Ed ympeilli & ympwyllat.<-
->y veird brython prydest ofer.<-
->ymryorsseu ymryorsed.<-

The first and third lines are unintelligible. The second, which seems to be a comment on the first, means "To the bards of the Britons (it is) inane poetry"; apparently, therefore, the first is a quotation. It is certainly reminiscent of a line in one of the additional stanzas of the

The italics denote the editor’s insertions; in the text they are his own; in the translation they are mine, because he has not been candid enough to own them. These italics provide the only comment which it is necessary to pass on this version. But attention may be called to two points. First, the preposition *by* in the editor’s quotation, which gives it the meaning required for his purpose, is his own insertion. Secondly, the word *Brython* on which the argument is founded is, in all probability, a misreading. To show this I must quote the fourth line:

->digawn gofal y gofan gorð,<-

that is, ‘Sufficient care to the smith (is his) hammer’, meaning, no doubt, ‘Let the cobbler stick to his last’. The line is quite simple, and the editor understands it<note>Except that he inserts "young" before "smith" because he thinks that the -an of gofan is a diminutive suffix. He is clearly ignorant of the fact that gofan is the full form of the word, representing the stem, which is seen in the plural gofein, in the name Gofannon, in the Irish genitive gobann, in the Gaulish Gobann-icnos, etc.</note>; but he cannot keep his fingers off a plain line like this.

<meta>This page continues the critique of Dr. J. Gwenogvryn Evans’s interpretation of Taliesin, specifically focusing on his lack of understanding regarding Welsh metrical structures and his subsequent errors in reconstructing verse lines.</meta>

# Taliesin.

To satisfy some perverse whim of his own, he turns it inside out, like this (Volume II, page 6):

->I'r gofan gofal ddigon gordd.<-

He upsets the metrical balance of the verse because he has no idea that such a thing exists. The <term>cynghanedd</term> in the original is of the form technically called <term>braidd gyffwrdd</term>, which consists of two words with similar initial syllables being placed in the middle of the line, so that the <term>rhagwant</term>, or caesura, comes after the first and divides the line into two equal, or approximately equal, parts; for example (*Myvyrian Archaiology*, 143a):

->Gwalchmai ym gelwir | gelyn y Saeson.<-

The verse under consideration is an obvious example:

->digon gofal | y gofan gordd.<-

Dr. Evans's lopsided caricature of the line betrays his complete ignorance of its structure; he clearly had no inkling of it. Now, line two is an imperfect example, which strongly appears to have been mutilated by a scribe who, like Dr. Evans, had no ear for <term>cynghanedd</term>. And, when one considers it, <term>beirdd Brython</term> is a somewhat odd expression<note>1</note>; surely, the reading must be <term>beirdd Prydein</term> as in 43.21. By making the substitution, the line is suddenly transfigured:

->y veirdd Prydein | prydest ofer.<-

It should be noted that <term>prydest</term> is the correct form of this word; <term>pryddest</term>, which Dr. Evans writes, is a modern mis-

---

<note>1 The normal form would be <term>beirdd o Vrython</term> as in 64.2, just as we say <term>seiri o Saeson</term>, not <term>seiri Saeson</term>; but the <term>o</term> would make the line too long. <term>Plwyf Brython</term> 72.23, 73.18, and <term>pobl Brython</term>, 77.13 ("the nation of the Britons") are different, the genitive here being the genitive of apposition, as in <term>tref Lundein</term>, etc. <term>Brython</term> is not an "adjective" as stated by Dr. Evans.</note>

...reading of the medieval spelling.<note>1 It seems to be a formation like *glodd-est*, derived from the stem of *prydu*, ‘to compose poetry’. In the Williams MS., in which medial (and final) ẟ is regularly written *t*, this word appears as *prydest*, 40a, 42a. In *cynghanedd* we find *prydest* corresponding in consonantism to *pryder* in *Myv.* 179a 28, and *prydein*, 151b 7.</note> In all probability, then, the author of this line, whoever he may have been, never wrote the “adjective” *Brython*, on which Dr. Evans, by manipulating a preposition, bases his argument that Taliesin was not a Briton.

The editor continues—

> His parentage, like that of Myrẟin, is wrapped in mystery; it might be unknown even to himself. Hence the play of fancy regarding his magical origin.
> 
> "'It was not of father and mother that I was born.
> I was created, in a new fashion, from nine constituents:
> From the essence of fruits; from primrose flowers; . . . 25.21."

We are now back again in the transformation poem “*Cad Godeu*”; and this is the interpretation of its mysticism.

A passage like that suggests that Taliesin sought to escape, in imagination, from a cruel experience. His father might have been a bird of passage—say a Norman baron: his mother might have perished, or abandoned him before memory began; thus leaving him utterly forlorn in the world, without kin or the knowledge of kin (pp. xxxi-ii).

So it is not the mystery of man’s existence that the poet attempts to express, but the mystery of his own parentage. Taliesin was a bastard! His father *might have been* a flitting Norman baron; his mother *might have abandoned* him. As deductions from the evidence, it will be agreed that these suggestions are preposterous. I prefer to say nothing further on this matter.

Taliesin scolds the monks for their praise of poverty, according, of course, to the editor’s translation of a passage which he does not understand. As for Taliesin himself—

# Taliesin.

He was worse than poor. The fate of Saint Patrick overtook him; he was captured and set to herd swine:

> I was a slave of Kynbyn; I was a herd besides. 26.21.

Kynvyn was the father of Bledyn, the founder of the historical house of Powys. “Kynvyn” here must be the namesake of Meredydd ap Bledyn, who died in 1132.

Here again, the quotation is from “Cad Godeu.” It occurs in one of the transformation passages; I provide it here with the two preceding lines so that it may be seen in its context:

| | |
|---|---|
| bum neidyr vreith y mryn. | I have been a speckled snake on a hill; |
| bum gwiber yn llyn. | I have been a viper in a lake; |
| bum ser gan gynbyn. | I have been a billhook (cutting?); |
| bum bwystuer hyn. | I have been a pointed beast-spear. |

Dr. Evans, of course, quotes his own translation of his own text (Volume II, p. 38), in which *ser* (“billhook”) has been changed into an imaginary *serw* from the Latin *servus*,¹ and *bwystuer* (which equals *bwyst-ver*, “beast-spear”), a natural and likely compound to denote a hunting spear, has been changed into *bwyst-ner* (“beast-lord”), an absurdly improbable designation for a herdsman. He changes *gynbyn* into *Gynvyn*, even though *b* and *v* were not easily confused in writing before the fourteenth century. The form *cynbyn* occurs as *chinbin* in the Black Book 95.8; Arthur contends at Mynydd Eidyn against *cynbyn* (“dog-heads”), and “they fell by the hundred before Bedwyr”; it seems to denote a race.² It may, of course, be an error in our text;

<note>2 Apparently a vague recollection of the mythical <term>Cynocephali</term>, Augustine, *Civ. Dei*, xvi, 8. The Irish equivalent is <term>conchend</term>. <page-num>III</page-num>

# Taliesin.

In that case, the most probable emendation is *ser gynhyn* ("cutting billhook"), since *b* and *h* were easily confused. This form occurs as a plural noun, *cynhynneu*, in the Red Book, column 1151, line 35, and the singular form of this word later became *cinnin* ("a snippet").<note>*Cynhynn* may derive from *kon-tend-*, in which *tend-* is the Indo-European root meaning "to cut," seen in its o-grade in the Latin *tondeo*. The root occurs in Irish as *ro-s-teind*; see Walde under *tondeo*. For *cy-* becoming *ci-*, compare the dialectal *cimint*. The final *-nn* (from *-nd-*) is required by the rhyme; *cymyn* is ruled out because it ends in *-n*, as in *cymynu*, "to cut."</note> Lastly, Dr. Evans changes *hyn* to *ar hyn* ("upon this"), which he translates as "besides." The lines consist of five and six syllables, and five seems to be the standard length. The fourth line has five syllables, with *buum* being a disyllable, of which *bum* is a medieval contraction. Since *r + rh* sounded like *r + h*, *bwystver hyn* is very likely a mistake for *bwystver ryn* ("pointed spear");<note>Probably *rhynn* in *gwaewaur rrinn* is related to the Irish *rind* ("point" of a spear, etc.) and the verb *rindaim* ("I stick" or "I thrust"); perhaps also the Welsh *rhynnaf* ("I thrust" or "I push")? The expression appears in our text as *Gwaywaur ryn* (44.10); the initial *r-* stands for *rh-*.</note> compare *gwaewaur rrinn* in the Black Book 46.3.

In any case, the main words are all clear, and there can be no possible doubt about the general meaning of the passage: the bard has been a snake and a viper, a billhook and a hunting spear. We do not need to investigate the meaning of this mysticism at the moment, but it is clearly consistent; yet the editor assumes the second couplet is autobiographical. He replaces the word for "billhook" with a hypothetical word for "slave," and he replaces the word for a "hunting spear" with an unbelievable compound, "beast-lord," which he translates as "herd." He quotes his distorted version of the text without indicating any of the changes he made; in fact, the reference he provides is 26.21, as if the version represented the original text rather than the distorted text from Volume II, page 38. And this is what he calls "summoning the poet to tell his own story."

<summary>The author argues that Dr. Evans distorted the original Welsh text of Taliesin by replacing words like "billhook" and "spear" with "slave" and "herd" to create a false biographical narrative. Philological evidence is provided to show the original mystical meaning of the passage as a series of transformations.</summary>

<page-num>112</page-num>

# Taliesin.

Taliesin’s bondage—demonstrated by citing a text as the bard’s own words in which improbable “emendations” replace clear readings and distort the plain meaning of the passage—is naturally treated by the editor as an established fact. Taliesin supposedly learned Welsh “in servitude, just as the youthful Patrick learned Irish”!

> Like him too, he ran away, 27.6.
> I wandered the earth before I came into contact with literature (p. xxxii).

“I wandered the earth” is taken as definite proof; it supposedly cannot mean anything other than “I ran away from bondage”!

Out of Powys he steps into Llwyvenydd, and sings:
> Its wild places are mine; its cultivated parts are mine . . . 65.13.

The *gwyleδ* and *llareδ* of the original do not mean “wild places” and “cultivated parts”; but truly, one cannot discuss all of these mistranslations. Taliesin sings—composing a finished poem in a language he just picked up as a “(swine)herd”; it is all so delightfully probable.

> In Llwyvenydd, he was not only beyond the power of Powys but once more under his native lord, Ranulf, the Earl of Chester, for whom he has a good word to say.
> Ranulf did not harass his enemies
> until Urien arrived one day in Aeron. 61.8.

The manuscript does not mention *Ranulf* and says the exact opposite about Ulph: “when Ulph came to oppress—*yny doeth vlph yn treis*.” The “good word” belongs to the editor himself, who changed *yny* (“where” or “when”) into *ny* (“not”). Then Owein Gwynedd appears on the scene:

> Taliesin felt, as everyone does, toward an invading stranger and, it is clear, opposed the power of Gwynedd in song or action (compare note 66). But he soon changed his attitude and wrote a poem to win over the new lord of Llwyvenydd. . . . He was received into favor, and no prince ever had a more loyal bard than Taliesin proved to be to the lord of the West (p. xxxiii).

Thus, Taliesin was a renegade Englishman—a Mr. Houston Stewart Chamberlain, whose Kaiser was Owein Gwynedd!

<page-num>113</page-num>

# Taliesin.

I will refrain from comment because it is clear that the accusation is as foolish as it is hateful.

“Such, in outline,” writes the editor, “is the life of the bard.” However, some additional details are added, which would be a waste of space to examine point by point. The treatment of the poem describing how Taliesin released Elphin from Maelgwn’s prison (p. xxxv) reminds one of the “testimony” in the Chirk Codex. Maelgwn is stated to be the son of Owein Gwynedd and is identified with Elphin, the latter name being considered an eponym. “To contend with Maelgwn—*amrysson â Maelgwn*” is translated as “to take the part of Maelgwn”; the name Elphin is suppressed to hide the absurdity of the identification, and the order of two passages is reversed to suggest that the first follows the second. But one other point must be noted because of the light it sheds on the editor’s mindset. Taliesin has a falling out with the bards. He wins the chair, and the bards are envious.

> I won the chair, and am the bard of the Hall.
> The bards are highly incensed—loud their anathemas.

The original of this couplet is:

->wyf barð neuað, wyf kyw kadeir:<-
->digonaf y veirð llafar llesteir, 8·17,<-

which means, “I am the bard of the hall, I am the occupant (*civis*) of the chair: I make the bards fall silent,” literally “I cause the bards a hindrance of speech.”<note>The incident in the Tale of Taliesin (Lady Guest’s *Mabinogion* iii, 337), in which Maelgwn’s bards are unable to say anything but “blerwm” in the presence of Taliesin, seems to be derived from this verse.</note> Dr. Evans, not understanding the meaning, sees in *llesteir* (“hindrance”) an imaginary *llysceir* (II, 10), which he interprets as “anathemas,” and reflections crowd upon him:

As it was in the days of Taliesin so it is still: if a man excel in any direction, were it only in industry and single-minded

# Taliesin.

...devotion to duty, there is no slander too foul for envy to whisper by those who wander upon every high hill, and play under every green tree (p. xxxiv).

It is unnecessary to linger over the confused phrasing ("for envy to whisper by those") of this outpouring, nor am I concerned with its application or the delicate way in which the slanderers are repaid in kind.<note>See Jeremiah 2:20 and 3:6.</note> I only wish to point out that it bears no relation to anything in the text but arises from the editor's own ideas, which he has read into a verse that he did not understand. He proceeds as follows:

> Taliesin revenged himself by studying the books of the bards,
> their round, and all that pertains to them.
> They bring forth what is in them:
> What is in them, that is what they are.
> What they are on tour, that is their true character. 20·22.

The original text is provided below with a translation:

| | |
|----------|----------|
| Ystyrywyt yn llyfreu | It is recorded in books |
| pet wynt pet ffreu, | How many winds, how many streams, |
| pet ffreu pet wynt, | How many streams, how many winds, |
| pet auon ar hynt, | How many rivers in their courses, |
| pet auon yð ynt. | How many rivers there are. |

What happened is this: the word *pet*, meaning "how many," is the British cognate (not a derivative) of the Latin *quot*. It is used to this day in Breton with a singular noun, just as it is in our text. Although it is a word familiar to every Celtic scholar, it was unknown to Dr. Evans; this is evident from his note on page 88: "22·1 *pet* . . . *pet* . . . *pet* ? clerical error for *pob*." In this instance, he mistook it for *beth*; and he turned *pet avon* ("how many rivers") into *beth a vont* ("what they are") in volume II, page 18. The rest follows, but not without the most violent distortion of the text, including the interpolation of an entire line composed by the editor himself and translated by him as "of the bards, their round, and all that pertains to them" (vol. II, p. 17). The evidence which he provides turns

on the words which he has inserted; he brings forth what is in himself, not what is in the text. And what is in him, that is what he is: Taliesin attacking the bards is Dr. Gwenogvryn Evans denouncing "the *Eisteddfodic* spirit" (*Bruts*, p. xvi), or unburdening his mind on the subject of "the Welsh Atomist," who "cannot interpret a dozen lines of early Welsh poetry, nor write a modern dozen with decency" (II, p. xiv). As the amended text is for the most part the product of the editor's imagination, it is natural that it should reflect his sentiments and publicly expose his pet dislikes. The Taliesin whom he sees in it and behind it is his own shadow—a distorted shadow, one is glad to admit, for Dr. Evans is not, like his Taliesin, a coward and a renegade.

Any detailed examination of the "amended" text and translation, and of the notes, is altogether out of the question here. Mr. Ifor Williams, in his review of Volume II, started to note the errors in the correction and rendering of the late and relatively easy poem "*Armes Prydein*," and after filling more than six large pages of *Y Beirniad* (1916, pp. 207-214) in small type, had to stop halfway through. I can only deal in a general way with Volume II and the notes, citing a few characteristic mistakes. These have been chosen almost at random; I have not looked for them specifically, but have merely marked them in pencil in my working copy when I have had occasion to refer to these portions of the work for the purposes of the paragraphs above. Nothing could persuade me to undergo the ordeal of systematically reading through the volume of "amended" text and "translation."

In the first place, Dr. Evans is ignorant of the meaning of a large number of old words which are familiar to Welsh scholars, and most of which were deciphered long ago. Many of his "emendations," as we have seen, are

He confuses *Madws* ('due time' or 'it is time,' known to Richards and Davies) with *mad* ('good'), and translates it as "It is well" (Volume II, p. 9). <page-num>117</page-num>

Page 39. *Llad* ("liquor")—equivalent to the Cornish *lad*—was not understood by the early lexicographers but has long been known to modern scholars (for example, Loth, *Voc. Vieux-Bret.*, 1884, p. 171) and is listed by Walde as a derivative of the Latin *latex*. The editor translates it as "wafer" in Volume II, page 27, line 2, and as "good" at the bottom of the same page. On page 149, *llestreu llad* ("drinking vessels") is translated as "goodly vessels"; this is interpreted as "ships," and the text is "corrected" accordingly.

*Meinoeth* or *meinyoeth* ("midnight"), derived from the Latin *media nocte*, was not understood until recently, but the clue to its meaning is found in our text. The reference is given in my *Grammar*, page 93; however, Dr. Evans, instead of investigating it, merely says, "Prof. J. M. J. falls into a strange error here" (note 68.13). His own theory is that the word in this passage should be "*meingoeth*." The passage that determines the meaning occurs in the poem on the "Plagues of Egypt" and reads (45.6):

> Decvet¹ veinyoeth mwyhaf gwynyeith ar plwyf kynrein,

that is, "Tenth (plague), at midnight the greatest vengeance on the ruling people"—an obvious reference to "About midnight will I go out into the midst of Egypt: and all the firstborn in the land of Egypt shall die" (Exodus 11:4-5). Dr. Evans, unaware that *veinyoeth* forms a perfect <term>proest</term> <note>a type of Welsh half-rhyme based on vowel variation</note> with *gwynyeith*, and that <term>proest</term> is used instead of rhyme in these poems, concluded that a large gap existed here and proceeded to fill it like this (note 45.7):

->Degved meini coeth roed inni gan Egiptiein<-
->Yna mabweith, mwyhav gwynyeith ar blwyv Cynrein.<-

Thus, *meinyoeth* ("midnight") becomes "*meini coeth*"! *Meini* is as old as the Welsh Bible, but it is not used by early modern bards, let alone medieval ones. The medieval plural is *mein*. *Meini coeth* ("refined stones") is a good

# Taliesin.

<page-num>118</page-num>

An example of the hopeless bungling of these “emendations”: *coeth*, meaning ‘refined (by fire)’, from the Latin *coctus*, is used for metals, not for stones (*mein*). *Roed inni* is slipshod modern Welsh (*roed* instead of *a roed*) for what would have been *an roðed* in the twelfth century. *Egiptiein* is a form invented by the editor himself. *Mabweith* is the missing word, intended to rhyme with *gwynieith*, and to mean, somehow or other, ‘slaughter of sons’! Can anything more helplessly futile be imagined?

The “amended” text bears witness on every page to an ignorance of the history of the language: medieval forms, spurious modern forms, dialect, and even slang words crowd each other in the most grotesque confusion. The traditional form *briallu* (25.23) gives place to *briall* (Volume II, p. 36), a bogus nineteenth-century form. *Eiðot ti* (Volume II, p. 38) is not older than the late fifteenth century; the medieval form is *teu*.¹ *Y Weryð* (Volume II, p. 6) for “the Ocean” is modern newspaper Welsh. *Hŵë* (Volume II, p. 72) is the South Walian colloquial corruption of *hyweð*. *Godrev* (Volume II, p. 22) is a spurious modern form coined by the editor himself², and is similar to the common mock-literary *hŷv*, which he also uses (note 25.9); the words are *godre* and *hy*. The former is from the root **tregh-* (as in the Greek *τρέχω*), and the latter from the Celtic **segos*; the final lenition of *g* disappeared in Medieval Welsh. The medieval *arogléuaf* (79.16), still retained as *ogléuaf* in North Wales dialects, is replaced by the modern artificial *aróglav* in the “emendation” in note 79.14. *Broled* (Volume II, p. 92), substituted for *molet*, is formed by adding the medieval verbal ending *-et* to the

---

¹ “*Medyant, as in meu, teu, eiðaw*,” says the Red Book Grammar, column 1124. In the late fifteenth and sixteenth centuries, this series of possessive adjectives was reformed as *eiðof, eiðot, eiðo*; and Dr. Evans uses *eiðot* in an “emendation” of a twelfth-century text.
² The usual modern false spelling of *godre* is *godrau*.

stem of the modern slang word *brolio* (‘to brag’), which undoubtedly comes from the English *brawl*.¹ *Rhonc* is a dialectal borrowing of the English *rank* (‘utter’); the explanation of “*rhonc wled* = a ‘swag’ feast”, note 13.14, is that Pughe gives *rhonc* the meaning “a swag” to correspond with *rhonca*, and Dr. Evans mistook Pughe’s “swag” for thieves’ slang. *Gwarogaeth*, Volume II, p. 172, is a late corruption of *gwr-ogaeth* (‘homage’). *Gan nad pwy*, Volume II, p. 24, is a recent senseless corruption of the South Walian *gynnag pwy* for *bynnag pwy*, a curious late inversion of *pwy bynnag* (‘whoever’); see Silvan Evans’s Dictionary under *cynnag*. The correct *ceneu* of the original is replaced in this text by the late *cenaw* (Volume II, pp. 80, 116) fabricated from the plural *cenawon* (properly *canawon*) in ignorance of Welsh phonetic law, and in defiance of the spoken form, which everywhere represents the standard form *ceneu*.² *Athraw* is a similar fabrication from the plural *athrawon*; the medieval singular is invariably *athro*, see references to twenty examples in my *Grammar*, p. 108. But Dr. Evans sees the bogus *athraw* in *Athraw ydygen* 42.6, which he changes to *Athraw yn·Ygen*, and renders as “a preceptor in Dygen”, Volume II, p. 192, thereby raising difficulties for himself, which need not detain us. What then is the explanation of the *Athraw* of the original text? The answer is simple: in medieval manuscripts proclitics like *a* and *y* are generally joined to the words that follow them; and the phrase in question, divided into its component words, is *A thraw y Dygen* (‘and beyond Dygen’), without the alteration of a single letter. A Welsh scholar would read it that way; but lest there should remain the faintest shadow of a doubt, it may be stated that the same geographical expression is found in a fuller and absolutely explicit form in the poetry of the *Red Book*: *ac o'r tu draw y Dygen* (*Skene, Four Ancient Books of Wales*, volume 2, p. 277).

As with words, so it is with idioms. Old idioms are misunderstood—several examples of this have been dealt with above (see pp. 66, 88, 89); and new-fangled constructions are brought into the text. Thus, in Welsh, absolute clauses are introduced by *a*; such as *a mi yn vyw* (*White Book Mabinogion*, column 504); *A mi . . . yn d'aros* (Dafydd ap Gwilym, 1789, p. 512); or *Paham, a mi yn disgwyl* (*Isaiah* 5:4); but in the last century a fashion arose, under the influence of English, to write<note>1 I say “write” advisedly, because in the *spoken* language the old idiom with *a* is still exclusively used.</note> *tra* instead, producing a construction which is neither Welsh nor English: *tra mi yn aros*,<note>2 In the hymn-books of today, this construction is attributed to Williams of Pant-y-celyn, as *Tra seren yn y ne'*. But Williams wrote *Tr'o seren yn y ne'*; see the 1811 edition, p. 867, where *tr'o* is a colloquial contraction of *tra fo*. Similarly *tr'w'i*, in the same work, p. 129, etc., for *tra fwyf i*.</note> “while I waiting”! This muddle-headed neologism we, of course, find in our “amended” text: *Tra mi 'm Uðugre*, volume 2, p. 144. The preverbal relative *y*, which correctly follows adverbs, as in *pryd y* (“at the time when”), is often used by uninstructed writers after conjunctions; and one of the first warnings given to learners of Welsh composition is not to insert it, for example, after *os*; yet in our “amended” text we find *os y dygwyð*, volume 2, p. 116. The omission of the relative *a* before a verb with a softened initial is unknown in Medieval Welsh, except after *mi*, *ti*, etc.<note>3 And except before forms of the verb ‘to be’ in constructions in which *a* never existed, and the lenition is analogical.</note>; it probably does not occur in Dafydd ap Gwilym,<note>4 Contraction with a vowel occurs: *Delwi wneuthum*, 1789 edition, p. 14, should be *delwi a wneuthum*, etc. (<term>sandhi contraction</term>).</note> and is rare in the

later *cywyddau*; it is absent from the Welsh Bible and is avoided by all reputable Welsh prose writers before the current generation. It began to become common in verse written in the free meters in the eighteenth century. Yet this elision occurs repeatedly in our "amended" text. Indeed, the editor himself refers to the dropping of the relative pronoun, assuming as a matter of course that it occurs in the original, and giving as an example *Taliesin gan* (Volume II, p. xii), which he supposes to mean "Taliesin sings." But no Welsh scholar needs to be told that *Taliesin gan* (59.2) means "the song of Taliesin," just as *Reget ud* (67.19) means "the lord of Rheged." Again, the shortened forms *’m* and *’th* of the pronouns usually written *fy* and *dy* occur in Medieval Welsh only after the single-syllable words *a*, *na*, *no*, *y*, and *o*. The free use of *’m* and *’th* is an artificial innovation—artificial because it corresponds to nothing in the spoken language—used by later writers of verse. We find this misuse of *’m* in the text before us: *a draetho’m tavawd* (Volume II, p. 36), and *ystyriaw’m awen* (p. 42). What these modernisms and grammatical mistakes show is not merely that the "emendations" containing them are worthless, but that the editor is unable to distinguish not only between medieval and modern Welsh, but even between good and bad Welsh.

Errors in the use and formation of verbal inflections abound in his text and notes. The third-person singular present subjunctive *él* is taken out of the dependent clause in which it appropriately stands in 63.4 and is treated as if it were indicative (Volume II, p. 114). The aorists *cant* and *darogant* are translated as futures, "shall sing and prophesy" (Volume II, p. 13); and this false translation is quoted in the Introduction (p. xxvii). The verbal noun *diffryd* is misspelled *diffrid* (Volume II, p. 58) and used for the third-person singular present-future indicative, the proper form of which is *differ*. The old third-person singular present...

# Taliesin.

<page-num>122</page-num>

The subjunctive *gwnech* ("may do") is altered to *gwrech*, rendered as "he wrecks" in Volume II, page 4, and to *gwnelher* in Volume II, page 80. The first is corrected in note 37.18,<note>1. *A Welsh Grammar* (1913) had appeared in the meantime; Strachan, in his *Introduction* (1909), had not discovered the form; see page 69, Note 2. That Dr. Evans looked up the verb in the *Grammar* is proved by his note 26.18.</note> where it is stated that *gwrech* "assumes a possible form *gwrychu*, to heap together." We are not told how *gwrychu* can mean "to heap together," how that can mean "to wreck," or how *gwrech* can possibly be a third-person singular present of a "possible" verb *gwrychu*. The old third-person singular present subjunctive *duwch* is "amended" to *duccwy* in Volume II, page 46, which is a misspelling of *dyccwy* (compare *dycco* in the *White Book of the Mabinogion*, column 465); but in note 28.20, the form *duwch* is recognized and explained as a "metathesis of *duc-hw*"!<note>2. For the true explanation, see Strachan, *Introduction*, page 69, and my *Grammar*, pages 339 and 113, x.</note> *A Cheneu* (60.15) is "amended" to *A theirei* in Volume II, page 90; the ending *-ei* affects no vowel or diphthong—the correct form is *taerei*, as every passing student knows. The old third-person singular present indicative *erlynyt* is "amended" to *erlid* in Volume II, page 42, which in Medieval Welsh is the verbal noun only: it is not a stem of the verb—*erlidiaf* is a modern reformation. In the old perfect *cigleu* ("I have heard"), *ci-* is the reduplication, and *-gleu* represents the root *\*kleu-*; in our "amended" text, an unheard-of passive form *ciglwyd* appears in Volume II, page 22, as if *cigl-* were the stem. Similarly, it is suggested that *deryδ* is a derivative of *taro* ("to strike") in note 69.9;<note>3. “Some committed the elementary mistake of . . . and gave ‘taraf’ as ‘the present indicative of taro.’” Central Welsh Board, *General Report of Examiners*, 1917, page 67. *Teryδ* is the same schoolboy error as *taraf*.</note> the stem of *taro* in Medieval Welsh is *traw-*, and the intended form would be *trewyδ*. The stem of *difa* ("to destroy") is *difa* itself; probably it is formed from *di-* and *ma(g)* ("place").

Compare *di-le-af*, 'I delete'; the *a* is of course kept throughout, or is changed to *e* before *i*, thus *di-va-wys*, *di-va-awδ*, *di-fa-ed*, *di-ve-ïr*, all quoted by Silvan Evans, under that entry; but in his note 56.9, our editor suggests *difir*, "will be destroyed", as if the stem were *dif-*! Following this childish "howler" is the oracular statement that Sir John Rhys's translation of the poem "may be assigned" "to the realm of twilight and darkness".

To the ordinary reader, phonological arguments are likely to be somewhat mysterious; they cannot be more incomprehensible than Dr. Evans's phonological pronouncements are to the phonologist. For example, "It looks as if *Mabin-ogion* were a corrupt form of *Mabonogion*. If it were based on *Maban* we should have *Mebin-ogion*", page xxvii, footnote. How *i* can come from either *a* or *o*; how it would affect a preceding *a* in one case and would not in the other; or rather how the editor conceives that such things can be, must remain a mystery. Again, what is known in Welsh philology as "vowel-affection" (Zeuss's "infectio") is a change in a vowel caused by a sound in the syllable that follows it; the only known "affection caused by ō" is that which is caused by ō in a lost ending, as the affection of *a* in *lleidr* from the Latin *latrō*. But the "affection caused by ō" in Dr. Evans's index, page 162, is as follows: "*Moryd* is often spelled *Morud*, the *y* being affected by long ō", note 77.11. The *o* here is not long; it is not in a lost ending; and it precedes the vowel it is supposed to affect. The statement, as far as it reveals its author's understanding of the principles of the subject, is on a level with the schoolboy's "A circle is a round straight line with a hole in the middle". It is the plain fact that Dr. Evans, when he deals with phonetics, does not know the meaning of the terms he uses: *ff* is an "explosive", and so is *th*, note 63.25. To speak of the

<meta>This page continues the critique of Dr. J. Gwenogvryn Evans's work, specifically focusing on his lack of understanding regarding Welsh metrics and phonetic symbols.</meta>

<header>Taliesin.</header>

<page-num>124</page-num>

"explosive *ff*" is like speaking of the "planet *Sirius*" or the "metal sulphur." He uses the sign $>$ instead of $<$ throughout, and discovers—or is informed of—the difference between them only just in time to paste a small slip of paper in front of the "Corrections" page with the following instruction: "For $>$ read $<$ wherever it occurs."

The ideas of Welsh metrics brought to bear on the construction of the "amended" text are of the crudest sort. Mr. Williams has shown (*Y Beirniad*, 1916, p. 207) how 30 lines out of 58 in one poem were altered in order to reduce the whole to a uniform length of nine syllables, even though the actual lengths arrange themselves in obvious patterns. He has also pointed out (*ib.* pp. 203-5) that numerous rhyming words have been changed simply because the editor was ignorant of the rules of rhyme in the oldest poetry. The rhymes which he failed to recognize are common in Irish verse and follow definite rules: the rhyming syllables end in different consonants, but they must be consonants of the same class, so that the rhyme is not a mere assonance; thus *-el* rhymes with *-er* and *-eδ* (voiced spirant finals), but not with *-ec* (an explosive final). Dr. Evans had not even dreamed of these things. We have seen above (pp. 103, 117) that he was also ignorant of the fact that <term>proest</term> (which Icelandic poets call "half-rhyme") often takes the place of rhyme in these poems; in <term>proest</term>, the vowels of the responding syllables vary.¹ Finally, his remarks on <term>cynghanedd</term> are on a par with his phonetic notes. He has never appreciated or understood this distinctive feature of Welsh poetry; he has no ear for its effects, and therefore has never been able to grasp its principles; the "secret of the bards," as it is sometimes called, is a closed book to him; see, for example, p. 108 above.

***

¹ For a detailed account of <term>proest</term> in Welsh, see the *Transactions*, 1908-9, pp. 24-31.

Let us turn then, as Dr. Evans would say, to paleography, which is his subject and in which no one will deny his proficiency. The Book of Taliesin on the whole gives little occasion for the exercise of his special skill; the manuscript, as he says, “is beautifully written, and one of the easiest to read” (page i). Therefore Skene’s reproduction was a good working text; its few errors were mostly obvious, such as *u* for *n*, or *c* for *t*. Dr. Evans’s text is, of course, free from such errors; and it contains the reading of the fragment at the end of the last page, which Skene says “is nearly illegible, only a few words being distinct” (*F.A.B.*, ii, 217). The reading, even where marked uncertain, seems correct; and an examination of the photographic facsimile cannot but give the impression that the decipherment of the lines was a great feat. It was, of course, accomplished many years ago, though probably thirty years after Skene abandoned the attempt as hopeless.

Dr. Evans tells us that “the style of the writing is that of about 1275—it certainly appears to be earlier than a manuscript dated 1282. But if written by an elderly man, it might be 25 years later” (page xliii, footnote). And again, “Other manuscripts written apparently by the same hand are the ‘*Gwentian Code*’ at the British Museum, and *Geoffrey’s Brut* at Mostyn Hall” (page 81). But by a peculiar perversity Dr. Evans hardly deigns to discuss matters on which he is entitled to speak: the first statement is huddled into a footnote at the end of the Introduction; the second occurs in a note in small type at the end of the Text. The manuscript is one of the easiest to read. “Alas,” says Dr. Evans, “it is also one of the most difficult to understand, because it is among the least faithful of transcripts,” p. i. He makes this statement apparently without a thought of how it reflects on his theory. Taliesin, he thinks, died about 1176; our manuscript was written about 1275. Thus all the mutilations so eloquently described by the editor on page xxxvi—“hundreds of lines . . . . marred in transcription; syllables, words, clauses, sentences, lines, . . . dropped,” etc., etc.—must have taken place in the work of the “chief of bards” within a short century of his death, while the works of less famous poets who indisputably lived in the twelfth century, such as Gwalchmai and Cynddelw, are in an excellent state of preservation. This supposition only needs to be stated to show how incompatible it is with common sense. “But alas! what text has suffered like the Taliesin text at the hand of scribes,” asks Dr. Evans on page xxxvi. I answer at once, “the Aneirin text”; and for the same reason. But what I wish to point out here is the editor’s sublime unconsciousness of the fact that the question so innocently put by him knocks his theory on the head.

The detection of scribal errors depends upon meaning and construction; errors are only suspected when the text as it stands is unintelligible or in some other way unsatisfactory. Even in such a case, there need be no error; the unintelligibility of the text may be due to our imperfect knowledge of its language, or only to the ignorance of the particular reader. Some knowledge of palaeography is no doubt essential for the emendation of such a text as this—knowledge, for example, of the fact that *u* and *n* are liable to be confused; that *m* may be mistaken for *in*, *ni*, *iu*, or *ui*; that *c*, *t*, and *r* are frequently confounded—but the fundamental requisite is an accurate knowledge of the language, and this, as we have abundantly seen, Dr. Evans is very far from possessing. <meta>This page continues the critique of Dr. J. Gwenogvryn Evans's edition of the Book of Taliesin, moving from a discussion of his questionable textual emendations to the palaeographical evidence regarding the manuscript's origins.</meta>

# Taliesin.

metrical grounds, to be absurd. But a large number of his emendations do not even have this justification and are therefore not included in the list. One sometimes wonders why he thought all this juggling with letters was necessary when he can, at one stroke, "correct" *Owein ap Vryen* into *Owein rhy·wyssid*, or *wyr Bryneich* into *i Arδunwent*, and can even introduce into his text whole lines written in a blundering travesty of Medieval Welsh that represent nothing in the manuscript.

The most interesting, and perhaps the most important, palaeographical question connected with the Book of Taliesin is whether it contains any evidence of transcription, direct or indirect, from a copy in "Hiberno-Saxon" or Insular script. Dr. Gwenogvryn Evans's theory is based on the supposition that nothing in the book is older than the twelfth century, and Insular script gave way to Continental script around the end of the eleventh century (*Y Cymmrodor*, ix, 146). If, therefore, the manuscript shows evidence of a prototype in the older character, the foundation of his theory is destroyed. The possibility of such a thing does not seem to have occurred to him; in his own field of palaeography, as in other matters, he is blind to everything that argues against his theory. It becomes necessary, then, to examine the evidence in the text for transcription from Insular script.

The question is not exclusively palaeographical, for all the Welsh written in Insular script appears in Old Welsh orthography (see above, p. 6), which is found in the later Continental script only when the scribe is copying literally or mechanically from an older document. The old handwriting and the old spelling go together; both belong to the earlier period, and the evidence of one confirms the other. In Old Welsh orthography, a mutated consonant is represented by its radical; the

--- Page 141 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical analysis of Welsh orthography, specifically the representation of the sound 'v' in medieval manuscripts, as part of a larger critique of Dr. Gwenogvryn Evans's paleographical theories.</meta>

<page-num>131</page-num>
# Taliesin.

mutation had occurred, but the spelling survived from the time when the mutated sound could still be regarded as a soft or loose pronunciation of the radical. The sound *v* (now written *f*) was written as *m* or *b* depending on whether it originated from one or the other—this could be distinguished by the sound being nasal in the former case; thus the modern *nifer* was spelled *nimer*, and *tref* was spelled *treb*. In Medieval Welsh, the sound *v* was usually written as *u* or *v* in the middle of a word (these were two forms of *one* letter), and *f* at the end; thus the words above were written *niuer* and *tref*. But *f* appears not only at the end of a word, but at the end of any element within a word; thus *trefred* is not written as *treuret* but *trefret* in our text (51.25, 57.22, 58.26), and the prefix *cyf-* appears as *kyf-* more often than as *kyu-*. The letter *f* also appears in the middle of words sometimes in certain other combinations, such as after *go-* (as in *gofyn*) or before a silent *y* (as in *dwfyn*), etc. In the first half of the fourteenth century, this medial *f* sometimes had an added *u* to show that it meant *v*, and not the hard *f* (like the English *f*), as in *kyfuanneδ* or *(g)ofuynny* (Pen. 14, W.B. Mab., p. 286). 

Now Dr. Gwenogvryn Evans argues (p. xliii) that a "form like 'trevbret' is not a 'mistake' but evidence of our manuscript being written at the very beginning of a transition period. After 1300, it was not an uncommon practice to flank (*b* and) *f* with a *v* or *u* when they had the sound of *v*". Why does he put "(*b* and)" in brackets? Because the statement is not true of *b*, which was not sounded as *v* after 1300 and is not "flanked" by *v* to indicate such a sound. Thus *vb* is taken to indicate the beginning of a period in which *vb* is never used for *v*. Furthermore, would it be believed that the *vb*, on which the argument is founded, actually does not exist in our manuscript? The form in the manuscript is *tref-* | *bret* (p. 45.11-12), with the *tref-* coming at the end of line 11 and the *bret* at the beginning of line 12. It is,

<summary>This page explains the historical development of Welsh orthography, specifically how the sound 'v' was represented by different letters over time. <meta>This page continues the technical analysis of Welsh orthography, specifically refuting Dr. Gwenogvryn Evans's argument that the form "trevbret" was a transitional spelling rather than a scribe's error during transcription from an Old Welsh source.</meta>

<page-num>132</page-num>
# Taliesin.

Despite Dr. Evans’s denial, it is a “mistake.” The explanation is as follows: A scribe was particularly prone to making a mistake when passing from one line to another. This was known to Dr. Evans in 1887, for in his *R.B. Mab.* <note>The Red Book of Mabinogion</note> he marked the end of a line with a stroke | where he found (under Rhys’s guidance) “any peculiarity in the orthography” (p. xvii). I followed his example in this in my *Elucidarium* (1894; see p. xv). The Old Welsh spelling of the word now under consideration would be *trebret*. The scribe began to write his usual *trefret*, and wrote *tref*, which brought him to the end of his line; just before beginning the next line, he instinctively looked at his source text again and, seeing *bret*, transcribed it mechanically. It is more likely that the mistake is his own, occurring as it does at the division of his lines, than that he was transcribing line-for-line a copy in which the error had been previously made. Thus, transcription from Old Welsh is practically certain, as there can be no other source for the *b*; and its position makes it probable that the transcription was direct.

Again, on page 56.24–25, we find the following couplet:

->ynamwyn gwen ystrat ygwelit<-
->gofur hag agwyr llawr lludedic.<-

> ‘Defending Gwen Ystrad there were seen
> A low rampart . . . and dejected, tired men.’

What is the *hag* which I have left untranslated? A final *g* can only mean *ng*, and there is no *hang* in Welsh. I need not discuss at length Dr. Evans’s attempts at emendation. In his note (p. 109), he confesses that II, 158.16 is a "bad shot," and suggests “*gofur hag: go·vurthawr*, feeble resistance”: a *t* is supplied, and the *g* is assumed to be an error for *wr*! The true explanation is very simple and obvious. In Old Welsh, *a* (“and”) was almost always written *ha*; and *a gwŷr* (“and men”) would be spelled *haguir*. This is what the scribe had before him;

<summary>The author argues that certain "errors" in the Taliesin manuscript are actually evidence of a scribe mechanically transcribing an Old Welsh original, specifically at line breaks. He illustrates this by explaining the presence of the letter "b" in "trebret" and the word "hag" as remnants of Old Welsh orthography.</summary>

<keywords>Dr. Evans, R.B. Mab., John Rhys, Elucidarium, Old Welsh, Gwen Ystrad, philology, paleography, transcription error, orthography, medieval Welsh scribes</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical analysis of scribal errors in the Book of Taliesin, providing further evidence that the manuscript was transcribed from an earlier source written in Old Welsh orthography.</meta>

<page-num>133</page-num>

# Taliesin.

He started copying it mechanically, but when he had written *hag*, he realized what it was and began again, this time writing it correctly in the spelling which he used throughout—*agwyr*. He doubtless intended to delete the *hag* later by placing dots under the letters, but he forgot all about it, as he often did, much to the bewilderment of Dr. Evans. That the *hag* is unnecessary is also shown by the length of the line, which should be eight syllables: in the other line, *ystrat* was a monosyllable, *strat*, in Old Welsh. Here, then, we have clear evidence of transcription from a copy in Old Welsh spelling; but as the scribe might have been copying the mistake of a previous copyist, there is no proof that the transcription here was direct.

The preposition *wrth* appears as *gurt* or *gurth* in Old Welsh, but in the scribe’s spelling it is, of course, *wrth*. In 61.6, he began to write the Old Welsh *g-* of his copy, but stopped before drawing the necessary tag at the top right-hand corner and put a dot of deletion under the unfinished letter. Dr. Evans, in his <term>diplomatic text</term>, represents the result in type like this: g̣62th; but in his notes, he ignores this revealing slip of the pen. Did he fail to see its plain significance? It shows the scribe in the act of checking himself in an error of <term>transliteration</term>, which proves that he was transcribing the poem from a copy in Old Welsh <term>orthography</term>.

Sir Edward Anwyl’s examples of Old Welsh spelling in the Book of Taliesin, referred to above on page 25, are the three following, all from one poem, "*Trawsganu Kynan Garwyn*": *trefbret* for *trefret*, which I have addressed; *pympunt* for *pymhwnt* (“fifty”),¹ 45.14; and *dymet* for *dyuet* (that is, *Dyved*), 45.20. There are at least four other examples in the same short poem, which takes up only twenty-one
