Although he has transcribed tens of thousands of instances where a final *-c* represents the modern *-g*, he has still not realized that *-c* is the only possible medieval spelling. In medieval texts, *-g* represents only the sound *-ng*. The full phrase here is *Myng kynnelw o Gynan*; see page 98, footnote, for the meaning.</note> in modern spelling *fy nghynnelw*—the Old Welsh form would be *mi cinnelu* (compare *mi coueidid*, *F.A.B.*, ii, p. 2), so the scribe seems to have discovered the meaning after writing the *M*; *Tegyrned*, 45·26, for *Teyrneδ* ("kings"); *dy gynan*, 46·4, for *y gynan*, modern *i Gynan* ("to Cynan").<note>Other minor signs appear, such as the use of *l* instead of *ll* twice in *A lafyn gwyarlet*, 45·18; etc.</note> Anwyl notes that his three examples "suggest that the poem was copied from a manuscript in which the spelling was consistently of an older type." It is clear that the evidence, when more fully presented, is not just suggestive but conclusive.

To both medieval and modern readers, the primary source of confusion in the old script was the similarity between the <term>minuscule</term> forms of *r* and *n*. The second stroke of the *r*, instead of being a small flourish attached to the top of the first, was extended down to the baseline, or very close to it. Initially, it had an outward curl at the bottom, but this soon became a sharp angle, as seen in the following example of northern writing from the Book of Lindisfarne (written around 700), taken from the reproduction in *Bibliographica*, iii, p. 271:

<image-desc>A woodcut facsimile reproduction of a line of Latin text in Insular minuscule script from the Book of Lindisfarne. The letters are rounded and heavy. The letter 'r' in the word 'denarium' features a vertical stroke that descends to the baseline, making it appear very similar to the letter 'n'. The final 'm' is represented by a horizontal abbreviation stroke (macron) above the 'u'.</image-desc>

->Ostendite mihi denarium<-

A survival of an earlier form is seen in *iudri*, alongside the more common forms in *iudnerth* and *clericis* in the following example. This is taken from the names written about the middle of the ninth century in the margin of page 218 of the Book of St. Chad, and reproduced from the full-sized photograph in Professor W. M. Lindsay's *Early Welsh Script*, plate ii:

<image-desc>A facsimile of hand-written Insular script reading: "iudri . f . iudnerth . De clericis".</image-desc>

->iudri. f. iudnerth. De clericis<-

There is a tendency to extend the first stroke below the line; this is more pronounced in the *r* of *pater* in the following example from the <term>englynion</term> in a tenth-century hand at the top of the first page of the Cambridge Juvencus manuscript, reproduced from a tracing of the words in the photograph which forms Professor Lindsay's plate vii:

<image-desc>A facsimile of hand-written Insular script reading: "dicones pater harimed presen".</image-desc>

->dicones pater harimed presen<-

In later handwriting, this is carried much further, and the first stroke of *r* is brought down as far as that of *p* or <term>minuscule</term> *s*, so that confusion of *r* and *n* is less likely when copying from a late than from an earlier type of Insular script. In some of the freer forms of the earlier writing, of which few samples have survived, *r* and *n* must often have been almost indistinguishable, as may be gathered from the following example of early ninth-century Welsh (or, as Professor Lindsay thinks, Cornish<note>1. The evidence is later and not conclusive; the **v**-shape of **u** shows that it belongs in any case to the Welsh type of script.</note>) script, given in his plate iv:

Now, in the Continental hands used in the twelfth century and later, *r* and *n* differ just as much as the *r* and *n* of ordinary modern print; see, for example, the frontispiece to this volume. Therefore, the confusion of *r* and *n*, especially if it occurs repeatedly, is a sure sign of transcription from a copy in Insular script. In the poem "*Trawsganu Cynan Garwyn*", in which we have found several traces of Old Welsh spelling, an example of this confusion occurs in the first line: *am arllofeis ket*, 45.10, for *am anllofes ket* 'bestowed a gift on me'; the correct form of the word is seen in *anllofet*, 58.26. Another example is probably *Nac*, 45.20, for *rac*. The error *-eis* (in *arllofeis*) for *-es* is also more likely to happen when copying from the old rather than the new script. The *d* written for *a* and then deleted in *kyfdarchet*, 45.22, suggests an original with an occasional tall-backed *a*, like *d*, such as our fourth specimen above. There is, therefore, paleographical as well as decisive orthographical evidence that

# Taliesin.

This poem was transcribed from a manuscript of the Old Welsh period.

To come back to the confusion of *r* and *n*, reference has been made above, on page 89, to *Ny rannet* (68.4) instead of *ry rannet*, and to Dr. Evans’s clumsy correction. We also find *Ny chyrchafi gogleδ* (65.10) for *ry chyrchaf*, and *ny golychaf* (64.2) for *ry golychaf*. The former *ny* was corrected to *neu* by Dr. Evans (II, 94), which is a palaeographically violent emendation <note>An unlikely correction based on the physical form of the letters</note>; he subsequently changed his mind and read it as *ni* (note 64.10) because his "Cheshire Gogleδ" <note>Gogledd, meaning North</note> seemed like a place to which his Taliesin would not wish to go. His list of scribal errors contains one good example of *n* for *r*: *mawnut* (26.1) for *mawrut*, which, however, he takes to mean *mawr uδ* <note>Great lord</note>. But *uδ* is hardly applicable to a sorcerer, and we are dealing here with *hut*, meaning "magic." Possibly also *Gnissynt* (61.3) for *gryssynt*. But although he notes these two errors of *n* for *r*, and imagines others, his theory affords no explanation for them. They are mere guesses, like his emendation of *magidawr* (52.20) to "*mygrδawn*" (page 106), which he suggests because he failed to see that *Alexander magidawn* was "Alexander of Macedon."

In 40.16 we come upon the scribe in the act of catching himself tripping; the phrase is *y gwin ar cwrwf ar meδ* ("the wine and the beer and the mead"). He wrote the second *ar* as *an*, but discovered his mistake before going on; he put a dot under the first stroke of the *n* to delete it, and added the tag to the second to make this into an *r*, as shown in the margin.

<image-desc>A small facsimile reproduction of a manuscript fragment showing the handwritten words "ar med". Under the letter 'r' in "ar", a small deletion dot is visible under the first stroke, illustrating how the scribe corrected the letter from an 'n' to an 'r' by adding a cursive tag.</image-desc>

The space between the *n* first written and the *m* is much wider than the usual space between words; that between the *r* and the *m* is normal; thus the mistake was discovered and corrected before the next word was written.

<page-num>138</page-num>

# Taliesin.

*arguaut*, because *w* was written as *u*, and the initial consonantal *w* of *wawd* would appear in its radical form as *gu*; and *ar*, not meaning "upon," would be *ar*, not *guar*; compare *ar i hit*, etc., in *Llyfr Llan Daf*, 159, etc. In the old script, the phrase would appear somewhat as shown in the accompanying block. <image-desc>The word 'arguaut' rendered in an archaic Insular script.</image-desc> In our scribe's orthography, it should be written *ar wawt*, with the *w* being represented either by *w* or the equivalent symbol 6. In 19.25, he writes *an gba6t*, copying the *r* as *n* and not mutating the *gu*; in 56.16, he writes *an wa6t*, mutating the *gu* this time, although his *an* shows that he did not understand the phrase; in 64.2, he has *an gna6t*, where the first *u* as well as the *r* is mistaken for *n*; the error immediately follows *ny golychaf* for *ry golychaf* mentioned above, the whole expression properly being *ry golychaf ar wawt* ("I praise in song"). But the most instructive case appears in 26.7, where the scribe writes *harð barð buð an gna6t ar wa6t*. Here, one of two things happened: either the scribe discovered his error and wrote the correction *ar wa6t*, intending to later delete the mistaken *an gna6t*, as he clearly intended to do with the *hag* noticed above on page 133; or some previous reader of the old manuscript, being at first puzzled by *arguaut*, <image-desc>The word 'arguaut' in Insular script with the correction 'ar wawt' written directly above it in a smaller, more modern hand.</image-desc> glossed it *ar wawt*, in the manner shown in the margin, and our scribe, as was his custom, incorporated the gloss into his text. The first alternative is perhaps less likely than the second; for if the scribe had discovered his error in 26.7, he would probably not have repeated it in 56.16 and 64.2. But in either case, the error proves that each of the poems in which it occurs is ultimately derived from a copy in Insular script.

There is thus ample evidence, though Dr. Evans has been blind to it all, that many of the poems in the Book of Taliesin have been copied from manuscripts of the Old

<meta>This page continues the technical argument regarding the dating of the Book of Taliesin, moving from specific scribal errors to broader paleographical and orthographical evidence, while critiquing Dr. Evans's theories on text glosses.</meta>

<page-num>139</page-num>
<header>Taliesin.</header>

Welsh period. The evidence is cumulative and consistent; it is partly orthographical and partly paleographical, and the pieces fit together in such a way that they lead to only one conclusion. This conclusion is further confirmed by the fact that a significant number of glosses have been integrated into the text. Dr. Evans recognizes this as one of the sources of the obvious corruption in the text as we find it, yet he imagines numerous glosses where none actually exist. However, he does not see how extremely unlikely it is that all the glossing and blending of glosses and text—which have undoubtedly occurred—could have happened in a work that was relatively modern, with its author having been dead for barely a century at the date of the manuscript.

When we examine Dr. Evans's list of glosses on page 144, we find that almost none of them are likely to be actual glosses. He explains that his abbreviation *g* means "a gloss on or for." By a "gloss for," we are meant to understand that the word described might have been a gloss on a word which is not in the manuscript, but might have existed in the scribe's source copy! Most of the "glosses" in the list are "glosses for"; for example, *ketwyr* (16.15) and *milwyr* (18.10) are listed as glosses for "*rheinyδ*," a word which does not exist in the manuscript—or anywhere else, as far as I know, outside of Pughe's dictionary. Why is it suggested that *milwyr* and *ketwyr* are "glosses for" this bogus *rheinyδ*? Because the rhyme is *-yδ*, and Dr. Evans did not realize that *-yr* formed a valid rhyme with *-yδ* during the period when the poem was composed (see above, page 124). *Mynych* (16.19) is called a gloss "for" *aml* because the editor thinks that the line is too long as it stands, which is not actually the case. Many of the others are suggested for a similar reason: *a oryw* (66.13) is called a gloss "for" *wnaeth* in a poem with lines of irregular length; apart from the fact that the *a* cannot

be omitted, is it possible to imagine that *wnaeth*, one of the most common words in the language, could have been glossed (that is, *explained*) by *oryw*? It may be true that *gawr* (53.10) should be *bloeδ*, because the latter rhymes (as does, or did, *gwoeδ*, now *gwaeδ*, which is a preferable emendation); but even if it is, it is highly unlikely that *gawr* was a gloss on the common word *bloeδ*, and that the gloss was written by the copyist *instead* of the word in the text. A much more probable explanation in such a case would be that the copyist attempted to improve the original by substituting a more effective word. “Gloss for” is an extremely improbable hypothesis in every case; thus, the bulk of Dr. Evans’s list is worthless. In one or two cases where the gloss is “on” a word in the text—such as *lliw* (43.19), a gloss on *ehoec*—the suggestion is not unreasonable. But he has missed all of the obvious cases. For example, there are three in the first part of “*Yspeil Taliessin*”, page 62. The second line of the poem ends with the words *a welhwyf yn kerth* (read *welhwyf*), meaning “what I see for certain”; this is followed by *wir* (“true”), which stands disconnectedly between this verse and the next. It is quite clearly a gloss on *certh* (from the Latin *certus*). It is mutated because the *yn* is implied before it—*yn wir*. Since *certh* had lost the primary meaning in which it is used in the poem, it is exactly the kind of word we would expect to be glossed. Dr. Evans does not understand it, and so he changes it to *gydnerth* (Volume II, page 112). He takes the gloss *wir* to be the modern exclamation *Gwir!* (“True!”), which he places at the beginning of the next line. The second example occurs in the line:

->Gwerth vy nat mawr vyδ y radeu.<-
->‘In recognition of my song, great are his gifts.’<-

In the text, *y vuδ* (“his bounty”) occurs immediately before *y radeu* (“his gifts”), and is clearly a gloss on it, because *rhad*

Dr. Evans is unfamiliar with the word *nat* (*nád*) "song," even though it still survives in *marw-nad* ("death-song"). He "emends" *Gwerth vy nat* to "*Gwyrth vy nuδ*" ("A marvel is my lord"), although *gwerth vy gwennwawt* ("by virtue of my minstrelsy") occurs in the most famous stanza of the *Gododin* (B.A., 6.22). ->pen maon am de¹ preid lydan.<-

After the obsolete word *maon* comes the explanatory *milwyr* ("warriors"), which is a very obvious gloss. It would be a waste of space to discuss Dr. Evans’s wild "emendations"; naturally, he did not recognize the gloss. It is, however, only fair to mention that he suspected *one* gloss on page 3 in a passage where there are many. In his note on 3.14, he states, "Dñi = Domini has no apparent place here." In his list of glosses on page 144, he identifies *dwvyn domini* (3.14) as a gloss. The passage is as follows:

->Ri rex gl’e am gogyfarch yn<-

->nullwys estis iste est.<-

Dr. Evans reads "inper .pgenie" in the third line. The page had served as the outer cover for the manuscript for a long time before it was bound. Considering its condition and the shapes of the doubtful letters here, it is strange that Dr. Evans did not adopt the reading of the Red Book (*F.A.B.*, ii, 304). if the first leaf had not been torn off.<note>The letters are not marked as doubtful in the reproduction, though they are obviously not certain; there is no note discussing the reading, and no reference to the reading of the Red Book. Amidst the grammatical and historical nonsense of the notes, we look in vain for the paleographical notes we rightfully expected.</note> Dr. Evans believes that *dwfyn dñi* is a gloss; *dñi* (*domini*) certainly is, but *dwfyn* can only be an error for *dofyd* ("of the Lord"), for which *domini* is a self-evident gloss. In fact, all the Latin words in the passage are glosses. The Welsh words arrange themselves into four very regular lines of eight syllables, rhyming in couplets, as follows:

->Ri am gogyfarch yn gelvyd<-
->A weleist<note>The attached pronouns *-i*, *-ti*, etc., are generally scribal insertions and do not count in the meter.</note> darogan dofyd<-
->Budyant uffern Ef dillyngwys<-
->Y thwr(y)f<note>The *y* in *twryf* is non-syllabic, or silent.</note> kaethnawt *Ef* kynnullwys.<-

> "The King asks me in a skillful manner,
> Have you seen the prophecy of the Lord?
> Hell's prey He has set free,
> Its captive host He has gathered together."<note>Compare: "Christus infernum despoliavit, et . . . raptos inde in paradiso collocavit."—*Elucidarium*, 1894, p. 187.</note>

That is clearly the original text. It would be difficult to imagine a more obvious case of interpolating glosses. Analysis of the scribe's mixture seems to show that in his copy, the division of the lines and the relative positions of the glosses were approximately as follows (omitting marks of contraction):

<meta>This page continues the philological analysis of the Book of Taliesin, specifically examining how Latin biblical glosses were mistakenly incorporated into the Welsh poetic text, causing confusion for later scribes and editors.</meta>

<page-num>143</page-num>
<header>Taliesin.</header>

As *ri* ('king') here denotes the Deity, it was glossed *rex gloriae* (king of glory); since the glossator (unlike our editor) knew the twenty-fourth psalm¹ by heart in Latin, he added *dominus fortis* (the strong Lord) below the line. Similarly, having explained <term>dofyd</term> as *domini*, he added *dominus virtutum* (Lord of hosts) below the line; *est is* and *iste est*, also an echo of the psalm, may be intended to indicate that the subject of <term>kynnullwys</term> is also He. The gloss *progenies* (offspring/lineage) explains the rare word <term>nawt</term>, which Mr. Ifor Williams has shown (without reference to this example) to mean 'race, nation', etc., in *Y Beirniad*, 1916, pp. 275-6. There was no room between the lines for a gloss on <term>twryf</term>, so it was inserted in abbreviated form in the margin; it was probably a reference to the biblical mention of this "turba magna" (great crowd) who had come out of the great tribulation (Rev. 7:9, 14), and the reading may have been *h(an)c(?)nemo din(umerare) pot(erat)*.² Whatever the exact form of the original may have been, it was clearly a mystery to our scribe, who copied it mechanically and not quite correctly.³ The restoration of the phrase is of less importance than the conclusion that it, like all the other Latin phrases in the passage, forms no part of the original text, and of this there can be no doubt. The suggested reconstruction of the scribe's copy seems to provide a

<meta>This page continues the philological examination of the Book of Taliesin, focusing on how Latin glosses were mistakenly integrated into the Welsh text and how orthographic evidence suggests the poems were copied directly from ninth-century manuscripts.</meta>

natural explanation for the curious positions in which we find the glosses in our text,<note>Nash, who failed even to see that *gle* was *gloriae*, calls the poem a "remarkable mixture" (*Tal.*, p. 71), which is much easier than analyzing it and making sense of it.</note> as the reader may see by comparing the two; but the fact that they are glosses does not depend upon the reconstruction, for it is proved by the meter, and by the origin and application of the phrases themselves. I have dealt with the lines somewhat fully because they supply the clearest proof of the scribe’s persistent habit of introducing glosses into the text. Dr. Evans has caught none of the allusions; his treatment of the passage (II, 70, 71) is a truly pitiable performance which, to use his own words, “reduces one to a melancholy, thoughtful silence.”

To summarize the conclusions to be drawn from the orthographic and paleographic evidence, we may state that it establishes the fact that many of the poems are derived from old copies, probably of the ninth century. Some of the errors are explicable only under the supposition that the scribe was transcribing directly from such an early copy; even where the evidence is not decisive on this point, as in the case of *hag agwyr* 56.25, another error in the same poem, *an wawt* 56.15, indicates direct copying of that poem. There do not appear to be any traces of twelfth-century spelling in these poems, and the probability is that the scribe copied them himself from manuscripts of the early period. He was well acquainted with the Welsh of his own day, and used a consistent contemporary orthography into which he transliterated what he copied. But much of what he copied was unintelligible to him, and he had no ear for meter or rhythm; consequently, he transcribed mechanically and incorrectly at times, and, imagining that glosses were meant

I have now dealt in some detail with the evidence and with the use Dr. Evans made of it to support his theory; however, some general questions regarding his work remain to be considered. He tells us (II, p. vi) that when he copied the Book of Taliesin, he "found the meaning of a multitude of passages as clear as daylight" (a foolish delusion!). "To account for the obscurity of the other parts, I conceived the theory that the 'sixth-century' work of Taliesin had been reworked in the twelfth"—*he*, Dr. Evans, supposedly conceived Matthew Arnold’s theory, which was explicitly set forth and defended in his *Celtic Literature*.<note>1. "Universal Welsh tradition connects the great group of British poets—Taliesin and his contemporaries—to the sixth century. In the twelfth century, another burst of poetry began for Wales . . . No doubt one cannot produce the original texts of the sixth-century poetry; no doubt we have this only as the twelfth and following centuries wrote it down; no doubt they mixed and changed it a great deal while recording it. What does "expanding" a theory mean if it does not include testing it at every point?—"and by degrees demolished my own superstructure to the last line." This is very unlike anything we see him do in the present work; whenever he has to tear something down, which happens quite often, he sets up something else in a frantic attempt to prop up the ramshackle structure. This is said (p. vii) to have taken place around the time when Professor Zimmer visited Tremvan, which was about 1900. Yet in 1906, Dr. Evans, in his *Black Book*, mentions Taliesin being associated with the court of Maelgwn (p. 161), and states that the sixteenth-century *Hanes Taliesin*, which tells of "Taliesin competing with the bards in the court of Maelgwn Gwynedd," "is confirmed by our twelfth-century manuscript" (p. xvi). In those years, he rightly prided himself on having no theory. In 1909, he wrote in a footnote to p. xxiii of his *White Book*:—

> Nothing is more dangerous to research than an attempt to bring discoveries into conformity with some system or theory. It leads to too much emphasis being placed on what supports it, and the undue neglect of what undermines such a theory or system.

It is regrettable that he did not stick to this opinion. But one must have some sort of working hypothesis, and I do not wish to suggest that he may not have accepted Matthew Arnold's idea for such a purpose. But the romanticized account of the burning of the pages is intended to give the impression that he had examined the traditional view "at every point" and completely destroyed it before coming up with his current theory. How thoroughly he had absorbed the known facts of sixth-century history can be gathered from the fact that in the *Black Book* (1906, p. ix), he mentions Maelgwn, who died in 547, as being present in 573 at the battle of Arderydd. But what concerns us now is that there is no sign in his current work of the "demolition" he describes. Urien, he says, “fought Ida’s successors and was pre-eminent among his peers as a military leader. So was Owain Gwynedd among Cymric princes: hence his *nom de guerre* of Urien,” p. xv. Again, Urien’s opponent Ulph is held by Dr. Evans to be Ranulf, Earl of Chester. If he could prove that the name did not exist in the sixth century, or that no person of that name could possibly be an opponent of the real Urien, it would be a case of demolition; but Frithwald, son of Ida, is also called Frithwulf (Oman, *op. cit.*, 242), and no such demolition is possible. The fact is, Dr. Evans’s theory is not the result of any detailed examination and demolition of Matthew Arnold’s theory, but represents his attempt to find a solution to the problem presented by the reluctance he knew was felt by scholars like Rhys and Anwyl to accept a sixth-century origin for any of the poems. He absorbed the skepticism that was in the air, and it became an intuition, an instinctive conviction within him. This is the foundation of his theory and the source of his confidence in it. <meta>This page continues the critique of Dr. Evans's scholarship, shifting from specific historical arguments to a broader examination of his methodology and the author's justification for providing such an extensive rebuttal.</meta>

Even if it is proved that I have made a mistake in every line, the **TIME OF COMPOSITION**, the **CHIEF ACTORS**, and the **GEOGRAPHY** will remain unaffected (II, p. xiii).

Can a structure stand if its foundations are destroyed? No; but it would be a mistake to suppose that Dr. Evans’s theory is *founded* on his readings of the text. These may be “proved” to be all wrong, but the theory “will remain unaffected.” They support it, no doubt; but it is not founded on them, or on any facts; the **ACTORS** and the **GEOGRAPHY** rest securely on the **TIME**, and this has its foundation in an inner conviction which nothing can shake. It gives him such strength that he feels he could wipe the floor with all his “precursors”:—

> Unregenerate man might delight in making our high priests bite the dust. It would be easy writing, and entertaining reading (p. xvi).

We have had an example of this “easy writing” in the passage about Cynan Garwyn and the “history of this sort.” It is not exactly “entertaining”; or, if it is, it is not at the expense of the “high priests.”

It may have occurred to the reader long before now to ask whether, if Dr. Evans’s work is as bad as I make it out to be, it was worth devoting all this space to a criticism of it. I answer in the affirmative for two reasons. The first is the reason I gave at the outset: “because in the process some constructive work can perhaps be done,” p. 38. It will be agreed that something positive has been achieved; I put it forward as a tentative suggestion; I claim no finality for it—in the present state of our knowledge of the subject, finality is far from being in sight. But criticism of false theories is necessary, and it is a method of discussion that has its advantages; it is an effective way of presenting more reasonable views, and it often helps the writer to form clearer ideas, because wrong-headed notions often suggest points of view which would

<meta>This page continues the critique of Dr. Evans's work, acknowledging his skill in producing accurate textual reproductions while arguing that his interpretations and scholarly theories are fundamentally flawed.</meta>

Dr. Evans knows this from experience: “I have never received an inspired answer to a ‘wise’ question; but the imprudent kind is likely to find a hot response”, p. vi^c. He fully accepts the position: “Better then a ‘howler’ that may herald the light than all the respectability of empty silence. I am content to become the whipping-boy of light and truth”, ibid.

The other reason is that for criticism of this book to be of real use, it had to be fairly full and systematic. It is often easy to pick out a large number of incidental errors and slips in a work which is sound on the whole; my task was to show not how many mistakes the book contains—this is impossible, for their number is legion—but that the whole work (except for the mechanical and diplomatic reproductions) is one huge mistake. Few would believe without conclusive proof that an editor of Dr. Gwenogvryn Evans’s reputation can be so utterly incompetent to deal with the questions which he sets himself to discuss in this book as he in fact proves himself to be. Dr. Evans is an honorary Doctor of Letters from two universities; but the distinction was conferred upon him for reproducing texts, not for interpreting them. He had done supremely well what had previously been done only imperfectly. He had for the first time supplied Welsh scholars with reliable texts to work upon. He had already published his reproductions of the Mabinogion and Bruts from the Red Book of Hergest, his facsimile of the Black Book of Carmarthen, and his superb edition of the Book of Llan Daf. In the latter he wisely entrusted the philological work to Sir John Rhys; but the laborious and valuable topographical work is his own; and his recovery of the original reading of the priceless Breint Teilaw, which a late medieval vandal has mutilated with knife and pen, is a service to Welsh learn-

He has tried to persuade scholars to cooperate with him in the preparation of his critical editions. Abuse is no answer to a definite proof of error.</note> They are jeered at, and accused of taking

Finally, I will only say that his friends were wiser than he was, and it is a pity that he did not follow their advice. It offends my sense of propriety to see *any purely temporary material bound up with perfect reproductions intended to last forever; but that all this trash should be printed in the best ink on the finest paper—* including 125 copies on Japanese vellum, and four copies of the concentrated nonsense of the smaller volume on actual vellum—to share the permanence of the text and facsimile, is sad indeed. Our investigation has, I believe, established the following conclusions:

The existence in the sixth century of a bard named Taliesin is a historical fact as well-authenticated as most facts of British history from that century.

The Book of Taliesin is a collection of poems claiming to be his work. The manuscript dates to the latter part of the thirteenth century; but many of the pieces show unmistakable signs of having been copied from ninth-century manuscripts.

Several of the poems are quite obviously of a later date than the sixth century; however, there are others which, without obvious anachronisms, deal with events of that century and with people traditionally associated with Taliesin. *The question is whether these can, in any sense, represent his work.*

<meta>This page continues the discussion of scholarship regarding the authenticity of the poems attributed to Taliesin and Aneirin, completing a sentence regarding Nash's views and contrasting them with the theories of Zimmer and other philologists.</meta>

<page-num>152</page-num>
<header>Taliesin.</header>

from materials originally dating to the sixth century" (see above, p. 21), he nevertheless believed that they were forgeries from the twelfth century. Zimmer, who was better acquainted with the evidence, believed them to be forgeries from the ninth or tenth century (see above, p. 45). He thought the Welsh only learned the names of Taliesin and Aneirin from Nennius; however, some clever individuals conceived the idea of composing songs like those these poets might have written, and succeeded—at least in some cases—in projecting themselves through imagination into the bards' period and perspective. The admitted forgeries and imitations from the twelfth century show no evidence of such a historical sense; are we to suppose that this sense was highly developed in the ninth century? Or is it more likely that the content of the poems in question was transmitted through the seventh and eighth centuries? Poetry is more easily remembered than prose—rhyme, alliteration, and meter aid the memory; and if we are to assume that the content was transmitted, we must assume that its original form was substantially that in which we find it recorded.

The question can be settled only by a careful study of the poems. As a result of the philological work completed in the last fifty years, we are in a much better position to understand the meaning of the poems than Robert Williams and Silvan Evans were in the 1860s, or Stephens in the 1850s—to say nothing of Nash, who was not a good scholar even for his time. The solution of many of the problems presented by the vocabulary justifies the expectation that many more will be solved in time; however, much work remains to be done, and we are perhaps more conscious of the limitations of our knowledge than the scholars of the mid-nineteenth century could have been. I propose, however, to provide a corrected text and translation of those historical poems from the *Book of Taliesin* that I have been

--- Page 163 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to the poems of Taliesin, specifically addressing the editorial methodology used for the current edition in contrast to that of Dr. Evans.</meta>

<page-num>153</page-num>
<header>Taliesin.</header>

...able to understand to some degree. I consider Dr. Evans’s method of altering the text—often without notice and always without indicating *what* has been altered—to be very misleading and unsatisfactory. It saves *him* trouble at the reader's expense, as the reader must constantly refer to the diplomatic text to verify the emendations. The manuscript reading should always be provided, whether in footnotes, marginal notes, or some other way. In the text of the following poems, everything that appears in the manuscript is printed in Roman Type. Letters or words that are not intended to be read are placed in round brackets ( ); my own additions are printed in *italics*. By omitting the italics and ignoring the brackets, the reader sees the manuscript text; by omitting everything in brackets and reading the rest, the reader has the amended text. 

However, I have not felt it necessary to follow the line divisions of the manuscript, in which the poems are written as prose, or to stick to its primitive punctuation. I have used capital letters at the beginning of lines and for proper names. I have not distinguished between the two forms of *w*, *r*, or *s*. I have used *v* for *u* where it has the *v* sound, and vice versa, since *v* and *u* were then *one* letter with two sounds. The letter *d* is printed as *ð* when it is sounded that way. When *g* means *ng*, it is printed as *ng*. Silent or extra *y* characters have been placed in brackets, such as *gwyst(y)l* or *(y)strat*, not because this is not a standard spelling in Medieval Welsh, but because it is not written in Old Welsh and does not count in the meter. When *y* forms a word on its own and has the clear sound that has now become *i* (meaning ‘to’, ‘his’, or ‘her’—now misspelled *ei* in the latter senses), it is dotted like this, <term>ẏ</term>, to distinguish it from the article and preverb *y*. The circumflex is used to mark long vowels where there is potential ambiguity. Mere conjectures are marked with a question mark (?).

<summary>The author critiques Dr. Evans's editorial methods and explains his own system for presenting the poems of Taliesin. He details his use of typography, brackets, and specific orthographic choices to balance manuscript accuracy with modern readability.</summary>
<keywords>Taliesin, Dr. Evans, diplomatic text, Welsh orthography, manuscript transcription, Medieval Welsh, philology, editorial method</keywords>

--- Page 164 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of Taliesin’s poems, specifically addressing the historical authenticity and scholarly interpretations of "The Battle of Argoed Llwyfein."</meta>

<page-num>154</page-num>
<header>Taliesin.</header>

First, I take "the Battle of Argoed Llwyfein", p. 60. This is, according to Nash, "the poem which, of the whole series, bears the most apparently genuine historical character"; his attempt to prove an anachronism in it has been addressed above, pp. 20–21. It rests on the assumption that *byδei kymwyawc* ('would be tortured') means 'said he would be tortured'. It is some excuse for Nash that Stephens had fallen into the error, *Lit. Kym.*, p. 267, following Lewis Morris; but Carnhuanawc, *Hanes*, p. 280, sees as clearly as Sharon Turner (see above, p. 21) that the words form part of Owein's speech. There is no "said" in the text, nor anything else to suggest that the poet regarded Ceneu ap Coel as being present. So far from compromising the authenticity of the poem, the words have exactly the opposite effect: Owein invoking the example of his ancestor is a detail which a late forger would hardly have thought of. As to Fflamδwyn 'the flame-bearer', Lewis Morris says that he "is supposed to be Ida, king of Northumberland", Stephens, *loc. cit.* Turner makes the same statement, *Vind.*, p. 247. Carnhuanawc, *Hanes*, p. 276, and Stephens, *loc. cit.*, accept the identification; but Skene identifies him with Deodric, son of Ida, on the grounds that the "Saxon Genealogies" expressly states that Urien "with his sons" fought against Deodric; he even suggests that the chronicler might have had this poem in mind, *F.A.B.*, i, 232. Stephens, after 1853, "had independently come to the same conclusion", *Y Gododin*, 1888, p. 67, footnote.

The following tag is added to the poem in the manuscript, as it is to six of the other poems to Uryen:

->Ac yny vallwyf(y) hen,<-
->Ym dyg(y)n angheu anghen,<-
->Ny byδif ymδ(y)irwen<-
->Na molwyf Uryen. 60.25.<-

<summary>This page evaluates the historical validity of the poem "The Battle of Argoed Llwyfein," refuting claims of anachronism and discussing whether the figure Fflamδwyn represents King Ida or his son Deodric. It also identifies a recurring poetic refrain dedicated to Urien found in the manuscript.</summary>
<keywords>Taliesin, Battle of Argoed Llwyfein, Nash, Stephens, Owein, Ceneu ap Coel, Fflamδwyn, Ida, Deodric, Urien, Welsh poetry, historical authenticity</keywords>

--- Page 165 ---
<meta>This page continues the linguistic and textual analysis of the poetic tag often appended to poems addressed to Urien, comparing its occurrences across various manuscripts like the White Book and the Red Book.</meta>

<page-num>155</page-num>
<header>Taliesin.</header>

> ‘And until I perish in old age
> In my death’s sore need,
> I shall not be happy
> If I praise not Uryen’.

The other occurrences are 57·11, 58·10, 59·4, 60·5, 62·14, 65·22. The only variations in the text are in the scribe’s own affixed pronoun after *vallwyf* (which he omits in 58, 59, and writes *i* in 60·5 and 65), and in the spelling and division of *ymðirwen*. A more defective copy of the lines is seen added to “Anrec Uryen” in the White Book and Red Book; the reading is identical in the two manuscripts: I quote the lines from the White Book as printed in *Y Cymmrodor* vii, p. 126:

->Ny dalywyf yn hen<-
->Ym dygyn anghen<-
->ony molwyf .i. vrien: Amen.<-

The initial *v* of *vallwyf* represents the radical *b* in our scribe’s copy, and the White Book scribe’s *d* must be an error for *b* (the two letters being somewhat similar in some old handwriting styles), for we can hardly suppose that our scribe repeated the same error seven times. The word seems to come from an obsolete verb *ballu* ‘to perish’, of which a compound *aballu* (from *\*ad-ball-*) has survived, for example, *yn aballu rac newyn* R. B. Bruts 197 ‘perishing from hunger’; compare Irish *atbail* ‘perishes’, *atballat* ‘they perish’.

Although the White Book scribe has *yn hen*, it is possible that at a very early period the adjective might stand in apposition to the subject of the verb without *yn*, just as it may be the complement of a verb without it, as in *gan aethant golluðyon* 57·1, ‘since they had become weary’, see above p. 19, and below p. 161.

*ymðirwen*. The scribe first copied this as *yn dirwen* 57·12; elsewhere *ymðirwen*, with the *ym* clearly joined in 59·5 and 62·15, and apparently separated elsewhere; `-ðyr-` only appears on this page, 60·6·26. On the whole, ‘in my happiness’ is less likely than ‘happy’; and *ymðirwen* is probably an adjective, like *ymðifad*, but with the intensive *dir* instead of the negative *di*. This implies that the base *gwên* is adjectival; the root may be *\*u̯en-* ‘to like, to desire’, compare the Sanskrit adjective *vanú-s* ‘eager, loving’; this is not the root of *gwên* ‘smile’, which the Irish *gen* ‘laugh’ shows to be from *\*gᵘʰen-* or *\*gʰuen-*.

*Na* in the last line is a very questionable use of the word, and it makes the line short. We can therefore confidently supply the correct reading from the White Book: *ony*.

It is quite possible that this tag might have been appended to one or more of the songs to Urien; but it probably has no place at the end of “Gweith Argoet Llwyfein”.

### Notes on “Gweith Argoet Llwyfein”.

Many translations of this poem have appeared. Lewis Morris's version, written in 1763, was printed in *Myvyrian Archaiology*, i, 54 (²48). Turner gives a version in his *Vindication*, p. 248; he acknowledges his debt to Mr. Owen [Pughe] for assistance in his translations, p. 247, footnote. Carnhuanawc gives a Welsh paraphrase in his *Hanes* [History], p. 280. Later versions are by Stephens, *Literature of the Kymry*, p. 266, *Y Gododin*, p. 67; Nash, *Taliesin*, p. 100; Robert Williams, *Four Ancient Books*, i, 365.

*Meter.*—The meter is the one called “cyhydedd naw ban” (*Dosparth Edeyrn*, p. xxxi); the normal length of the line is nine syllables, but in old examples an extra syllable very often occurs; in this poem there are 13 lines of nine syllables and 8 lines of ten syllables, assuming the emendation in lines 8 and 10 suggested below. At line 20 there is a change in the meter, marking the end of Urien’s speech and introducing the concluding verses. The meter is 5, 5, 6, with the two fives rhyming and the rhyme carried on in the third syllable of the six (i.e., *vrein*). This unit of meter is the last part of “cloglyrnach” (see prosody books, e.g., *Dosparth Edeyrn*, ibid.), and is of the same length as, and in fact is a mere variant of, “toddaid byr”—the couplet of 10 and 6 syllables which forms the first component part of an ordinary englyn. It is certainly one of the very earliest forms of verse in Welsh.

The system of rhymes is obvious. Note that *-awc* (line 11) rhymes with *-awt*; and *-yl* (line 16), and *-yr* (lines 17, 22), rhyme with *-yd*. The last syllable of the “toddaid byr” rhymes with the ends of the verses that precede and follow it.

*Title.*—See note on line 20.

Line 1.—*E*, that is, the article *Y*, should certainly be omitted, especially as *Bore* has a dependent genitive. The article does not occur in the piece except in line 6, where it means ‘that’. The systematic omission of the article is a good sign of antiquity. It is

--- Page 168 ---
<meta>This page continues the scholarly linguistic analysis of the poem "The Battle of Argoed Llwyfain," providing detailed philological notes on specific lines, word mutations, and possible scribal errors in the manuscript.</meta>

<page-num>158</page-num>
<header>Taliesin.</header>

It is, of course, absolutely wrong to mutate the *B* of *Bore*, as Dr. Evans does (II, 88); in Medieval Welsh, no word is mutated at the beginning of a sentence except proclitics, such as *fy* and *dy* (radical forms *my*, *ty*).

Line 2.—Both verbs are in the present tense; the *-u* of *gynnu* is not an ending, but part of the stem. Regarding *cynnu* (sets), compare *Pan dyggynnu nos* 21.10 (when night falls), and *hid(y)l meu yt gynnu*, F.A.B., ii, 282 (copiously my flood of tears falls).

Line 5.—The scribe seems to have read the *u* of his copy as *w* and added the *y*.

Line 6.—If *arhos* (to stay) is from \**ari-soss-* (see my *Grammar*, 343), *eiryos* is a possible variant of it, since *is* or *es* before a vowel might become consonantal *i*, which would affect the *a* to *ei*.

Line 8.—I take *dodynt* to be *doδynt* (compare *rho-δynt*) or *dodynt* from *dodaf* (possibly for *doδaf*, "I give" as well as "I put", *Grammar* 332), rather than *doδynt* ("they have come", for *doethynt*), as I suppose this to be a relatively late analogical formation, the old perfect being *dy-vuant* (compare *dyvu* above). The phrase *yg gwystlon* would be *in guuistlon* in Old Welsh, which can also be transliterated as *yn wystlon*; but there is no absolute line of demarcation between the two constructions—we have *ymhell* and *yn bell*, etc. The literal meaning seems to be "would they give [men] as hostages?" It is, however, more likely that the original phrase was simply *a dodint guuistlon* ("would they give hostages?"), which creates a line of normal length.

Line 9.—*dwyrein* (rising) may be an <term>adjective</term> as well as a noun, e.g., *haul ddywrain* (rising sun), <note>D.G. likely refers to the poet Dafydd ap Gwilym</note> D.G. 16, 65; compare *cywrain* (skillful). The phrase seems to be a compound (like *haeδ-wawd*, "deserving of praise", D.G. 413, or *erlid-lanw*, "hunted by the flood", D.G. 195) meaning "rising for battle." I have provided a free translation.

Line 10.—It is difficult to believe that this line was not originally the exact counterpart of the challenge, namely:

> Ny doδynt (yng ng)wystlon, nyt ynt parawt.

It does not seem possible that *nyt ydynt* can be an ancient construction; but it is not easy to account for its appearance here. The *nyt* may quite well be a miscopy of the *ing* of *inguuistlon*, as *t* and *g* are liable to be confused in <term>Insular script</term>; and the rest of the phrase may have been indistinct in the copy.

Line 12.—I take *lew* to be a mutation of *glew*, not of *llew*.

Line 14.—*am gerenhyδ*; question: *a gerenhyδ* ("O kinsmen") or *mi cer . . .* ("my kinsmen").

Line 15.—There seems to be no foundation for the supposition that *eidoeδ* means "banners." It represents a British stem \**ati-*, which may well come from the root \**petē-*, like the Welsh *edeu* (thread).

--- Page 169 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological analysis of the poetry of Taliesin, focusing on linguistic etymologies, metrical structures, and the identification of scribal errors in the manuscript source.</meta>

<page-num>159</page-num>
<header>Taliesin.</header>

English *fathom*, German *Faden* ‘thread, string’, Gothic *fatha* ‘hedge’. The singular might be *eidyn*, and *Caer Eidyn* at the end of the Northern Wall (see above, page 81) may mean ‘the fort of the line, hedge, or wall’.

Line 20.—The phrase *gweith Ar-* seems to be a gloss or a scribal insertion taken from the title. I have therefore interpreted it as *A rac Coet Llwyfein* (‘and before Llwyfein Wood’), taking *rac* in its literal and original sense. The line might be one syllable too long, so we might read *A rac gweith coet Ll.* (‘and from the battle of Ll. wood’), or *A rac Argoet Llwyfein* (‘and before A. Ll.’), which could be regularized in its meter by omitting the *A* (‘and’); however, none of these possibilities is as likely as the natural and simple reading suggested, especially since *rac Coet* means the same as *yn Argoet*.

Line 22.—The line should consist of six syllables, and an extra syllable is improbable in a six-syllable line divided into two groups of three. The long word seems to be *ryvel*; however, the abstract ‘war’ or ‘warring’ is obviously less likely to have been used by the bard than the concrete ‘blood’. We may therefore confidently assume that the original word was either *gwaet* (Old Welsh *goit* or *quoit*) or *creu* (Old Welsh *crou*); I choose *gwaet* because of its alliteration with *gwŷr*. How either word could have been mistaken for *ryvel* is not entirely clear; but if *quoit* were misread as *gueit(h)*, the scribe might have substituted the unambiguous *ryvel* for it (*gweith* meaning ‘battle’ or ‘work’; *ryvel* meaning ‘war’).

Line 23.—<term>gwerin</term> means the ‘crew’ of a ship or the ‘rank and file’ of an army; here it clearly refers to the *men* (“the men were splendid”). Translators have interpreted it in the distinctively modern sense of ‘common people’.

The initial letter of *gan* as well as *cân* would be *c* in Old Welsh, so the *g* is in any case the scribe’s own spelling; the sense seems to require *cân*. This implies that *gwerin a grysswys* is a nominative absolute, followed by a new sentence beginning with the verb *cân* with its radical initial, which is exactly what happens in the <term>Gododdin</term> line: *Gwyr a gryssyassant, buant gytneit* (B.A. 9.3; compare line 10 and 2.21).

The use of *einewyð* for *eiliewyð* (‘minstrel’) is a mistake often made by the scribe. This shows that the letter *l* was short in his source copy and makes it fairly certain that his source for this poem was written in the same hand as the one from which he copied “Gweith Gwen Ystrad,” which was in Insular script.

Line 24.—*Arinaf y* is obviously a corrupt reading, but any emendation can only be a guess. The *y* is the scribe’s own affixed pronoun (compare above, p. 142, note 2, and p. 155, lines 6-7). The suggested emendation assumes the reading of *li* as *n*, as in the preceding line, and the common error of mistaking *m* for *ri*. The error of reading *us* as *m* (transliterated as *f*) is the same one we meet with in line 21 of the next

poem, where *uis* was read as *im*, the down-stroke of the *s* being taken for the last stroke of the *m*. Compare *lliaws blynedd* (many years), *Myv.* 275 b 43, and *lliaws awr* (many hours) in the same place 142 b. 3. I have not found an old example of the compound *amliaws*, but it is a very probable one, as shown by *amnifer*, and the frequency of *lliaws* in these poems. For *amnifer* see *Myv.* 151a, *amniwerwch* 167 b 59, *amnivereit* *B.T.* 1·4. *Am lliaws* as two words cannot be assumed, for this use of the preposition *am* does not seem to be old. But the meaning is made fairly certain by the rest of the line.

All the translators, from Lewis Morris to Dr. Evans, have gone hopelessly wrong over the last two lines because they failed to understand the last three words; they took *nat* ‘song’ to be the conjunction *nat* ‘that not’, and understood *kynnyδ* in its modern sense of ‘increase, prosperity’. Carnhuanawc alone is candid enough to admit that he does not understand this line. Dr. Evans renders it, “but I prophesy that, for a year, they will not prosper,” which is no better and no worse than the others. It is to Carnhuanawc’s credit that he saw that the poem could not end in such bathos. *Nât* is ‘song’, see above page 141; *wy* is a medieval form of *yw*, modern *i’w*, ‘to their’, see my *Grammar*, pages xxvii, 277; *kynnyδ* ‘victory’, compare *ar kinit* (that is, *ar gynnyδ*) *B.B.* 51·13, ‘victorious, in the ascendant’; *kynyδwys* *W. B. Mab.*, column 37, ‘he conquered, annexed’; *kynyδu*, *R. B. Bruts* 254·5, ‘to conquer’; *kynyδasant* in the same place 112·18, ‘conquered’. Hence *nât wy kynnyδ* means ‘a (or the) song to their victory’.

The poem that naturally comes next for consideration is “the Battle of Gwen Ystrat”, pages 56-7. Stephens takes Gwen Ystrad to be Winsterdale (near Windermere), which seems rather far west for the men of Catraeth to be fighting Angles, whom he assumes to be the enemy in the poem. Skene identifies it with the valley of the Gala Water, *F.A.B.*, volume ii, page 412, because he sees in Garanwynion the name of the scene of Arthur’s eighth battle (in Castello Guinnion), which he believes to have been fought in Wedale i, 55, and because Gala seems to be mentioned in Galystem. Stephens takes the latter to be a reference to a previous battle, *Y Gododin*, page 73; and this seems to be right, for the description of this battle ends naturally with the surrender of the enemy, line 22. It seems safer to look for Gwen Strat nearer Catraeth, and

<header>Taliesin.</header>

### THE BATTLE OF GWEN YSTRAT.

1 The men of Catraeth arise with the dawn
Around their prince, the victorious raider of cattle.
Urien is he, in the minstrel’s song;
The banquet of princes is subject to him;
5 Warlike, he is named the lord of Christendom.
The men of Prydyn advanced (?) in hosts
To Gwen Ystrat, the territory (?) of the fighter of battles.
Neither field nor woods afforded
Shelter to the aggression when it came.
10 Like waves loudly roaring over the land
I saw impetuous men in hosts;
And after the morning battle, mangled flesh.
I saw the crowd of three regions dead.
A rueful, sullen cry was heard;
15 Defending Gwen Ystrat were seen
A low rampart, and dejected, tired men.
At the gate of the ford I saw bloodstained men
Laying down their arms before the hoary weirs.
They made peace, for they had become weary,
20 With hand on cross on the shingle of Granwynion.
The leaders named their hostages—
The waves washed the tails of their horses.

I saw men haggard (?), ragged (?),
And the blood that stained <note>their</note> clothes,
25 And keen, intense fighting in the battle;
The battle-coverer—they took to flight when they understood:
Prince of Rheged, I marvel that he was challenged.

I saw a noble band (?) around Urien,
When he contended with his foes at Llech Velen.<sup>a</sup>
30 His spears<sup>b</sup> were supplied at need.
May the desire for battle come to Urien.
->And until I perish, etc.<-

-><sup>a</sup> Gloss: Galystem. <sup>b</sup> Gloss: the blade would be in the men’s shields.<-

---

### NOTES ON “GWEITH GWEN YSTRAT”.

This and the preceding poem are the only two of which translations appear in the *Myvyrian*. The version of this is by Evan Evans, i, p. 52 (248). *Carnhuanawc* paraphrases lines 1, 2, 11-14, *Hanes*, p. 280. Stephens, in *The Gododin*, p. 73, quotes a very loose metrical version from Edward Jones’s *Musical and Poetical Relicks*, 1794, p. 20. Nash’s

<summary>This page contains a modernized translation of the Welsh poem "The Battle of Gwen Ystrat," celebrating Urien of Rheged's victory, followed by scholarly notes on previous translations.</summary>
<keywords>Urien of Rheged, Gwen Ystrat, Catraeth, Taliesin, Welsh poetry, Myvyrian Archaiology, Evan Evans, battle of Granwynion</keywords>

--- Page 173 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological analysis of the poetry of Taliesin, specifically focusing on the scholarly reception and technical metrics of "Gweith Gwen Ystrat" (The Battle of Gwen Ystrat).</meta>

<page-num>163</page-num>
<header>Taliesin.</header>

The translation in *Taliesin*, p. 98, has been referred to above on p. 19. Robert Williams's version appears in *F.A.B.*, i, p. 343; and Dr. Evans's in volume II, p. 159. All these versions are very inaccurate. Evan Evans omits what he cannot understand, and Carnhuanawc attempts only a few lines. Dr. Evans does not have a single line right; his version bears almost no relation to the original.

**Metre.**—The metre is “cyhydedd fer” (*Dosp. Ed.*, pp. xxix-xxx); the length of the line is eight syllables, but, as mentioned before, an extra syllable may be added. In the amended text above, I have left nine lines of nine syllables within the first 22 lines; however, as seen in the following notes, this number should probably be considerably reduced. After line 22, the metre becomes “cyhydedd naw ban”; this confirms the suggestion already made that these lines do not belong to the poem. The change of metre which we saw in the first poem is quite different from this, because “toddaid” and “toddaid byr” have regularly been joined to other metres—it is indeed their function: “cyhydedd naw ban” + “toddaid” forms the metre “gwawdodyn”; “cyhydedd fer” + “toddaid byr” forms “byr a thoddaid,” and “clogyrnach,” which is a variant of it. But a change from one *length* of equal lines to another, such as from “cyhydedd fer” to “naw ban,” is improbable.

**Title.**—The poem has no title in the manuscript.

**Line 3.**—*ar wawt*, see above pp. 137-8; the error as well as the word *hag* in line 16 shows that the poem was copied from an Old Welsh manuscript. *einewyδ* is the error we have already encountered in the first poem in line 23; see the note on p. 159 and the next note.

**Line 4.**—*Kyfeδeily*. No doubt *eilieuid* (that is, *eiliewyδ*) looked rather like *eineuid* in the old manuscript, so a reader inserted the correct reading *eili* in the margin, probably with some mark against the letters (which looked like *ein*) in the line. But our scribe copied them as *ein* all the same, and inserted the marginal *eili* (in his own spelling, *eily*) into the text where it happened to fall; evidently *kyfeδ* was the last word in the line. On the scribe's habit of introducing marginal notes and other glosses, see above pp. 140-4.

*gofyn*. I am correcting the *n* to *δ* not because *n* and *δ* cannot rhyme, but because the *n* of *gofyn* is double (*gofynn*) and cannot correspond to the single consonant. *Gofyδ* (“is under”) is the opposite of *gorfyδ* (“is over,” meaning “conquers,” etc.). Due to the shortness of the long strokes in his copy, the scribe seems sometimes to have confused a straight-backed *d* with an *n*; compare line 21.

**Line 5.**—*—rwy* is the perfective particle *ry* (older *ro*) joined to the infixed accusative pronoun *y* (“him”); substituting the modern *fe* for *ro*, the construction is *fe'i henwir*. The scribe failed to understand it and took it for an incomplete word. On *bedyδ*, see the note on line 1 of the next poem, p. 173 below.

<sig>M 2</sig>

<header>Taliesin.</header>

Line 6.—*Prydein* is the usual error for *Prydyn*. Although I have marked the correction of *dwythein* to *gyrchyn* as conjectural, I think it is probable. A verb is required here, and *gyrchyn* fits exactly. It would be spelled *circhint* in Old Welsh; in Insular script, *ir* is often *very* similar to *ui* (as the second stroke of *u* was sometimes drawn below the line like the first stroke of *r*), and *c* and *t* might be confused, as in later hands; we only have to assume that our scribe read *circh* as *tuith*. He would naturally make it rhyme with *Prydein*.

Line 7.—*Ystrat* is a monosyllable, generally written as *strat* in Old Welsh. The word *ystadyl* seems to be *stadal*, which occurs in the *Book of Aneirin*, 37·9, where spelling and meter prove it to be a disyllable; *stadal vleiδiat* ‘defender of a border’? compare *bleiδyat ryt*, ibid. 25·10. In our text, we also have *ystadyl tir Penprys*, 65·4.

*kyvygyδ* ‘fighter’: *cyvygu* ‘to fight’, *gor-chyvygu* ‘to surpass in fight’, i.e., ‘to conquer’, now *gorchfygu*. Dr. Evans reads *kyny gyδ*; I have only two facsimiles to consult, but I think the manuscript reading is *kyu-*. In any case, *u* is the correct reading.—Since the above was typeset, Mr. Gwynn Jones has been kind enough to verify the reading for me in the original manuscript. He writes, “I believe the letter is *u*, though it is very easy to mistake it for *n*.”

Line 8.—Perhaps the first *na* should be omitted.

Line 9.—*tut faint*, as if obliterated early—but one searches in vain for the desired paleographical notes in Dr. Evans’s book. It begins the line and seems to be a line-division error, consisting of an incorrect repetition of the *tid* that formed the last syllable of *choedyδ* in the copy.

*δy* is the Old Welsh *di* half-transliterated; likewise in 46·4, see above, p. 134. The medieval form is *ÿ* ‘to’.

<margin>||</margin>
*dyvyδ*, present tense. The present after *pan* in a past sense survives in Medieval Welsh: *a phan δaw nit oeδ . . .* ‘and when he came there was not . . .’ W.B.M., c. 74.

Line 10.—Probably *eu* should be omitted.

Line 11.—*gwychyr* (though spelled *guichr* in the Oxford alphabet, if it is the same word), is a disyllable *guichir* as a gloss on *effrenus* in the Juvencus Codex. Similarly in Medieval Welsh: *Eryr gwychyr gweith Veigen* (7 syllables), *Myvyrian Archaiology* 205b.

Line 13.—*twr(w)f* is common in these poems for the modern *torf* ‘throng, host’, see above, p. 142, and below “Yng Ngorffowys”, l. 17. On the present line (56·23), Dr. Evans has the “wise” note, “Gweleis twrwf = I saw thunder! Read: torov = torv”, mistaking, as usual, the old *twrwf* for the modern *twrw*, ‘noise, thunder’.

Line 14.—*gohoyw*, compare 20·15; *govaran*, ‘wrathful’, *Book of Aneirin* 8·15; *gochlywit* should perhaps be *clywit*, reducing the line to 8 syllables.

<meta>This page continues the philological commentary on the poem, offering line-by-line analysis of specific words, potential scribe errors, and geographical references in early Welsh literature.</meta>

<page-num>165</page-num>
<header>Taliesin.</header>

Lines 15 and 16.—See pages 132-3 above. The meaning of *llawr* as an adjective is elusive in some passages; but in some cases, it seems to mean ‘cast down’; for example, “There was no contention in which a host was not *llawr* to him,” B.A. 11.8. Compare *portheis i lawrwed*, B.T. 19.15, alongside *porthi heint a hoed*, B.B. 51.5.

Line 18.—*gofedon*. The scribe seems to have read *r* as *u* and transcribed it as *f*.

Line 19.—See above, page 19.

Line 20.—Perhaps *gryt* represents the original word, possibly an oblique case *cric* (*cryg*) of *croc* ‘cross’, for which *croes* was a gloss.

*G(a)ranwynyon*. This seems to be the name of a ford or place on the river. There is a reference to this description of the enemy being brought to bay at Granwynion in the ninth-century poem “Armes Prydein”: *Blaen with vôn Granwynyon kyvyng oedyn*, B.T. 14.23, meaning ‘Front against back [i.e., closely packed] at Granwynion, they were hard-pressed’. *Granwyn* also occurs in 43.2.

Line 21.—*Kyfedwynt*. See the note on *gofyn* in line 4.

*kywym don*. I have no doubt this is an error for *kywystlon*, as *tl* is (like *cl*) very frequently mistaken for a straight-backed *d*, and vice versa. The word *kywystlon* would be written in Old Welsh as *ciguuistlon*, with the *s* being like the familiar continental *r* but with its stroke brought down a little below the line. The scribe copied *cigu* as *kyw*, as usual; the *uis*, which had the form *uıꞃ*, he read as *im* (the tag of the *s* being merged in the top stroke of the following *t*), and wrote them as *ym*; he read *tlon* as *don*. He evidently had no idea what the line meant. (For *cigu*- compare *cigueren* B.A. 38.1, *ciguereint* 38.21. It might have been *cou*-, compare *coueidid*, *F.A.B.* ii, 2. But the vowel of the syllable was *ui*, not *i*, so that there were two *u*’s in any case.)

Line 22.—*Gwanecawr*, the plural of *gwanec* ‘wave’; the plural *gwenyc* would give a line of the correct length.

*gollychynt*. The scribe seems to have hesitated between *golchynt* and *gwlychynt*, but *golchi* is the verb used for waves. In Old Welsh we find (rarely) a silent *i* medially between spirants, such as *centhiliat* for *centhliat*; and as *l* before *ch* may be voiceless, the original may have had *guollichint* for *golchynt*.

*rawn* ‘horsehair’, specifically “the hair of a horse’s tail,” according to Pughe.

*kaffon* ‘horses’ for **cafon* from *cabones*, the plural of *cabō* (which occurs as a Latin gloss, see Walde under *caballus*), of which *caballos* is a diminutive. The latter gives the Welsh *cafall*. The change from *f* to *ff* in *kaffon* and the modern *ceffyl* is obscure. *Tyllei garn gaffon* B.A. 26.21.

As with the concluding lines of the previous poem, none of the translators has come anywhere near understanding these two lines.

Evan Evans and Carnhuanawc omit them. The translations of the others are:

> “They are a feast for the worms rising out of the earth.
> ‘The pale birds of prey are wet with grasping the gore.’”—Nash.
> “The tribes revel over the rising wave.
> The billows protect the hair of their captures.”—R. Williams.
> “(Owein’s) chieftains know the triumphant note; as
> it is poured forth, they hide the tumult in their hearts.”
->—Dr. Evans.<-

The average number of words correctly translated is *one*. Nash has none, for even ‘are wet’ is not right for ‘made wet’. R. Williams has ‘billows’ and ‘hair’, and Dr. Evans has ‘chieftains’.

Line 23.—I have already referred twice to the break here. The meter changes from 8/9 to 9/10. The initial G of this line is much larger in the manuscript than ordinary Gothic initials in the middle of the line; and Dr. Evans represents the difference graphically in his diplomatic reproduction. I believe lines 23-27 and 28-31 are fragments of another poem or poems—snatches remembered by the minstrel who recited them to the Old Welsh writer. The large initial G and the Gothic G of line 28 suggest that these lines formed two paragraphs in the original manuscript, marked off from the preceding lines and, to a lesser degree, from each other.

*gospeithic* (?) *gospylat* (?). The suggested translations are mere guesses.

Line 24.—*vaglei* ‘stained’, from the Latin *maculo* ‘to cover with spots, to stain’.

Line 28.—*reodic* ‘noble’; *reodic rec* ‘noble gift’, *Myvyrian Archaiology*, 195a 39; plural *ryhodigyon*, *Book of Taliesin*, 33·21: derivative of *ryhawt*, 63·1. I have translated *ran reodic* as ‘noble band’, but this is very doubtful. I am inclined to think that *reodic* here is a misreading for *Deodric*, a word which would mean nothing to the ninth-century minstrel, and for which he would be likely to substitute a known word. ‘I saw the fate of Deodric at the hands of Uryen’ (?) For *am* in this sense, see the note on line 20 of “Uryen Yrechwydd” below.

*amwyth*. See my *Grammar*, p. 371, iv (2). Dr. Evans’s note “*amwyth ae*. Inadmissible construction” is one of his incomprehensible utterances on points of grammar. *Amwyn* is always followed by *a*; see *Grammar* 371, lines 4, 5, and his own *Introduction*, p. xx, near the bottom.

*Llech Wen*.—The rhyme is *-en* (single *n*) and *wen* is in full *wenn*; the rhyming of single with double consonants was the one thing that was not done. I take *Wen* to be an error for *Velen*. *Llech Velen* is ‘yellow stone’, and it seems clear that *Galystem* is a gloss on

it, representing a corruption of the later name—Old English *geolu stán* ‘yellow stone’. Was the battle at Galston, when Urien was fighting in Aeron (Ayr)?

Line 30.—*wytheint*, ‘blades’? The line *En llogborth y gueleise vitheint* B.B. 72 begins an *englyn* which is followed by others in a series where the beginning is repeated with slight variations: in the other *englynion*, *wytheint* is replaced by *ciminad* ‘felling’ or ‘cutting down’, *gottoev* ‘spear’, or *arwev* ‘arms’. In B.T. 20·18·19, *gwytheint* seems to be echoed by *llafnawr* ‘blades’, line 24. In 36·13, the *a* after it seems to be an error. The glossator seems to have understood it as ‘blades’; the gloss explains why they had to be supplied. The line ends at *llaf* | and the omission of the second *yn* seems to be a line-division error.

Line 31.—The curious error *Urọwyn* (with the *o* under-dotted) is repeated in a slightly different form in the *Uruwyn* of 62·14. The Old Welsh spelling of the name is *Urbgen*; see *Y Cymmrodor*, ix, page 173. The *o* in the scribe’s first mistake is clearly a misreading of *b* in a flat-topped script. The *u* in the second mistake is his usual transliteration of medial *b* as *v*. In the old script, *e* was tall like the *l* of modern writing; before *n*, it was sometimes (very rarely) turned back below the line (instead of forward on the line), thus: <image-desc>a script character for ‘e’ resembling a tall loop curving back below the line, similar to a cursive long s</image-desc>; see two examples in Professor Lindsay’s Plate xiii, *accentis* line 3, *accidentis*, line 9. There is a peculiar broken form of it in *nomen* in the last line of the specimen on page 136 above. It seems that our scribe took this rare and peculiar *e* for the contraction for *ui* (compare Skene *F.A.B.*, ii, page 2, last line), which had the form <image-desc>a script contraction for ‘ui’ consisting of a letter ‘v’ with an extended upward stroke and a descending ‘i’ attached to the end</image-desc> made up of a *v* with the second stroke carried up and an *i* hanging from its end. He regularly transliterates *gui* as *wy*. Of course, where *Urbgen* was written in the ordinary way, he regularly transliterates it *Uryen*. (The spelling *urbgen* represents the pronunciation *urvyen*, where *y* equals English *y*; but the *v* had vanished in pronunciation before the twelfth century.) I am indebted to Professor Lindsay for the form of the contraction for *ui*. It is, as Skene calls it, an Irish contraction; but “in abbreviation, the Welsh and Irish systems are practically identical,” *Early Welsh Script*, page 40.

In the above translations, as well as in those that follow, I have aimed for literalness rather than effect, so that the reader may more easily and fully understand the original, in which alone it is possible to study the wording and appreciate the form. The definite article is not used in either of the poems except once as a demon-

--- Page 178 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological analysis of the Book of Taliesive, following a discussion on scribal errors and linguistic forms, specifically completing the word "contrastive".</meta>

<page-num>168</page-num>
<header>Taliesin.</header>

...strative; I do not wish to lay too much stress upon this, for it is a feature of the language of the oldest poetry that persisted and was imitated in the twelfth century. The vocabulary is purely British and Latin; it does not contain one word borrowed from Irish or English, not to mention French. No doubt later forms have in some cases been substituted for earlier ones, but it has not been done to the extent of seriously affecting the meter. The chief difficulty of the poems (apart from the lack of intelligence of copyists) lies in the number of old words which they contain—not in the style, which is perfectly simple and quite unlike the involved and labored diction cultivated by the twelfth-century bards. It is not only simple but reticent in a high degree. The effect is gained as much by what is left unsaid as by what is said. In *“Gweith Argoet Llwyfein”* we see the blustering Fflamddwyn; we hear his mocking challenge; Owein's defiance; Urien's speech to his men, unfolding the plan of attack; of the battle itself, nothing; we infer from the speech what took place and are told only the result. The pause is marked by a change of meter; we drop suddenly from the high-pitched flowing movement of the speech to the low staccato of the “toddaid byr”; then for the final note of triumph, the original rhythm is for a moment resumed. In *“Gweith Gwen Ystrat”* we have glimpses of the battle at different stages, so vivid that the mind instinctively fills in the details. The invaders come in hosts like waves rolling over the land; in the morning battle they are cut up; the remnant defend the valley; weary, they lay down their arms—the hoary weirs behind them; they make peace, hand on cross; they name their hostages; the waves wash the tails of their horses. That picture is drawn from nature; it is the work of a man who painted what he had seen. It is drawn with a clear eye and

unerring hand; it bears the impression of reality. The manuscript evidence traces it back to the ninth century. Should we stop there? Is this a depiction of a ninth-century battle created by an unknown artist who changed the proper names and attributed his work to Taliesin? It seems to require a stronger faith to believe that than to accept the tradition.

Reference has been made above, on page 85, to Cynddelw’s allusion to “Gweith Argoet Llwyfein” in his elegy for Owein Gwynedd. The passage is as follows:—

> Yngwrfoes yngorfaes kyf(y)rgein;
> Yngorvawr gwryawr goradein;
> Yngwryal yngwryd Ywein
> Yngorun aergun argyfrein¹
> Yn aergad yn A(e)rgoed Llwyvein;
> Ym penn dreic dremynt oet kelein.
->—Wms. MS. 36a, Myv. 152a.<-

> ‘In manliness in the field <note>he was</note> superb;
> In a mighty onrush <note>he was</note> swift;
> In attack <note>he fought</note> with the valor of Owein
> In the tumult of the war-lord’s doom
> In the battle in Argoed Llwyfein;
> In the dragon’s head the glance was death <note>literally: corpse</note>.’

That is the twelfth-century style, in which grand words are piled up with very few connecting words, and clarity is the last thing considered. If my interpretation of the fourth line is correct, Cynddelw believed that the ‘war-lord’ was killed in the battle; we have the testimony of “Marwnad Owein” <note>The Elegy of Owein</note>, provided below, that Owein killed Fflamdwyn, but neither of the Taliesin poems states that

---

¹ In the manuscript, an <insert>e</insert> is inserted above the line in a later hand after the letter "a" in this word. It is, however, probable that *argyfrein* is the correct reading. It is a fairly common word and is usually understood to mean ‘obsequies’ <note>funeral rites</note>; but in such a line as *Kyn noe argyvrein e waet e lawr* (B.A., 2·10), ‘before his death his blood was shed,’ it is used, as I interpret it above, in a more general sense as ‘death.’ The Old Welsh word *arcibrenou* (equivalent to *argyvreinou*) is a gloss on “sepulti” <note>buried</note>.

<page-num>170</page-num>
<header>Taliesin.</header>

he was killed at Argoed Llwyfein; it was, however, natural to assume that his defeat at Argoed in one poem and his death at the hands of Owein in the other referred to the same event, and there may have been a tradition to that effect. There can be no doubt that the “Ywein” of the passage above is Owein ap Uryen, or that “the battle in Argoed Llwyfein” is the battle commemorated in our poem. But when Cynddelw, in the earlier eulogy of Owein Gwynedd, speaks of a battle which he himself had seen, “above the Caer, above the wood of Llwyfein”:

> Gweleis aer uch caer uch coed Llwyfein, *Myv.* 150a,

and in which, he goes on to say, Owein Gwynedd and his men distinguished themselves, it is obvious that he cannot possibly be referring to our “battle of Argoed Llwyfein”. As stated above on page 71, there was undoubtedly a Llwyfein in Flintshire; and Dr. Evans may be right in supposing on page xxi that Cynddelw is speaking here of the battle in which Owein Gwynedd defeated Henry II near Coleshill in 1157 (Lloyd, 497). His mistake consists in the natural but incorrect supposition that the two Llwyfeins are one and the same; this error of merging two places into one became the starting point of the new geography and the eponym theory; it brought Taliesin into the twelfth century, and made Owein Gwynedd his hero. But Llwyfein, as we have seen, was a fairly common place-name; and as the word means ‘elms’ it must have denoted a ‘wood’ wherever it is found; there is therefore no conceivable reason why there should not be a *coed* <note>wood</note> Llwyfein in Flintshire in the twelfth century and a *coed* Llwyfein in the North in the sixth. But Cynddelw has another reference to our poem of Argoed Llwyfein, which shows when and to whom he regarded Taliesin as having sung it. A notable feature of the poem is that it does honor not only to Uryen and his son Owein but to their *gwerin* <note>host or people</note> or

<summary>The author argues that Cynddelw mistakenly conflated two different locations named Llwyfein—one in the North from the 6th century and one in Flintshire from the 12th century—leading to chronological errors in the study of Taliesin. This geographic confusion led some to incorrectly place the 6th-century poet Taliesin in the 12th century.</summary>
<keywords>Argoed Llwyfein, Cynddelw, Owein Gwynedd, Owein ap Uryen, Flintshire, Coleshill, place-names, Taliesin, historical geography</keywords>

<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>171</page-num>
<header>Taliesin.</header>

army. The use of this very word by Cynddelw shows that he has this poem in mind:

->Ny bu warthlef kert Kynverching werin<-
->O benn Talyessin bartrin beirtring.<note>1</note>—*Myv.* 169a.<-

'Not inglorious was the song of the army of the house of Cynfarch
From the mouth of Taliesin of bardic lore, exemplar of bards.'

This comes from the elegy for Rhiryd Vleidd. According to Cynddelw, then, Taliesin sang not to the men of Gwynedd, but to the men of Kynverching, or the house of Cynfarch—that is, to the army of the historical Urien and Owein, who were respectively the son and grandson of Cynfarch; see the pedigree (from Harl. 3859) in *Y Cymmrodor*, ix, 173. This twelfth-century reference to Taliesin is not mentioned by Dr. Evans, which proves how true his 1909 remark is (quoted above on p. 146) about "undue neglect of what saps" a theory; "undue neglect" is, however, much too mild a term to apply to his treatment of the evidence that tells against him.

I come now to the simpler songs in praise of Urien: "Uryen Yrechwydd", 57.14; "Eng ngorffowys", 58.13; and "Dadolwch Uryen", 65.5.

### URYEN OF YRECHWYDD.

1 Uryen of Yrechwydd, most generous man in Christendom,
Much do you give to the men of the world;
As you gather, so you scatter.
Happy the bards of Christendom while your life lasts;
5 It is greater happiness to the hero's eulogist;
It is greater glory that Uryen and his sons live,
Since he is the chief, the lord paramount,
Stronghold of the stranger, foremost fighter.
The Lloegrians know it when they converse:
10 They have suffered death and many vexations—
The burning of their homes and the taking of their attire,
And many (?) a loss and much tribulation,
Without finding deliverance from Uryen Rheged.
Rheged's defender, famous lord, anchor of <note>his</note> country,
15 My heart is <note>set</note> on you of all <note>men of</note> renown.
Intense is your spear-play when <note{the din of</note> battle is heard.
To battle when you go, you wreak vengeance,
Houses on fire before dawn in the van of the lord of Yrechwydd,
The fairest Yrechwydd and its most generous men:
20 The Angles are without security on account of the bravest prince.
Of the bravest stock, you are the best that is,
That has been and will be,—you have no peer.
When men gaze on him widespread is the awe.
