### NOTES ON "URYEN YRECHWYDD"

Nash's text was corrupted, and his translation of that (*<term>Tal.</term>* 106) is very inaccurate. R. Williams's version appears in *<term>F.A.B.</term>*, i, p. 344; it is also inaccurate. Dr. Evans, ii, 77, corrects some of the errors of his predecessors, but falls into other errors of his own: however, he comes closer to the meaning of the original here than in most of his efforts.

Meter.—Half-lines of five syllables, rhyming in pairs, and forming lines of ten syllables. That ten syllables is the unit is shown by "Dadolwch Uryen" below, in which, in some cases, the arrangement of the rhymes within the compass of the ten syllables is different. When the lines are divided into short periods, as here, we can hardly assume long or short lines.

Title.—The poem has no title in the manuscript.

Line 1.—*<term>Bedydd</term>* is not "baptism," but an abstract or collective noun denoting the baptized as opposed to the heathen. Another form is *<term>bedysawt</term>* from *<term>ba(p)tizāti</term>*, which became a feminine singular noun, as in *O'r croc creulet y deuth guared i'r vedissyaud, <term>B.B.</term>, 41*, "From the bloody cross came salvation to Christendom." In late literature it is spelled *<term>bydysawd</term>* and used for "universe" on the mistaken supposition that it is a derivative of *<term>byd</term>*.

Line 2.—*<term>roðyð</term>*; the *<term>-yð</term>* is the ending of the 2nd person singular present indicative, now *<term>-i</term>*; Modern Welsh *<term>rhoddi</term>* "you give." So in line 3.

Line 3.—The second half of this line is a syllable short. The missing syllable is perhaps *<term>mal</term>* before *<term>yt</term>* to correspond to *<term>mal y</term>* in the first part. In the later language the forms are *<term>val . . . velly</term>* "as . . . so," but *<term>velly</term>* is a reduction of *<term>val hyn</term>*, and the demonstrative *<term>hyn</term>* need not have been used originally.

Line 5.—*<term>clotvan</term>* "highly praised" from *<term>clod</term>* "praise" and *<term>ban</term>* "high"; it is often used as a noun, as in *<term>Myv.</term>, 188 b 18, 194 b 37*; hence we may translate it as "hero." *<term>clotrydd</term>* "freely praising," compare *<term>gwawdrydd</term>* the epithet of Aneirin; here used as a noun, "eulogist." The first noun depends on the other in the genitive, like *<term>Reget uð</term>*, etc.

Line 6.—The rhyme is taken up in line 9 and continued in line 10.

Line 7.—This and the next line are two syllables long; the redundant syllables are doubtless the repeated *<term>yn</term>* introducing a complement, which might be omitted in the early period; see the note on *<term>yn hen</term>* above, p. 155.

*<term>arbennic</term>* is a noun, as in line 26, not an adjective qualifying *<term>oruchel wledic</term>*.

<meta>This page continues the philological notes on the poem "Uryen Yrechwydd," providing detailed linguistic analysis of specific lines and terms found in the manuscript.</meta>

<page-num>174</page-num>
<header>Taliesin.</header>

Line 8.—*pellennic* is usually a noun, ‘stranger’, in Medieval Welsh (plural *pellennigyon*, R.B.M., p. 162): and *dinas pellennic* would hardly be used for *dinas pell* ‘far city’. Words like stronghold, fort, wall, etc., are frequently used figuratively for ‘defender’ in the old poetry.

*Keimat* is a ‘fighter’ (from *camp*), not a ‘companion’ from *cam* ‘step’. Dr. Evans renders both halves of this line correctly.

Line 9.—*Lloegrwys* ‘men of Lloegr’. In Geoffrey’s *Brut*, Lloegr is the central part of the Island (of Britain), R.B.B., p. 60. I am not aware that the name has been explained; but it seems to denote the territory of the Angles. In line 20 the people are called *Eingl* ‘Angles’, which, it need hardly be pointed out, is historically correct for the opponents of Urien. There are no *Saeson* <note>Saxons</note> for Dr. Evans to “demolish”.

Line 12.—*Ac eimwnc*. It seems as if the scribe of the copy had begun to write a word like *eirim* (*eirif*), and then gone on to write *minic* (*mynch*), which our scribe copied as *mwnc*. The word *mnych* occurs in line 10, but its repetition is not improbable.

Line 14.—*clot*, from the Aryan root \**klutóm*, ‘that which is heard, fame’, may also be the perfect passive participle \**klutós*, and so may be an adjective.

Line 15.—The first half-line is long: the relative form *yssyδ* is used as in Medieval and Modern Welsh; in Old Welsh the verb would be the simple form *ys* or *yw*.

The scribe puts a stop after *arnat* and uses a capital *O* in *O pop erclywat*, thus connecting it with the following lines, and therefore understanding it to mean something like ‘By all report’. Such a break in the middle of the line is not improbable; but the sense it gives here can hardly be the right one. In any case *erclywat* is a perfect passive participle here, and in the next line a passive verb (the same form with the verb ‘to be’ understood). It may be added that *o bob . . .* and *o bawb* are commonly used after verbs of choosing, appreciating, etc.

Line 17.—There can be no doubt that the ending of the verbs should be that of the 2nd person singular present indicative, to rhyme with the next line.

Line 20.—*am* ‘on account of’, ‘at the hands of’; this proves the meaning assumed in the note on line 28 of the last poem, p. 166.

Line 23.—The article *y* makes the line too long, and must be omitted.

Line 24.—*am* ‘around’, as in line 2 of “Gweith Argoet Llwyfein”; the use of the word in this sense, even with personal suffixes, is put beyond doubt by *llu nef ymdanaw*, 11·9, ‘the host of heaven around Him’.

*gocnaw*, perhaps ‘triumphant’. *Gwyr a aeth Odoδin, chwerthin ognaw*, B.A. 2·13, ‘Men went to Gododdin with exultant laughter’.

<summary>This page contains philological commentary on the poem "Uryen Yrechwydd," analyzing specific Welsh terms such as pellennic, Lloegrwys, and clot. The author examines scribal errors, grammatical structures, and historical context regarding the Angles and the territory of Lloegr.</summary>
<keywords>Taliesin, Welsh philology, Urien, Lloegrwys, Angles, etymology, Gododdin, Medieval Welsh grammar</keywords>

--- Page 185 ---
<meta>This page continues the linguistic analysis of Taliesin's poetry, specifically focusing on vocabulary in the works of Cynddelw and Prydydd y Moch, before presenting the poem "Yng ngorffowys."</meta>

<page-num>175</page-num>
<header>Taliesin.</header>

Probably: *chwerthin wanar*, ibid., line 18, does not help. Cynddelw has *wryd wognaw* ‘of triumphant (?) valor (manliness)’ *Myv.* 168b; and Prydydd y Moch has *teyrn kedyrn cad ognaw* which apparently means ‘prince of mighty ones victorious in battle’, ibid. 203b. The word may possibly be a form related to *gogoniant*, but with a different vowel gradation.

Line 25.—*llïaws* should probably be *mawr*, or some similar monosyllable; *mawr* is probable because it alliterates with *maranhedd*, compare lines 4, 5, 8.

*maranneð* ‘store, provision, supplies’, etc.; see for example R.B.M. 12·18.

Line 26.—The second half-line is long: *arbenhic* may be a substitution for a disyllable such as *rieu*, or *unben* (Dr. Evans), suggested by such a phrase as *arbenhic milwyr*, W.B.M., column 123.

### AT HOME.

1 In [his] home since he has given [me]
Honor and welcome, he has endowed me with mead;
He has endowed me with the mead of his glory,
And [has given] fine lands to me in abundance,
5 And great abundance of gifts and gold,
And gold and gifts unnumbered,
Innumerable, and has granted [my] desire.
It is to grant my desire in order to gratify me
That he kills, that he hangs, that he rears, that he feeds,
10 That he feeds, that he rears, that he kills again.
He gives refreshment to the men of the world.
The world indeed does homage to you
At your will. God has made you
Master in assault for fear of your onslaught.
15 Inspirer in battle, defender of country,
Country’s defender, battle inspirer,
Usual around you is a host’s tramping,
The tramping of a host and the drinking of beer.
19 Beer to drink and a fine homestead
20 And fine clothing have been bestowed on me.
The people of Llwyfenydd greet you all
In one chorus, great and small.
The song of Taliesin entertains you.
24 You are the best of all I have heard of
25 &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;as to your merits.
And I will praise your works.
->And until I perish, etc.<-

### NOTES ON “YNG NGORFFOWYS”.

Nash’s translation appears in his *Tal.* 113; R. Williams’s in *F.A.B.* i, p. 346; Dr. Evans’s in II, 107. All are for the most part hopelessly astray.

*Metre.*—The metre is “cyhydedd fer” (see above, p. 163). In this poem each line of eight syllables is divided into two rhyming half-lines of four. The rhyme is in some cases carried over two or three lines; but in ten consecutive lines, 5-14, the half-line rhyme is limited to the line. An extra half-line is added to line 24; it would be unsafe to assume that a half-line is missing, as the three half-lines form the unit of the metre called “rhupunt”, which might have been used just before the end, like the “toddaid byr” in “Gweith Argoet Llwyfein” above.

The poem is full of what is technically called “cymeriad” (‘taking

<summary>This page features a modernized translation of the poem "At Home" (Yng Ngorffowys) along with scholarly commentary on its poetic meter and critiques of previous translations. It also provides a diplomatic transcription of the original Welsh text arranged in columns to illustrate its structural and rhyming patterns.</summary>
<keywords>Taliesin, Yng Ngorffowys, Welsh poetry, cyhydedd fer, rhupunt, Llwyfenydd, poetic meter, translation analysis</keywords>

--- Page 187 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical analysis of Welsh prosody and specific line notes for the poem "Yng Ngorffowys" (At Home), following the metrical description from the previous page.</meta>

<page-num>177</page-num>
<header>Taliesin.</header>

up'), which is regarded by the bards as a desirable, if not necessary, ornament of verse, the absence of which is called "<term>tor cymeriad</term>". It consists of the repetition of words or sounds from one line in the next line, and it is obviously a convention designed to aid the memory. Where the meaning suggests what is to follow, the sequence is called "<term>cymeriad synhwyrol</term>", and no other "cymeriad" is necessary. The examples of "cymeriad" in this poem are as follows:—(1) "<term>Cymeriad geiriol</term>",—a word at the end of a line repeated at the beginning of the next; *ryfeδ* lines 4-5, *achyfriv* 6-7, *rac* 10-11, *byt* 11-12. This is an early form of "cymeriad" which survives in later verse only in the connection between the <term>englynion</term> in a "<term>cadwyn</term>" or "chain". A more elaborate form of it, which is very common in the Taliesin poems, consists of the repetition in reverse order of the words of a half-line, such as *meδ meueδwys* line 2, *meueδwys meδ* line 3; the other examples in this poem are 7-8, 9-10, 17-18, 18-19, and 15-16, in which the whole line is repeated in inverse order. We can quite confidently amend the corrupt second half of line 5 in conformity with this principle of "cymeriad".—(2) "<term>Cymeriad cynganeddol</term>",—consonants repeated: *anllofet*, line 20, *Llwyfenyδ*, line 21; this is only recognized at the beginning of the two lines by the late prosodists.—(3) "<term>Cymeriad llythrennol</term>",—the repetition of the initial consonant of the line; this is the commonest form in later verse, but only one example occurs in this poem, lines 16-17.—(4) Most of the other sequences are examples of "<term>cymeriad synhwyrol</term>"; and probably the carrying on of the line-rhyme into the next line was regarded as a "cymeriad".

Dr. Evans, who knows nothing whatever about these things, violates half the "cymeriadau" in his "amended" text.

**Title.**—The poem has no title in the manuscript.

**Line 1.**—*Eg gorffowys*. The *eg* is the preposition *yng*, a mutation of *yn*. The *g* is the usual symbol for *ng*. Dr. Evans thunders against the spelling *yng ng*- in almost every book he publishes; here his diatribe is in Volume II, page xii. Consonants are not to be doubled (even in separate words!) "except under the accent," by which he means after an accented vowel. We must write *yn Efyn* instead of *yn Nefyn* (as if one insisted on *in Ewport* for *in Newport* in English). He implies that the two consonants are never written in full in manuscripts, which is untrue, for *yn n-*, *ym m-*, and *yg g-* are frequent. Further, the preposition *yn* followed by the nasal mutation is accented, see my *Grammar*, pages 173-5; but Dr. Evans is unable to understand this, and will probably continue to rave against doubling "except under the accent." In the early seventeenth century it was a "merry jest" to pretend that *yn Nyfed* was *yn yfed* (Owen’s *Pembrokeshire*, i, 9, 310 note); Dr. Evans writes *yn Yved* in all seriousness, page xvii, see above page 55, and

# Taliesin.

is angry with others because they are not equally lacking a sense of humor.

*gorffowys*, literally "rest"; here probably in the sense of "residence, mansion, or manor"; compare *Powysfa Dewi* in *Llyfr Llan Daf* 158, 260. Dr. Evans takes this to be *gor Bowys*, "The border of Powys"; he imagines that in the compound *gor-or*, it is the prefix *gor-* that means "border"! Of course, *or* ("border") has no *g-*.

*chedwys*, 3rd person singular aorist of a derivative from *ced* ("gift").

Line 2.—*meueδwys*, 3rd person singular aorist of a derivative of *meueδ* ("property"), literally "mine," from *meu* ("my"): hence *meueδwys* means "made mine."

Line 3.—*y orvoleδ*. I have translated this literally, without attempting to decide whether it means "for which he is famed," "for his praise," or "in which he glories."

Line 4.—*ryfeδ* from **pro-med-*, "beyond thought or measure," where **med-* is the root meaning "judge, think, or measure" (*Grammar*, 378). It is used in a more primitive sense than in Medieval and Modern Welsh, in which it has come to mean "strange."

Line 5.—*ac eur ac awr*. The Old Welsh word for "gold" is *aur* (that is, *awr*), which is the regular derivative of the Latin *aurum*. The form *eur* was originally the adjective, derived from *aureus* (*Grammar* 106), and cannot make sense here. The "<term>cymeriad</term>" makes it practically certain that *ac eur* is an error for *o cet* (*o ged*), or *a chet*; the former makes better sense. I suggest that the original text was written this way: *hoc& hacaur*; that the scribe misread *hoc* as *hac*, and could make nothing of the ampersand (&), for which he substituted *eur*, which may have existed as a gloss on *aur*.

*achyfrivet*. For *ach-* as a variant of *angh-*, compare *achen* beside *anghen* ("need") (*Grammar* 151). *-et* is a perfect passive participle ending.

Line 6.—*achyfrivyant*. *-yant* is the present active participle ending; it adds only a shade of meaning to the adjective *achyfrif* = *anghyfrif* ("numberless"); compare *dilys* and *dilysiant*.

Line 8.—*Chwant oe roδi*, literally, "Desire, [it is] to give it"; *Chwant* is the nominative absolute; *oe* is *wy*, now *i'w* ("to . . it"); see the next note.

Line 9.—*Yt* is the oblique relative "that," which is used because the antecedent is an adverbial phrase (preposition + noun, *i'w roδδi*), exactly as *y* is still used; thus *i'w roδδi y llaδδ* means "[it is] to give it that he kills (his fat stock)."

*gryc*, obviously the 3rd person singular present indicative of *crogaf* ("I hang"): "he hangs (the carcasses)."

*vac*, 3rd person singular present indicative of *magaf* ("I rear, bring up"), etc.

*vyc*, 3rd person singular present indicative of an obsolete verb **mygaf* from **muk-* (not related to *myg*, *edmyg*, etc., from **smik-*); the primitive perfect passive participle or supine stem is represented by *mwyth*, *moeth* ("luxury"), *mwyth*

<meta>This page continues the philological notes on the poem, specifically examining terms from lines 10 to 17 and focusing on etymological derivations and manuscript variations in spelling.</meta>

<page-num>179</page-num>
<header>Taliesin.</header>

*werin* 'well-nurtured host', *Myv.* 206a; a secondary perfect passive participle is *myget*, as in *emys grawthvrys grawnvyged*, ibid. 208a, 'horses, fast-trotting, grain-fed'; *Gwytuiled gyfred grat(y)vyged grawn*, 169 a1, '(horses) as fleet as wild beasts, for fleetness fed on grain'. The verb means 'to foster, to pamper', and is almost synonymous with *magaf*, and is sometimes used with it, as here; for example, *Ny mag . . . Ny myc*, 204 b lines 1 and 2.

Line 10.—*yn rae*. The preposition *rhag* comes from an adjectival form \*prokos, cognate with the second element in the Latin *reciprocus*: *yn* with the adjectival *rhag* forms the adverb 'progressively'.

Line 11.—*racweδ* is 'provision' literally and etymologically; as seen above, *rac* is an extension of the original \*pro, and *weδ* is \*uida, from the root \*ueid- 'see'. It seems generally to mean 'preparation'; but many words with this meaning are used for the 'provision' of food.

$\dot{y}$ veirδ $\dot{y}$ byt. The definite article $y$ before $byt$ is suspect; there can be no doubt that $veirδ\ y$ is a (bardic) substitution for $δynyon$, as in "Uryen Yrechwydd" line 2 (see above, p. 171), where $δynyon\ elvyδ$ (synonymous with $δynyon\ byt$) is used in making almost the same statement. Although *dynion* is not found in Cornish and Breton, it may well be old in Welsh; if it is not, it may be a substitution in these phrases for a collective noun like *dynin* (24.17); compare *gwerin*, from *gwŷr*.

Line 13.—The proclitic *dy* was probably *d'* before a vowel from the earliest period. It may be, however, that the original reading here was *i'th ewyllis*; see the next note.

*ewyllis*. This word is generally spelled *ewyllys*; but in the W.B.M. we find *i'th ewyllus yδ ydym*, c. 90, 'we are at your will', and in the next line *ewyllwys*, but lower down *ewyllus* again. It seems as if the $u$ was a rounding of $i$ due to the influence of the $w$; in that case, *-ys* is a later modification of *-us*, and the original form is *-is*. The spelling *ewyllis* occurs elsewhere in our text, 13.17.

Line 14.—*ygnis . . . . dy bris*. The exact form of these words is also doubtful, though the meaning is clear. In Old Welsh, both *i* and *y* were written as *i*, and the distinction between them in our manuscript depends entirely upon its scribe. Probably *gnis* should be *grys* (despite *gnissynt* in 61.2.3 and *gnissint*, B.A. 37.15), from \*grd-t-, cognate with Irish *grēss* ('attack') from \*grendt- (Fick-Stokes, 118), allied to Sanskrit *gṛdhyati* ('strides out'), and Latin *gradior*, *gressus*. In 66.19, *dy bris dy brys* should probably be *dy breu dy brys*, found inverted as *dy brys dy breu*, line 24, 'bursts, breaks'; the form is therefore probably *brys*, cognate with Irish *brissim* ('I break'), and allied to English *burst*, etc. (Fick-Stokes, 184). The *b* is thus the Old Welsh radical which the scribe should have mutated: *dy vrys*.

Line 17.—*twr(w)f*. See the note on "Gweith Gwen Ystrat", line 13.

--- Page 190 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological examination of the poem attributed to Taliesin, focusing on linguistic analysis of specific words in lines 20 through 23, including the evolution of Welsh phonology and the survival of archaic terms.</meta>

<page-num>180</page-num>
<header>Taliesin.</header>

p. 164 above. The medieval *twr(w)f* or *twr(y)f* (silent *w* and *y*) interchanged with *twrw* in which the last *w* is nonsyllabic, being a modification of the consonant *f* ($=v$). Similarly *cwr(w)f*, *cwrw*. The final nonsyllabic *w* has now become a syllable *twr-w*, *cwr-w*.

Line 20.—*imi ry*: read *ry’m*. This emendation has actually been made by Dr. Evans, II, p. 108.

Line 21.—*van* is certainly not *vann* as taken by R. Williams and Dr. Evans, for it rhymes with *achlân*, and the rhyme is carried on in the next two lines. It clearly means ‘men’ or ‘people’; it might be from Latin *manus* in the sense of ‘a band or body of men’. But I see no reason to doubt that the Aryan **manu-* (Sanskrit *mánu-s* ‘man, mankind’) survived in Celtic, since it is found in Germanic. Troude records an old Breton *man* ‘homme’, which he compares with the German *Mann*; but it cannot be derived from the Germanic since in Germanic the stem is *mann-* (for *manų-*, see Kluge s.v. *Mann*); in Welsh, the Old English *mann* appears dialectally as *mŏn*, in *hwsmon*, etc. That *mân* ‘people’ is not used in Medieval or Modern Welsh is no argument against its occurring in this poem, if it is genuine, for obsolete words like *mân* and *caffon* are to be expected, and are fewer in these poems than they would be but for substitutions. The word seems to have survived as an adjective *mân* ‘plebeian’, as when D. ap Gwilym says that he is not a *gŵr mân* (Pughe, s.v. *mân*), where *mân* can hardly be the adjective ‘small’ used with plural nouns, as *cerrig mân*, etc.

*achlân*. This word, like some other adverbs such as *yrháwg*, preserved its old accentuation in Medieval and Early Modern Welsh, being used commonly by the bards down to the end of the sixteenth century; it means ‘wholly, altogether’. In Old Welsh, the accent generally fell on the last syllable, and the original rhythm and rhyme of such a poem as this may be seen by restoring the accentuation of the rhyming words in such lines as these; thus:—

->Llwyfenyð vân | a'th eirch achlân,<-
->Yn un trygân | mawr a bychân.<-
->Taliessin gân | ti ae diðân.<-

Incidentally, the restoration also shows why *vann* is absolutely impossible, as well as Dr. Evans’s *trigan(n)* for *trigant*, and generally why a double final cannot rhyme with a single.

Line 23.—*ae*. I leave this unchanged because the old form of the relative was *ae* (Old Welsh *ai*, *hai*); and I leave the initial of *diðan* unmutated because the accusative relative was followed by the radical, as in *E bede/u ae gulich y glav*, B.B. 63, ‘the graves which the rain wets’. In ordinary Medieval or Modern Welsh, the phrase under notice would be *ti a ddiðdan* ‘(it is) thou whom it entertains’.

Line 25.—Regarding this half-line, see the note on meter above. *Wrδlideu* is probably an error for *evrllideu* ("merits"), although a compound *gwrδ + llideu* is not impossible. The second element is also found in the compound *delideu* ("merits"), which Prydydd y Moch uses in the singular form *dlid, Myv. 204.* In Breton, "This word is only used in the plural *dellidou*, with the meaning of merits, a devotional term," according to Troude s.v.

## EULOGY OF URYEN.

> 1 Most valiant chief, I will not slight him;
> I will seek Uryen, and to him I will sing.
> When my warrant comes, I shall receive a welcome
> And the best place under the chieftain.
<margin>
I am not
ever much concerned with
the princes whom I [see]
</margin>
> 5 I do not care much what [bidding I receive]
> I will not go to them; I will not be with them:
> I will repair to the North at the beck of princes.
> Even if it were for much that I gave a pledge,
> I do not need to reckon it; Uryen will not refuse me.

10  The lands of Llwyfenydd, their wealth is mine,

20  I will no more renounce him than the most high God.
    Your princely sons, most generous men,
    Get their plunder in the lands of their enemies.
->And until I perish, etc.<-

### Notes on "Dadolwch Uryen".

*Translations:* Nash in his *Taliesin*, p. 107; R. Williams in *F.A.B.*, i, p. 352: Dr. Evans in II, 95.

*Meter.*—Lines of ten syllables divided into rhyming half-lines of five, the meter of “Uryen Yrechwydd”, see above p. 173. But in this poem three lines, 5, 6, 16, have internal rhyme instead of the half-line rhyme; see note on line 5.

*Title.*—*Golwch* means ‘praise’; and the prefix *dad-* may effectively be merely intensive, as in *dat-gan* ‘to declare’. In twelfth-century titles *dodolwch* generally means ‘reconciliation, propitiation’, and is applied to poems addressed to patrons who have been offended; but there is no suggestion of Uryen’s displeasure in this poem. It implies that the bard was about to return to him after an absence in Gwynedd.

*Line 1.*—*Lleuuyδ*, i.e. *Llywyδ*. The spelling in the scribe’s copy was probably *Louid*; he misread *Lou-* as *Lleu-*, *ou* being the regular Old Welsh form of Medieval *eu* as well as of *yw* with obscure *y* (see *Gram.*, p. 31); on seeing his mistake he wrote another *u* to make the *u* into double-u, i.e. *w*; though not generally used by him, two *u*’s might stand for *w*, as in *B.B.* 2·1; conversely *w* might stand for *uu* (=*vu*) as in *wtic* for *vudic* *B.B.* 83·11, *wt* for *vuδ* do. 86·3. As the scribe himself sometimes uses *e* for *y* (see above p. 126), he left the *e* uncorrected. Dr. Evans (like Nash) guessed *Llywyδ* ‘chief’; but as he cannot assume an Old Welsh original, he has no explanation to offer of the curious spelling, which he finds it convenient to ignore.

*echassaf.* The prefix is probably *ech-* ; and *echas* seems to bear the same relation to *dias*, as *ehofn* (*echofn*) does to *diofn* ‘fearless’ and

# Taliesin.

Apparently the root idea is similar in both pairs. *Dias* seems to be an adjective in *glew dias dinas e lu ovnawc* B.A. 10.8, ‘bold, fearless, [he was] a stronghold to a timid host,’ and a noun in *dinas y δias* do. 15.4, ‘a stronghold [was] his valour’; *am-δias* and *diassed* are adjectives, used with *gwrhyd* ‘manliness’ and *greid* ‘courage’ (Breton *gret* ‘courage’, Troude, s.v.), as *gwrhyt amδias* B.A. 1.2, *gwrhyt diassed* B.T. 59.8, *greid am-δias*, *Myv* 145 a 3. Hence I take the superlative *echassaf* to mean ‘most valiant’. It cannot easily be from *cas*, for from *c*- only *ach*- is possible (and *achas* exists), which can become *ech*- only before *i* or *y*. The previous renderings of these opening words of the poem, based upon the mistaken derivation from *cas*, are—

> Though the chief is angry.—Nash.
> The lion will be most implacable.—R. Williams.
> The Chief I do not dislike.—Dr. Evans.

Line 3.—*kynnwys* ‘admission’ rendered correctly by R. Williams; see Silvan Evans, s.v. But *cynnwys* is something more than mere ‘admission’; it is still used dialectally in Gwynedd for ‘welcome admission, encouragement’.

Line 4.—*eilassaf* ‘chieftain’. Cf. *Hywel haelaf, vaur eilassaw, gorescynhwy* B.B. 76.7 (final *-aw* = *af*); *men yδ ynt eilyassaf (e)lein* B.A. 14.14 ‘where the chieftain’s blades are’; *dor angor beδin, buδ eilyassaf* do. 15.5 ‘the door, the anchor of an army, victorious chief’; *llu eilassaf* B.T. 64.22, context obscure, possibly ‘chief of a host’.

<margin>of CL</margin>
Line 5.—As it stands, the line means ‘Not much do I care ever for the race that I see’. *Byth* ‘ever’ comes in awkwardly, and seems to be the scribe’s guess to rhyme with his *gwehelyth* (which, however, he spells *gweheleith*; cf. *gwehelieith*, *F.A.B.*, ii, p. 225). The original word may have been either *pi*, Medieval *py*, *by* ‘what’, or *pet* ‘how many’. The next word I take to have been *guahaud*, which the scribe read as *guahalid* and understood as *gwehelyth*, which makes the line too long. The mistake of reading *u* as *li* is the converse of the error of reading *li* as *n*, which we have come across more than once; with *d* for *th* he would be familiar; also with the vocalism of the supposed *guahalid*, with which compare *Ligualid* above, p. 58. I cannot explain *welaf* except as the scribe’s substitution for *gaffaf* or *gahaf*, which made no sense with his misreadings. I take the original line to have been—

->Nyt mawr ym dawr by gwahawδ a gaffaf,<-

to be rendered as above. The line has internal rhyme, and therefore was not necessarily divided into two fives; *dawr* forms a good rhyme with *gwahawδ*: and the line is of the right length.

*Byth* is most readily explained as derived from Irish; but as it is

<meta>This page continues the philological notes on the poem "Dadolwch Uryen," specifically analyzing linguistic derivations and scribal errors in the manuscript, following the discussion of terms like *echassaf* and *cynnwys*.</meta>

# Taliesin.

found in Cornish and Breton also, such a derivation is doubtful; though as a British word its formation is obscure; see Thurneysen's *Alt-Irischen Gram.*, p. 222. Since, however, it is probably a late insertion here, the date of the poem is not affected.

Line 7.—*armeiteyrnedd*, so joined in the MS [manuscript], as if the scribe had run on from the final *t* of -*meit* to the initial *t* of *teyrnedd*. The most probable reading seems to be *anmeit* ‘nod, beck’; an Old Welsh plural is *enmeituou*, a gloss on “per nutus” <note>by a nod</note>. In the later language, metathesis occurred, and the word is now *amnaid*. With *anmeit teyrnedd*, compare *deorum nutu* <note>by the will of the gods</note>, Cicero, *Cat.* iii, 9, 21.

Line 8.—*laweredd*—The -*edd* was added by the scribe because he did not realize that the -*er* of *lawer* formed a good rhyme with the -*edd* of *wystledd*.

Line 9.—*hoffedd*. The original meaning of *hoffi* seems to be ‘to value, appreciate’, etc., from which the modern ‘to like’ is naturally derived (compare English *dear*: ‘precious; loved’). The proverb *Hanner y wledd hoffedd yw* means ‘Half the feast is the appreciation of it’ (not “is fondness”, as Pughe renders it). *Tripheth a hoffa kerdd*, *Red Book*, col. 1141, ‘three things that give value to a song’. *Gor-hoffedd*, *R. B. Bruts* 215, means ‘boasting’ (compare English *over-weening*), from which sometimes *hoffedd* means ‘boast’. Here, however, the word is used in its primary sense of ‘setting a value upon’.

Line 12.—*delideu*. There seems to be no foundation for Pughe’s ‘hard substance; metal’, except his silly derivation of the word from *dèl*, which he renders as “obdurate”. Here it makes the line too long, and seems to be a compound (compare *dilideu* ‘feasts’, *Myv*. 144 a 27) substituted for the archaic uncompounded *llideu* ‘feasts’, the plural of *llid*, which survives in Breton as *lid* ‘feast’, plural *lidou*; the exact phonetic equivalent appears in Old Irish as *líth* ‘feast’. The same substitution as the one above occurs in our text in 19.8; thus,

> Gwneynt eu peiron     a verwynt heb tân,
> Gwneynt eu (de)/llideu      yn oes oesseu.
> ‘They make their cauldrons which boil without fire,
> They make their feasts for ever.’

*gorefrasseu*, apparently ‘superfluities, luxuries’. It is difficult to fix the meaning of *efras*, but it seems to denote a ‘resource’ of power or wealth: *ys evras gwrth* (read *gwrdd*) ‘he is mighty of resource’, *F.A.B.*, ii, 271; the *efras* of the white town by the wood is ever ‘her blood on the face of her grass’, ibid. 285; *llew lluch efras* ‘lion of impetuous might’, Gwalchmei, *Myv*. 144b; *gwr gwrdd i evreis* ‘man of mighty resources’, ibid. 143b; *evras cad* ‘fighting force’, Prydydd y Moch, ibid. 212b; *Pedyr per y evras* 227a; cf. 152 a, b, *B.B.* 86.8. In the only medieval example of *gorevras* known to me, it seems to be

# Taliesin.

newly compounded: *Gwr gorevras, gwas gwenwyn, Cynddelw, Myv.* 184a, ‘(he was) a man of great might, a youth of virulence’.

Line 15.—*ieith* ‘nation’, so used as late as the fifteenth century; see an example in a couplet by Gutun Owain in my *Gram.*, p. 34.

Line 16.—*goleith*. All the lexicographers have gone wrong in taking this to be a compound of *lleith* ‘death’. Mr. Williams has seen that it means ‘to avoid, evade, elude’. *Pughe's* examples are *Dywal dir fydd ei olaith*, which he renders “The death of the fierce is certain”, though it obviously means ‘The fierce must be avoided’, and *Ni ellid ei olaith*, which he renders “he could not be slain”, instead of ‘there was no escape from him’. A good example is *Ny oleith lleith yr llyfyrder* ‘he will not shirk death from cowardice’, *Cynddelw, Myv.* 176a, *Strachan, Intr.* 233. *Anoleith* ‘inevitable’ (not “deathful” as given by Silvan Evans); *lleith anoleith* ‘inevitable death’, *B.T.* 15·13, *Prydydd y Moch, Myv.* 211 b 4.

Line 17.—*gweyhelu henwn*. This half-line is obviously corrupt, and I am unable to suggest any satisfactory emendation.

Line 18.—*Nyt oeð well*; the *oeð* is superfluous; the reading might be *Nyt gwell* or *Ny well*; cf. *Na well, W.B.M.* c. 84.

*gerwn*, a scribal error for *garwn*.

*kyn ys gwybyðwn*: *kyn* ‘before’, *Gram.* 446. The distinction between *gwybod* ‘to know’ (*savoir*) and *adnabod* ‘to recognize/be acquainted with’ (*connaître*) goes back to British, for it is found in Breton as well as in Welsh; but *ad-nabod* is literally ‘to recognize, to be acquainted with’; and as *gŵyð* still means ‘presence’ it is possible that **gwyðbod* might be used in Early Welsh for ‘meeting’ or ‘*making* the acquaintance of’, which is the sense required here (as its Greek cognate εἶδον might be used for ‘seeing’ a person in the sense of ‘meeting’ him). The meaning of the sentence can hardly be doubtful, and the use of *gwybyðwn* points to an antiquity well beyond the Middle Ages.

Line 19.—The article is hardly ever found before *weithon* (=*weith hon*) in Medieval Welsh; and its regular omission (while *y prytwn, y wershon*, etc., are never without it) may be a survival from the period when the article was not essential before the demonstrative *hwn, hon*.

Line 20.—The scribe inserted *y*, which makes the line too long, because *namyn* meant nothing to him but ‘except’; he took the phrase to mean ‘except for the most high God’, a reservation which weakens the declaration for no intelligible purpose. The meaning is clearly ‘I will no more forswear Uryen than I will forswear the most high God’. *Namuy(n)* is literally ‘not more’; it was used, like its Irish cognate *nammā* in the sense of ‘only’ to strengthen a word meaning ‘but’; thus Irish *acht . . . nammā* ‘but . . . only’, Old Welsh *honit nammui* ‘but only’; in Welsh the word for ‘but’ came to be omitted, and *namwyn* itself was used for ‘but’ (cf. the collo-

# Taliesin.

...quial use of *only* for "*but*" in English). In the Shirburn Brut, it occurs as "*only*" after *nyt* in the passage of Lludd corresponding to W.B.M., c. 192·21: *nyt na mwy* "*not only*" (I. Williams, *Lludd a Llevelys*, p. 18; in the W.B. it was wrongly copied as *nyt mwy*). In Medieval Welsh, *namwyn* has no other meaning; in Old Welsh, it occurs as an adverb qualifying "*but*", and as "*but*" itself (Book of Llan Daf, p. 120), and apparently as "*only*" (ibid., p. xliii). Its use here in its literal and etymological sense of "*not more*", with a dependent genitive of comparison *Duw uchaf* ("than the most high God"), which was unintelligible to the medieval scribe, takes the phrase back beyond the Old Welsh period.

*dioferaf* is the verb for which the verbal noun is *diofryd*, just as the verbal noun of *cymeraf* is *cymryd*, of *differaf* is *diffryd*, of *adferaf* is *edfryd*, and of *gochelaf* is *gochlyd* (*Grammar*, p. 391). This well-known survival of Aryan vowel-gradation in Welsh was unknown to Dr. Evans, who "amends" *dioferaf* into "*diovrydaf*" (II, 96).

Line 22.—Medieval *ysclyvon* would be written *sclimon* in Old Welsh (cf. *Grammar* 159, lines 17-18). Here we have another proof that *l* was short in the scribe's copy. He read the *l* as *i*, and the four strokes of *im* as *rr*.

It is probable that some lines are missing at the end of this song.

It has been seen in the notes that there is a considerable amount of evidence that the three songs to Urien mentioned above have been copied from a manuscript in a flat-topped hand of the ninth century, and there are many grammatical and etymological indications that the matter is even older. It does not seem necessary to me to argue that the Urien in whose honor they were written is the historical Urien. They have every appearance of having been composed to be sung in his presence. He is referred to interchangeably in the second and third person. The singer addresses him as "thou," and then, addressing the company, speaks of him as "he." Is this also a piece of ninth-century realism?

I include below the elegy on Owein, son of Uryen (67·18), which was referred to at some length above on pages 87 and following.

# THE DEATH-SONG OF OWEIN.

1 The soul of Owein son of Urien,
      may the Lord have regard for its need.
  Rheged's prince, whom the heavy sod covers—
      his judgment was not shallow.
5 At supper time [he heard] the acclaimed song of praise;
      at the wings of dawn [he hurled] the sharpened spears,
  For no equal is to be found
      for the prince of radiant cheer.
<margin>nature?</margin>
  Reaper of enemies, captor—
10    heir of his father and grandfathers.
  When Owein killed Fflamdwyn,
      it was no more [of a feat] than sleeping.
  The vast host of England sleep
      with the light in their eyes;
15 And those who did not flee quickly
      were bolder than they needed to be.
  Owein punished them fiercely,
      like a pack of wolves chasing sheep.
  A worthy man above his many-colored harness,
20    who would give horses to those who asked.
  Though he might have hoarded them like a miser,
      he distributed them for the sake of his soul.
  The soul of Owein son of Urien,
24    may the Lord have regard for its need.

### NOTES ON "THE DEATH-SONG OF OWEIN".

Translations: Nash, *Taliesin*, 108; R. Williams, *F.A.B.*, i, 366; Dr. Evans, II, 125, regarding which see above, pp. 87–90.

*Meter.* The meter is "awdl gywydd" (*Dosp. Ed.*, p. lxvii). The unit is a line of fourteen syllables divided into two seven-syllable half-lines; the last syllable of the first half-line rhymes with any final syllable from the second to the fifth of the second half-line. In some cases, it rhymes with *two* words in the latter half, such as *clotvawr* in line 5, with *gwawr* and *gwaewawr* in line 6, and *drut* in line 17 with *cnut* and *dylut* in line 18. The end of the full line rhymes throughout; in this poem the rhyme is *-eit*. The line is now regarded as a couplet, and the half-lines as lines; I have numbered them accordingly above. The meter was rarely used by the <term>cynghanedd</term> poets, who preferred the ordinary <term>cywydd</term>: there is an example by Dafydd ab Edmwnd in Peniarth MS. 77, p. 392, beginning—

->The happiest place in North Wales<-
->Is the court of mead and the gardens of Anglesey.<-

But stanzas of three couplets of this meter later became one of the favorite forms of <term>penillion</term> to sing to the harp; a good example is Lewis Morris's "Caniad y Gog i Feirionydd", of which one stanza may be quoted:

->Dear to the birds of the world<-
->Is their freedom throughout the woods;<-
->Dear to the infant is the milk<-
->Of his exceptionally happy nurse;<-
->Oh! I would not say for my life<-
->How dear Merioneth is.<-

The oldest extant poem by Meilyr, the earliest historical medieval bard, is in this meter, *Myv.* 142a; it was composed at Mynydd Carn in 1081. Some of the oldest Irish poetry is written in stanzas of four half-lines of seven syllables each. Sanctan's hymn, *Irische Texte*, i, 49, which contains a relatively large number of obscure passages, is in that meter; it is attributed to the Briton Sanctān, who is said to be the son of Samuil Pennissel (*Lives of Brit. Saints*,

<summary>This page concludes the translation of the "Death-song of Owein" and provides a detailed technical analysis of the "awdl gywydd" meter used in the poem, comparing it to later Welsh and Irish poetic forms.</summary>
<keywords>Owein ap Uryen, Marwnat Owein, awdl gywydd, meter, Welsh poetry, cynghanedd, penillion, Meilyr, Mynydd Carn, Sanctān, Lewis Morris, Dafydd ab Edmwnd</keywords>

--- Page 199 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical and linguistic analysis of the "Death-song of Owein," focusing on the poem's meter and specific word origins.</meta>

iv, 175), son of Pabo, third in descent from Ceneu ab Coel (ibid., iv, 38); the author would therefore be a contemporary and kinsman of Owein ap Uryen. The internal rhyme of the "awdl gywydd" occurs in the early Latin hymns. Kuno Meyer (*Primer of Irish Metrics*, p. 9) quotes as an example:

> Conclamantes Deo dignum  hymnum sanctae Mariae.
> ut vox pulset omnem aurem  per laudem vicariam.

There is thus no reason at all to doubt that the meter is as old as the late sixth century; both the rhyme-system and the length and division of lines are probably much older.

Line 1.—*ap*. In Medieval Welsh generally this is written *vab*, but the *v* had already disappeared in the twelfth century, e.g. *Owein ab Urien*, B.B. 64·7. In the scribe's copy the form would doubtless be *map*.

Line 2.—*gobwyllit*: the *o* was begun as *6*, i.e. *w*, by the scribe, which shows that this poem also was copied from a manuscript in Old Welsh orthography, in which the prefix *go-* is always written *guo-*. Dr. Evans notes the error, but has nothing to say about it.

On lines 1 and 2 see above pp. 87-8.

Line 3.—*ae* is the old form of the relative, which when accusative was followed by the radical initial of the verb; see note on line 23 of "Yng Ngorffowys" above, p. 180.

*tromlas* 'heavy green' occurs as an adjective in 54·20 qualifying *cadwyn* 'chain', the phrase denoting the prison of earth, the grave.

*cywydeit*, derivative in *-eit* (verbal noun ending, compare *gorδyfneit*, *synnyeit*) of *cywyd* 'mind', written *kyvid* in B.B. 76·13, where final *-d=d* (not *ð*).

Line 5.—This line is a syllable short in the manuscript. The expression *kerδ glyt clotvawr* (read *glotvawr*) is complete, and *kerδeu clyt* is not a probable emendation. The missing word therefore probably came after *Iscell*; and the internal rhyme of line 3 suggests that it rhymed with *clyt*; the word *pryt* fulfils this condition, and exactly fills the gap in the sense; see below.

*Iscell*, according to Richards, is 'broth, pottage, gruel, supping'; the English *sup* is literally 'to imbibe', and is allied to *soup*; one might *sup*, or partake of *iscell*, at any hour, but as *supper* in English has come to denote the last meal, so the time <note>Greek: *par excellence* or "pre-eminently"</note> for *iscell* was the evening; thus *pryt* 'time' is required to bring out the contrast with 'dawn' in the next line.

*kerδ glyt*. This *clyt* has nothing to do with *clyd* 'sheltered', (which is from \**klit*- originally \**kl̥t*- , root \**kel*- 'cover'), but comes from the genitive \**klutī* of the neuter noun whose nominative-accusative singular was \**klutóm*, which has given Welsh *clod*; the root is \**kleu*- 'hear'. Briefly, the old genitive of *clod* survived in the expression *cerdd glyd*,

which was still used in the twelfth century, e.g. by Cynddelw in *Myv.* 190a quoted above, p. 98 fn. It occurs in the late part of "Anrec Urien": *Ef a δaw byt ny byδ kerδ glyt, ny byδ kelvyδ*, *F.A.B.*, ii, 292, *Y Cym.*, vii, 125, 'A time will come when there will be no song of praise, when there will be no accomplished (singer)'.

Line 6.—This line is linked to the preceding one by the "cymeriad cynganeddol" *iscell* / *escyll*, making the reading of the two words certain and disproving the ignorant "emendation" of II, 124.

*escyll gawr* should undoubtedly be *esgyll gwawr*, for the words occur in 42·13 in the same connection: *glessynt escyll gwawr, escorynt (wy) waywawr* 'the wings of dawn became grey, they brought forth spears'.

*llifeit* 'whetted'; a whetstone is still called *maen llifo* in North Wales.

Line 9.—*Medel* is usually a feminine noun (*y fedel*) meaning, like its Irish cognate *methel*, 'a company of reapers'; but it seems also to have been a masculine noun in Welsh, meaning 'a reaper', e.g. *y medel B.B.* 45·9; in this sense it has been replaced by *medelwr*. Cf. *medel e alon, B.A.* 26·15, 'reaper of his enemies'.

*gefeilat* is the derivative in -*(i)ad* of *gafael* 'to seize'; this ending, when it affects the stem-vowels, as here, denotes the agent; see p. 98 fn. The word therefore means 'seizer, captor'.

Line 10.—On *teit* 'ancestors' see my Gram. p. 219. The bard is thinking, not of Owein's "grandsire" (II, 125), but of his ancestors, Ceneu and Coel.

Line 11.—Nash renders this line "When Fflamddwyn slew Owain", with a footnote stating that "The ordinary construction would give 'When Owein slew Fflamddwyn'. But in an elegy on Owain the circumstances of his death are more naturally mentioned [why "more naturally" than the exploits of his life?]; and the reading renders the following lines more intelligible," *Tal.*, p. 109. By the "following lines" he means l. 12, which he completely misunderstood. How it is "more intelligible" that Owein should punish his enemies "following" his death, Nash does not say; the poet is, of course, continuing his account of Owein's prowess. R. Williams adopts Nash's rendering, and we are assured in a note (ii, 418) that this "is the natural construction". Professor Powel observes that "if by 'natural' is meant 'in accordance with Welsh usage', then the assertion cannot be accepted"; it is, in fact, the exact reverse of the truth. In a direct statement, a noun object may precede the noun subject, as *Dygystuδ deuruδ dagreu, F.A.B.*, ii, 282, 'tears afflict the cheeks'; but this is very rare, and in a dependent clause such an inversion is hardly to be found. The order is subject-object; as *Ac velly y kavas Kulhwch Olwen, R.B.M.* 143, 'And

<meta>This page continues the technical and linguistic analysis of the poem "Death-song of Owein," providing evidence for the poem's early composition and correcting previous misinterpretations.</meta>

[it was] so that Kulhwch won Olwen’. Carnhuanawc and Stephens understood the line in its natural sense, ‘When Owein killed Fflamddwyn’; see Professor Powel's note in *The Gododin*, p. 69. On Dr. Evans's mistranslation, see above, p. 90.

Line 12.—This line appears to be correct, for the <term>cymeriad</term> *kyscit* in the next line makes the reading *kysceit* practically certain. It means literally ‘it was no more than sleeping’, which means, I suppose, that it required no greater effort on the part of Owein.

*noc et* is usually written *noget* or *nogyt* in Medieval Welsh. The *et* is an extension also found in Breton *eget* ‘than’ and Cornish *ages*. The *n-* in Welsh is the old ending of the comparative tacked on to the original *oc* ‘than’: see my *Grammar*, pp. 243, 447.

The early modern prosodists allowed an initial consonant to be brought forward to the end of a preceding word to form a rhyme, which was called <term>odl gudd</term> ‘hidden rhyme’; thus, *wy’* followed by *lawen* could rhyme with *hwyl* (J. D. Rhys, 1592, p. 284; in reproducing information supplied to him, J.D.R. completely misses the point and thinks that the words *wy’ lawen* actually are *wyl awen*, which would form an ordinary rhyme, rather than a “hidden” one). *Fflamδwyn* and *vwy n-* form an <term>odl gudd</term>; but it is unthinkable that the author of this poem intended such a thing; <term>odl gudd</term> is a comparatively late device—I cannot recollect even a medieval example of it. To the author, *vwy* here was clearly *vwyn*, and probably ‘than’ was *ocet*. The Irish forms suggest that *mwy* comes from \**māiōs*; I have suggested a doublet \**māison*, formed with the ordinary British comparative ending (equivalent to the Greek -ιων), as the origin of *mwyn* (*Grammar*, 98). Even if that is not the case, *mwyn* must have been formed at an early period on the analogy of other comparatives, for its existence is proved by *namwyn* alongside *namwy*, of which the former alone survived and became *namyn*; in this, the *n* cannot come from *no*, for *no* ‘than’ is not used after it; see the note on line 20 of the last poem, p. 185. All this goes to prove that this couplet was written before the Old Welsh period when *mwyn* was already obsolete.

Line 15.—*hayach* with the negative usually means in Medieval Welsh ‘not much’ or ‘hardly at all’; here it must mean ‘actively’ or ‘energetically’, a more primitive sense from the root \**segh*-; compare the Sanskrit *sáha-s* ‘mighty’, etc., and *sáhasā* ‘(forcibly), suddenly, precipitately’.

Line 16.—The last word on page 67 of the manuscript is *oeδynt*; a piece of the top corner of the leaf has been torn off, so that only *ch* remains of the first word on page 68; there is a dot before it, which represents the bottom of the *a*; the rhyme proves that the word ended in *-ach*. There is room for only three letters before *ch*; I have measured *hy* in lines 20 and 21, and find that their width, along with that of *a*, fits the space exactly. It appears to me that *hyach* fits the sense exactly as well.

The author also provides a manuscript-based reconstruction of a damaged word in line 16.</summary>

--- Page 202 ---
<meta>This page continues the linguistic analysis of the poem attributed to Taliesin, specifically examining grammatical particles and the structural convention of repeating the opening word at the poem's conclusion.</meta>

# Taliesin.

Line 21.—*Kyt* is a proper conjunction, followed immediately by the verb, such as *kyt tybyckych*, . . . *kyt keffych* (W.B.M., c. 480). The *as* that separates it from the verb here could, therefore, only be the infixed objective pronoun of the 3rd person singular or plural. The only possible antecedent is *meirch* (horses); but *cronni* (to hoard) is hardly a word that would have been used for horses. (The singular infixed pronoun after *kyt* is *ef* in "Dadolwch Uryen," line 17; see above, p. 181.) But we have seen in "Gweith Argoet Llwyfein," line 12 (p. 156 above), that *kyn as* was used with the imperfect for "before (he would)" in the sense of "rather than," where *as* cannot be an infixed pronoun and seems to be a conjunction meaning "that." I take the construction here to be the same. In an ordinary temporal clause, with the aorist or present subjunctive, no conjunction follows *cyn* (Grammar, p. 446). It occurs with the infixed pronoun *ys* in "Dadolwch Uryen," line 18 (p. 181 above).

*calet* (hard); as a noun, it has two meanings: (1) "hard fight" (e.g., B.B. 65.13, B.A. 20.21); (2) "hard man," in the sense of Matthew 25:24. I have translated it as "miser," although a line in one of the "Englynion Eiry Mynydd" (*Myv.* 358a) tells us that:

->Nid cybydd yw pob caled.<-
->‘Not every hard man is a miser.’<-

Line 22.—*ry*, which the scribe stupidly copied as the negative *ny*, is the perfective particle, which is positive and emphatic. The impersonal *rannet* has no expressed object; it means "there was a distribution." Regarding *rac y eneit*, see p. 89 above.

The last word of the poem repeats the first. This became a rule in Irish poetry: "The concluding word of every poem must repeat either the whole or part of the first word (or first stressed word) of the poem" (Kuno Meyer, op. cit., p. 12). The object is to provide a catchword to lead back to the beginning, so as to end the poem with the couplet or stanza with which it begins. The word *eneit* at the end of our poem forms a <term>cymeriad</term> with the *Eneit* of the first line, which is therefore meant to follow it, just as *Kyscit* of line 13 follows *kysceit* of line 12; see the notes on <term>cymeriad</term> above (pp. 176–7). The scribe writes only the beginning of the couplet that is to be repeated; see examples of abbreviated first lines, or merely first words, repeated with a <term>cymeriad</term> in B.B. 36.10, 70.11. There are many examples in the oldest Irish poems; for example, Ultan’s Hymn, which begins *Brigit be bithmaith*, ends *ron soera Brigit*, which is followed in the manuscript by *Brigit bé* (see *Ir. Texte*, i, 25). Since the form of <term>cymeriad</term> on which the repetition of the first word is based is a distinctive feature of Welsh verse, frequently occurring as a link between lines in the middle of a poem (e.g., in "Yng Ngorffowys" above), it seems probable that the device was borrowed by the Irish bards from the British. In Welsh

<meta>This page continues the analysis of Taliesin’s poetic structure, specifically examining the repetition of opening lines as a structural device rather than a strict requirement like the Irish "cymeriad."</meta>

# Taliesin.

We also have the repetition of opening lines without "cymeriad," as in B.B. 24·8 and 71·11, which shows that the "cymeriad" was not the essential feature it became in Irish, but only a help to secure the repetition of the first lines.

Line 23.—The abbreviated first line appears thus in the MS: *Eneit. O. ap vryen.* Thus the reading which Dr. Evans asserts to be "impossible" is repeated and confirmed—a fact which he passes over in silence.

The subject of this poem is not the Owein ap Uryen of the medieval imagination—the Knight of the Round Table, hero of the tale of "The Lady of the Fountain," the "Chevalier au Lyon" of Chrestien de Troyes, or the Ywain of "Ywain and Gawain"—but the historical Owein ap Uryen who fought with his father against the sons of Ida in the latter half of the sixth century. He is seen not in the glamour of romance, nor even through the haze of a century or two, but as the real prince of Rheged by a man who knew him and loved him. Such idealization as the poem contains consists of that heightening of the actual which is expected and taken for granted in an elegy. I believe that the impression the reading of the poem will leave on most readers is that it breathes an intimacy which could hardly be simulated in a production founded on hearsay or tradition.

It is interesting to note the comment on this poem which a fifteenth-century bard has written on the top margin of page 68:

->goreu ynghymry o gerdd taliessin benn beirdd<-
->‘The best in Wales of the work of Taliesin, Chief of Bards.’<-

This opinion is no doubt chiefly based on the form; the poem is an exquisite example of a meter that was still in use. The requirements of the rhyme are more than met: one "prif-odl" or "chief-rhyme" binds the couplets together into a single whole, and the "cymeriad" at the end introduces the opening couplet, which serves as a most effective conclusion. But apart from the form, which is

# Taliesin.

not more elaborate than, in its way, that of “Yng Ngorffowys,” the subject matter of this poem seems to indicate that it is the work of a man of more mature years. This agrees with the view that the poems are genuine, for an elegy on Owein would naturally have been written many years later than songs addressed to his father.¹

What has been said above about the Owein of the elegy is also true of the Urien of the songs. He is not a medieval Sir Uryance, or even a traditional hero, but the actual living Urien, Prince of Rheged. That the sense of reality which—despite all hyperbole—the songs undoubtedly convey in the description of Urien as a current terror to the Angles and a personality who commands the respect of those around him, in the expression of the bard’s personal obligation to him, and in the account of the good things of Llwyfenydd in which he himself participates—that this could have been simulated in the ninth century does not appear to be entirely probable; but Voltaire has said that “it is always to the improbable that the skeptic is ready to listen.”

An old tradition connects Taliesin with Llyn Geirionnydd, situated above the Conway valley about two miles west of Llanrwst. Medieval evidence of the tradition is found in one of the lines added at the end of “Anrec Uryen” (*Y Cymmrodor*, vii, p. 126):

> mineu dalyessin o (iawn) lann ² llyn geirionnyð
> ‘And I, Taliesin, from the bank of Llyn Geirionnydd.’

<note>² It is very unlikely that *iawn* here is anything but *lann* misread as *iaun*, which was the spelling of *iawn* until the end of the twelfth century.</note>

# Taliesin.

He would, therefore, have been brought up in the outskirts of the court of Maelgwn at Degannwy. This fits well with his position in the songs. At the court of Urien, he was a visitor. Undoubtedly his stay was long, for he was provided with a fine home. But he leaves and comes again. “I will return to the North,” he says in “Dadolwch Uryen,” a song which might well have been entitled “Urien Revisited.”

There are several other historical poems in the *Book of Taliesin*, all more difficult than those mentioned above, but not, therefore, *less* likely to be authentic. The other poems to Urien are the following: (1) “Ar un blyneð,” page 59, in which the reference to him “with his horse under him at the battle of Mynaw (Goðeu)”—see above, page 73—appears rather incoherently, and is followed by a number of mysterious lines, ending with a description of Urien’s prowess. (2) “Arðwyre Reget ryssed rieu,” page 61, which contains the reference to Ulph and to Urien coming to Aeron; there are many other historical allusions in the poem, including a list of Urien’s battles. (3) “Yspeil Taliessin,” page 62; some inserted glosses in the first part of this are noted above, pages 140-1. It refers to Urien as a “defender in Aeron,” 63.5, and contains a more picturesquely figurative description of Urien than anything contained in the poems translated above; but its difficulties are formidable.

“Anrec Uryen,” which has been referred to several times in the pages above, does not appear in the *Book of Taliesin*, but is found in the separate portion of the *White Book* (*Y Cymmrodor*, vii, 125), and in the *Red Book*, column 1049 (*F.A.B.*, ii, 291). Stephens saw that the poem consists of three unrelated parts; of these, he considered the first and third authentic and the second late (*Arch. Camb.*, 1851, pages 204 and following). <meta>This page continues the discussion on Thomas Stephens’s analysis of the poem "Anrec Uryen," specifically debating the authenticity of its different sections compared to the views held by William Forbes Skene.</meta>

# Taliesin.

Skene, ignoring Stephens's clear-sighted analysis of the poem and speaking of his view of the second part as if it applied to the whole, attempts to prove that the whole is old (*F.A.B.*, i, 211-4); but Stephens is undoubtedly right in his view regarding the second part. This is of the same character as the medieval predictive poems; and, as Stephens observes, no poem that can be attributed to Taliesin “assumes the predictive form” (op. cit., p. 210). The first and second parts are in the “<term>rhupunt</term>” metre, which consists of lines of twelve syllables divided into three fours, of which the first two usually rhyme together and the third carries the “chief-rhyme”. In both of these parts, the chief-rhyme is *-yδ* throughout, with the variant *-yr* appearing once (at the end of the first part according to Stephens’s division); this identity of rhyme provides a sufficient reason for them having been run together, without the assumption that the second was deliberately composed as an addition to the first, to which it bears no relation. The first part deals with Urien, and with Ieuaf, Keneu, and Seleu, whom Skene takes to be the sons of Llywarch Hen, p. 212. The third part, which has nothing to do with the others, consists of tags. The first of these is the well-known assertion of Urien’s preeminence, which, with two emendations noted below, reads as follows in the White Book:

> ->Uryen o Reget, haelaf ẏssẏδ,<-
> ->Ac a vu ẏr Adaf, ac a vẏδ¹;<-
> ->Lletaf ẏ gledẏf,² balch ẏ gẏnted,<-
> ->Or tri thëẏrn ar dec or Gogled.<-
> ->Urien of Rheged, the most generous that is,<-
> ->That has been since Adam, and that will be;<-
> ->Of the broadest sword—proud in his hall—<-
> ->Of the thirteen princes of the North.<-

# Taliesin.

This was followed by “whose name I know, Aneirin Gwawdrydd the poet, and I, Taliesin,” etc., as noted above on page 194. This seems to be a nonsensical couplet created by a scribe because he misunderstood a previous copyist’s statement that he did not know the name of the poet—whether it was Aneirin or Taliesin. Finally, there is the defective copy of the *yny vallwyf* tag, quoted above on page 155.

There are references to Urien in other poems. “Teithi etinygant” (p. 41) contains the following line (42.6):
->A thraw y Dygen     meu molawt Uryen.<-
->‘And beyond Dygen, mine is the praise of Urien.’<-

Perhaps Maelgwn’s kingdom extended as far as Dygen on the current English border, and “beyond Dygen” meant beyond his boundaries. In “Golychafi gulwyδ” (p. 33), the poet says that he sang before Brochvael of Powys and before Urien (33.6):
->Keint rac uδ clotleu yn doleu Hafren,<-
->Rac Brochvael Powys a garwys vy awen;<-
->Keint yn advwyn rodle ymore rac Uryen.<-
->‘I sang before a famous prince in the meadows of the Severn,<-
->Before Brochvael of Powys who loved my muse;<-
->I sang on a fair lawn on a morning before Urien.’<-

This poem is a curious mixture of mythology and reminiscence. Preceding the lines above is the statement, “I sang before the sons of Llyr at Aber¹ Henvelen” (33.3). Lower

# Taliesin.

Down, following some lines which appear to be later interpolations, comes the famous passage about the release of Elphin, 33.19:

> Dodwyf Deganhwy y̆ amrysson
> A Maelgwn vwyhaf y̆ achwysson;
> Ellyngeis vy arglwyd yng ngwyð deon,
> Elphin pendefic ryhodigyon.

> ‘I came to Deganhwy to contend
> With Maelgwn of greatest prerogatives;
> I set free my lord in the presence of good men,
> Elphin, chief of nobles.’

This is followed immediately by the statement “I was in the battle of Godeu with Lleu and Gwydion . . . I was with Brân in Ireland,” 33.23. The poem ends with the charming glimpse of the Celtic other-world of which Rhys has given a substantially correct¹ translation in his *Celtic Folklore*, p. 678. It is to be noted that all the historical personages named in the poem are the contemporaries of the historical Taliesin.

“Kychwedyl am doðyw”, p. 38, treats the cattle raids and battles of Owein. “When he returned to Erechwyð² from the land of the Clydemen, not a cow lowed to her calf,” 38.21. The poem raises many interesting questions. Who, for example, is the Mabon mentioned in it? In Roman times Deus Maponus was the Apollo of Rheged; three inscriptions in his honor have been discovered at Ribchester, Ainstable and Hexham respectively (*C.I.L.*, vii, nos. 218, 332, 1345). One statement about Mabon in the poem is intelligible: “Unless they were

The Latin and Irish genitives on the stone prove that the nominative was *Votepo-rix*; this would give *Guotepir* in Old Welsh, as *Maglo-rix* gives the Medieval *Meilyr*. It follows that *Vortipori* in the text of Gildas is not the vocative of *Vo(r)tiporius* but an error for *Voteporix*. In fact, Mommsen’s only authorities for *Vortipori* are the printed editions of 1525 and 1568. The oldest manuscript (C, 11th century) has *uortipor\**; the next oldest (A, 12th century), *uortipore*; the latest (D, 14th century) *uertepori*. Dr. Lloyd rightly writes the name *Voteporix* in his *History*, 1911, p. 115; and Mr. Ifor Williams analyzed it and showed its relation to *Godebawg* in *Y Beirniad*, 1915, pp. 275-6. “Mydwyf Taliesin deryð,” on page 69, has been understood to be an elegy on Cunedda. In the *Myvyrian*, page 60, it is titled “Marwnad Cunedda” [Elegy of Cunedda]; but the sole source of the *Myvyrian* text is the Book of Taliesin, in which the poem has no title. Thus, for “Marwnad Cunedda,” there is no manuscript authority. Cunedda was Maelgwn’s great-grandfather, according to the Harleian pedigree (*Y Cymmrodor*, ix, 170). He came from Manaw Gododdin in the North and drove out the Irish from Gwynedd with great slaughter 146 years before the reign of Maelgwn, as we learn from the excellent authority of the “Saxon Genealogies” in a passage reproduced in our frontispiece. Possibly the “cxxxxvi” [146] which has been written in words in our facsimile had an 'x' or two too many; however, the great-grandfather of Maelgwn must belong to the early part of the fifth century and could hardly have been the subject of an elegy by Taliesin. Stephens addresses this difficulty in an article in the *Arch. Camb.*, 1852, reprinted in *The Gododdin*, p. 356; he says:

> ‘This poem has been a great stumbling-block in the way of all rational accounts of Taliesin and his poems. Is it an ancient or a modern poem? If read without any doubts as to the chronology, the poem carries with it all the marks of antiquity; there is an utter absence of any romantic or fictional element; it has all the appearance of an historical poem, and possesses all the attributes which belong to the other poems of Taliesin.

His proposed solution consists of an attempt to prove, despite the evidence from Nennius, that Cunedda lived until about 550! It is a strange manipulation of figures which Skene rightly dismisses as “very inconclusive,” *F.A.B.*,

<note>^1 It is, of course, absurd to suppose that this poem is a “satire”; and there is no more reason for supposing that *trawsganu* [cross-song] is a “satirical song” than there would be for taking *traws-gynghanedd* to mean a “satirical cynghanedd.”</note>

<summary>This page discusses the historical and chronological difficulties in attributing the poem "Marwnad Cunedda" to Taliesin, given that Cunedda lived long before the poet’s time. It critiques previous attempts by scholars like Stephens to reconcile this timeline by adjusting historical dates.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the authenticity of the poem "Marwnad Cunedda," specifically focusing on D.W. Nash's critiques and his misunderstandings of the Welsh language.</meta>

<header>Taliesin.</header>
<page-num>203</page-num>

ii, 418. Nash suggests another explanation (*Taliesin*, p. 85):

> It is, however, possible that this poem may originally have been a production of the sixth century. There would be a reason why one of the bards who served the pleasures of the court of Maelgwn Gwynedd should have selected as a subject for his poetry the praise of the renowned ancestor of that chieftain.

This suggestion overlooks the fact that it must at any time have seemed somewhat unreal to mourn the death of a person who has been in his grave for more than a century. As Nash admits the possibility of the poem’s genuineness only to reject it, it seems strange that he did not focus on the apparent anachronism as proof of his case: Taliesin mourning Cunedda is just the sort of mistake that a medieval fabricator would be likely to make—it is, in fact, a mistake that the medieval copyist did make. Nash’s argument is, however, quite different: he takes *canonhydd* to mean “according to the canons,” so that the line which contains it must be late, and continues—

> Like the rest of its class, the piece exhibits no marks of antiquity in its language or sentiments; on the contrary, it is smoother and more polished than many other pieces in the same collection.

That a person who is so ignorant of Welsh as to translate *i gilydd* as “the retreat,” and to miscopy *ei ofn ai arswyd* ‘his fear and dread’ as *ei ofn ai arfwyd*, translating it as “fear of his arms,” should take it upon himself to dictate the rules regarding marks of antiquity in the language is a piece of impudence which is unfortunately not unique in the criticism of these poems. Every Welsh-speaking child understands *i gilydd* means ‘each other’; yet it is a phrase that goes back to Primitive Celtic, for it is found not only in Breton (*e gile*), but also in Irish (*a chēle*). Thus the phrase, though still repeated by us every day, was used by our ancestors more than a thousand years before the time

--- Page 214 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of the poem "Marwnad Cunedda," refuting earlier claims that it lacks marks of antiquity and detailing the failures of previous translators to understand its difficult Welsh text.</meta>

<header>Taliesin.</header>
<page-num>204</page-num>

of Taliesin. But this poem does not contain many phrases that are easily understood; so far from showing “no marks of antiquity,” it shows more than most. The text is more corrupted than that of the majority of the songs to Uryen; and the poem contains a relatively larger number of rhymes of the early type. Professor Rice Rees, in his *Essay on the Welsh Saints*, p. 114 fn., regards it as “perhaps the earliest specimen of Welsh poetry still existing”; it was composed, he suggests, not by Taliesin, but by an earlier bard of the same name! Its difficulty is such that it has entirely baffled the translators: Stephens’s version is mostly wild guesswork; Nash’s I have already spoken of—it is characteristic of him to talk about the “sentiments” of a poem which is entirely beyond his comprehension; Robert Williams is content, as he usually is in cases of difficulty, with producing mere nonsense. Dr. Evans translates his own revision of the poem, which is not even in the meter of the original, and in which, as usual, half the “<term>cymeriadau</term>” are violated.

The poem laments the death of a representative of the house of Cunedda who has been killed in a fratricidal war with his relatives of the clan of Coel Godebawc. Cunedda’s father was Edern; his mother is said to have been Gwawl, daughter of Coel; in the poem the descendants of Cunedda are called Edyrn, and the descendants of Coel, Coeling or Coelyn. In the original form of the poem the dead prince was referred to by name at least three times and by lineage twice. Taking the latter first, these five references are as follows: (1) In line 43 of the poem he is called *mab Edern*, 70·11; this is literally true of Cunedda, and if it is the original reading it would convince the medieval glossator or copyist that this obscure poem which names Cunedda several times must be an elegy on him. But in poetry, and even the prose of antiquity, “son” may

--- Page 215 ---
<meta>This page continues the linguistic analysis of the poem "Marwnad Cunedda," specifically examining terms for lineage and identifying the subject of the poem by analyzing meter and word choice.</meta>

<header>Taliesin. 205</header>
<page-num>205</page-num>

denote a "descendant," in the singular as well as in the plural; for example, Mephibosheth is called the "son of Saul," his grandfather (2 Sam. 19:24), so the expression is ambiguous. When the person meant is otherwise known, he may be called the "son" of his ancestor, just as every man is a son of Adam. The next reference clears up the ambiguity. (2) In line 21, the deceased prince is called *yscynyal Cunedaf* (69.21), which the scribe did not understand, for he wrote the *ys* separately; the "<term>cymeriad</term>" with *ascwrn* confirms the reading *yscynyal*. This is a participial form (e.g., *wybyr yscynnyal*, B.A. 15.11, "sky-ascending"); as a noun meaning "ascendant," it was replaced by *esgynnyδ*. It may seem strange that this should be used for a descendant; nevertheless, it is a fact. The degrees of descent are usually given as *mab* (son), *ŵyr* (grandson), *gorŵyr* (great-grandson), *goresgynnyδ* (great-great-great-grandson) (e.g., *Ancient Laws*, ii, p. 86); it is obvious that originally *ŵyr* and *gorŵyr* must have been followed by *esgynnyδ* and *goresgynnyδ*, and thus *esgynnyδ* would be the great-great-grandson. I do not wish to suggest that *yscynyal* is used here in this strict technical sense, though it would be exactly right, for we do not know how old the narrowed sense can be, and it is more likely that by *yscynyal Cunedaf* the poet meant simply "descendant of Cunedda." (3) Line 15 of the poem reads as follows (69.17):

->Marw Cunedaf a gwynaf a gwynit.<-

The end-rhyme is *-yδ*; the last words should therefore be *a gwynaf, a gwynyδ*, or rather *ae cwynaf, ae cwynyδ* (see note); this is made certain by the "<term>cymeriad</term>" and by a parallel passage. The line should be eight syllables long; as it stands, it is ten. It seems, therefore, that the name *Cunedaf* has been substituted for a name of one syllable. (4) The prince’s death is referred to in line 29 as *Aδoet hun* ("the death of *hun*") and (5) in line 50 as *difa hun o Goeling* ("that *hun* was slain by the Coeling"), *o* being the regular

<summary>The author provides a linguistic analysis of the poem "Marwnad Cunedda," arguing that terms like "mab" and "yscynyal" refer to a descendant rather than Cunedda himself. He highlights metrical errors where a one-syllable name was likely replaced by the three-syllable name "Cunedaf."</summary>

<keywords>Cunedda, Taliesin, Marwnad Cunedda, Welsh genealogy, yscynyal, esgynnyδ, cymeriad, Coeling, Welsh metrics</keywords>

--- Page 216 ---
<meta>This page continues the textual analysis of the poem, moving from the identification of Cunedda's descendant to a specific paleographical correction that identifies the subject as Rhun, son of Maelgwn.</meta>

<header>Taliesin.</header>
<page-num>206</page-num>

preposition used to introduce the agent after a verbal noun.¹ In Old Welsh, the demonstrative pronoun *hwn* ('this one') is written *hunn*; the final *-nn* is written quite regularly even in syllables that became unaccented later. Our scribe must have been quite familiar with *hunn*, which he writes as *hwn* (43:24); the fact, therefore, that he wrote *hun* here, not once but twice, proves that the original word was not *hunn*. In an Insular hand, in which the tall strokes are little longer than others, *h* and *r* were liable to be confused; and as *hun* makes no sense in either of these passages, it seems that it must be an error for *run*, and that the poem is an elegy on *Rhun*, the son of Maelgwn. This is in accordance with everything we know of *Rhun*; almost the only information that tradition has handed down to us about him is that he fought against the men of the North, and that Taliesin sang on one of his expeditions (see above, p. 47). Maelgwn is not mentioned, but there is clearly a gap in the poem, and the bard is mainly concerned with Rhun’s descent from Cunedda and Coel.

In the printed editions of the poem (I am, of course, not referring to the diplomatic text), the meter is disrupted, and internal rhymes are confused with end rhymes; none of the editors has guessed what the meter is or understands the nature and position of the rhymes. As these things must be determined before any verbal emendations are attempted, I insert notes on meter and rhyme before the text. It will be seen that the rhymes not only bear witness to the great antiquity of the poem but also throw further light on the early system of rhyming.

<note>¹ Difa is a verbal noun: when the verb is transitive, the agent of the verbal noun is placed before it with *i*, or after it with *o*, as I pointed out in *Y Geninen*, 1887, p. 181. The latter construction, though obsolete in the spoken language, is familiar from its frequent use by the translators of the Bible, such as *cyn canu o’r ceiliog* (before the cock crows), Matthew 26:34; for other references, see Pedr Hir’s *Key and Guide* [1911], pp. 26, 27.</note>

<meta>This page continues the technical analysis of the poem's structure, transitioning from the identification of the subject as Rhun to a detailed study of the meter and rhyme patterns used by the author.</meta>

<header>Taliesin.</header>
<page-num>207</page-num>

### THE METER AND RHYMES OF "MARWNAD RHUN".

The meter is "Byr a Thoddaid." This is composed of "cyhydedd fer" and "toddaid byr"—in other words, of lines of 8 syllables interspersed with couplets of 10 and 6 syllables. The end of the "toddaid byr" (the 10 and 6 syllable couplet) must rhyme with the group of 8-syllable lines to which it is joined; this "main rhyme" may also occur in the 10-syllable line, two or three syllables from the end, as in the first line of an ordinary englyn; however, this is optional. In the "toddaid byr," the short second line is connected with the first either by a correspondence of consonants at the end of the first and beginning of the second—as in *gwir* and *gwrawl* in lines 47–48—or by the end of the first rhyming with the third syllable of the second, as in *tydwed* and *wyneb* in lines 21–22 (-*ed* and -*eb* is considered a good rhyme). It is unlikely that any lines in this meter were originally too long or too short; lines of 9 syllables would confuse the meter with the one later called "gwawdodyn," which consists of 9-syllable lines mixed with "toddaid" couplets (10 and 9 syllables). Extra syllables are in all cases doubtful in 8-syllable lines, and probably the whole of "Gweith Gwen Ystrat" (lines 1–22, above p. 161) should have been regularized—for example, by substituting *udd* for *wledig* in line 2, or omitting *rwy* in line 5, as well as making the corrections suggested in the notes.

The main rhyme in the first fifteen lines is -*ydd* [-*yδ*], with variants -*yr* and -*yl* as follows: *kyfergyr*, l. 7; probably *myr*, l. 8; and *gyfyl*, l. 12.

These lines are followed by three lines with the rhyme -*un*, and two with the rhyme -*wm* or -*wrn*. I do not know if any minimum number of rhyming lines was required, but I cannot help thinking that a gap <note>lacuna</note> exists here, consisting of the last lines of the -*un* group and the first lines of the -*wm* group. It may be suggested that one of the rhyming words in the lost lines of the first group may have been the name *Rhun*.

Lines 21 to 28 have the rhyme -*wyd* [-*wyt*]; the irregularities here are due to textual corruption and are addressed in the notes.

Lines 29 to 38 have the rhyme -*af*, with variants -*agh* [-*aγ*] and -*aw*. It has been supposed that the fact that *Cunedda* rhymes here with -*af*, and is even spelled *Cunedaf* [*Cuneδaf*], proves that the poem is quite late. Dr. Evans says that there is no *Cunedaf*, but there is a *Cunedda* [*Cuneδa*], for which his authority is "B(ruts) 317"! But he says "the rhyme demands a final *v* here. Read *vyn cu nav*"! The explanation for the spelling *Cunedaf* in our text is that the scribe (or a commentator whose "corrections" he copied) was as ignorant as Dr. Evans of the old system of rhyming and believed that "the rhyme demands a final *v* here." As already stated above (p. 91), the oldest spelling of this name is *Cunedag*, which is seen in our frontispiece, line 14; this represents the pronunciation *Cuneddagh* [*Cuneδaγ*], in which the *gh* [*γ*] is the soft mutation of *g*.

<vocab>Marwnad Rhun, Byr a Thoddaid, cyhydedd fer, toddaid byr, chief-rhyme, englyn, gwawdodyn, Gweith Gwen Ystrat, Cunedda, Cunedaf, Cunedag</vocab>
