--- Page 218 ---
<meta>This page continues the linguistic analysis of the poem "Marwnad Rhun," moving from the discussion of dental rhymes to a detailed examination of the phonetic properties and rhyming conventions of soft spirants and nasals in Early Welsh poetry.</meta>

<header>Taliesin.</header>
<page-num>208</page-num>

...which was pronounced as a very soft *ch*; it shares the same relationship to *ch* that *v* does to *ff*, or that *δ* does to *th*. Due to the general loss of *γ* in Welsh, the name became *Cuned*a. But before it was lost, *-aγ* formed a suitable rhyme with *-av* (written as *-af*), and also with *-aw*.

The correspondence of *γ* and *w* is also seen in the next group, lines 39 to 44. The rhyme is *-ew*: but two of the rhyming words, *cyflew* and *anaelew*, are spelled this way because the scribe thought that "the rhyme requires a final" *w*. The words are *cyfle* and *anaele*, which must have been originally *cyfley* and *aeley*; thus *-eγ* rhymes with *-ew*.

An important result follows. We have seen in this and other poems that *δ* frequently rhymes with *r* or *l*; but we have found no example of *δ* rhyming with *f* (*v*). Again, in the above examples, we find *f* rhyming with *γ* and *w*, but not with *δ*. It seems, therefore, that the rhyming of <term>soft spirants</term> was not indiscriminate; they were instinctively divided into two classes: the clear (dental) *δ, l, r*, and the dull (labial and guttural) *f, w, γ*; and clear were rhymed with clear, and dull with dull. Examples of the former rhymes are very numerous; the latter are comparatively rare. We find, however, in the old poems in B.B. 18–27 the following rhymes, which confirm the above classification:—(1) clear: *reuveδ, argel*, p. 20; *merweryδ, evengyl*, 22; *glythwyr, dievyl*, 26;—(2) dull: *gouchaf, daγ* (of course spelled *da*) 24; *tangδef, arwyreγ* (spelled *tagde, arvere*) 25: see *tangδef*, B.A. 26·14, *arwyre* B.T. 56·14. The distinction does not apply to <term>explosives</term>, for *b, d, g* are rhymed with each other indiscriminately.

The last rhyme-group in the poem, lines 45 to 50, has the rhyme *-yng*, with the variant *-ynn* in *unbyn*, line 48. The spelling *-ing* in *dieding*, line 45, and *Coeling*, line 50, represents the medieval and modern modification of *-yng*. A <term>double nasal</term> can correspond to any combination of consonant and nasal because the nasal sound predominates; thus *lwm* (i.e., *lwmm*, for *m* is always double) rhymes with *ascwrn* in lines 19–20. This is why the third-person plural of the verb so often ends in *-yn, -an*, etc., in these poems; it is not because it was pronounced *-yn* or *-an* when the poems were written, but because *-ynt* and *-ynn* formed a suitable rhyme. Late copyists, who themselves dropped the *-t*, naturally imagined that the bard had also dropped it. A good example of nasal rhymes occurs in 41·3·4, where all the following are rhymed together: *dybyδynt, brynn, ronynn, mechteyrn*. In line 13 of our poem, we find *Etwyn* and *Coelyn* rhyming; the *wy* must be *ir* misread as *ui* (one of the most common errors, see above p. 164, lines 4–6), and the rhyme is *Edyrn* and *Coelyng* (the name *Edwin* rhymes with *breenhin* B.B, 75·7). The *-yng* of *Coelyng* is probably for *-ygn* from *-ikni*, the plural of *-iknos*—the <term>Celtic</term> suffix used to form <term>patronymics</term>, as seen in the Gaulish *Toutissicnos*.

<summary>This page provides a linguistic analysis of Welsh rhyme schemes, specifically distinguishing between dental and labial/guttural spirants and explaining the phonetics of nasal rhymes. It also discusses the historical evolution of specific names and suffixes, such as Cunedda and Celtic patronymics.</summary>

'son of Toutissos'; the plural occurs in the North Italian Todi inscription: *Tanotaliknoi* 'sons of Dannotalos'. ## THE DEATHSONG OF RHUN.

1 I am Taliesin, ardent in song;
I rejoice in Baptism,
Baptism, the grace of the fullness of the Lord.

Between hill and sea and river
5 The strongholds of Cunedda are shaken
In Durham and Carlisle:
The conflict of kinsfolk shakes them.

Like the surge over the seas, wave <note>devouring</note> wave,
The brave devour each other,
10 For war has been waged in the land,
Like the sigh of the wind among ash trees;
Cousin fights his fellow <gloss>relative</gloss>—
Edern’s sons and the Coeling, their kinsmen.
The bards adorn (?) <note>their</note> ingenious conceits:
15 It is the death of Rhun that I mourn and shall mourn.

Mourned is <note>he who was our</note> bulwark <note>and</note> stronghold—fearless,
Peerless, matchless;
In the depths (?) of <note>his?</note> deep wound, matchless.
. . . . . . . . . .

<header>Taliesin.</header>
<sig>P 2</sig>

His answer to insult (?) was hard and short,
<margin>20</margin>He was harder toward an enemy than a bone.

Descendant of Cunedda, until the cutting of the sod <note>of his grave</note>
->His honor was kept;<-
A hundred times before he was laid on a bier,
He joined battle with the men of Bernicia;
<margin>25</margin>There was sung, out of fear and dread of him,
A song of woe before a portion of earth became his covering.
A horde like a pack of hounds around a thicket beset him—
->Cowardice is worse than death!<-

By the death of Rhun, I mourn the fall
<margin>30</margin>Of the court and circle of Cunedda.
For the tide of the sea, for the salmon
Of the salt water, for herds and abundance, I shall fail.
The song of the bards that they sing, I will sing,
And the meters they count, I will count;
<margin>35</margin>There are numbered as princely generosity
A hundred steeds such as Cunedda <note>gave</note>.
He gave me of his milk cows in summer,
He gave me horses in winter,
He gave me sparkling wine and oil,
<margin>40</margin>He gave me a guard of serfs against ill omen.
He was a brave attacker in an encounter—
Ruler, chieftain with the appearance of a lion.
The land of the enemy would be ashes before the son of Edern
->Before the overthrow of Edern's sons.<-

<margin>45</margin>He was brave, fearless, generous—
Openhanded to many requests.
He bore his shield in the very forefront,
<note>Though</note> his captains were brave.
It is the fall of a noble king;
<margin>50</margin>It is a tragedy that Rhun was slain by the Coeling.

### NOTES ON “MARWNAD RHUN”.

Translations: Stephens, reprinted in *The Gododin*, 362; Nash, *Taliesin*, 82; Robert Williams, *F.A.B.*, i, 257; Dr. Evans, II, 121.
Line 1.—I consider *gwawt* to be the phrase *arguaut (ar wawt)* which the scribe has repeatedly miscopied; see pages 137–138 above.
Line 2.—*Ry godolaf*; compare *ry golychaf* 64·2, page 138 above. The consonant structure *gwawd|godoli* implies a *d*, not *ð*. *Godoli* ‘to rejoice in’ : *Godolei gleðyf e gareð, godolei lemein e ryvel* B.A. 12·20, ‘He rejoiced in

--- Page 222 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological analysis of the poem "Marwnad Rhun," specifically focusing on lines 3 through 9.</meta>

<header>Taliesin.</header>

...the sword of his vengeance (?), he rejoiced in leaping into war'; *O winweith a meddwieth dygodolyn* in the same place 15.9, *godolei o heit meirch* 37.20. Irish *tol* 'will, desire'.—Baptism = Christianity, see page 173.

Line 3.—The line is a syllable too long in the manuscript. Probably <term>eidolydd</term> is an error for <term>Dofydd</term> [the Lord]; this is suggested by the line *Nis rydraeth ryfeddau (cyfoeth) rhwyddau Dofydd*, B.B. 27: 'Not [all the] wealth of the Lord's works can relate it' <note>specifically, what will be done on judgment day</note>, where <term>cyfoeth</term> is an obvious gloss on <term>ryfeddau</term>, confirming the old meaning of 'abundance' <note>see note above, page 178</note>. <term>Rhwydd</term> as a noun (originally 'course') survives only as an abstract noun suffix, and in the phrase *Duw'n rhwydd!* ('God prosper [you]'). The corresponding form <term>riadh</term> in Scottish Gaelic means 'interest'. Perhaps 'produce' would be close to the meaning (cf. *rhwyddheu, rhwydd*, B.A. 29.19, 22), and as applied to the Deity, 'blessing' or 'grace'. <term>Ryfeddau</term> means 'abundance' or 'fullness'; compare: "From his fullness we have all received, and grace for grace" (John 1:16). I am unable to explain the error of <term>eidolydd</term> for <term>Dofydd</term>, unless there was some confusion in the copy between <term>ryfeddau Dofydd</term> and <term>ryfeddau elfydd</term> ('the fullness of the earth'), which seems to have been a common phrase; compare *remedaut elbid* in the Juvencus Codex (F.A.B., ii, p. 1). Although <term>eidolydd</term> appears to be a misreading here, it seems to be a real word, as it occurs again, spelled <term>eidyolyd</term> in 74.16, though Silvan Evans states that "the only known instance of its occurrence" is here (69.10).

Line 4.—<term>a hallt</term>, see above page 68, footnote.

Line 5.—The line is a syllable too long; <term>creisserydd</term> is a ghost-word; the translators take it to mean 'burner' (from *crasu*, 'to bake'!), except Dr. Evans, who makes it 'crosier-bearer'! It must be a disyllable, and it seems possible that it represents the scribe's bungling of <term>ceirid</term> (that is, <term>ceyrydd</term>). The medieval plurals of *caer* <note>fort or city</note> are *caereu, ceyrydd*, and *caeroedd*; and as they are all re-formations, it is impossible to say that any one is older than the others.

Line 6.—See pages 61 and 58–59 above.

Line 7.—<term>kyfat-wt</term>. The error is partly due to the line-division. The word must be a disyllable; and <term>cyfnawt</term> satisfies the rhyme, meter, and sense; regarding <term>nawt</term>, see page 143 above.

Lines 8, 9.—The couplet is two syllables short, and there is no catchword at the end of the first line; a word with the consonants of <term>llupawt</term> would most likely be some form of this word itself. The verbal noun used participially is possible and accounts for the omission, because to a scribe who understood little of meter or subject matter, it might seem to be an error of repetition in the copy (as such metrical correspondences often seem to Dr. Evans). <term>Kyfan</term> should, doubtless, be <term>cyfun</term>, a form used several times in the poem. <term>Llupaw</term> is perhaps a derivative from the Latin *lūpus*; compare the English *to wolf*.

<header>Taliesin.</header>

Line 10.—*ywhel* is the scribe’s transliteration of *iuhel*, which is undoubtedly his misreading of *ribel* (that is, *ryvel*), the first stroke of the *r* being mistaken for *i* which was often produced below the line.

Line 12.—*kefynderch|yn y glin* (the *i* or *u* begun as *o*). There seems to be a line-division error, and incorrect copying of what the scribe failed to understand. It is difficult to amend the line; but when it is remembered that *ch* is generally a transliteration of *c*, the suggested emendation will not seem improbable. Although not so close to the MS reading, *kyvygit* ‘fights’ is perhaps more likely, owing to its echo in *kyvyl*, than *kynnygn* ‘opponent’, which is also a syllable short. That *kyfach* is a gloss on *kyfyl* seems evident.

Line 13.—*Etwyn*; *ir* misread as *ui*, see the note on rhyme above. *Edyrn* seems to be used as an adjective meaning ‘heavy’ by the late medieval bards; as in *baich edyrn o bechodeu* <note>a heavy burden of sins</note>, *Myv.* 313a 18; but this use does not seem to be early. In B.A. 4.17 there seems to be a reference to the war between the Edyrn and the sons of Coel Godebawc:

> Edyrn diedyrn amygynt dir
> A meib(yon) Godebawc, gwerin enwir.

The plural verb *amygyn* ‘defended’ shows that *Edyrn* is plural; *diedyrn* is obviously *diedyng* (*dieðyng*) made to “rhyme” with *Edyrn*; as a matter of fact, it rhymes well enough without the scribe’s emendation, and we have here another example of nasal rhymes *-yrn*, *-yng*, *-ynt*. We can translate the couplet then—

> ‘The generous Edyrn defended [their] land
> Against the sons of Godebawg, faithless host.’

Line 14.—Perhaps *gwiscant* is an error for *gwëant* (Old Welsh \*<term>guegant</term>) ‘they weave’; <term>canonhyð</term> is probably the word transcribed <term>kallonyð</term> in 41.19: “A ŵyr kerð gelvyð, py gêl kallonyð”, ‘he who knows a cunning song, why does he conceal his wisdom?’ We have found that the scribe sometimes copies *li* as *n*; here he seems to have read *ll* as *n*; in that case the word is a derivative of *call* <note>wise or cunning</note>.

Line 15.—*a gwynit*; the rhyme shows that the ending should be *-yð*; *a gwynaf a gwynyð* has an exact parallel in the first line of “Anrec Uryen”, *Y Cymmrodor*, vii, 125;

> Gogyvercheis, gogyfarchaf, gogyferchyð,
> ‘I have greeted, I greet, I shall greet.’

In my *Grammar*, p. 333, vi, I have shown that *-yð*, which became the ending of the 2nd person singular present, would probably be common to all three persons originally in accented verbs, and I referred to traces of it in the 3rd person. These two examples seem to be clear cases of its use in the 1st person, in both cases future, as opposed to the present in *-af*. Another example, hitherto unexplained, of *-i* for old *-yð*, used in the 1st as

<header>Taliesin.</header>

well as the 2nd person singular, also future, occurs in *Mi ae guaredi, a thi ae gueli*
B.B. 94·5 ‘I will deliver them, and you shall see them’. The
relative *a* should probably be *ae* with the radical; see above p. 189.

Line 16.—*tewδun* should probably be *tew-δin* ‘thick fort’; it may
be intended for a <term>proest</term> with *-un* of the other lines, as it is unlikely
that the *i* was rounded to *u* by the *w* in Early Welsh as it seems to
have been later after *u* in *Creuδun*. But as noted above, a rhyme is
not absolutely necessary in this position. Note the rhyme *diarchar*,
*dychyfal*, connecting the two lines; see p. 207, ll. 11-13.

Line 17.—*dy-* with spirant, negative. We are wholly dependent
on the scribe here, as *i* and *y* are not distinguished in Old Welsh.
The prefix is *di-*, and there is no reason to suppose that it might not
be followed by *ch* (for the usual *g*) like *dy-*, *ry-* and *go-*; e.g. *gochlyt*
next to *goglyt*, etc. The initial following these prefixes was sometimes
doubled in British; the *ch* represents *cc*.

Line 18.—*dyfynveis* should be three syllables: it also occurs in
*dwvyn dyfynveis* B.A. 29·15, and in the singular as *diẏnvas* (3 syllables) in
“Anrec Uryen”, l. 14 (*Y Cymmrodor*, vii, 125). Possibly this spelling
supplies a clue to the formation.

Line 19.—*cwdedawd*; the suggested emendation assumes *gurte*
was misread as *cudet*.

Line 21.—There is no reason for *gatwet* where the rhyme requires
*gatwyt*. The scribe misread *i* as *e* in several words in this poem,
e.g. *dwet* line 26, *veδei* line 43.

Line 24.—*Dychludent*; *irc* was read as *lut*, and *ei* was read as *-et* and transcribed
*-ent* (the 3rd person plural ending is *-ynt*, not *-ent*, in the imperfect).

Line 25.—*ef* is a scribal insertion which makes the line too long;
likewise in lines 41, 45, 47.

Line 26.—*oergerδ*; *gerδet* for *gerδ* is clearly a scribal error. The
use of *oer* for ‘sad, painful’, etc., survived in poetry down to the
16th century and later.

*dwet*: the scribe took *cluit* for *duet*; the confusion of *d* and *cl* is
common, see above p. 165: *e* for *i*, see the note on line 21.

Line 27.—*ebrwyt*, apparently an error for *erchwys* ‘pack’, possibly
written *ehruis*, and read *ebruit* by the scribe.

*gweinaw* makes the line too long, and is meaningless. A verb in
the 3rd person singular aorist is required, which should be a monosyllable with
the initial of *gwaeth*; *gwant* ‘struck, attacked’, satisfies the conditions.

Line 28.—*aδwyt* seems to be an oblique case, after (*n*)*oc*, of *aδoet*
cf. *yn ẏ aδwyt* ‘at his death’ 43·13, *rac aδwyt*, *F.A.B.*, ii, 235.

Line 29.—*Aδoet*. The use of abstract and verbal nouns without
prepositions in an oblique case of cause survived in poetry; an exact
parallel to the construction here is seen in the following couplet from

# Taliesin.

The elegy on Ieuan ap Hywel Swrdwal by Hywel Dafydd, written about 1475 (Pen. MS. 100, f. 125):

->Marw Ieuan aeth fy mron i<-
->Mor oer â thrum Eryri.<-

> “By the death of Ieuan my breast has become
> As cold as the ridge of Eryri.”

*dimyaw*, which seems to be a late denominative meaning "to annihilate" (from *dim*), is perhaps substituted for *dymhun* (rhyming with *Run*), which appears to mean "overthrow"; see the note on line 49.

Line 30.—*am grys*. Rhys, in *Celt. Brit.*, 1884, p. 119, says that “the cognate Irish *criss* always meant a girdle,” and suggests that *crys* is a girdle here; but there is no evidence that *crys* or its Breton equivalent had that meaning. As the meter requires a disyllable, there can hardly be any doubt that the correct reading is *gwregys*. Pedersen explains this as **gwe-grys*; even if this is correct, the Breton *grouis* shows that the metathesis is too old to allow us to assume that *grys* is simply an error for *we-grys*. Another explanation of *gwregys* is assumed by Walde under the entry *rῑca*.—The “girdle” was undoubtedly, as Rhys suggests in the cited passage, the emblem of authority of the <term>Gwledig</term>, representing the gold belt which was the badge of office of the <term>Dux Britanniarum</term>.

Lines 31, 32.—The scribe seems to have jumped from one *am* to the other and written the second phrase first; he then wrote the second, and, not perceiving the rhyme which had been thus misplaced, neglected to insert the usual marks for transposition. The word *hydyrver* ("tide") hardly gives a satisfactory sense and may be a misreading.

*fwrn*; it is clear that “furnace” makes no good sense here; *swrn* is suggested by *am swrn am gorn Kuhelyn*, B.A. 26:13; *swrn* means a “great quantity or number”; compare L. G. Cothi, 459, line 20 of the poem.

Line 33.—*a ogon a ogaf* for *a wodon a wodaf* (*w* is generally lost before *o*, as in the prefix *gwo-*); the scribal error consists of the common misreading of *t* as *c*, the original form of the verbs being probably *guotont* and *guotam*, from a verbal noun *guotim* derived from *guaut* (“song”), like *noddi* from *nawdd*, etc.

Line 34.—The analogy of line 33 shows that *refon* contains the error so often made in these lines of reading *i* as *e*; the scribe made the same mistake in *rim*, which he ought to have transliterated as *rif*; but he read the *i* as *e*, and the *m* as *ill*, and so took the word to be *ereill*. The original reading was probably *hai rim*, that is *ae rif*. Possibly *rif* means “rhyme,” and a play on the word was intended.

Line 35.—*Ryfedawr*; probably *rifedor*, with *-edor* being the strictly correct medieval form of the passive ending usually written *-etor* (Grammar, 334).

# Taliesin.

*ervlawδ*. The dictionaries' explanation of *blawdd* as 'activity' and as an adjective 'active' can be traced back to an incorrect guess by William Llŷn; see Dr. Davies, under the same word. As the word may be the exact phonetic equivalent of the Sanskrit *mūrdh-án-*, it occurred to me several years ago that it might be the same word, meaning 'head, leader, chief', and so on. The examples provided by Silvan Evans, which completely contradict the meaning he assigns to them, seem to confirm this: *Ef vlawδ kyfrieu* 'he is the chief of his fellow-princes'; *Blaidd blaengar, blawdd trydar trais*, where *blawdd* is obviously in apposition with *blaidd blaengar* ('forward wolf'); *tarw trin, an (? read ar) vyδin blawdd*, 'leader of an army' immediately followed by *arbenic llu* 'chief of a host' (B.B. 97·13). Turning to the compounds, under *aerflawδ* we find *aervleiδ, aervlawδ teyrneδ* 'battle-wolf, battle-leader of kings', and *nawδ aerflawδ aerfleiδ*; under *cadflawdd* again, *Cof cadflawδ a'm cawδ a'm carai* 'the memory of a battle-chief who loved me grieves me'; what sense did Silvan Evans imagine "battle-tumult" made in that line, or in his next example? *Dos . . . ar gatvlawδ* 'Go to the battle-chief'—where the bard addresses a messenger whom he is sending to Llywelyn ap Madawc, *Myv.* 281a. In the B.T. we have *uδ tra blawδ* 63·4, where *blawδ* is an adjective. Therefore, I have translated *ervlawδ* as 'princely'.

*anaw*. Mr. Ifor Williams has demonstrated that this word does not mean 'muse, music', and so on, as the dictionaries claim, but rather 'bounty, largess', etc., in *Gemau'r Gogynfeirdd*, 1910, pp. 100-2.

Line 36.—The line has an extra syllable, and *kyn* should be omitted; *kymun* is the Old Welsh *cimun* partly transliterated; it should be *kyfun*, a word also used in line 8, and compounded in lines 17 and 18. The repetition of a word is a noticeable feature in some of these poems; this is why I believe the emendation on page 172, line 3 above is not improbable; see the note on page 174. The meaning of *kyfun* here is 'like, similar to', as in *dy-chyfun* 'matchless', line 17; compare *cimun idaw* 'an equal to him', B.A. 38·14.

Lines 37-40.—*Rymafei*. In B.B. 9·1 we find *De-us, re-en, rymawy awen*; this contains an extra syllable, and the facsimile appears to show that an attempt was made to delete the *y*, which would result in *rymaw awen*; thus *rymaw* seems to mean 'grant' or 'grant me', treating the *m* as the infixed pronoun 'me': 'grant me a muse'. In B.T. 3·12 we have *rymawyr dy weδi* followed by *rymgwares dy voli*, which we may interpret to mean 'may [my] prayer to Thee be granted to me', and 'may praise of Thee save me'; the latter, which is certain, confirms the former. In 4·2 occurs *rymawyr ym pat[er] ym pechawt*: the first *ym* is an unconscious repetition of the *-m-* and makes the line too long; the meaning seems to be 'may [my] prayer be granted to me in sin'. Finally, *Reen, rymawyr titheu kerreifant om kareδeu*, 35·22; here *titheu* seems to be an error for *inheu* supplementing the *-m-*; for 'may there be

<meta>This page continues the philological notes on the poems of Taliesin, moving from general terminology to specific line-by-line analysis of omens and word stems.</meta>

# Taliesin.

The phrase "given me" cannot have "thou" as its subject, as the subject is "forgiveness of my sins." It seems we have only two forms surviving in prayers: *rymaw* "grant me"; and *rymawyr* (which equals *rymaw-wyr*), a present subjunctive passive (properly impersonal) meaning "may there be given to me." These are the parts of the verb mostly used in prayer; compare *Dyro inni heddyw* ("Give us today") and *Gwneler dy ewyllys* ("Thy will be done"). Although the evidence is confused by misreadings because the phrase is obsolete, it seems to justify the assumption that *rymafei* stands for *ry-m-awei*, meaning "he gave me."

Line 40.—*un trew*, literally "one sneeze." This was a bad omen; compare: "I hear one sneeze. . . . I will not believe an omen (<gloss>coel</gloss>); for it is not true (<gloss>certh</gloss>, see page 140 above): the Lord who created me will fortify me," from the *Black Book of Carmarthen* 82·4·6, regarding starting on a voyage. Superstitions connected with sneezing are widespread. The custom of saying "God bless you" to one who sneezes is well known; in ancient Greece, the salutation was "*Ζεῦ σῶσον*" ("Zeus save me"); among the Romans, it was "Salve" ("Health"); in India, it is "Live," to which the person who sneezed replies, "With you." Similar salutations were and are used among the Hebrews and Muslims (see Tylor, *Primitive Culture*, 1891, volume i, pages 100-1). It was variously regarded as a good or bad omen; see Liddell and Scott under the entry *\*πταίρω*. In India, "It is a bad omen, to which among others the Thugs paid great regard on starting on an expedition" (Tylor, page 101). Likewise, "in Celtic folklore, there is a group of stories turning on the superstition that anyone who sneezes is liable to be carried away by the fairies, unless their power is counteracted by an invocation, such as 'God bless you'" (Tylor, page 103). There is probably significance in the "one" sneeze: "The rabbis . . . say that before Jacob, men never sneezed but *once* and then immediately died," hence the "salutary exclamation" (I. D'Israeli, *Curiosities of Literature*, Routledge, 1893, page 48). See other references in Tylor, pages 97-104.

Line 41.—*ogressur*. There seem to be two stems: *cryss-* (as in *gwŷr a gryssyassant* in the *Book of Aneirin* 9·3·10, etc.), which seems to be allied to *cerdd-ed* ("to walk" or "to travel," with vowel gradation *er : ry*); and *gryss-*, connected with the Latin *gradior* and *gressus* ("to step"). As the form in the text may be a compound of the former with *gwo-* or *go-*, there seems no reason to amend it. The *e* may stand for *y* (original *i*); see the note on line 21.

*cyfle*. Here again, the literal meaning seems to give the required sense. The word now means "opportunity" or "convenience," but formerly it also meant "convenient place" (see Silvan Evans under this entry). The latter, which is the more primitive meaning, is clearly derived from "adjacent place" (*cyf-le*); hence probably *o gyfle* means "at close quarters."

Line 43.—*Lludwy*. That this is the Early Welsh sound is proved by the fact that it is the only possible original of the two modern forms: (1) North Walian and modern literary *lludw*; compare *Gronw* for *Gronwy*.

<meta>This page continues the philological notes on the poems of Taliesin, providing detailed linguistic analysis of specific word forms and grammatical structures in the text.</meta>

<page-num>218</page-num>
<header>Taliesin.</header>

etc.; (2) South Walian *lludy* (or *lludu*), compare *Mowddy* for *Mawddwy*: see *Grammar*, § 78, i (2), ii (1).

v\epsilon\delta\epsilon i; *e* for *y*, original *i*; see the note on line 21. The forms of the verb "to be"—*bydd*, *byddei*, *bu*, *oedd*, etc.—were not preceded by the relative particle *a* when they followed immediately after the complement, or (in other words) where they correspond to *yw* in the present tense; the relative is regularly used when it is itself the subject, thus *a vy\delta ei* corresponds to *sydd* in the present tense. Footnote 3 on page 120 above refers to these former cases.

*cywlat* ("adjoining country"), chiefly used as a "possessive" (or *bahuvrīhi*) compound meaning a "man of an adjoining country" or "an enemy"; here it appears to be used literally, as in the *Book of Aneirin* 31·18.

*Edern*, probably the old genitive *Edyrn* forming a perfect rhyme with *Edyrn* in the next line; though, of course, a "proest" or half-rhyme is possible.

Line 44.—*anaelew*. The word is *an-aele*; compare *aele* in Prydydd y Moch, *Myvyrian Archaiology* 207 a, Elidir Sais 240 b, and Gwalchmai 149 b; also *aele* and *anaele* in *Aneirin* 355 b. Dr. Davies attributes *anaeleu* to Dafydd Benfras, but that is a misreading for *anadleu* (220 b 35). *Anaeleu* in the *Book of Taliesin* 14·4 and *Myvyrian Archaiology* 191 b should be *aeleu* ("sorrows"), probably the plural of *aelaw*; *anaeleu* (315 b) is a compound of this. *Aele* may be from \**ad-leg*- (as it is probable that *d* might be treated this way before as well as after a front vowel) from the root \**legh*- ("to lie" or "lay"); compare Irish *do-lega* ("who destroys").

Line 45.—*di-e\δ-yng*; *e\δ-yng* uses the prefix *a\δ*- from \**ad*-, intensive; it has the same meaning as *cyf-yng*; *di*- is a negative.

Line 46.—*Amryfreu* ("manifold"): *Gwae gebydd o'i gabl amryffrau* (the variant reading *gyfrydd* is obviously a misreading), *Myvyrian Archaiology* 271 a, meaning "Woe to the miser because of his manifold iniquities"; with dissimilation, *r . . . l* for *r . . . r*: *Amryfleu donyeu dynyadon*, *Myvyrian Archaiology* 201 b, meaning "Various are the gifts of men"; used as a noun: *fruith a freu a fop amriffreu*, *Black Book of Carmarthen* 38·1, meaning "fruits and berries (?) and every diversity."

*agheu* (that is, *angheu*) would be spelled *ancou* in Old Welsh (it appears with that spelling in Old Breton; see Loth, *Vocabulaire*, 39); *archeu* would be spelled *arcou*. By merely misreading *r* as *n*, the latter would be converted into the former.

*dychyfyng* in meaning equals *di-gyfyng* (*Myvyrian Archaiology* 155 a 26, 164 a 10); see the note on line 17.

Line 47.—*Goborthi*: the *-i* is the old ending of the third-person singular imperfect, a variant of *-ei*, which is modern *-ai*.

*yman- | regorawl*. The most probable reading seems to be *yman ragorawl* ("in the foremost place" or "in the van"), since *rhagori* is used in this sense, as in *Ragorei, tyllei trwy vy\delta inawr*, *Book of Aneirin* 9·19 ("he went in the van, he cut his way through armies"). Whether the following word is *gwir* or *gwŷr* would not be indicated in the Old Welsh

spelling, in which both would appear as *guir*. The scribe evidently took it for *gwir*, and he may be right; for the meaning, compare English *very* from Old French *verai*, French *vrai*. The word connects in sound with *gwrawl* in the next line, but the metre requires a pause at the end of the long line so that *gwŷr gwrawl* is improbable—apart from the tautology "manly men."

Line 49.—*Dymhun* seems to mean "overthrow" or "fall." Gwalchmai has *Dymhunis ton wyrδ wrth Aberffraw* ("A green wave broke near Aberffraw"), repeated with variations in his "Gorhoffedd," *Myvyrian Archaiology* 144a. The prefix is probably *dym-* as in *dym-chwelyd* ("overthrow"); and the stem probably stands for **ch(w)un*, perhaps cognate with Middle Breton *c’houen* ("lying on one’s back"), Irish *fōen* (***sōen*) allied to Latin *supīnus* (Fick-Stokes, 305); *u* is the regular Welsh equivalent of Old Irish *ōe*. The author’s habit of repeating words makes it probable that this was the word used in line 29 to rhyme with *Rhun*.

*chyfatcun*: the *h* may be taken to be the scribe’s. It would be too risky to amend this to *catcun* ("war-lord"), as *amatgun* occurs in 2·4·10, though the meaning there is obscure. I take *cyf-at-cun* to be an adjectival compound of *cun* ("lord, prince"), and therefore to mean "noble" or "princely." The construction would also admit of a substantive in this position, as in *cawr o ddyn* ("a giant of a man"); see the next note.

*thal gwin*: the *h* as before. It has been shown above, on page 209, that *gwin* cannot possibly be the rhyming word. As the scribe constantly misreads *ir* as *ui*, we can be fairly certain that his *gwin* represents *girn* in his copy; it cannot be an error for *gwyn* ("white"), for that would be *guinn* in the copy, and could not be mistaken for *gwin*. Now *girn* can hardly be anything but the last syllable of the word *teyrn*, which would have been written *tegirn* or *tigirn* in the copy; he read it as *tlguin*, and supplied the *a* to make it *tâl gwin* (which Nash alone of the translators accepts as the reading). The modern *o* introducing a noun dependent on an adjective, as in *truan o ddyn*, was *a* in Medieval and Old Welsh (see above, page 129, lines 5-8); hence *cyfatcun a deyrn* ("a noble prince" or "king"), literally "a noble <note>one</note> of a king."

Line 50.—*Kamda* seems to be an error for *kamdra*, literally "crooked turn," hence "ill turn" or "calamity." But it may be that the original was *cam bu* (that is, *cam fu*) and that *b* was misread as *d*, and *u* as *a*. The meaning would be substantially the same.

*diva o*: see above, page 206 and footnote.

*Coeling*: "Whether Coeling is a proper name or not, is unknown," says Nash, *Taliesin* 84. It was unknown to him, and apparently to everybody else at that time, and to Dr. Evans in our own days. Stephens and R. Williams render it "believer," Nash "testimony," and Dr. Evans "superstitious (dread)"! There is, of course, no foundation of any kind for any of these renderings: *-yng* is not a suffix that forms

I have not bothered to look up Edward Davies’s translation in his *Claims of Ossian*, but Nash says that he translates the last line as “the race of a colonial city”! None of the translators understood the force of the *o* before *Coeling*; Stephens thought it introduced the object! Davies renders it as “of,” while the others use “from.” Each of them is attempting to translate with the help of a dictionary from a language whose grammar they do not know. Stephens takes *dymhun* to be *dim hun* (“no sleep”); Nash understands it as *dymunaf* (“I desire”). Dr. Evans’s *Cysc, vad gun* (“sleep, dear lord”) for *cyfatcun* shows that he is more ignorant of Welsh grammar than the quarryman poet, Glan Padarn, the author of “*Cwsg, fy anwylyd di-nam*.” Nash renders the last two lines in this way:

> I respectfully request a share of the banquet, and a recompense in wine.
> This has been with difficulty restored from testimony.

With this triviality, which must have seemed plausible enough to those with no knowledge of the language, and absolutely convincing to Nash himself, contrast the incoherent raving of Robert Williams:

> Sleepiness, and condolence, and pale front,
> A good step, will destroy sleep from a believer.

Did he think the poet was insane? Stephens’s translation is only slightly more sensible; and Dr. Evans alone understands his own. The rendering of these lines is typical of the whole: <term>obscurum per obscurius</term>. I refer to it only to show that no value should be attached to judgments based on such an understanding of the poem as it reveals.

Many points of detail remain doubtful, as the notes show; most of these difficulties will undoubtedly be solved when all the old poetry has been systematically studied. But I believe it can be claimed that the general meaning of the poem is clear throughout. In the opening triplet, the bard declares that he is a supporter of the Christian cause, of which the house of Cunedda were the guardians, as the upholders of Roman traditions. Though Cunedda’s own name is British, those of his immediate ancestors were Latin: Edern (Æternus), Padarn (Paternus), and Tegid (Tacitus); and *Peisrudd* (“of the red tunic”), the epithet of his grandfather Padarn, probably referred,

<meta>This page continues the philological and historical discussion of Cunedda's titles, specifically how his role as "Gwledig" connects to Roman military authority and later English concepts of kingship.</meta>

Cunedda’s title of “<term>Gwledig</term>” was no doubt, in full, “<term>Prydein Wledic</term>” (23.21), which is clearly Welsh for “<term>Dux Britanniarum</term>”; and it is undoubtedly because his rule was “recognized as that of the <term>Gwledig</term> or perpetuator of the command of the <term>Dux Britanniarum</term>”² that he exercised his authority as overlord of the British kings. Later, “<term>Prydein Wledic</term>” was in turn translated into Old English as *Brytenwalda*, *Bretwalda*, etc., and Æthelstan’s “*Brytenwalda ealles ðyses iglands*” corresponds to “*rector totius huius Britanniae insulae*.”³ Although Maelgwn, Cunedda’s great-grandson, is not, as far as we know, actually styled “<term>Gwledig</term>,” it is evident that he retained something like his ancestor’s position. Gildas, who describes him as excelling the British kings not only in stature but in power, calls him *Insularis Draco*, “probably meaning by that,” as Rhys says, “the Dragon or Leader of the Island of Britain.”⁴ It is remarkable that in the next paragraph Rhys, speaking of “Maelgwn’s son and successor Rhûn” making war in the North, suggests that it was “probably

Rhonabwy asks the meaning of this and is told that the tall man is Rhun vab Maelgwn Gwynedd, whose privilege it is that everyone shall come and take counsel from him.<note>R. B. Mab., pp. 159-60.</note> “The privileged position held by Rhun in the story,” says Dr. Lloyd, “may well be an echo of a real predominance held by him as *gwledig* in succession to his father.”<note>*History of Wales*, pp. 167-8.</note> But apparently the sons of Coel, or some of them, rebelled against him: a couplet in the *Gododdin*, as we have seen, speaks of the Edyrn defending their land against the faithless sons of Godebawc. Our poem tells us of a war between kinsmen, the sons of Edern and the sons of Coel, in which *Run* was slain by the Coeling—Run, who was as generous as the hostile Gildas admits his father to have been.<note>A reference to Rhun fighting in the North is seen in B.T. 29.17: “the fortification (*mygedorth*) of Rhun frowns (*a wc*) between Kaer Rian and Kaer Riwc, between Din Eidyn and Din Eidwc”; the second and fourth names I cannot identify, but Kaer Rian must have been near Loch Ryan, and Din Eidyn is Edinburgh; between these lay the land of the Coeling. In the dictionaries, *mygedorth* is given as “funeral pile,” which is only the usual bad guess. The meaning is clear from B.A. 8.5: “ashen [spears] were sown from the fingers [literally four divisions] of his hand from the *mygedorth* of stone”; and R.B.M. 301.10: “the *mygedorth* of the host of Gwendoleu at Arderyð.”</note> With the death of *Run*, the bard laments the fall of the court and girdle of Cunedda, the *Gwledig*.

<meta>This page continues the analysis of the poem's subject, arguing that it commemorates Rhun ap Maelgwn rather than his ancestor Cunedda, aligning with the historical context of the sixth century.</meta>

<page-num>223</page-num>
<header>Taliesin.</header>

the nineteenth-century translators, and knew less of sixth-century history. He transcribed the dead king’s name twice as “*hun*”, and in one place expanded it to “Cunedda,” thereby making that line two syllables too long. Now, is it likely that the monosyllable *hun* represents Cunedda? Or that in the statement “By the death of *x* I mourn the fall of the court and girdle of Cunedda,” *x* is Cunedda himself? Is it not obvious that the meaning is that by his death something greater than himself has fallen—a great tradition inherited from an illustrious ancestor? And if there could be any doubt about the answer to this question, it is settled by the fact that a few lines earlier he is explicitly called the “Descendant of Cunedda.” Thus, when the poem is subjected to the criticism without which it cannot be understood at all, the “stumbling block” is found to be a landmark. The poem commemorates a war which could hardly have been fought either before or after the time of Taliesin, and laments the fall of the power of Cunedda in the person of his descendant Rhun ap Maelgwn, Taliesin’s contemporary.

The result of our survey of the historical poems is that, excluding allusions to mythological characters such as Brân, they relate to persons and events of the middle and latter part of the sixth century. Like the poems of the bards of all ages, they contain references to famous ancestors of the persons in whose honor they are sung: Ceneu ap Coel, the ancestor of Uryen; Cyngen, the ancestor of Cynan Garwyn; Cunedda, the ancestor of Rhun. The poet either addresses the princes to whom he sings directly, or speaks of them as contemporaries, living or recently deceased. He sings their praises in the manner which we know was customary then because it roused the anger of Gildas; but his eulogy is not a mere repetition of conventional phrases—it has charac-

The survey concludes that Taliesin's historical poems focus on real contemporary figures and their lineages rather than just mythological themes.</summary>

--- Page 234 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument for the historical authenticity of Taliesin’s poetry, transitioning from the specific analysis of his poems about the descendants of Cunedda to a broader assessment of his style and the evidence for a sixth-century origin.</meta>

<page-num>224</page-num>
<header>Taliesin.</header>

character and life. He mentions many battles, some fought in earlier generations; one or two he says he has himself witnessed, and the vividness of his descriptions in these cases stamps his *gweleis* ‘I saw’ as a true word and not a mere figure of speech. The poems are in a variety of meters, but there are phrases and turns of expression, and a certain delight in antithesis and repetition, which are characteristic of all, and indicate very clearly that they are the work of one man. He calls himself Taliesin. I submit that the reasonable conclusion is that the poems are the work of the bard of that name, who is stated in the “Saxon Genealogies” (our oldest and best authority) to have flourished in the time of Ida of Northumberland, 547-559, and whom “the universal Welsh tradition,” as Matthew Arnold says (see above p. 145 <note>footnote</note>), “attaches to the sixth century.”

Let us consider for a moment the only possible alternative—a twelfth-century origin is not a possible alternative, for the evidence is decisive that the poems were copied from a ninth-century manuscript. What does the assumption that they are fabrications of the ninth century imply? It implies that there lived in Wales at that time a man (or let us say men, if a conspiracy is more likely), who had learned a good deal about men and things in the sixth century, not only in Wales but in the North; who had developed in a remarkable degree the modern historical sense of the unity of time, and avoided anachronisms, especially references to later events; who was a great realist—mixed his pronouns to produce the effect of actuality, simulated emotions, drew vivid pictures of ancient battles; who was a rare poet and a great master of the Welsh language; and who applied his extraordinary gifts to forge poems to attribute to Taliesin.

Thomas Stephens, who was by far the greatest critic

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the authenticity of the poems of Taliesin and Aneirin, specifically addressing the views of nineteenth-century scholars like Zeuss and John Rhys regarding the transition of sixth-century content into medieval written forms.</meta>

<page-num>225</page-num>
<header>Taliesin.</header>

in the nineteenth-century study of old Welsh literature, and whose tendency is acknowledged to have been toward skepticism rather than belief, believed these poems to be genuine. It is true that he did not understand them very well; but he understood the proper names and could perceive the meaning of many passages, and his instinct in these matters was sensitive and sure. Zeuss, the founder of Celtic philology, and his editor Ebel, have no difficulty in accepting the possibility of verses by Aneirin and Taliesin surviving and being transcribed into medieval spelling, *Gram. Celt.*, 1871, p. 965. But, as we have seen, the scholars of the previous generation did not regard any such possibility as worthy of serious consideration. When Rhys says (see above p. 23) that the poems "date in some form or other, from the 9th century, if not earlier," he is not attacking the tradition, but only qualifying a statement that their language is medieval. He does not bother to deny a sixth-century origin, but speaks of "the poetry commonly assigned to the sixth century," and leaves it there. His argument is as follows (*Welsh Phil.*, 1879, pp. 138-9):

> As to the language of this poetry, it is generally not much older, if at all, than the manuscript on which it is written. I say the language, for the matter may be centuries older, if we may suppose each writer or reciter to have adapted the form of the words—as far as the reduction of the mutable consonants is concerned—to the habits of his own time, which one might well have done unintentionally, and so, perhaps, without changing much of the content. . . . The poems attributed to the <term>Cynfeirdd</term> (early bards) belong, as far as we are concerned now, to the Medieval period of Welsh, though the meter, the allusions, and the archaisms which some of them contain tend to show that they date in some form or other from the 9th century, if not earlier.

It is necessary for us to examine these words carefully. The words "the 9th century, if not earlier" inevitably convey the meaning, and are doubtless intended to mean, "the

<summary>This page analyzes the scholarly consensus of the late 19th century regarding the authenticity of early Welsh poetry, focusing on how 6th-century poems could have been preserved through linguistic updates into the medieval period.</summary>

--- Page 236 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of John Rhys’s dating of the Taliesin poems, arguing that the presence of archaic forms and historical allusions supports a much earlier origin than the 8th or 9th century.</meta>

<page-num>226</page-num>
<header>Taliesin.</header>

"the 9th century, or possibly the 8th." The reasons for attributing poems that appear in a medieval form to that period are threefold: meter; allusions; archaisms. It is not a logical order, for a reason concerning content is sandwiched between two reasons concerning form. Let us take them in the following order: (1) outer form, or meter; (2) inner form, or language—archaisms; (3) content—allusions. (1) Some of the meters were obsolete by the twelfth century, and all are old; but what possible reason can there be for supposing that they are not older than the eighth century? Did not Gildas's *<term>praecones</term>* have meters? What historical or traditional reason is there for the assumption that there was a prolific invention of meters in the eighth century? (2) *Archaisms* may prove that a composition is old; but how can they determine a limit to its age? Do archaisms prove that it is not older than the period just before they became obsolete? (3) *Allusions*. The allusions in the poems we have studied above refer to men and things of the sixth century or earlier. How can allusions to men and things of the sixth and earlier centuries prove that the poems were composed in the eighth or ninth? It is clear that the two reasons of form are not arguments *against* the tradition—they support it more than they oppose it; and the reason of content is decidedly in favor of the tradition, and entirely against Rhys's view. How could he draw such a conclusion from such premises? What made him fix on the ninth or possibly the eighth century? Had he any knowledge of any gifted poet or poets busily engaged in faking early poems around that time? None at all. He writes in his *Hibbert Lectures*, 1888, pp. 543-4:

> It is convenient to follow the long-established custom of speaking of certain Welsh poems as Taliesin's, and of a manuscript of the 13th century in which they are contained as the *Book of Taliesin*. These poems represent a school of Welsh bardism, but in reality we know nothing about their authorship.

<summary>The author challenges John Rhys's dating of the Taliesin poems to the 8th or 9th century, arguing that meter, archaisms, and 6th-century allusions actually support an earlier origin. He points out that Rhys himself admits the lack of evidence for authors "faking" these poems during the later period he suggests.</summary>
<keywords>Taliesin, John Rhys, Book of Taliesin, Welsh poetry, meter, archaisms, 6th century, Gildas, bardism, historical allusions</keywords>

--- Page 237 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of John Rhys’s dating of the Taliesin poems, specifically addressing his flawed linguistic assumptions regarding the transition of the Welsh language from an inflected to an uninflected state.</meta>

### Taliesin. 227

He knew nothing—he had no historical or traditional evidence for his statement. He made it because he saw that these poems are as old as anything in Welsh, and he believed that the Welsh language could not have existed in its uninflected form before the eighth century. This belief followed necessarily from his assumption that the language of the sixth century was identical to the fully inflected language of the first; but it has no other foundation. He himself admitted that this was an assumption he could not prove; see p. 28 above. Thus, when Rhys dismissed tradition and contemptuously ignored the considered view of Stephens—which was based on the facts within the poems—his own view rested on nothing but an unproved assumption.

He made this assumption based on a major error: he thought he had discovered the actual British speech of the sixth century in the archaic Irish of the Ogam inscriptions (*Welsh Philology*, 1879, p. 140). In the Latin inscriptions, he says, the names “have their endings Latinized”; but he believed that in the Ogams he had found the true British forms. He strongly maintained this erroneous view (*op. cit.*, pp. 154 ff.); and though he formally renounced it shortly afterward (*Celtic Britain*, 1884, p. 215), his statement about the poetry of the *Cynfeirdd* <note>The earliest Welsh poets</note> was made while he held that view and was dictated by it.

This can be clarified by taking a concrete example typical of the argument: he thought that *maqui* in the Ogams was the genitive form of the sixth-century version of the Welsh *mab* <note>son</note> (see *Welsh Philology*, pp. 155-156). He had also read the genitive *Brohomagli* on a stone dating not much later than the sixth century (ibid., p. 264). He concluded that Taliesin could not have written *mab Brochvael* (45.21)—even in its oldest spelling, *map Brochmail*—but must have written *maquos Brohomagli*, which ruins the meter! When he discovered his error, he did not abandon his conclusion,

<summary>The author critiques John Rhys's linguistic theory, which incorrectly dated early Welsh poetry to the 8th century based on the flawed assumption that 6th-century British speech was still fully inflected. Rhys's error stemmed from misidentifying archaic Irish Ogams as British speech, leading him to wrongly conclude that the meters of Taliesin were chronologically impossible.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the critique of John Rhys’s linguistic theories, specifically addressing his belief that the Welsh language underwent a sudden, late transformation, and introduces the views of the scholar Zimmer regarding the dating of early Welsh poems.</meta>

<header>Taliesin.</header>

for he still believed that, although *maqui* is Irish, the contemporary British form was *mapi*, which for the purposes of the argument amounts to the same thing. But there is no evidence for this at all, and it involves the absurd assumption that a new language was suddenly developed in Wales around the end of the seventh century, and also that an identical new language was suddenly developed in Brittany at about the same time. I believe he had considerably modified his views on this point in recent years—no scholar was ever more ready to revise his theories in the light of new facts. For example, in 1893 he made no comment on a statement by Dr. Evans in a work on which they collaborated (*Llyfr Llan Daf*, p. xlvi) asserting that a document written in Welsh dates back to Teilo’s time; and when he read the proof of pages 190-1 of my *Grammar* in 1912, in which it is argued that the loss of inflections had likely already occurred in the sixth century, he raised no objection.

Holding the view that the poems are spurious, it is not surprising that Rhys was never interested enough in them to make them the subject of sustained and serious study. He provided translations of some of them and parts of others, but he never systematically studied their prosody and vocabulary. Thus, although he had the insight to perceive that their meter, language, and content bore witness to their antiquity, he never fully grasped the true extent and force of the evidence he mentions.

Zimmer had his own reasons for asserting that the poems are even later fabrications. He analyzed the tract "Saxon Genealogies" and discovered very convincing proof that it was written toward the end of the seventh century, was expanded with interpolations, and was then copied by Nennius at the end of the eighth century (see above, p. 44). But he was unaware of the fact that a- may be

<meta>This page continues the critique of Zimmer's theories, specifically his argument that Nennius miscopied the name of the poet Neirin, which Zimmer used to claim that early Welsh poems were late forgeries.</meta>

In making what he no doubt believed to be a brilliant suggestion, Zimmer forgot that ‘and’ was usually *ha* before a consonant in Old Welsh, and it appears as *ha* in the very oldest piece of written Welsh (the *Surexit* memorandum noticed below). From the assumption of an error where there is no error, Zimmer deduced the further assumption that the Welshman Nennius had never heard of “Aneirin.” From this particular assumption, he deduced the general assumption that Welshmen generally had never heard of any of the sixth-century poets. From this egregious fallacy of arguing from a special case to a universal rule, he deduced the further assumption that the Welsh people only learned even the names of these poets from Nennius’s compilation, forgetting again that the Nennian form *Neirin* is not found in Welsh.<note>¹ Since the Welsh, by Zimmer’s supposition, only learned the name from Nennius, they must have received it in the form *Neirin*. It is found later among them as *Aneirin*; this is therefore a late form with an added *a-*, since the supposition that they obtained it from another source is excluded *ex hypothesi*. And this form, which is demonstrably late according to his own hypothesis, is the form which Zimmer assumes to be the original form misunderstood by Nennius!</note> From this last assumption, it follows that Neirin and Taliesin were first heard of in Wales at the end of the eighth century, that they became famous only during the ninth, and consequently that the poems attributed to them were forged in the ninth or tenth century at the earliest. Zimmer’s “emendation” is an ill-considered piece of tampering with a good text;<note>² I have always regarded it as a mare’s nest; see my note in *Y Beirniad*, 1911, p. 59. I believe no one would have accepted it for a moment if not for the exaggerated deference formerly paid to every suggestion made by a German scholar. Anwyl derived *Aneirin* from</note> but even

# Taliesin.

If it were sound, the superstructure built upon it is about as extreme an example as can be found of a pyramid balanced on its apex.

This view, conceived in error and later supported by preposterous fallacies, has been accepted without question and repeated by other writers. I have not accepted it myself; instead, when I have had occasion to refer to the <term>Cynfeirdd</term>, I have expressed the opinion that some of the poems attributed to them may be genuine, even though they were naturally modernized by copyists.¹ My study of the language resulted from an interest in its poetry. Approaching the subject from that perspective, I have been naturally impressed by the evidence found in the poems. The argument based on inscriptions was not conclusive; it was a mere hypothesis and could be fallacious. Rhys, who was the first Welshman to apply strict modern methods to the study of the language, was conscious of the importance of induction—the process of drawing inferences only from facts. This method yielded fruitful results in his hands, especially when compared to the unproductive philosophizing

<meta>This page continues the critique of the methodology used by earlier scholars (specifically referring to Rhys) regarding the authenticity of early Welsh poetry. It argues that over-reliance on physical evidence like inscriptions, while ignoring the value of tradition and the conservative nature of written language, leads to incorrect conclusions.</meta>

# Taliesin.

of the old etymologists. But perhaps he was more prone to regard as facts only those of the more tangible sort, and consequently to undervalue the importance of tradition. Tradition does not relate precise facts; however, he did not fully realize that tradition is itself a fact, not always to be dismissed by the hasty assumption that all men are liars. Nor could he be trusted in all cases to draw the correct inference from his facts. Inscribed stones are undoubtedly hard facts; but it does not follow that the inflected forms used in Latin and primitive Irish represented contemporary British speech. The rising and the setting of the sun are facts; but it does not follow that the sun spins in a helix around the earth. Facts may be correctly observed and wrongly interpreted. In the case of our poems, a wrong interpretation of certain facts—less justifiable than the assumption of geocentric astronomy—has been allowed to override the combined testimony of tradition and history, as well as the internal evidence of the poems themselves.

There are few inscriptions in Early, or even Medieval, Welsh—in fact, very little Welsh was ever carved in stone before the nineteenth century. Furthermore, the Welsh on a modern tombstone is not the local living dialect, but the language of the Bible, which was in some respects archaic even in the sixteenth century. For example, it preserves the final *-t* of the third person plural of verbs, which the spelling of the <term>Black Book</term> (*dygan*, *deuthan*, 2.5.8) shows was already becoming obsolete in the twelfth century. What false conclusions might a philologist of the distant future reach regarding the history of the language if he had only inscribed stones to rely on, and took no account of the fact that the written language may preserve ancient forms?

The earliest existing inscription in Welsh appears to be the one on the so-called “stone of St. Cadvan.” Westwood

He uses the lack of Welsh stone inscriptions and the archaisms of Bible Welsh to illustrate how physical evidence can be misinterpreted by philologists.</summary>

# Taliesin.

...thought the lettering belonged to the seventh or eighth century, *Arch. Camb.*, 1850, p. 95. Rhys examined the stone and confirmed the substantial correctness of Westwood's reading; to him, it remained "a crux"; *Arch. Camb.*, 1874, p. 243. See below, Appendix I.

The oldest piece of written Welsh is undoubtedly the *Surexit* entry in mixed Welsh and Latin on page 141 of the Book of St. Chad. It is the record of the settlement of a dispute concerning a piece of land, with the first witness being "Teliau." It certainly appears, on the face of it, to be what Dr. Evans—under the supervision of Rhys—stated it to be: "a copy of a document of Teilo's time," *Book of Llan Dav*, p. xlvi. If that is so, and it is highly probable that it is, then Rhys's hypothesis of a sixth-century British language is finally discarded; and the language of Teilo's time is the Welsh of the poems attributed to Taliesin. The matter is discussed below in Appendix II.

To argue from the absence of contemporary documentary evidence of Welsh in the sixth century to the non-existence of the language at that time is to commit the error of taking "the first attestation of a fact to be the date of the fact itself" (see p. 43). It is more—it proves too much; for by the same reasoning, neither British nor Welsh existed in the sixth century, which, of course, is absurd. There are no records of the early periods of languages; if philologists had been limited to actual records, there would have been no science of comparative philology. In the absence of documentary evidence, there are other data by which the date of the formation of the language can be approximately determined, just as distances that cannot be directly measured can be calculated with equal certainty if the calculation is correct. I have dealt with this evidence above on pages 27–34; I will only add here one point which has a bearing on the

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the dating of the Welsh language, focusing on how early orthography and sound changes (like soft mutation) prove that the language's development began much earlier than the oldest surviving written records suggest.</meta>

# Taliesin.

...transmission of our poems. The orthography of the ninth century is not something that would have been invented at that time; instead, it must have been preceded by a long literary tradition. As we have seen on pages 130–131, a <term>mutated consonant</term> was represented by its <term>radical</term> (original) form. The change that resulted in the Welsh <term>soft mutation</term> had already begun in the <term>British</term> language itself; this can be demonstrated with mathematical certainty, for the change is caused by vowels<note>I have simplified this for clarity, but *l*, *r*, or *n* following the consonant has the same effect as a vowel.</note> flanking the consonant, and it occurs in Welsh in the same positions where the consonant was surrounded by vowels in British. Take, for example, the British **Maglo-cunos*, Old Welsh *Mail-cun*, and Medieval and Modern *Mael-gwn*: the *c* is mutated to *g* in the final form because it was positioned between vowels in the first form. This mutation is determined by the British form, not by the Old Welsh form. Though it only appears in writing after the Old Welsh period, it cannot have arisen after that period, since the *lc* of the pre-mutation period becomes *lch*, as seen in *calch* ("lime") from the Latin *calcem*.

The <term>initial mutations</term> perhaps offer even better proof: "one son" is *un mab*, because in British it was **oinos mapos*, where the *m-* followed the *-s* of the masculine **oinos*. However, "one mother" is *un fam*, because in British it was **oinā mammā*, where the *m-* stood between vowels, following the feminine **oinā*. There is no other possible way to explain this difference, and no other possible conclusion except that the mutation dates back to the British language. The difference that arose in British and persists today cannot have been lost during the time in between. The fact that the mutation is not written in Old Welsh simply means that the orthography did not reflect sound differences that already existed in the spoken language. This shows that even the evidence of written records may be quite misleading unless it is interpreted in...

<summary>This page argues that Welsh soft mutation originated in the ancient British language rather than the Old Welsh period, using phonological rules and examples like "un mab" and "un fam" to prove it. The author explains that written records often lag behind spoken language changes, meaning ninth-century orthography reflects a much older literary tradition.</summary>

--- Page 244 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument for an early dating of Welsh literary tradition by analyzing how phonetic changes (mutations) in Early Welsh were perceived and recorded by neighboring cultures, suggesting that Old Welsh orthography reflects a system established as early as the sixth century.</meta>

# Taliesin.

...the light of phonetic laws. Now, the soft mutation of *m* in Early Welsh was an *m* pronounced loosely, moving toward a *v* sound but still retaining its nasal character; it still sounded to the English like a kind of *m*. It became *m* in English speech and remains as *m* in *Elmet* and *Leeming* (see pp. 71–72). But the mutated <term>tenues</term> (*p*, *t*, *c*) were heard as <term>mediae</term> (*b*, *d*, *g*) by the English and Irish. For example, *trindod* from the Latin *trīnĭtātem*—spelled *trintaut* in Old Welsh—was borrowed from Welsh and appears in Old Irish as *trindōit*<note>The final -*t* in Irish was sounded as *d*, and the ending is -*ōid* in Modern Irish. It is only in the modern forms that it can be determined whether Old Irish written *t* was sounded as *t* or *d*.</note>. Likewise, *Cadvan* from the British \**Catu-mannos*, spelled *Catman(n)*<note>*Y Cymmrodor*, ix, p. 170.</note> in Old Welsh and borrowed in the seventh century, appears as *Cædmon* in English. And the seventh-century *Cadwallon* was called *Cædwalla* by the English, even though his name was spelled *Catguollaun*<note>Ibid., p. 157.</note> much later in Old Welsh.

As the difference between a mutated and an unmutated consonant arose in the British language, it must naturally have existed in the sixth century, though it would necessarily have been less distinct at that time than it was later. Thus, Old Welsh orthography, which can be traced back to the seventh century in the "Saxon Genealogies," is even more easily explained as having been formed in the sixth century. It must have been preserved almost unchanged until the ninth century and was used with very little modification for two more centuries. Two results follow: (1) It is not necessary to assume that sixth-century poems were transmitted orally and written down for the first time in the ninth century. The existence of a continuous literary tradition, which the orthography proves, makes it possible—and therefore probable—that they were transmitted in writing. (2) If our

<summary>This page explains how early phonetic shifts in Welsh consonants were recorded by the English and Irish, providing evidence that Old Welsh spelling conventions originated in the sixth century. This supports the theory that early Welsh poetry was preserved through a continuous written tradition rather than just oral transmission.</summary>

--- Page 245 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the antiquity of Taliesin’s poems, arguing that if a ninth-century manuscript had survived, it would represent the sixth-century originals with minimal linguistic variation, much like the gap between Milton and modern readers.</meta>

# Taliesin.

If a scribe’s ninth-century copy had been preserved, it would present us with the poems almost in the form in which they would have been written down in the sixth century. The period that separated Taliesin from the ninth-century scribe would be about equal to the time that separates Milton from us. Welsh spelling probably changed less during that period than English spelling has since the seventeenth century. In the spoken language, consonants had been somewhat modified, just as vowels have been in English; but in both cases, the language was practically the same at the end as at the beginning of the period. Unfortunately, we only have a copy of the ninth-century manuscript made about four and a half centuries later, by which time it had become difficult to read and was probably incomplete. It is, however, reassuring to know that our oldest copy is only one step removed from a version in which the poems were written—linguistically speaking—practically in their original form.

I have discussed the historical poems above for the obvious reason that these alone contain direct evidence of their date, so an examination of them must form the foundation of any discussion regarding the authenticity of the Taliesin poems. But there are others whose claims will have to be considered if the claims of the historical poems are accepted. There is no absolute, hard-and-fast line to be drawn between the historical and the mythological poems, for some of the latter contain references to Taliesin’s contemporaries. For instance, "Daronwy," which speaks of the "magic wand of Mathonwy," also mentions Cynan and Rhun (pp. 28–29); and "Golychafi gulwyd," as we have seen above on page 197, refers to the historical figures Brochvael, Urien, Maelgwn, and Elphin, as well as to the mythological sons of Llŷr, and to Lleu, Gwydion, and Brân. There is thus evidence...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the antiquity of Taliesin’s poems, moving from historical evidence to the author’s use of mythological allusions to establish the age and context of the work.</meta>

# Taliesin.

The author of the historical poems was accustomed to alluding to mythological characters. These allusions assume the reader's acquaintance with the mythology. Some of them are references to tales preserved in the *Mabinogion*; in “Cadeir Ceridwen,” for example, the poet says that “Gwydion ap Dôn . . . conjured a woman out of flowers, and brought swine from the South . . . and formed horses and . . . saddles out of fungus” (p. 36). These allusions are followed by others to stories that have been lost. The authenticity of the poem is not disproved by the fact that a religious passage has been added at the end, which contains a reference to Bede and late-period rhymes. Some of the references in the poems are to more primitive forms of the tales than the medieval versions found in the *Mabinogion*: Manawyt and Pryderi know of the fairy region of Caer Sidi; the passage is as follows (34.8):

> (Ys) kyweir vyng kadeir yng Kaer Sidi;
> Nys plawd heint (a) heneint a vo yndi,
> Ys^1 gŵyr Manawyt a Phryderi;
> Teir oryan (y) Ha(m t)vgan^2 a gân recdi,
> Ac am y banneu^3 ffrydyeu gweilgi,
> A(r) ffynhawn ffrwythlawn ys (syð)^4 oduchti;
> Ys whe(ga)ch^5 no(r) gwin gwyn y^6 llyn yndi.

> ‘My chair is prepared in Caer Sidi;
> Disease and old age afflict no one who is there,
> As Manawyt and Pryderi know;
> Havgan’s three organs play before it,
> And around its peaks are the streams of the ocean,
> And above it is a fruitful fountain;
> Sweeter than white wine is the liquid within it.’

---

<note>1: *Ys* is formed of a conjunction of indefinite meaning, followed by the infixed pronoun *-s*, which here means ‘it’; in “Gweith Arg. Ll.”, line 9, page 156, it means ‘him’. In the *Nys* just above our *Ys* here, the *-s* is also ‘him’, the antecedent of the relative ‘who’; *nys* is thus ‘not him’, which I have rendered as ‘none’.</note>
<note>2: In the text *y am tan*, the *y* is a later addition above the line.</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the poem *Preideu Annwn* (The Spoils of Annwfn) and its relationship to the *Mabinogion* and Irish mythology, specifically focusing on the etymology of Caer Sidi and theories regarding the origins of Welsh mythic tales.</meta>

# Taliesin.

Pryderi,” 54.19. The poem describes an expedition, or expeditions, led by Arthur to Caer Sidi, which also seems to be indicated by other names such as Caer Vedwit, Caer Rigor, and others. Mention is made of “the cauldron of Pen Annwfn... which will not boil the food of a coward.” Pen Annwfn is the title of Pwyll in the *Mabinogion*; and the title of the poem, written in a later hand but likely traditional, is *Preideu Annwn* (‘the Spoils of Annwfn’), which shows that Annwfn is simply another name for Caer Sidi.<note>¹ *Sidi* is the Welsh equivalent of the Irish *sid* (‘fairy-land’). Its location beneath the sea in our poem (as in the Irish tale of Laegaire, which also features music and liquor—see Nutt, *Bran*, volume I, pages 182–184) and the synonym *Annwfn* in Welsh suggest that the name is derived from **sēd-*, the long *ē* grade of the root **sed-*, since *ē* becomes *ī* in Celtic. The root means not only ‘to sit’ but also ‘to sink’ (for example, in the words *sediment* or *subsidence*); its long *ō* grade occurs in Welsh in *sawdd* (‘subsidence, submergence’) and *sodi* (‘to sink’), in which the initial *s-* also remains unreduced. *Sidi* functions as a genitive following *Caer*, and would regularly represent a genitive **sīdiī* from a derivative in *-io-* of **sēd-*.</note> Alfred Nutt, in *The Voyage of Bran*, volume II, pages 13–17, compares the *Mabinogion* stories of Manawydan and Pryderi with corresponding Irish tales and says—

> “I believe it will be conceded that the Welsh and Irish stories owe their similarity to an origin in a common body of mythic romance, where the chief actors were the sea-god Manannan and a supernaturally begotten semi-mortal son of his.”

He then explains the various theories that have been proposed regarding the origin of the Welsh tales: briefly, (1) they derive from a common stock of Celtic mythology; (2) they were borrowed from the Irish people of Gwynedd; (3) they were brought to Wales by Cunedda’s men, who had been in contact with the Irish of the North; or (4) they were borrowed from Irish storytellers of the seventh and later centuries. Under any theory except the last, these tales were circulating in Wales in the sixth century, because Cunedda had come from the North and had driven out the

<meta>This page continues the argument regarding the origins of Welsh mythology, specifically addressing and refuting the theory that these tales were borrowed from Irish sources during the settlement of the Irish in Gwynedd.</meta>

# Taliesin.

Irish out of Gwynedd about the year 400. But why all this assumption of borrowing? It rests on the perfectly unfounded assumption that the British had no traditional lore of their own. If Celtic myth survived among the Irish, why did it *not* survive among the British? The answer is that it did; and the proof is in the tales themselves. The names of the characters provide a sufficient rejection of the borrowing theories; the name of *Manawyd*, for example, is quite a different formation from that of his Irish counterpart *Manannan*; the root is the same, but the difference in suffixes cannot be explained by any assumption of borrowing; thus, while the later *Manawydan* shows the influence of the Irish name in the added suffix, the Welsh must have known of him as *Manawyd*, or its British equivalent, from the twilight of the gods in Celtic times. *Pwyll* and *Pryderi* cannot be derived from Early Irish, which had no *p*. The name of *Gwydion* son of *Dôn*, is purely Welsh in form and has no Irish equivalent; his British godhood is reflected in *Caer Gwydion*, the Welsh name of the Milky Way. *Lleu's* name, converted into *Llew* in the Mabinogion, is the equivalent of the Irish *Lug*; but it occurs also in Gaulish, the twin-sister of British; the continental *Lugu-dūnon*<note>¹ Λουγούδουνον . . . νῦν δὲ Λούγδουνον 'Lyons', Dio Cassius; Λουγόδουνον 'Leyden', Ptolemy.</note> which survives in *Lyons*, *Laon*, and *Leyden*, occurs, with its elements reversed, not only in *Din-lle(u)* near Carnarvon, but in an old *Din-lle Ureconn*,<note>² Red Book, col. 1047, *F.A.B.*, ii, 288.</note> or Wrekin Dinlle, in a district where Irish influence cannot be assumed. Still less can *Lugu-balion*, the old name of Carlisle (see p. 59), be assumed to be borrowed from Irish. The Irish had no monopoly on *Lug*; indeed, their language has no cognates of the name,<note>³ Rhys, *Hibbert Lectures*, p. 408.</note> while Welsh has *lleu* 'light'

<summary>The author argues that Welsh mythology is an independent tradition rather than a borrowing from Irish lore, using linguistic evidence of names like Manawyd, Pwyll, and Lleu to prove their native British origins. He highlights that shared names like Lug appear in Gaulish and across Britain in areas unaffected by Irish influence, supporting a common Celtic heritage.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument regarding the linguistic and mythological links between the British and Irish, specifically using word roots to prove that the British had their own native traditions rather than merely borrowing from the Irish.</meta>

# Taliesin.

B.A. 4·21, *go-leu* ‘light’, *lleu-fer* ‘luminary’, *lleu-ad* ‘moon’, *llew-ych* ‘illumination’, *llychwr* ‘daylight’ (see above, p. 103). In the vocabularies of the two languages, where strict phonetic tests of origin can be applied, it is found that the borrowing is mainly on the side of the Irish.<note>¹ One might say that Irish is permeated through and through with British elements. It is therefore not to be expected that Irish influence in British would be of very great extent. —*Pedersen, Comparative Grammar*, i, p. 24.</note> Borrowers of words are borrowers of ideas; but it is not necessary for my purpose to reverse the argument and show that the theorists have confused creditors and debtors—it is sufficient to insist on the obvious truth that the two races inherited the fundamental concepts of their respective mythologies from their common ancestor. Words generally persist only in association with ideas; and it is inconceivable that British names of British gods could have survived except in traditions concerning them. In our medieval versions, the old tales have been edited and rationalized. “The very first thing that strikes one, in reading the *Mabinogion*,” says Matthew Arnold, “is how clearly the medieval storyteller is looting an antiquity whose secrets he does not fully understand.”<note>² *Celtic Literature*, p. 51.</note> It is also true, no doubt, that they contain elements which have been borrowed from Irish and other sources. But the allusions in our poems are not to the *Mabinogion*, but to more primitive versions of the stories with many details now lost.

Again, the mythological passages are not to be separated from the so-called “transformation” passages, or from the mystical passages which accompany both. Thus, “Kat Godeu”, 23·9, opens with a transformation passage beginning “I have been in many forms” (for the forms see above, p. 104); this is followed by mystical

Repetition in inverse order of the words of a half-line also occurs (*pet wynt pet ffreu, pet ffreu pet wynt*), as in the historical poem "Yng Ngorffowys"; see above, p. 177. The first rhyme in the poem is <term>ymae</term> / <term>a ganho</term>; the old form of <term>canhò</term> (3rd person singular present subjunctive) is <term>canhoe</term> (*Gram., p. 328*). In <term>ymae</term>, I had conjectured (ibid., p. 349) that the original diphthong was *oe*; not having noticed this rhyme, I previously had only the Cornish plural <term>ymons</term> as confirmation (p. 350). The poem also contains the first example in the manuscript of the scribe bungling the phrase <term>ar guaut</term>, which he writes as <term>an gba6t</term> instead of <term>ar wawt</term>; see above, p. 138. This proves that the poem was copied from a ninth-century manuscript, probably the same as the exemplar of the historical poems, in which the error is repeated.

At other times the poet asks questions, as in "Mabgyfreu Taliessin", 27.13,

> "Why is one night moonlit, and another [so dark] that you do not see your shield out of doors? . . . Why is a stone so heavy? Why is a thorn so sharp? Which is better, the stem or its branches? . . . Who is better off [in] their death, the young or the gray-haired? Do you know what you are when you are sleeping—whether body or soul, or a bright angel? Skilled minstrel, why do you not tell me? Do you know where night awaits day? . . . What supports the structure of the earth in perpetuity? The soul . . . who has seen it, who knows it? I marvel that in books they know it certainly. The soul . . . what is the shape of its limbs? . . . Death is established in all lands alike; death is over our head, and its veil is wide. . . . Man is old when he is born, and always grows younger",

27.19-28.17. The poem ends with the usual added prayer, which in this case is not even in verse. The reference to monks (<term>myneich</term>), 27.15, occurs in a corrupted line and is probably a misreading, like <term>beirδ</term> for <term>dynion</term> in "Yng Ngorffowys".
