What is the meaning of it all? Dr. Evans believes that the mysticism “definitely marks the period of the composition of the poems” as the twelfth century. Why? Because “Under the year 1200 Richard of Hoveden breaks his narrative to give a brief summary of the maxims of the philosopher Secundus”! (pp. xl-xli). The transformation passages, as we have seen (pp. 101-115), are treated by him as the cryptic autobiography of his twelfth-century Taliesin, of which he has spelled out enough to enable him to give “the life of the bard in outline” (p. xxxiii). When that was all in print, he was “reminded” by Dr. Mary Williams<note>2</note> of the well-known parallel to these passages in a poem attributed to the ancient Irish bard Amorgen. So he writes in his preface: “The transformation passages, which are a feature of Taliesin, have not been discussed” (p. iii); and he has the audacity to declare, after learning of the parallel, that “It is fatuous folly to

<header># Taliesin.</header>

As to the date, Nutt writes as follows in a footnote to page 92.

> Regarding the date of the poems attributed to Amairgen, it must be noted that they appear in those manuscripts of the *Lebor Gabála*, or *Book of Invasions*, which provide what may be called the second edition of that work—the one that identifies Cessair as the first immigrant to Ireland. However, the poems are heavily glossed, and it is almost certain that they were part of the original edition of the *Lebor Gabála*. Since this edition was known to Nennius in the early ninth century, it must be a product of the eighth century at the latest. It is quite possible that the poems are essentially as old as the first arrival of the Goidelic Celts in Ireland.

<margin>
¹ *Cycle mythologique*, pp. 244-6.
² *Hibbert Lectures*, p. 549.
</margin>
Without going so far as to support the last statement—except in the very general sense that the underlying ideas are as old as that—I believe there can be no doubt about the antiquity of the poem mentioned above. The resemblance between it and the Taliesin poems I have quoted is closer than has been recognized; it is not limited to the transformation lines, but also includes the claim to occult knowledge expressed through questions. I am unaware of anyone besides Dr. Evans suggesting that either the Welsh or the Irish bard borrowed from the other. The relationship between them is that they both inherited the same traditions, the existence of which is as certain in Britain as in Ireland and, as we shall see, is more directly documented. However, d'Arbois de Jubainville¹ has proposed the theory, which Rhys² has adopted, that Amorgen’s “I am” retains a more archaic form of the underlying concept than Taliesin’s “I have been.” To use Nutt’s concise summary, the argument is “that what is claimed for the poet is not so much the memory of past existences as the ability to take on all shapes at will; this is what puts him on a level with and enables him to overcome his

<meta>This page continues the critique of John Rhys's theories regarding the Irish bard Amorgen and the Welsh bard Taliesin, specifically focusing on the linguistic differences between "I am" and "I have been" in relation to the doctrine of metempsychosis.</meta>

super-human adversaries,” cited in the same place. It is clear that the proof rests on mere speculation, not on facts; it is a weak attempt to dismiss the idea of transmigration. Rhys “fails to see the point of the boast” in Taliesin’s “I have been,” though one would have thought that the claim to knowledge of previous states, and to the possession of their attributes, is clear enough; but Amorgen’s “I am” is clear to him, and he goes on to speak as if “I am” obviously and naturally meant “I can, whenever I wish, take the form of.” If “I am” means all that,<note>1 “When I make a word do a lot of work like that,” said Humpty Dumpty, “I always pay it extra.” — *Through the Looking Glass*, chapter vi.</note> how did it become “I have been” in Taliesin? This is inexplicable to Rhys; and he leaves it at that, without realizing that a theory which fails to account for the facts is inherently flawed. If, on the other hand, we take Taliesin’s “I have been” to represent the literal meaning, Amorgen’s “I am” presents less difficulty; it is perhaps not the historic present so much as the present tense that expresses the persistence of identity<note>2 As we say of a fifty-year-old man, “He is a Llandovery boy” or “an Oxford man”; or, as we may say in Welsh, French, and German, “I am a member since 1900,” whereas in English we must say “I have been.”</note>—the emphasis is placed on the possession of the attributes. Dr. Evans has helpfully quoted, on page iv, a translation of some verses by Empedocles containing the words, “I have been a youth, and a maiden, and a bush, and a bird, and a gleaming fish in the sea,” which show that the Pythagorean doctrine was expressed a thousand years before Taliesin’s time in a formula identical to his. That metempsychosis is the underlying concept in these passages is proved by the last quotation made above, page 242, from “Mab-gyfreu Taliesin.” The text is—

->Hynaf vyð dyn pan anher,    a ieu ieu pop amser,<-

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the doctrine of metempsychosis (reincarnation) in Welsh poetry, specifically addressing a paradox found in the poems of Taliesin and comparing it to Druidic teachings as recorded by Caesar.</meta>

# Taliesin.

meaning "man is oldest when he is born, and younger and younger always," which shows that the medieval scribe took it for a mere senseless paradox. But the meter consists of half-lines of five syllables: in the first half-line, the <term>copula</term> *vyδ* should be omitted, and *hynaf*, due to the copyist’s misunderstanding, reduced to *hên* ("old"), which is more probable than *hŷn* ("older"); in the second, one *ieu* should be omitted; the line then reads:

->Hên dyn pan anher,     a ieu pob amser,<-

"Man is old when he is born, and younger always." Whether we read *hên* or *hŷn* is a mere matter of clarity; the first statement can have no intelligible meaning except that man existed long before he was born. The second, I believe, means that he never attains in this life the age at which he had arrived in previous existences when he was born.

This doctrine is evident in the transformation poems. The determined refusal to see it, and the resort to any tactic—the assumption, for example, that "I have been" is a meaningless perversion of "I am," which in turn is assumed to be a mystical condensation of "I can, at will, assume the shape of"—rather than admit the plain meaning of plain words, springs from nothing but a false linguistic theory that relegates the poems to a comparatively late age. In that age, they stand meaningless, isolated, and irrelevant—an insoluble enigma to those who put them there.

Let us move them back to their traditional setting, within a reasonable distance of Druidic times, and see what happens then. Take first Caesar’s account of the teaching of the Druids, *B.G.*, vi, 14; I quote Professor Oman’s free rendering, *England*, etc., p. 28:

> The chief doctrine of the Druids is that the soul does not perish, but at death passes from one body to another, and this

<summary>The author argues that Taliesin's poems explicitly describe the doctrine of metempsychosis, a belief consistent with Caesar's accounts of Druidic teaching. He criticizes scholars who dismiss this literal meaning in favor of linguistic theories that date the poems to a much later period where they lose their context.</summary>

--- Page 258 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of Druidic influence on the poems of Taliesin, specifically comparing Caesar's observations on the transmigration of souls with Welsh bardic tradition.</meta>

# Taliesin.

> belief they consider a great incentive to courage, since the fear of annihilation may be put aside. They hold many discussions concerning the stars and their movements, about the size of the world and the universe, about nature, and about the power and attributes of the immortal gods.

The quotation is common enough; but anyone who looks at it again and compares it with the typical extracts above from the mythico-mystical poems of Taliesin must, I think, be impressed by the light it throws upon them. Caesar puts at the forefront, as the doctrine on which stress is particularly (*in primis*) laid, the immortality and transmigration of souls; and transformation passages “are a feature of Taliesin.” The words “they hold many discussions” are less vivid than the original *multa disputant*; in the poems, we have echoes of <term>bardic</term> disputations concerning the measure of the earth and what supports it, and about natural problems (*de rerum natura*), with many allusions to the ancient gods. Caesar, it should be stated, is speaking of the druids of Gaul; but in the preceding section, he states that it was generally believed that the system was invented in Britain and brought over to Gaul, and adds that in his own day, those who wished to master it thoroughly went to Britain to learn it. The ideas associated with rebirth are, as Nutt has shown, implicit in traditional Irish literature; but here we have direct historical evidence that <term>metempsychosis</term> was the cardinal doctrine of the British predecessors of the Welsh bards.

The classical evidence concerning the religion of the Celts has often been brought together; it is given in a convenient form by Nutt, *op. cit.*, pp. 107-112.—(1) He cites first a quotation made by Clement of Alexandria from a lost work by Alexander Polyhistor, written between 82 and 60 B.C., to the effect that Pythagoras was a disciple of the Galatians and the Brahmins. This has no

<summary>The author argues that Caesar's accounts of Druidic belief in the transmigration of souls provide historical context for similar themes in Taliesin's poetry. He suggests that these doctrines originated in Britain before spreading to Gaul and were foundational to the predecessors of Welsh bards.</summary>
<keywords>Taliesin, Julius Caesar, Druids, transmigration of souls, metempsychosis, Celts, Alexander Polyhistor, Pythagoras, Welsh bards, Keltic religion</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of classical accounts regarding Druidic doctrine, specifically focusing on the immortality and transmigration of souls as recorded by Greek and Roman historians.</meta>

# Taliesin.

value except as evidence of similarities of doctrine giving rise to the theory. (2) Then comes the testimony of Caesar quoted above. (3) Diodorus Siculus, about 40 B.C. (apparently quoting from Posidonius of Apamea, who wrote between 100 and 80 B.C., see *Mon. Hist. Celt.*, i, 1911, p. 314), says—

> Among them the doctrine of Pythagoras prevailed—namely, that the souls of men are undying, and that after completing their term of existence they pass into another body. Accordingly, at the burial of the dead, some throw letters addressed to their departed relatives upon the funeral pyre, believing that the dead will read them in the next world.

The last sentence illustrates the vividness with which the belief in a future life was held by the Kelts; this is emphasized later by the statements of Valerius Maximus and Pomponius Mela that agreements were made for the repayment of loans and the settlement of accounts in the next world. (4) Ammianus Marcellinus quotes the Greek historian Timagenes, who wrote about 20 B.C., to the effect that the druids, following the precepts of Pythagoras, were organized in close corporations, busied themselves with questions of occult and other matters, and believed souls to be immortal. (5) Strabo, about 19 A.D., mentions the studies of the druids in natural science and moral philosophy, and states that they taught the immortality of souls. (6) Valerius Maximus, about 20 A.D., wrote of the druids:

> They would like us to believe that the souls of men are immortal. I should be tempted to call these breeches-wearing gentry fools, were their doctrine not the same as that of the mantle-clad Pythagoras.

(7) Pomponius Mela, about 44 A.D., says that they profess to know the size and form of the earth and the world, the movements of the heavens and the stars, and the will of the gods, and teach that souls are undying, and that

<summary>This page compiles historical evidence from classical authors like Diodorus Siculus, Strabo, and Valerius Maximus to demonstrate the Druidic belief in the immortality and transmigration of souls. These accounts link Druidic philosophy to Pythagorean thought and highlight the practical intensity of their belief in an afterlife.</summary>

<keywords>Taliesin, Druids, Pythagoras, immortality of the soul, transmigration, Diodorus Siculus, Valerius Maximus, Pomponius Mela, Kelts, Metempsychosis</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the analysis of classical accounts concerning Druidic beliefs, specifically focusing on the poet Lucan’s descriptions of the soul and the distinction between primitive shape-shifting and the formal doctrine of metempsychosis.</meta>

# Taliesin.

there is another life in the underworld (*ad Manes*). (8) Lucan’s famous passage about the druids in the *Pharsalia*, written about 60-70 <term>A.D.</term>, is quoted from Matthew Arnold’s version, *Celt. Lit.*, p. 42:

> To you only is given the knowledge—or ignorance, whichever it may be—of the gods and the powers of heaven. From you we learn that the destination of a man’s spirit is not the senseless grave, nor the pale realm of the ruler below; in another world his spirit still survives; death, if your teaching be true, is but the passage to enduring life.

As Nutt points out, this version by no means gives the full force of the Latin. Lucan says that the same spirit animates a body (*regit artus*) in another world; and “passage to” should be “center of.”

After making every allowance for repetition and indirect information, we can hardly avoid concluding from the evidence that <term>metempsychosis</term> formed the central doctrine of the druidic system. It was not the mere instinctive belief in shape-shifting, with which observers would be familiar in the folk tales of other races, but the conscious formulation of the principle of transmigration, which reminded them of the teaching of <term>Pythagoras</term>. It represented a fixed point in the system, around which gathered many speculations, more or less flexible, about nature and the gods.

Belief in shape-shifting, or transformation, is universal among primitive races. It is not a formal doctrine, but an expression of the natural working of the primitive mind. It has not spread, like a rumor, from race to race, but has sprung up spontaneously everywhere. Primitive man has a vague conception of something that constitutes a person’s identity—the essence of his being, the thing that says “I.” But it is conceived of as purely material. When a man is turned into a dog, it is his human body that has assumed a dog’s shape—that is why he remains the same person.

<summary>The author examines Lucan’s writings on Druidic beliefs in the afterlife and concludes that metempsychosis was a central, organized doctrine rather than simple folk shape-shifting. It describes the primitive view of identity as being tied to a material essence that can change forms.</summary>
<keywords>Lucan, Pharsalia, Druids, Matthew Arnold, Nutt, metempsychosis, Pythagoras, transmigration, shape-shifting, afterlife, identity</keywords>

--- Page 261 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of Druidic beliefs, transitioning from the discussion of Lucan’s descriptions of the soul to how primitive shape-shifting evolved into a formalized doctrine of transmigration, comparing these Celtic ideas to Greek philosophical traditions.</meta>

<page-num>251</page-num>

# Taliesin.

The concept survives in tales of rebirth where a portion of a person’s body—his blood, his heart, or his ashes—is consumed by the woman who is to become his mother. Welsh tales are full of shape-shifting. Lleu is wounded and flies away as an eagle, but the eagle bears the same wound. Llwyd’s wife and her maidens turn themselves into mice; because she is pregnant, the mouse she becomes is also pregnant. This is transformation, pure and simple. 

But the druids do seem to have taken the next step and abstracted the soul. The transformation of the body became the transmigration of the soul. Physical continuity was no longer a necessary condition for the persistence of identity; the principle of persistence resided in the soul, which was thus conceived to be immortal. However, the soul must still be clothed in a body, and when one body is worn out or destroyed, it acquires another. This explains the belief in previous existences in various bodies, both animate and even inanimate. It also suggests a future existence, perhaps in another body in this world, but ultimately in an ever-young body in a happier world. 

The concept of <term>metempsychosis</term> is, as Nutt has shown, too old and widespread among the Celts to have been borrowed from Pythagoreanism through the Greeks of Marseille. Greek metempsychosis is a parallel development. In Thrace, the same original ideas resulted in <term>Orphicism</term> and the Dionysiac mysteries; participation in these enabled the initiate to free his soul from the restraints of the body, “to have a respite from woe.” Pythagoras did not invent his doctrine; he systematized Orphic beliefs. “The strong likelihood,” says Nutt (*op. cit.* p. 134), “that the affinity between the Greek and Celtic Elysium myths is due to prehistoric community rather than to historic contact justifies a similar presumption in the case of the rebirth myths.” Perhaps the classical writers, when they compared the druidic doctrine

<summary>This page explores how Celtic myths of bodily transformation evolved into a formal belief in the immortality and transmigration of the soul, arguing that these views developed independently of Greek influence.</summary>
<keywords>metempsychosis, transmigration of the soul, druids, Celts, shape-shifting, Alfred Nutt, Pythagoras, Orphicism, Welsh mythology, rebirth, Lleu, Llwyd</keywords>

--- Page 262 ---
<meta>This page continues the comparison between Celtic beliefs and Pythagorean philosophy, focusing on how the bards utilized mythological and philosophical concepts for poetic effect rather than for rigorous logic.</meta>

<page-num>252</page-num>

# Taliesin.

to that of Pythagoras, were wiser than they knew. The entire subject is discussed very thoroughly by Nutt¹ in the *Voyage of Bran, vol. ii.*

The druids had, no doubt, developed a basic system of philosophy; however, poetry is not the appropriate medium for logical reasoning. The bards treated the same subjects through poetry—that is, through allusion and emotion. In our poems, we seem to find an echo of that ancient bardic tradition. The poet refers to questions of natural philosophy not to solve them, but to highlight the wonder and romance of knowledge, as well as the power of the person who possesses it—usually himself. He tells "the fairy tales of science," as science was understood at that time. The ancient concept of the Otherworld fascinated him; his seat was prepared in Caer Sidi, where no one suffers from the disease of old age. His treatment of pre-existence is imaginative rather than philosophical; the lists of his transformations are largely dictated by the rhyme. He uses ancient mythology for its poetic value, as Christian bards have always done. He mentions the gods mainly in connection to his own previous existences: he was with Dylan in a fortress; he was with Brân in Ireland; he sang before the sons of Llŷr at Aber Henvelen. We can now understand a passage such as the following, 25.21 (see above, page 109):

> It was not from a father and mother that I was made (*digonat*). In the beginning (*am creu*, compare *dechreu*), I was created out of nine elements: from fruits, . . . . from primroses, from the flowers of the hill . . . from earth . . . from water was I made . . . I was brought into existence by the spell of Math before I was on earth (*kyn bum*

<summary>This page explores how Welsh bards adapted Druidic philosophy and mythology into poetry, emphasizing the wonder of knowledge and the concept of pre-existence over formal logic. He was in existence long before he was born; he was created out of fruits and flowers, earth and water by Math and Gwydion, the great enchanters among the gods, who, according to a tale to which he refers elsewhere (p. 36; W.B.M., c. 100), fashioned a wife for Lleu out of the blossoms of the oak, and the blossoms of the broom, and the blossoms of the meadow-sweet.

I have to content myself with discussing the mythical and mystical poems, and the very interesting questions arising out of them, in this mere outline. A full discussion would excessively expand this volume and delay its appearance. But I think enough has been said to show that these poems, which were mist and mystery to those who looked at them through glasses focused on the ninth century, become clear when we focus for distance; and the mist, with most of the mystery, vanishes.

I must also forgo for the present any attempt to discuss the Taliesin legend and its relation to the mystic poems. To Rhys, who even made myths of Urien and Owein ap Urien, Taliesin was of course a myth. I will only say that the sixteenth-century tale of Taliesin does not prove it. Similar stories were current of Pythagoras at an early age in Greece; he had been Euphorbus in the Trojan war, and had passed through many metamorphoses. The tale of Hopkin ap Philip no more disposes of Taliesin than that of Heraclides of Pontus disposes of Pythagoras.

Before leaving the mystico-mythical poems I would point out that the very difference between them and the

eulogies and elegies points to the genuineness, at least, of the latter. It is improbable that a medieval forger would refrain from attempting to color his counterfeit eulogies with what he conceived to be the “opinions” of Taliesin. In reality, however, for a eulogy to be acceptable, it had to be realistic and unambiguous, not allusive and mystical. It was a branch of <term>bardism</term> that had been practiced from time immemorial, and its rules were well understood. Praise of princes did not originate with the <term>praecones</term> whom <term>Gildas</term> describes, for <term>Posidonius</term> of <term>Apamea</term> bears witness to it flourishing at the beginning of the first century B.C.:

> The Celts, even when making war, are accompanied by a class known as parasites, who dine with them. These men sing their praises before large assemblies, and also to any individual who cares to listen to them. They have also a class known as Bards (Βάρδοι), who play the music. These, too, are poets and set out their virtues in odes.—*Mon. Hist. Celt.*, i., p. 331.

The poems dealt with above are those which seem to me to have a claim to be considered genuine. To prevent any possible misapprehension, I desire to emphasize the statement already made: that I by no means regard all the poems in the Book of Taliesin as being likely to be, in any sense, as old as the sixth century. Mr. Ifor Williams has shown that the long poem “Armes Prydein Vawr”, 13.2, belongs to the ninth or early tenth century; see above p. 53. All references to Cadwaladr—and so probably the poems containing them—are late. I see from Dr. Evans’s index that the name does not occur between pages 31 and 74, where most of the poems dealt with above are found; it appears then on pages 76, 77, 78, and 80. These last poems, with their references to Gwydyl Ffichti and Nordmyn Mandi, are obviously late. Poems dealing with Alexander and Irish myths, and the plagues of Egypt, as well as the religious poems, I should guess to be late. The Song of

"The Wind" (36.21), which Dr. Evans believes to be the earliest composition of *his* Taliesin, is late; it rhymes *traet* with *gwaet*, and *troet* with *coet*. In Early Welsh, *troet* and *traet* were *troyet* and *trayet*, rhyming with *-et* (see *Grammar*, 32); and it rhymes *yma* (*ymann*) with *da* (*day*). It has nothing in common with the mystical poems; it is, in fact, an ordinary riddle. I quote the three opening lines and three others:

> Dechymic pwy yw:    creat cyn dilyw,
> Creadur cadarn     heb gic, heb ascwrn,
> Heb wytheu, heb waet,     heb pen a heb traet . . .
> Ef ymaes, (ef) yng koet,     heb law a heb troet . . .
> Ac ef yn gyflet     ac wyneb tydwet . . .
> Ef ar vôr, (ef) ar tir,     ny wŷl, ny welir.

> ‘Guess what it is: created before the flood,
> A mighty creature, without flesh, without bone,
> Without veins, without blood, without head, without feet . . .
> In field, in forest, without hand, without foot . . .
> And it is as wide as the face of the earth . . .
> On sea, on land, it sees not, is not seen.’

We may compare this with the following riddle from the *Flores*<note>¹ Quoted by G. A. Wood, in a paper on “The Old English Riddles” in *Aberystwyth Studies*, i, 1912, p. 42, who provides the reference Migne, *Patrologia Latina*, xc, 539 ff.</note> of Bede:

> Dic mihi quae est illa res quae caelum totamque terram replevit, silvas et surculos confringit, omniaque fundamenta concutit, sed nec oculis videri aut manibus tangi potest. (Solution: Ventus).

There are other “wind” riddles, such as Aldhelm’s, beginning—

->Cernere me nulli possunt, nec prendere palmis.<note>² G. A. Wood, *op. cit.*, p. 41. The reference given in the bibliography, p. 8, to Aldhelm’s riddles is Migne, vol. lxxxix.</note><-

Since the time of Sharon Turner, it has been well known that some of the poems in the collection are later than others. I think I have shown above that the view that the earliest of them are no older than the 8th or 9th century was originally reached on grounds external to the poems themselves. It did not arise from any knowledge of Welsh metrics, or any close critical study of the text, for which, perhaps, the time was not yet ripe. In recent years, much good work has been done on the vocabulary of the old poetry by Mr. Ifor Williams in his study of Aneirin (see p. 35 fn.) and by Professor Loth. It is a matter of common knowledge that the meanings and explanations of old words given by Pughe in his dictionary are not reliable; but it is not so generally realized that he guessed completely. Sometimes he borrows his meanings from some of the old glossaries, which, however, are largely guesswork too. But he never mentions any authority, or gives any indication whether he is recording a fact, a tradition, or his own guess; there is no “perhaps” or “probably” in his columns; he is the perfect charlatan who claims omniscience. By setting down his baseless conjectures with the same solemn assurance as the most ordinary facts, he has deceived even scholars in the past. The whole of the old vocabulary must be thoroughly overhauled, and no meanings should be taken on trust. Silvan Evans has brought together a good number of examples of some words, but, as we have seen, he is too ready to accept the guesses of his prede-

The twelfth-century poets use a large number of words which are obsolete in medieval prose; in this, they are clearly following a poetic tradition, so their works are of great value for the study of the ancient vocabulary. For this reason, the whole of the *Myvyrian* poetry should be carefully indexed; this has been done partially by Mr. Williams for the purpose of his study of Aneirin. I understand that Mr. Gwynn Jones proposes to do it systematically after comparing the text with the original manuscripts where possible. This is the kind of work that must be carried out before we can make the most effective use of the material available for the study of the ancient poems. Regarding the study of Taliesin, I fully recognize that the work done in these pages is only a beginning, even for the poems dealt with in some detail. I have been largely occupied with the necessary preliminary work of clearing the ground of rubbish. But I intend, if I am able, to pursue the investigation and possibly return to the subject in the pages of the *Cymmrodor* some years from now, with the kind permission of the Editor.

# APPENDIX I.

### THE STONE OF CINGEN.

This stone, which was in the churchyard at Towyn, Merioneth, early in the nineteenth century (*Cambro-Briton*, ii, 1821, p. 121), and is now in the church, has been called the "Stone of St. Cadfan," undoubtedly because the church is dedicated to Cadfan, and perhaps

<image-desc>A line drawing of four views of an inscribed stone pillar, labeled 1 through 4 at the base. View 1 shows a small cross at the bottom and a vertical line of insular script starting with a cross. View 2 shows multiple lines of horizontal and vertical script with a small cross. View 3 shows a single long vertical line of script. View 4 shows two distinct blocks of script at the top and bottom of the stone face.</image-desc>

because an older antiquary thought, like Ab Ithel, that *guadgan* on the stone was an old spelling of Cadfan! It hardly needs to be said that the stone has nothing at all to do with Cadfan. It was described by Westwood in *Arch. Camb.*, 1850, pp. 90-95, and interpreted by the "philological skill" (in the same place, p. 95) of Ab Ithel, pp. 96-100. It had previously been illustrated by Edward Lhuyd in Gibson's Camden, p. 622, according to Westwood, *Lap. Wall.*, p. 158; the edition is not named, but it must be the second one from 1722, from which it was copied into Gough's Camden. I have not seen these engravings, as the Bangor Library contains only the first edition of Gibson's Camden, and no copy of Gough's. Pennant gives an engraving of the stone in his *Tour in Wales*, ii, 1784, plate v, from a drawing in Lhuyd's papers, as he says on page 93; but according to Westwood's description, the drawing differs somewhat from the Gibson engraving. Westwood's engraving in the *Arch. Camb.*, 1850, opposite p. 90, was made by means of the *camera lucida* from rubbings of the stone; and his lithograph in *Lap. Wall.* is a mere copy of it. Hübner's engraving, *Inscr. Christ. Brit.*, 126, is, like the accompanying block, a reduced reproduction of a tracing of it.

<summary>This page discusses the historical documentation and various engravings of the Stone of Cingen, refuting the traditional belief that it is related to St. Cadfan. The author cites several 18th and 19th-century antiquarian sources and describes the technical methods used to create visual reproductions of the stone's inscriptions.</summary>
<keywords>Stone of Cingen, St. Cadfan, Towyn, Merioneth, Ab Ithel, Westwood, Edward Lhuyd, Camden's Britannia, camera lucida, Welsh archaeology, inscriptions</keywords>

--- Page 271 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Stone of Cingen, specifically addressing the scholarly debate between Rhys and Westwood regarding the authenticity and transcription of the inscriptions.</meta>

<page-num>261</page-num>
<header>Taliesin.</header>

Rhys wrote a short note on the stone in *Arch. Camb.*, 1874, p. 243, as stated above on p. 232. In the volume for 1897, pp. 142–146, he discusses it more fully and suggests that it is not genuine. I was under the impression that he had written the second article, but I was misled by a footnote in *Lives of the British Saints*, vol. ii, p. 7, which refers to the latter article as if it were the former. Therefore, I did not see the longer article until these notes were written and typeset; in some ways it confirms the conclusions I had reached; in the following paragraphs, references to it, added in proof, are included in <note>note tags</note>.—In this second article, Rhys provides drawings of the four sides of the stone, correcting Westwood's engraving as reproduced here. The fracture on face 4 comes between the *l* and *t* of *molt*, through the stem of *p*, and between the *a* and *r* of *tuar*. Other points are noticed below. But in some respects Westwood's drawings are obviously better; the *gu* at the top of side 2 is given in Rhys's drawing as a slightly irregular *n*, but in his transliteration he reads it as *gu* and makes no mention of the discrepancy!—Rhys could make nothing of the inscription; he thinks side 3 "is a jumble cut by somebody who had a superficial acquaintance with Old Welsh" (p. 145), which is rather a desperate conclusion. He thinks sides 1 and 2 may be genuine, but "though there is uniformity of lettering throughout," he thinks these "may have served as models for the lettering of the rest"; and this suggestion is made only because he could not "decipher its meaning" (p. 146). Why was the **N** not imitated? But the idea that old lettering could be thus perfectly imitated and used to inscribe nonsense is too absurd to consider seriously.

There are clearly two inscriptions on the stone, beginning with the crosses. Westwood suggested several possibilities regarding sides 3 and 4; Ab Ithel took 3 to be the continuation of 1, and 4 the continuation of 2, reading in each case over the top of the stone. Why over the top? It seems more natural to suppose that the adjacent sides belong together.

->I.<-

Side 1, with its two uncial **N**'s, appears to be the earliest inscription; and it seems as if the two lines in large lettering on side 4, the next side to the left, were the continuation of it. The two marks after *celen* are more likely to denote that the inscription is to be continued than to mark its end. The three lines in small letters at the top of 4 were probably added later. If this is so, the first inscription is—

| | |
|---|---|
| † <term>cingen celen</term> | That is: Cynien's body |
| <term>tricet</term> | &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;lies |
| <term>nitanam</term> | &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;beneath |

<summary>The author critiques the scholarly assessments of the Stone of Cingen, specifically challenging Rhys's doubts about its authenticity. The author proposes that the inscriptions are genuine and provides a modern translation of the first section as a funerary marker for Cynien.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philological analysis of the name Cingen as found on the stone, comparing it to other historical figures and examining its linguistic evolution from British to Modern Welsh.</meta>

<page-num>262</page-num>
<header>Taliesin.</header>

With regard to the reading, there is only one doubtful point. Westwood took the IN in *Cingen* to be a ligatured UN; and in Pennant’s figure, the bottom of the I is actually joined to the N, which, if correct, would imply the ligature. *Cungen* is a possible old spelling of the name. On the other hand, Westwood’s drawing shows no connection, so that it seems safer to read *Cin*, as Hübner does. <note>Rhys also reads *Cin*.</note>

There seems to be no doubt about the *g*. It was probably of the same shape as the other *g*’s—an Insular *g* with the hook inverted. Westwood shows the continuation of the top stroke in dotted lines as if it were faint or worn; <note>Rhys in his drawing gives the whole top stroke boldly, as if there were no doubt about it, but curiously says that “it may possibly be a *c*”.</note>

Ab Ithel, in the work cited above on page 100, believed that this *Cingen* was Cyngen the father of Brochvael, and grandfather of our old friend Cynan Garwyn (see above page 201); he calls him “the son of Cadell”, confusing him with the ninth-century Cyngen; he means Cyngen ap Mawgan, who lived in the early part of the sixth century and was a contemporary of Cadfan. Hence, the stone was supposed to belong to the first half of the sixth century, though the lettering clearly indicates a later date. The fact is, this *Cingen* is not the same name as that of Cyngen ap Mawgan; this has an old medial *g* which would now be a consonantal *i* (see page 201, footnote 2); the *-gen* of this name is the same as that of *Urbgen* which is now *Urien*; while the name of Cyngen ap Mawgan has an old medial *c*—it was spelled *Cincen* (see page 201), or in full *Cincenn* as in the *Book of St. Chad*, page 141, line 4 (see page 269 below). The phonetic history of the two names may be summarized thus:

| | (1) | (2) |
|---|---|---|
| British | *Cuno-genos* | *Cuno-cennos.* |
| Old Welsh | *Cingen* | *Cincenn.* |
| Modern Welsh | *Cynien* | *Cyn-gen.* |

Note that the *ng* of the second name in its modern form is sounded as in *Ban-gôr*, or like the *ng* of the English word *finger*. But the name on our stone is the first, and would now be *Cynien*. In Old Welsh records, the names are sometimes spelled with *o* in the first syllable under Irish influence; and both names occur together repeatedly in *Llyfr Llan Daf*: *Concen abbatem Catoci*, . . *Congen abbatem Ilduti*, page 152; *Concen abbas Carbani vallis* . . . *Congen abbas Ilduti*, 154 and 155. It might be worth while examining the Trallong stone again to see whether in the inscription *Cunocenni filius Cunoceni* the last *c* has a tag which makes it a *g*, as it is rare in these inscriptions to find father and son of the same name. <note>Rhys does not know</note>

The text also corrects historical misidentifications of the person named on the stone and suggests further examination of related inscriptions.</summary>

--- Page 273 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological analysis of the name Cingen and related terms found on the stone, transitioning from a discussion of medial consonants to specific vocabulary and the reading of a second inscription.</meta>

<page-num>263</page-num>
<header>Taliesin.</header>

the name with *g*, and writes as if the *g* on the stone could represent an original *c* at this early period.

*celen* is the medieval *celein* (modern *celain*), meaning ‘dead body’. As noted above on page 241 footnote, and below on page 272, note 15, *e* is probably the oldest spelling of the diphthong *ei*, so that *celen* is quite regular for *celein*. The construction in *Cingen celen* is the same as that in *Taliesin gân* (‘Taliesin’s song’), etc.; see page 121.

*tricet* is probably the 3rd person singular present indicative, being an old middle form with *-et* from \*-*eto*. As *-et* is an imperative ending, it became obsolete in the indicative, with only *-it* and *-awt* remaining as long forms in that mood.

*nitanam*. The modern *o dan* (‘under’) stands for Old Welsh *guotan*, in which the element denoting ‘under’ is *guo-* (cognate with Latin *sub*); the *tan* is also found in *am dan*, where *am* determines the meaning ‘about’. The determining element here is \**ni* (‘under’), which survives in the English words *nether* and *beneath*. The form *ni-tan* was replaced by *guo-tan* in Welsh, but it seems to survive in the Breton *in-dan* and Medieval Breton *endan* (‘under’), probably for n<gloss>a</gloss>-dan from *ni-dan*.

As for the suffix *-am*, this occurs in Old Welsh instead of the medieval *-aw* in *racdam*, a gloss on ‘sibi’, and *-aw* is used adverbially in *heibiaw* from *heb*. But the *-am* is more probably the 1st person singular form, modern *-af*; the first person singular is used adverbially in *isof* (‘below’) and *uchof* (‘above’) (as well as the more usual 2nd person singular forms *isod* and *uchod*). In any case, *nitanam* is clearly the adverb ‘beneath’.

## II.

The second inscription, on sides 2 and 3, seems to consist of proper names. There can be no doubt about the first six letters *tengru*. Westwood reads the next letter as *g*; but it is so unlike the other *g*’s on the stone that this is at least doubtful. In Pennant’s engraving, it appears as ⌉, and I am inclined to read it as *i*. <note>Rhys’s drawing gives it as ı, that is, an ordinary *i*.</note> The next letter is read as *ci* by Westwood, and it appears as *ci* in Pennant’s drawing; but in Gibson’s *Camden*, it is given as *n*, and Westwood says “it looks like *n*”. Clearly it is *n*, and the small circle read as *c* must be accidental. <note>The circle thus guessed to be “accidental”—that is, not part of the inscription—is according to Rhys “a hole made for a gate-hinge”, p. 144. Still, Rhys thought the *n* was *ci*; he thought the *c* ran through the hole and did not see the first stroke of the *n*.</note> The first eight letters then read *tengruı̡n*.

The initial *t-* may be the honorific prefix, as in *T-eilo*, etc., leaving *engruin* for the name. Where *ng* had not disappeared in prehistoric times before *r*, it tends to become *gg*, which gives Old Welsh *c*, modern *g* (see *Grammar*, p. 151); and *ui* in the last syllable may become *y* as in *meheryn* from Old Welsh *maharuin*, *namyn* from *namuin*, etc. Hence

<page-num>264</page-num>

### Taliesin.

*Engruin* may be an old form of *Egryn* (late Old Welsh *Ecrin*). *Llan Egryn* is close by; and "there is in the parish, to the northeast of the church, a place known, as early as the time of Elizabeth and James I, as *Croes Egryn*, but there is no cross there now," *Lives of the British Saints*, ii, p. 415. Could this be the missing cross?

The next name seems to be *malted*; the *d* lost in the fracture is supplied from the drawings of Lhuyd, who saw the stone before it was broken. This would be *Malltedd* in Modern Welsh; the name that comes nearest is *Mallteg* in *Llan-fallteg*. If the missing letter was *c*, the names would be identical; and we have only Lhuyd’s evidence for the *d*.

The *gu* at the end of the line seems to be connected with the *adgan* in the next line, giving *guadgan*. This would be *Gwaddian* in Modern Welsh. As *gwa-* and *go-* are interchangeable, the name may be the same as that of St. *Gothian* (found also as *Guoidiane* and *Guidiane*), now called *Gwithian* in Cornwall, *Lives of the British Saints*, iii, pp. 249-51. <note>Rhys is again astray in his phonetics when he supposes that *adgan* may be the equivalent of a later *Adgan* or *Atgan*.</note>

The small letters at the end are given by Lhuyd as <image-desc>a left curly brace grouping "mc" on the top line and "crta." on the bottom line</image-desc>. The last letter, however, in our drawing from the rubbing is more like *R* than *a*; and the first in that line is probably *a* (not *c*). Westwood, after giving Lhuyd’s reading above, ignores the *c* after the *m*, giving no reason at all for omitting it in his reading; it occurred, of course, like the *r* underneath it and part of the *t*, in the part of the stone chipped off. <note>Rhys’s drawing shows the beginning of the *c* after the *m*, and he says that in the last line "before the *a* there would seem to have been a *t*." I should guess the *c* after the *m* to have been an *a*; could the supposed *t* have been *p*, making *map*? But these two short lines are too imperfect to be interpreted; and in any case, they probably represent a later addition, like the short inscription at the top of side 4.</note>

On side 3, the imperfect second letter looks like the beginning of an *r*; but Lhuyd, who saw it whole, read it *n*. <note>Rhys also believes it to be *n*.</note> Rhys thought he found traces of an *n* at the end, completing the name *Marciaun*. <note>Westwood could not find the *n*, and Rhys in 1897 "looked for it in vain." Can it be an unfinished name, or was there a contraction mark for *n* originally above the *u*?</note> The reading then is—

->*anterunc dubut marciau(n)*<-

It is not easy to explain *anterunc*; but for a reason that will appear later, I guess it to be not a name but a preposition or adverb. Original *inter* is *ithr* in Old Welsh, but when compounded it is *athr*, as in *cyf-athr-ach*, and in *athrywyn* from the Latin *interven-*; the form

<header>Taliesin.</header>

*athr* implies the British word *anter*, which may have survived in Early Welsh. The second element *unc* may be the second element of *rh-wng* (from \**per-ong-* or \**per-onk-*; the *k* is implied in the *th* instead of *δ* in *rhyngthaw*, etc.). The literal meaning would be the same as that of *rhwng* (‘between’); and it might denote ‘including’ or ‘together with’, compare page 66 above.

*Dubut* is either an abbreviation of *Dubutuc*, or a short form without the suffix *-uc*. The name *Dubutuc* occurs in the Latin genitive as *Dobituci* and in Ogam as *Dovatuceas*; in Modern Welsh it is *Dyfodwg*, and in Irish *Dubthoch* or *Dubthach* (now *Duffy*), *Lives of the British Saints*, iv, page 291.

Rhys identified *Marciau(n)* with the name which is *Merciaun* in Old Welsh, *Meirchawn* in Medieval Welsh, and now *Meirchion*. The language of the inscription is pre-Old or Early Welsh.

### III.

Lastly, there is the little inscription at the top of side 4. The first line is clear: *molt*. The second character in the second line was read as *l* by Westwood, which seems most improbable to me. It looks more like the contraction for *et* (‘and’). <note>In Rhys’s drawing, as in Pennant’s, there is no curve at the bottom, but the down stroke is straight; it is thus even more like 7, the symbol for ‘et’, which might be right-angled, as in Hübner, op. cit., no. 175. Rhys places it nearer the second *c*, so that the two resemble 7c—that is ‘etc.’—all the more. He, however, reads the symbol as *i*.</note> Though Westwood acknowledged the third letter was *c* (that is, not a complete circle), he insisted on reading it as *o*; and the clear *p* after it (given as such by Lhuyd) he claimed was *d*, because, of course, he did not know the Welsh word *petuar* (‘four’), which is as plain there as if it had been written yesterday. I read this inscription as:

->molt<-
->c7cpe<-
->tuaR<-

The word *mol* is probably the Medieval Welsh *moll* (‘tomb’), as seen in the Black Book of Carmarthen [B.B.] 20·8; in Old Welsh *ll* was written as *l*, compare page 134, footnote 2. The six letters at the end are *petuar* (‘four’) as already noted. In Old Welsh this word is spelled *petguar*; but *g* was never pronounced in the word; in Old Welsh orthography *gu* was the conventional symbol for the consonantal *w*, treated as the mutation of *gw*. Although *gu* is old, the unconventional *petuar* here is another indication that the inscription is earlier than the Old Welsh period.

If the first word is *mol* (‘tomb’) and the last *petuar* (‘four’), what are the letters that come between them? My guess is as follows:

<vocab>Taliesin, Early Welsh, Old Welsh, Medieval Welsh, Modern Welsh, Ogam, Dubutuc, Marciau(n), petuar, molt, Westwood, Rhys, Black Book of Carmarthen</vocab>

mol t c & c petuar, that is, ‘the tomb of T(egryn), C(ynien) and others, four (and four others)’. The four others would be Malted, Guadgan, Dubut, and Marciaun, leaving *anterunc* as a connective.

<note>Rhys reads “*molt cic petuar*, which means either ‘the mutton flesh of four’ or ‘a wether [a castrated ram] is flesh of or for four’ ”, p. 145. His usual sense of humor deserted him here. I may say that at first *moltcic petuar* ‘mutton for four’ haunted me, not as a possibility, but as a sort of mocking parody of the unknown solution. It seemed too incongruous to be considered seriously as a possible reading of an inscription on a tombstone. Whatever the solution is, it surely cannot be this.</note>

### IV.

There is, of course, much that is doubtful in the above attempt at explaining the stone—it is not expected that what has baffled all previous attempts should reveal its secret all at once. But the first inscription is, I think, clear; and it is enough for my present purpose, because it is an example of *Welsh*, not *British*, carved in the seventh century.

If my conjecture as to *Egryn* is correct, the stone can perhaps be approximately dated. Egryn is said to be the son of Eneilian, daughter of Cadfan ab Iago (the prince, not the saint). This Cadfan died early in the seventh century; his son, the great Cadwallon (*Cadwalla*), died, according to the *Annales Cambriae*, in 631; we should therefore probably not be far wrong in supposing that Egryn, Cadfan’s grandson and Cadwallon’s nephew, died about 660.

There was no tradition of Welsh being carved in Roman capitals; it had only been written in half-uncial script. Script forms were already in use in Latin inscriptions. The Llanillteyrn stone (Hübner, no. 64), attributed by Rhys probably to the sixth century, has in *Vendumagli* the uncial epsilon (ε) of this stone, and a *d* like that of *dubut* on side 3; but its *g* is a good Insular form. The *Catamanus* inscription at Llangadwaladr, in memory of Cadfan ab Iago, was probably carved soon after his death—there seems to be no ground whatever for the supposition that it was set up by his grandson Cadwaladr. It has an uncial ε with the cross stroke not touching the back <note>as Rhys draws two of the e’s on this stone</note>; it has the <term>R</term> with the curved second part horizontal; it has the square-bottomed u; and the last *m* of *omnium* is a minuscule m, as seen here; Hübner’s plate is a poor drawing of this inscription (no. 149). The *u*’s on the Llangaffo stone (Hübner, no. 148) are even more like our u’s; one *e* is minuscule, the others are uncial like our e’s; but the Llangaffo stone has not been dated.

<note>I do not think Rhys realized the importance of this inscription. He recognized in 1897 that it was Welsh, but refrained from suggesting...</note>

---

### NOTE.

The writing on this stone is half-uncial, of much the same type as the writing of the Book of St. Chad (which in my *Early Welsh Script*, p. 3, p. 4, I have foolishly called "uncial"). The persistent use of uncial *e* (ϵ) may be due to the difficulty of cutting the "bow" of half-uncial *e* (e) on stone. The two uncial *n*'s in *Cingen*, in contrast to the half-uncial *n* of *celen*, etc., may be a deliberate way to distinguish the name from the rest of the sentence, the equivalent in fact of our
->CINGEN<-
->lies here.<-

That an inscription with two uncial *n*'s and the remaining *n* being half-uncial must be older than an inscription with all the *n*'s in half-uncial is not impossible, but it is by no means certain. Scribes sometimes found the half-uncial *n* to be an awkward letter. In particular, the combination *in* was so similar to *m* that some Insular scribes preferred **in** (with an uncial *n*) while others wrote **In** (with a tall *i*). Furthermore, half-uncial *n* so closely resembled half-uncial *r* that Continental scribes used the uncial *n* in their half-uncial script, while Insular scribes often substituted a "cursive" *r* (with the shaft extending below the line). In short, if an uncial letter is to appear in a half-uncial script, one might expect that letter to be *n*.

W. M. LINDSAY.

<summary>This page concludes the author's comments on the need for new drawings of the inscription and provides a technical note by Professor W. M. Lindsay regarding the stone's half-uncial script. Lindsay explains that variations in the letter 'n' were common scribal practices and do not definitively prove the inscription's age.</summary>

## APPENDIX II.

### THE SUREXIT MEMORANDUM.

The oldest known piece of written Welsh is the second entry on page 141 of the *Book of St. Chad*. A reduced facsimile of the page is included here; the original measures about 12 inches by 9, but this small plate will allow the reader to follow the argument. The block was made from an unaltered photograph of the page, kindly sent to me for this purpose by the Dean of Lichfield. Large collotype facsimiles of the page are provided in Evans and Rhys’s *Book of Llan Dâv*, page xliii, and in Lindsay’s *Early Welsh Script*, page 46.

->I.<-

The manuscript contains the Gospels in Latin, written in a large half-uncial hand. Palaeographers do not agree on its date. The editors of the Palaeographical Society date it to "about 700." E. Heinrich Zimmermann, in his *Vorkarolingische Miniaturen*, Berlin, 1916 (four folio volumes of plates and one octavo volume of text, of which there is a copy in the Bodleian), dates it to the "second quarter of the 8th century." However, Dr. H. M. Bannister of the Bodleian, to whom I am grateful for this information, frequently finds himself "at odds" with Zimmermann’s dating. Zimmermann bases his work on miniatures, "which often slavishly imitate the original model before the copyist." Professor Lindsay agrees that Zimmermann’s dates are unreliable. In his *Early Welsh Script*, page 3, he leans toward an early date; though he clearly considers it improbable, he does not entirely rule out the possibility that the manuscript could be attributed "to Teilo’s time"—Teilo died around 580. The fact is that Insular scribes, once they had established their beautiful half-uncial hand, maintained it very conservatively for a long period. It is acknowledged that the study of these early Insular manuscripts is only in its early stages, so no one can be definitive about them. There is sufficient historical evidence that the *Book of Lindisfarne* was written around 700; however, although the handwriting is similar, the *Book of St. Chad* seems to belong to an earlier generation. I have examined facsimiles of its pages in the *Book of Llan Dâv*, in Dr. Scrivener’s *Codex S. Ceaddae Latinus*, and the Dean of Lichfield’s *Story of St. Chad’s Gospels*, and I have found no instance of the n-like *r* that is common in the *Book of Lindisfarne* and shown in the facsimile on page 134 above. The *r* in the *Book of St. Chad* is the uncial R or, rarely, the sprawling form *r*, as seen in <term>petuar</term> and <term>tricet</term> in the inscription above, side 4, page 260; this form is found on the early 7th-century stone of Catamanus

<summary>This page introduces the "Surexit Memorandum," the oldest surviving example of written Welsh, located in the Book of St. Chad. It explores the difficulties in dating the manuscript, contrasting various scholarly theories and analyzing specific palaeographical details such as the formation of the letter 'r'.</summary>
<keywords>Surexit Memorandum, Book of St. Chad, Welsh Palaeography, half-uncial script, manuscript dating, E. Heinrich Zimmermann, Professor Lindsay, Insular scribes</keywords>

--- Page 279 ---
<meta>This page continues the palaeographical analysis of the Book of Saint Chad, comparing its script to the Book of Lindisfarne and describing the historical record of its gift to the church at Llandaff.</meta>

<header>Taliesin.</header>
<page-num>269</page-num>

(Cadfan ap Iago, see page 266), and is common in sixth-century manuscripts. The clubbed tops of strokes are perfectly flat in the Book of Saint Chad, but in the Book of Lindisfarne, they tend to incline upwards, sometimes presenting a saw-like appearance like the top serifs of old-face type; see *Facsimiles of Biblical MSS. in the Brit. Mus.*, plate xi. Clearly, the Book of Saint Chad is earlier than the Book of Lindisfarne, but how much earlier does not yet seem possible to determine.

### II.

The Welsh entries were written in the manuscript when it was at Llandaff. It has been in the possession of the Church of Lichfield since the episcopate of Wynsige, 974–992; and his signature, “† Wynsige presul,” on page 1, “most probably,” as the Dean suggests, “marks the reception of the book,” *op. cit.*, page 11.

Page 141 contains the last words of Saint Matthew’s Gospel, placed by the original scribe in the middle of the page within a border, as seen in our plate. The first entry on the page records the gift of the book to Llandaff. It reads as follows:

->Ostenditur híc quod emit + gelhi + filius · arihtiud · hóc euange<-
->lium · de cingal · et dedit · illi *pro* illo equm optimum · et dedit<-
->*pro* anima sua istum euangelium · deo et sancti teliaui · super<-
->altare<-
->+ gelhi + filius · Arihtiud . . , . . , et + cincénn + filius ·<-
->gripiud . . ,<-

> ‘It is shown here that Gelhi, son of Arihtiud, bought this Gospel from Cingal, and gave him a “best horse” for it, and for the sake of his soul, he gave this Gospel to God and Saint Teliau<note>Taking the genitive form to be an error for the dative; otherwise, the *et* (and) connects dissimilar terms, and the order of words is improbable in Latin of this late date.</note> upon the altar.
> 
> (Witnesses:) Gelhi, son of Arihtiud . . . and Cincenn, son of Gripiud . . .’

The handwriting in which this deed of gift is written is similar to that of the Bodleian *Liber Commonei*, dated 817, and was therefore assigned by Bradshaw, undoubtedly correctly, to about the same date. The *Surexit* entry, which follows, was assumed by him to be later, though the hand has every appearance of being much older. The δ at the end of the agreement means, according to Professor Lindsay, *op. cit.* page 46, ‘deest’ (it is missing), and refers to the obelus mark before the continuation

This consists of the names of the witnesses near the bottom of the page, for which there was no room above the text. The Anglo-Saxon names which crowd the bottom of the page around this part of our memorandum belong, of course, to the Lichfield period. The memorandum reads as follows:

> Surexit tutbulc filius liuit hagener tutri dierchim · tir telih · 
> haioid ilau
> elcu filius gelhig haluidt iugureτ amgucaⁿt pel amtanndi ho diued <margin>E T?</margin>
> diprotant gener tutri o guir imguodant ir degion guragun tage
> rodesit elcu guetig equs tres uache, tres uache nouidligi **nam** ir
> ni be câs igridu dimedichat guetig hit did braut **grefiat** gue
> tig nis minn tutbulc hai cenetl in **ois oisou ð**
>
> † teliau t' gurgint t' cinhilinn t' sps t', tota familia teliaui,
> delaicis
> numin m' aidan, t' signou m' iacou t' berthutis t' cinda t' quicumque custo-
> dierit benedictus erit, quicumque frangerit maladictus erit 3''—

Which may be translated as follows (the numbers in brackets refer to the notes):

> Tutbulch, son of Liuit and son-in-law of Tutri, arose to claim the land of Telich, which was (1) in the hand (2) of Elcu, son of Gelhig, and the tribe of Iudguoret. They contended (3) long (4) about it (5). At last, they dispossessed (6) the son-in-law of Tutri of [his] right (7). The good men (8) urged (9) one another, "Let us make (10) peace." Elcu afterwards gave (11) a horse, three cows (12), and three newly-calved (13) cows, only (14) so that there might be (15) no hatred (16) between them (17) regarding [his] possession (18) afterwards until (19) the day of doom. Tutbulch and his people will require no title afterwards for ever (20).
>
> † Teliau witness, Gurgint witness, Cinhilinn witness, Spiritus witness, etc., the whole family of Teliau; from the laity, Numin son of Aidan witness, Signou son of Iacou witness, Berthutis witness, Cinda witness; whoever shall keep it shall be blessed, whoever shall break it shall be cursed.

### NOTES.

(1) *haioid*, see my *Gram.*, p. 287; also above pp. 180, 189; medieval *a oed* 'which was'.

(2) *ilau*; the *i* is not the modern *i* 'to', which is *di* in Old Welsh; if *i* is not for *ī* (that is, *in*) as in line 6, it may be that *ilau* is for *il-lau*, with the assimilation of *n* to *l*, as with *m* in *ymywn* (*m=mm*), see *Gram.*, p. 416; *l* for *ll*, see note 4. The form *lau* shows that the final *v* had disappeared in the earliest period after *w*, though it is still preserved medially where *o* takes the place of *aw*, as in *llof-rudd* 'red-handed'.

(3) *amgucant* is undoubtedly the medieval *amugant* 'they contended'; the *g* represents inorganic γ, as in *petguar*, see above p. 265,

--- Page 283 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological commentary and linguistic analysis of the Old Welsh legal text found in the Book of Saint Chad, specifically focusing on the etymology and grammar of individual words as interpreted by scholars like John Rhys.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>271</page-num>
<header>Taliesin.</header>

which might come even before vocalic *u* (of either sound) as in *Catguc* Book of Llan Daf, 161. Rhys’s explanation of the verb as *amguocan-t*, 3rd person singular aorist of *am-o-gan* (*gogan*), Book of Llan Daf, p. xliv, is not only improbable in meaning (*gogan* is ‘satire, slander’, etc.), but involves the improbability that *-ant* here is not the same as in the next line.

(4) *pel*, medieval and modern *pell* ‘far’, used of time, as still in *bellach* ‘henceforth’, as pointed out by Rhys; compare Breton *pell a zo* ‘it was a long time ago’; *l* is regularly used for *ll* in Old Welsh, compare above p. 134, fn. 2.

(5) *amtunndi*; Rhys notes that *tir* (land), now masculine, might have been feminine also, since it was originally neuter. The *d* is not used in the medieval and modern inflection of *tan* (under), but there is no reason why it should not have been used once with all prepositions. The *nn* is written because the syllable is closed by the two consonants $n \delta$. (As noted above, p. 263, *nitanam* is probably the form of the *first* person, in which $d = \delta$ is not used at all).

(6) *diprotant*. Rhys has no note on this word. It might be for *dipriotant* from *priawt* (owner/property), from Latin *prīvātus*; but the omission of the *i* makes this doubtful. It is more probably a compound of *brawd* ‘judgment’, which has given *difrodi* ‘to despoil’; we have seen that the initial might have been doubled after *di-*, see p. 214, note on l. 17, and double *b* would become *p*, now *b*, as in *aber*, etc., *Gram.*, p. 132.

(7) *gwir* as a noun was used in the sense of ‘right, justice’, e.g. *kedwis gwir y dir ae deyrnged, Myv.* 248 b 33, ‘he kept his right to his land and his tribute’, compare 238 a 27, B.B. 68·1; *dyvôt brennhin Morcannhuc . . . . dy gunethur guir ha cyfreith B. L. D.* 120 ‘the king of Morgannwg shall come to do justice and right’. In Breton, the noun *gwir* means “right, founded claim,” Troude, s.v.

(8) *degion* ‘goodmen’, plural of *da*, originally *dag* ‘good’. This plural is written *deon* in B.T. 33·21, see above p. 198. The *ir* is the definite article.

(9) *imguodant*. Rhys “with much hesitation” connected this with *dy-wed-af* ‘I say’. It is certain that the *d* cannot stand for modern *d*, but must mean modern $\delta$ <note>the voiced 'th' sound in 'this'</note>. The stem of *gwe\delta-i* ‘prayer’ is *\*gwo\delta-* (cognate with Greek $\pi o\theta\acute{\epsilon}\omega$, *Gram.* p. 130), and seems to fit here, compare English *pray, prithee*. The prefix *im-* is reflexive.

(10) *guragun* ‘let us make.’ The old form of the stem *gwna-* of *gwnâf* ‘I make, do’ is *gwray-* (cognate with English *work*, Greek $\acute{\epsilon}\rho\gamma o\nu$); the *r* remained in Cornish and Breton, but it became *n* early in Welsh, and we appear to have here the only trace of *r* except perhaps in the old perfect *guoreu* and possibly in Early Welsh forms *gwrith, gwreith*.

--- Page 284 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological analysis of Old Welsh terms, specifically focusing on notes 11 through 19, which detail verb forms, nouns, and paleographic observations found in the manuscript.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>272</page-num>
<header>Taliesin.</header>

copied by scribes who did not understand them; see *Grammar*, pp. 152, 367.

(11) *rodesit*, an old extended form of the 3rd person singular aorist active; elsewhere it occurs only as a poetic archaism, *Grammar*, p. 326, ii (5).

(12) *uache*; *ch* here equals *cc*, and *e* equals *ae*. The word is the Latin *vaccae*.

(13) *nouidligi*. The Welsh *llo* ('calf') is for \*lloe (*Grammar* § 78 i (1)), which represents the original \*logi-, although the Irish *lōeg* implies \*loig-. The form \*newyδlo would be an adjective like the modern *cyflo* ('with calf'), and its plural \*newyδly (to agree with *vaccae*) is represented regularly by *nouidligi*. The final *-i* cannot be the verbal noun ending, which was *-iv*, written as *-im*, as in *erchim*, line 1.

(14) *nam*, with the vertical contraction for *m*, is doubtless itself a conventional abbreviation of *nammuin*, as in *nam seith*, B.T. 54·24, for *namyn seith* 55·7·12, etc. Though used literally in one of our poems (see p. 186), Irish shows that the word meant 'only' from the earliest times. The meaning here seems to be that he 'only' did it for the sake of peace.

(15) *ir ni be*; *ir* is the preposition *yr*, now *er*, used with *ni* as a conjunction meaning 'so that not'; *be* is for *bei*, the 3rd person singular imperfect subjunctive, since *e* seems to be the oldest spelling of the diphthong *ei* (see p. 169, last line, p. 263, note on *celen*), and compare *F.A.B.* ii, 2·7·8, *per*, *couer* for *peir*, *coueir* rhyming with *meir*. Compare also *Teliau* for *Teiliau*, now *Teilo*.

(16) *câs*; the small circumflex is in the original ink; perhaps it is intended for the ordinary accent ('); in any case it seems to show that a vowel was lengthened before *s* in a monosyllable at this early period.

(17) *igridu* 'between them'; see *Grammar*, p. 405.

(18) *di medichat*; the *di* here is 'from' representing the original \*dē; in the old spelling it cannot be distinguished from *di* 'to', from the original \*do. Rhys has gone seriously wrong in analyzing *medichat* as *medic* ('medicus') + *hat* which he confesses should be *-hagat* at this period; 'reconciliation' from *medicus* is rather far-fetched as well. The root appears to be \*med- 'to enjoy, possess'; from which comes a verb-stem *meδych-* (compare *heδychu* from *heδ*); from this comes the abstract noun *meδychad*, spelled *medichat* 'possession' (Modern Welsh *meddu* 'to possess', *meddiant* 'possession').

(19) *hit*. The shaft is that of *h*, not *b*; whether the completion of the circle was intentional (to make *bet*, which is synonymous) or not, the scribe seems to have decided on *hit*. The Dean writes in a letter: "In the same heavier ink <note>which appears when he dips his pen</note> he (I suppose it is the original scribe) has touched up the sides of the first letter of *hit*, but not the cross filling stroke at the bottom, as though he desired to emphasize that the letter was intended for an *h*, not a *b*."

<meta>This page continues the philological notes from the previous section, skipping from note 16 to note 20 and then providing an extensive analysis of proper names and phonetic structures found in the manuscript.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>273</page-num>
<header># Taliesin.</header>

(20) *in ois oisou*. It is interesting to note that the Biblical *yn oes oesoedd* is so ancient a translation of *in saecula saeculorum*. The old plural of *oes* was *oisou*, which appears as *oesseu* in the Book of Taliesin: *oes oesseu* 15·15, *yn oes oesseu* 19·9, quoted above on page 184. The latter is the exact expression used here; and if it occurred only in the poem, it might have been considered comparatively late.

t' = *testis* (witness); m' = *map* 'son'. I have used the apostrophe to denote the contraction mark above the letter.

**Proper names.** — It should be noted first that the name *Gelhig* occurs both in the deed of gift and the *Surexit* entry, and it has the older form with the final *g* in the latter. Rhys is strangely puzzled by this name, but its composition seems quite clear. As *l* stands for *ll*, the first element seems to be *Gell-*, which is seen as the second element in *Anda-gelli* (on the Gelli Dywyll stone, see Rhys, *Welsh Philology*, page 388), the meaning of which is unknown; and the second element is *hig*, Modern Welsh *hy* ('bold') from \*segos (related to Greek ἔχω, Sanskrit *sáhaḥ* 'might', etc.), which occurs as the first element in the Gaulish *Sego-maros*, *Sego-vellauni*, and others.

While γ, written as *g*, appears at the end of a word after a short *i* (modern *y*) in Early Welsh, there is no trace of it anywhere after a long ī (modern *i*) as in *rhī* ('king') from *rīg-* (related to Latin *rēg-*). This is because the quality of the long *i* was different, being more closed, and the *g* did not become γ, but rather the consonantal *i*, which was merged into the *i* before it. Hence we have *Tut-ri* here from *Teuto-rīg-*.

*El-cu* is an Irish name, which also appears as *El-con*, and later in a Welsh form *El-ci*; see the index of the *Book of Llan Dav*. The second element is *cū* ('hound'), stem *con*, Welsh *ci*.

*Gurgint* is *Gwrin* in Modern Welsh. The name occurs as *Guurgint Barmbtruch* in the Welsh genealogies (*Y Cymmrodor*, volume ix, page 178). There is a *Tref-wrin* or Wrinston near Cardiff, and a St. Gwrin is the patron of Llanwrin, Montgomeryshire (*Lives of British Saints*, volume iii, page 208).

*Cinhilinn* is the Irish name *Cūchulinn*, using the stem *Con-* instead of the nominative *Cū-* for the first element. The name usually occurs in Welsh as *Cuhelyn* (*Cuhelin* in the *Black Book of Carmarthen* 9·9). In the *Book of Llan Dav*, page 76, there is a document witnessed by Dubricius "with his clerics Vbeluiuo, Merchguino, and Cuelino"; of these three, the second, if not the first, became a cleric at Llandaff under Teilo, along with others, of whom Cuelinus may be our Cinhilinn.

*Numin?* is modern *Nefyn*. *Aidan* is the Irish name. *Signou* has the common element *-gnou*, also appearing as *-gnoe*, and in Modern Welsh as *-no*, as in *Tud-no*, *Mach-no*, etc. *Cinda* looks like an Irish name; a document from the time of Teilo's successor Oudoceus is witnessed by a layman named *Condaf*, in which the *-f*, which cannot be old in any case, seems to be the addition of the compiler (*Book of Llan Dav*, page 140).

<meta>This page continues the philological examination of proper names in the Llandaff manuscript and begins a comparative analysis of the dates and scripts of the manuscript's Welsh entries, specifically contrasting the "deed of gift" with the "Surexit" memorandum.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>274</page-num>
<header>Taliesin.</header>

Names in the deed of gift: *Arihtiud*; it is difficult to explain the first *i* except as the silent *i* between spirants (see page 165, note on line 22, *gollychynt*). If so, *ht* is the spirant *th* (written as *dt* in *luidt* in the *Surexit* entry, line 2), and the first element is *Arth*-; the second is *iud*, modern *uδ*, as in *Gripiud*, now *Gruffuδ*. Thus, the name would now be *Arthuδ*. *Cingal* may be an error for *Cingual*, modern *Cynwal*. *Cincenn*, see above, page 262. As both *Cincenn* and *Gripiud* are quite common names, it is absurd to assume that this *Cincenn ap Gripiud* is related to the *Griphiud ap Cincen* who died, according to the *Annales*, in 814, and especially to imagine that in such a case they would likely be contemporaries.

### III.

As the Welsh entries were presumably written after the manuscript was presented to the Church of Llandaff, it was natural to assume that the deed of gift is older than the *Surexit* memorandum which follows it on the page. Bradshaw dated the former to the early ninth century and the latter to the tenth century (*Collected Papers*, page 460). Dr. Evans suggested that the memorandum is a copy of a document from Teilo’s time (B.L.D., 1893, page xliv). Seebohm, in his *Tribal System in Wales* (1895, page 182), suggests that Gelhi from the deed of gift may be the same person as Gelhig from the *Surexit* entry, whose son held the land claimed by Tutbulch. In that case, of course, the document dates centuries after Teilo, and the witness “Teliau” is “the saint long at rest,” just as “Deus omnipotens” (*God Almighty*) is a witness to other deeds in the manuscript (*Tribal System*, page 179). Bradshaw’s “tenth century” date is rather late even in this case, and Seebohm can only suggest that “the second record may have been written after the transaction” (page 182), where “after” must mean anywhere from 60 to 100 years later!

Professor Lindsay, in his *Early Welsh Script* (1912, pages 2, 3), ventured to differ from the authorities and suggest that the *Surexit* entry is older than the deed of gift, “on three grounds: (1) the appearance of the ink, (2) the script, and (3) its position on the page.” The first ground is inconclusive, because later insertions in manuscripts often appear older than the original writing, simply because the ink is of a poorer quality. Regarding the second, Professor Lindsay says: “The validity of the second will, I imagine, be admitted at once; for these crude majuscule letters have a far older appearance than the minuscules of the deed of gift.” He adds (page 3), “Certainly the script is, to my mind, exactly the kind of script that would likely be used at a quite early time.” This seems to me incontestable. The *r* in the *Surexit* entry is the majuscule R throughout; there is no trace of the *n-like r*, either short or with an extended stroke as in the *Ostenditur* of the deed of gift; the *s* is the tall s, as in the Book

<summary>This page analyzes the orthography of Old Welsh names like Arihtiud and debates the chronological order of the Llandaff manuscript entries, supporting Professor Lindsay's theory that the Surexit entry features an older style of script than the deed of gift.</summary>
<keywords>Arihtiud, paleography, Llandaff Manuscript, Surexit memorandum, Henry Bradshaw, Professor Lindsay, Welsh orthography, majuscule script, minuscule script</keywords>

--- Page 287 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of the Welsh entries in the Book of St. Chad, specifically addressing the paleographical arguments regarding the dating and sequence of the "Surexit" memorandum and the deed of gift.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>275</page-num>
<header>Taliesin.</header>

of Lindisfarne (see the facsimile above, p. 134), and it appears rarely in the Book of St. Chad—not the ninth and tenth-century *p* form; and the *f* is distinctly antique. The difficulty is understanding why Bradshaw dated this hand as *tenth century.*

Regarding the argument based on the position of the entry on the page, Professor Lindsay invites his readers "to imagine for themselves the appearance the page presented before any entries were made on it, and to consider what particular part the writer of the earliest entry would probably choose. He would not be hampered by a lack of space; the whole page, with the exception of the middle portion, lay blank before him. . . . Would he not then place it exactly where the *Surexit tutbulc*, etc., was placed, with the record itself above and the witnesses' signatures below the already occupied middle portion?" This is not in itself convincing; but it becomes more so when we ask the supplementary question: Would the scribe, with all the space available within the border, be likely to crowd his entry at the very top, as the deed of gift is crowded?

Assuming the priority of the *Surexit* entry, the difficulty remains as to how it could have been written before the book was presented to the Church. Professor Lindsay suggests that "dedit" <note>gave</note> is used for "restituit" <note>restored</note>—the deed records its restoration to the Church; and he refers to other examples of "gift" being used instead of "restoration" in such entries in manuscripts. Still, this is at least a little improbable. A much more likely explanation seems to me to be that the deed of gift is not the original deed but a *copy* of it. This supposition solves the difficulties in the simplest possible way: it is consistent with the *original* deed of gift being older than the *Surexit* entry, however old that may be; and it accounts for the deed we have being in a much later hand than that entry.

I had arrived at this point in my attempt to solve the riddle of these entries when I wrote to the Dean of Lichfield, the Very Reverend H. E. Savage, D.D., who has been working on the manuscript, briefly stating the problem as it presented itself to me. He very kindly sent me, with his reply, a copy of his valuable paper, *The Story of St. Chad's Gospels*, 17 pages large quarto, with six plates, contributed to the *Transactions of the Birmingham Archaeological Society* in February 1915. In this paper, he contests Professor Lindsay's theory; he holds, rightly, that the appearance of the ink is "quite inconclusive"; as to the position on the page, he says that "opinions may differ rather widely on a probability of this kind"; and regarding the script, Bradshaw's "verdict carries too much weight to be lightly set aside", especially since Professor Lindsay adduces no evidence in support of his view. Evidence regarding the antiquity of the script is, however, not lacking; and the entry contains not only the older spelling *Gelhig* of this

<sig>T 2</sig>

<summary>This page evaluates paleographical and structural arguments regarding the chronological order of the Welsh entries in the Book of St. Chad, specifically weighing Professor Lindsay’s spatial analysis against the possibility that the deed of gift is a later copy.</summary>
