<meta>This page continues the analysis of the dating and placement of the Welsh entries in the Book of St. Chad, focusing on the physical evidence of the stylus rules on the parchment to determine the order in which the "Surexit" entry and the deed of gift were recorded.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>276</page-num>
<header>Taliesin.</header>

name, but archaic forms which we can hardly admit to have been even copied in the tenth century, knowing as we do the modernizing habits of Welsh scribes. I communicated the conclusions I had reached regarding the script and language to the Dean and pointed out that Professor Lindsay had not used the strongest argument regarding the position on the page. If the deed of gift had already been on the page, filling the whole space between the left and right borders, the writer of the *Surexit* entry underneath it would almost certainly have followed his lead and begun his lines in the very margin. The fact that he begins at about the same distance from the margin as the half-uncial text must mean that there was nothing but that text on the page when he wrote his entry. It seemed to me that the scribe of this entry had used the vertical stylus rule made by the scribe of the text to mark the beginning of his lines, but that the scribe of the entry began his lines just outside the rule, while the original scribe began just inside it. The Dean examined the manuscript and found that the rule extends down as far as the *s* of *saeculi* in the last line of the text, and no further. This explains why *teliau*, below the text, is a little nearer the margin than the upper part of the entry—the scribe had nothing to guide him there. The Dean examined the manuscript again under favorable conditions and found that the stylus line "extended upward to the first line of the *Surexit* entry, but apparently not above that. The top of it is traceable between the *S* and the *u* of *Surexit*, running parallel with the downstroke of the *S*." He confirmed this later in words which I take the liberty of quoting: "I feel quite sure that this is so, for I had the verification of other eyes besides my own; among them was the keen sight of a Rossall boy who was present when I examined the manuscript. He could see clearly without a magnifying glass what I (and others) could only be certain of with that aid. And he was positive that there was no trace of the rule to be seen above that point." Now, that seems to me to account for the position on the page adopted by the scribe for the beginning of his entry. He began at the top of the stylus mark, although it did not leave room before the lines of the Gospel text for the whole of his record. I think it will be agreed that this is quite conclusive. It shows exactly why the *Surexit* scribe began where he did; he did not begin there because another entry had already been crowded in at the top, but rather that entry was crowded in at the top because he had begun there.

It is now clear why the scribe of the deed of gift spread his entry across the whole space between the borders: there was no stylus rule to mark off the margin, and his space was limited. It had occurred to me, as noted above, that he was merely copying the

<summary>The author argues that the "Surexit" entry was written before the deed of gift, using the physical evidence of a stylus rule on the manuscript to prove that the scribe was following existing margins. The placement of the deed of gift at the top of the page was a result of the "Surexit" entry already occupying the guided space below.</summary>

--- Page 289 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument regarding the physical evidence on the page of the Book of St. Chad, specifically the placement of the "Surexit" entry relative to the original text and other additions, providing further paleographic evidence that the entry is a copy.</meta>

<page-num>277</page-num>
<header>Taliesin.</header>

the original deed. The Dean agrees; it had occurred to him as well, independently of my suggestion, which he had forgotten at the time. He writes: “It has the appearance of being an abbreviated extract. The dots after <term>Arihtiud</term> and after <term>Gripiud</term> in the last line suggest this. And the full account of the gift, with the names of all the witnesses, may well have been written at the end of the book as a kind of <term>colophon</term>, in what was later, at any rate, bound as a second volume that was still in existence when the Sacrist’s roll at Lichfield was compiled in 1345. The end of St. Matthew’s Gospel does not seem to be an appropriate place for such a record of the <term>Codex</term> coming into the possession of the Church of Llandaff. And the statement has every appearance of referring to the original gift, rather than to the restoration of a lost <term>MS.</term>” It seems strange that the significance of the dots and commas after the names of the only two witnesses mentioned had not occurred to anyone before; it is one of those illuminating discoveries which are obvious once made and decisive in their implications.

I think, therefore, that it can be said that the priority of the *Surexit* entry has been proven beyond a doubt. The question of the entry's date remains. The script is clearly separated by an entire period from the early ninth-century <term>minuscules</term> above it; I am unable to guess what late characteristics Bradshaw imagined he saw in it; it seems old-fashioned even for the eighth century, for while <term>majuscule</term> forms continued to be used in formal writing, the complete absence of <term>minuscule</term> <term>r</term>, for example, in the careless ordinary handwriting of this entry is hardly conceivable at that time. In the current state of our knowledge of early <term>Insular script</term>, it does not seem possible to say more than that the entry is probably, as suggested in *Early Welsh Script*, p. 3, a very early copy. Regarding the question of whether it is the original document or a copy, Professor Lindsay sends me the following note: “A faint indication that the Teilo entry is a copy of the original is the abbreviation er̄ ‘erit’. This unusual symbol would naturally be used only if the scribe were pressed for space. However, there is plenty of space on the page of the St. Chad book. (This I mention in my *Notae Latinae*, p. 340.)” To this one might perhaps add that the vertical zigzag <term>contraction</term> of <term>m</term> was normally used only at the end of a line where there was no room for the wide ordinary <term>m</term>; but in our entry, it occurs in <term>nam</term> in line 4 before the end of the line, where there was plenty of room for the <term>m</term>. Taken together, these peculiarities of <term>abbreviation</term> provide strong presumptive evidence that the entry is a copy.

The assumption that the witness Teliau is “the saint long at rest” is only made necessary by the supposition that the entry is the original document. The manuscript itself is probably

<summary>The author argues that the "Surexit" entry in the Book of St. Chad is an early copy rather than the original deed, citing the use of unnecessary abbreviations and space-saving contractions as evidence. The Dean of Lichfield supports this by noting that the entry's placement and punctuation suggest it is an abbreviated extract from a lost original volume.</summary>

--- Page 290 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of the Surexit entry in the Book of St. Chad, arguing that the inclusion of Teilo's (Teliau's) name in the witness list suggests he was alive at the time the original document was drafted, rather than being invoked as a deceased saint.</meta>

<page-num>278</page-num>
<header>*Taliesin.*</header>

...is later than Teilo's time, belonging perhaps to the middle of the following century; and if the entry is a record made at the time of the agreement, Teilo could not be a witness to it in person. If, on the other hand, the entry is a copy, it may just as well be a copy of a document from Teilo's time as a copy of a later document. The assumption that the witness is "the saint long at rest" is not naturally probable; there is no comparison for it, as "Deus omnipotens testis" <note>God Almighty is witness</note> cannot seriously be considered an exact comparison. In fact, "Spiritus testis" <note>The Spirit is witness</note> occurs in this very list, where "Spiritus" is undoubtedly the Holy Spirit. Not a single example has been brought forward of a dead saint's name being invoked as a witness to a deed in the same way the name of the Deity is invoked. On the other hand, deeds or copies of deeds existed at Llandaff in the twelfth century in which the living Teilo's name heads the list of witnesses; it appears as "Teliaus archieps" <note>Archbishop Teilo</note> in <term>B. L. D.</term>, 121, 122, or "scs Teliaus" <note>Saint Teilo</note>, p. 126, or "archieps Teliaus", p. 127, the titles "archbishop" and "saint" having obviously been added by the compiler. Now, since examples of this can be cited, it must be admitted as more probable than something for which it can only be said that "it might have been"; and in this case, I think it may be stated that, if not for supposed difficulties with the date, no one would have thought of suggesting that Teilo in the list of witnesses is anything other than what it seems to be. The copy is more accurate than those in the <term>B. L. D.</term>: the name is simply Teilo—not "Saint Teilo." There is no suggestion that it is an invocation of the sainted patron—it is surely more like a copy of the signature of the living man.

If the <term>MS.</term> <note>manuscript</note> was written in the seventh century, the Gelhi who presented it to Llandaff cannot, of course, be the same person as the Gelhig who is the father of Elcu, one of the parties to the dispute in Teilo's time. But he may have been a descendant of Elcu, as the name Gelhig ran in the family; and the original deed may have been in his possession, as it would be his legal title to the land. In that case, it is possible that he may have had the deed copied into the book even before he gave it to the Church. Indeed, there appears to be no particular reason why the Church authorities should write it there; it concerns none of their property, and they already had plenty of documents under Teilo's hand that were of more interest to them. But it is easy to understand why an abbreviated copy of the deed of gift should be entered above it later; this shows that the book was a gift from Gelhi, and thus explains why a copy of a title deed belonging to him appears on the page. Seebohm points out that there is a Telich in Gower (<term>B. L. D.</term> 239); but as there was at least one other Telich (ib. 125, 255), it is not certain that this is the one meant. The claimant, Tutbulch son of Liuit,

<summary>The author argues that Teilo's name in the witness list refers to the living man, not a posthumous invocation, suggesting the document is a copy of an older deed. He also proposes that the manuscript's donor, Gelhi, may have had his own family land titles copied into the book before donating it to the church.</summary>

--- Page 291 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument for the 6th-century origin of the Surexit entry, moving from the witness of Teilo to the identification of Tutbulch and Liuit through historical records and place-names in Monmouthshire.</meta>

<page-num>279</page-num>
<header>Taliesin.</header>

...seems to have lived in Monmouthshire; as Seebohm points out, in a record probably from between 961 and 967, Morgan Hen is said to have restored to Llandaff territories belonging to it in the time of Dubricius, Teilo, and Oudoceus. Among these is "Machumur, i.e., Lann Liuit," its boundary reaching "across to Is Guaessaf of Liguallaun, son of Tutbulch," *ibid.* 241. Here we have the names of Tutbulch and Liuit; and Lann Liuit belonged to Llandaff in the sixth century. It adjoined land belonging to a son of Tutbulch. Lann Liuit was, of course, the ecclesiastical name; such names usually date from the foundation or dedication (in this case probably under Dubricius, given its position near Llanvaenor), so that we may conclude that the place was named after a sixth-century Liuit. I am not aware of any occurrence of the name Liuit except in this ancient place-name and in our entry. The founder of Lann Liuit seems to be the only Liuit known; it is therefore reasonable to suppose that the claimant Tutbulch ap Liuit was his son, and hence a contemporary of Teilo. Liguallaun ap Tutbulch owned land bordering on Lann Liuit; his name had become traditional when the boundary was drawn out at the restoration in 961–967; he cannot therefore be the Riuguallaun ap Tutbulch, *ibid.* 264, who was living in the reign of Rhydderch ap Iestyn, 1023–1033. It need not be assumed that Liguallaun is an error—*l* and *r* are not easily confused; the first element of his name is the same as that of Liuit's; this would be natural if he were his grandson. Lastly, we have seen that two of the witnesses, one cleric and one layman, may possibly be identified with a clerical and a lay witness of other deeds of the same period; but I do not think that any one of the names can, with the least likelihood, be identified with any name in the later records of Llandaff.

The probability, then, is that this entry is a copy of a document from Teilo's time. There would be a reason for it being written in Welsh: the parties to the agreement would probably not understand Latin. If this is so, the view maintained above regarding the vernacular of the period needs no other proof; the language of the sixth century was identical with that of the Taliesin poems.

<summary>The author identifies historical figures Tutbulch and Liuit in Llandaff records to argue that the Surexit entry is a copy of a 6th-century document. This suggests that the Welsh language of that era, used in legal agreements for laymen, was the same as that found in the Taliesin poems.</summary>

## CORRECTIONS AND ADDITIONS.

Page 1, line 11 of text; for skeleton read skeleton
Page 6, fn. This footnote is perfectly correct as far as it goes; but a closer examination of the poem clearly reveals the metrical scheme. The first twenty lines have nine syllables each, forming <term>“cyhydedd naw ban”</term>, in stanzas of four, two, five, five, and four lines respectively; see *F.A.B.*, ii, pp. 3, 4. These are followed by a stanza of <term>“gwawdodyn byr”</term> consisting of two lines of nine syllables and a <term>“cyhydedd hir”</term> unit of nineteen. It is in the middle of this stanza that the supposed “change of meter at 4.7” occurs. But the stanza is immediately followed by a similar one of two nine-syllable lines and a nineteen-syllable unit. This is followed by five units of <term>“cyhydedd hir”</term>. Thus, the poem begins in the meter <term>“cyhydedd naw ban”</term> and ends in <term>“cyhydedd hir”</term>, the transition from the one to the other being smoothly made by two stanzas of <term>“gwawdodyn byr”</term>, which is a standard meter consisting of a familiar combination of the two.

Page 22, line 4; for Sainsbury read Saintsbury
Page 30, line 1; though, etc. The second alternative is far less likely than the first. The false concords quoted on this page are much more naturally explained by the assumption that the inscribers had no awareness of the difference in form between the <term>nominative</term> and <term>genitive</term> cases. In later inscriptions, *-i* is added even to consonant stems in the nominative, as in *crux Salvatoris quae preparavit Samsoni Apati . . . pro anima Iuthahelo Rex* (see *Arch. Camb.* 1899, p. 148), by which is meant *quam preparavit Samson Abbas . . . pro anima Iuthaheli Regis*. It is admitted that this is merely bad Latin; I maintain that this is the only sensible explanation for similar phenomena in earlier inscriptions. And it shows not merely that the inscribers confused Latin declensions, but that they had lost the sense of distinctions of case.

Page 32, line 3; for despised read despised
Page 32, line 25; for tataguen read *tat aguen*

The frontispiece shows that the manuscript clearly contains two words, *tat aguen*, which is of course correct, for the expression is not a compound but a noun with a dependent genitive. Zimmer is not to be trusted in small matters of this kind. There ought to be a diplomatic reproduction of the whole text; this is work that Dr. Evans should do instead of wasting his

Page 62, line 14; delete one **c** in manuscript.

Page 70, line 15; Urien had, etc. The reason for associating him with Gower was no doubt the fact that in the French and English romances he is called Uryens or Uryence of Gorre or Gore. The distortion of Welsh names in the romances does not follow regular phonetic laws; and as *t* is lost before *r* in Breton (e.g. *breur* ‘brother’), as in French (e.g. *frère*), the reduction of *Catraeth* to *Gorre* or *Gore* is not a more violent change than, for example, the softening of *Gwalchmei* into *Gawain*. It was natural, and perhaps inevitable, that 15th-century manufacturers of pedigrees should take the *Gore* of the romances to be Gower.

Page 75, line 3; Godөu a Reget. Mr. Phillimore, in Owen’s *Pem.*, part iii, p. 284, writes, “As a man’s or woman’s name Rheged admits of being derived from a low Latin form of *Receptus, Recepta* (cf. *bedydd* from *baptisma*)”. This statement emboldens me to suggest an explanation of the name *Rheged* which I hesitated to insert at the end of this paragraph before it was printed off. I had not seen the above note, in which my idea of the derivation is anticipated and therefore to that extent confirmed.—There was no *pt* in British; original *pt* had become *kt* in Celtic; thus \**septm* became \**sektm*, giving Irish *secht*, Welsh *seith* ‘seven’. Latin *pt* becomes *fft* in *Eifft* ‘Egypt’, but generally it was treated as *tt*, a Low Latin form, and *tt* regularly becomes *th*, as in *ysgrythur* from *scriptura*, and *pregeth* from *precepta*. But in Low Latin this *tt* might be simplified, thus *setimus* occurs in the third century for *septimus* (Loth, *Mots Lat.*, p. 124): hence *bedydd* from \**baptidio*, and possibly *cyngyd* ‘thought’ from *conceptio*; thus *Rheged* may be from *receptus, recepta*, as suggested by Mr. Phillimore. Now this is the adjective commonly used to describe that part of the island which lies to the south of the Southern Wall; thus Orosius (quoted by Camden, *Brit.*, 1594, p. 607) writes: “*Receptam partem insulae a ceteris indomitis gentibus vallo distinguendam putavit (Severus)*” <note>Severus thought that the recovered part of the island should be separated from the other untamed tribes by a wall.</note>. Other writers use *receptae provinciae* <note>recovered provinces</note>, see *ibid.*, and cf. later editions. Nennius uses the latter expression: “Severus . . . . in order that he might make the *receptas provincias* <note>recovered provinces</note> safer from barbarian incursion, drew a wall and a rampart from sea to sea . . . ,” § 23. The adjective thus used to distinguish the territory defended by the Wall from that beyond it would naturally apply more especially to the district nearest the Wall; it is here that the distinction was chiefly felt; and it may be that *Godөu* ‘the forest’, or perhaps ‘the wild’, denoting the unre-

Page 77, line 5 from the bottom of the text; *Eiðin*. This is a late and incorrect form. The earliest example I have found of the mispronunciation *Eiðin* occurs in a poem by the fourteenth-century bard Rhisserdyn, *Myv.* 290 b, last line, where he seems to refer to Clydno Eidyn, compare 291 a. As shown by the spelling *Lliwelyδ* for *Llywelydd*, see above page 59, the sounds of Northern names had become uncertain before that period, and old spellings were often misread.

Page 92, footnote, line 10; for the obscure vowel *e*, written *y* in Welsh, read the obscure vowel *ə*, written *y* in Welsh. This was quite correct in the last proof that left my hands. I am, however, indebted to him for many small corrections of this kind, all of which, with this exception, were necessary and right.

Page 210, line 41 of the poem, second word. The second bracket enclosing the *f* has dropped out; read *Dy(f)wal*

Page 212, line 2; for *medwieth* read *medweith*

Page 221, footnote 4. Dr. Hugh Williams, in *Gildas*, page 77, sides with Zimmer against Rhys on the grounds that *insularis* is probably “intended to wound.” But Gildas is not subtle; and however violently he rails against Maelgwn, he clearly does not attempt to belittle his power. The meaning of *insularis* must be decided by that of *insula*. Not only is *Ynys* used traditionally in Welsh for Britain, but *insula* was so used in Latin long before Gildas’s time, as the quotation from Orosius in the above note on Godeu and Rheged shows. Gildas uses it in no other sense; in fact, Dr. Williams himself affirms that *insula* “in Gildas has no meaning except Britain,” page 48. If Gildas’s *insula* always means ‘Britain,’ I submit that his *insularis* must mean ‘British.’ Clearly *insularis draco* is only Gildas’s bombastic way of saying <term>dux Britanniarum</term>; and Rhys shows an insight which Zimmer and Dr. Williams lack when he says that “It was more congenial to” Gildas’s “style to describe him in that way than to call him simply *Dux Britanniae*,” *Welsh People*, page 107.

The author argues that "insularis" should be understood as "British," reflecting Maelgwn's status as a leader of Britain rather than being a derogatory term.</summary>
